<a name="secCORE"></a>

# ★ Core Concepts Every Finance Interview Tests

<div class="howto"><strong>Start here.</strong> These are the concepts that come up in almost every finance interview, whatever the role, analyst to associate, equity research to FP&amp;A to risk. Learn these cold and you can reason your way through most questions. The 1,064 Q&amp;A that follow go deeper by specialty; this section is the spine that ties them together.</div>

## 1. The Three Financial Statements and How They Link

This is the single most asked interview topic. You must be able to say it in 60 seconds.

- **Income Statement (P&L)** shows profitability over a period: Revenue minus expenses gives Net Income. It uses accrual accounting, so it records revenue when earned and expenses when incurred, not when cash moves.
- **Balance Sheet** is a snapshot at a point in time: Assets = Liabilities + Shareholders' Equity. It always balances.
- **Cash Flow Statement** reconciles Net Income to the actual cash that moved, in three buckets: Operating, Investing, Financing. It exists because profit is not cash.

**How they link (say this):** Net Income from the P&L flows to the top of Cash Flow from Operations and into Retained Earnings on the Balance Sheet. The Cash Flow Statement adjusts Net Income for non-cash items (depreciation) and working-capital changes; its bottom line, the net change in cash, updates the Cash line on the Balance Sheet. Depreciation on the P&L reduces the PP&E asset on the Balance Sheet. Debt raised or repaid hits Financing on the Cash Flow and the debt line on the Balance Sheet.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "Walk me through the three statements." Answer: name each, its purpose and time dimension, then the one sentence on how Net Income and cash tie them together. Then be ready for the follow-up in concept 2.</div>

## 2. The Classic Flow-Through: Depreciation of 100 (tax rate 30%)

The most common follow-up. Walk it slowly and it is easy.

- **Income Statement:** Depreciation of 100 is an expense, so pre-tax income falls 100. At a 30% tax rate, taxes fall 30, so Net Income falls **70**.
- **Cash Flow:** Start from Net Income down 70, then add back the 100 of depreciation because it is non-cash. Cash from Operations rises **+30** (the tax saving). Net change in cash is +30.
- **Balance Sheet:** Cash is up 30; PP&E is down 100 (the asset depreciated). The asset side nets to **-70**. On the other side, Retained Earnings is down 70 from lower Net Income. Both sides fall 70, so it **balances**.

<div class="keytake"><b>Why it matters:</b> Depreciation is a non-cash expense that actually creates cash through the tax shield. This one example proves you understand accrual vs cash and how the statements interlock.</div>

## 3. Working Capital and the Cash Conversion Cycle

**Working Capital = Current Assets - Current Liabilities.** In modeling we usually mean *operating* working capital: receivables + inventory - payables.

<div class="formula"><b>Cash Conversion Cycle (days)</b><br> CCC = DSO + DIO - DPO <div class="where">where: DSO = days sales outstanding (receivables), DIO = days inventory outstanding, DPO = days payables outstanding</div></div>

An **increase** in working capital (more receivables or inventory) is a **use of cash**; a decrease is a source of cash. Growing companies often consume cash even while profitable because they fund receivables and inventory. A shorter CCC frees up cash.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "If working capital goes up, what happens to cash?" It goes down. Retailers negotiate long payables and fast inventory turns to run a negative cycle (Amazon), effectively financing growth with suppliers' cash.</div>

## 4. Accrual vs Cash Accounting (why profit is not cash)

Accrual accounting matches revenue to when it is earned and expenses to when they are incurred, regardless of cash timing. This gives a truer picture of performance but means **Net Income can be high while cash is negative** (a company booking sales on credit) or the reverse. This gap is exactly why the Cash Flow Statement exists, and why analysts trust cash flow over reported earnings when the two diverge.

## 5. Enterprise Value vs Equity Value (the bridge)

<div class="formula"><b>The bridge</b><br> Enterprise Value = Equity Value + Total Debt + Preferred + Minority Interest - Cash &amp; Equivalents <div class="where">Equity Value (market cap) = share price x shares outstanding</div></div>

- **Equity Value** is what belongs to shareholders.
- **Enterprise Value** is the value of the whole operating business, what it would cost to buy the operations free of capital structure. You add debt (an acquirer assumes it) and subtract cash (an acquirer gets it, reducing net cost).

**Match the metric to the value:** EV pairs with pre-interest, pre-capital-structure metrics (EBIT, EBITDA, Revenue). Equity Value pairs with after-interest metrics (Net Income, EPS). So EV/EBITDA and P/E, never EV/Net Income.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "Why subtract cash?" Because cash could immediately be used to pay down the purchase price; you are buying the business, not its bank balance. "Why does EV use EBITDA and P/E use Net Income?" Capital-structure neutrality.</div>

## 6. The Three Valuation Methods and When to Use Each

| Method | What it is | Tends to give |
|---|---|---|
| **DCF** | Intrinsic: present value of future free cash flows | An intrinsic value, sensitive to assumptions |
| **Comparable Companies** | Relative: peer trading multiples (EV/EBITDA, P/E) | Current market view, a "trading" value |
| **Precedent Transactions** | Relative: multiples paid in past M&A deals | The highest, includes a control premium |

DCF is theoretically purest but assumption-driven. Comps reflect where the market prices peers today. Precedents reflect what acquirers actually paid (usually highest because of control premiums and synergies). Good analysts triangulate all three on a **football field** chart.

## 7. DCF Mechanics

Value a business as the present value of the cash it will generate.

<div class="formula"><b>Unlevered Free Cash Flow (FCFF)</b><br> FCFF = EBIT x (1 - tax) + D&amp;A - Capex - Change in Net Working Capital <div class="where">discount FCFF at WACC to get Enterprise Value</div></div>

<div class="formula"><b>Terminal Value (Gordon growth)</b><br> TV = FCF final x (1 + g) / (WACC - g) <div class="where">g = perpetual growth, must be below long-run GDP (2-3%). Alternative: exit-multiple method, TV = EBITDA x an EV/EBITDA multiple</div></div>

Steps: project 5 years of FCFF, compute a terminal value (Gordon or exit multiple), discount everything to today at WACC to get Enterprise Value, then bridge EV to Equity Value (concept 5) and divide by shares for intrinsic price. **Terminal value is usually 60-80% of the total**, so its assumptions dominate; always run a sensitivity on WACC and g.

- **FCFF** (unlevered) is discounted at WACC and gives Enterprise Value.
- **FCFE** (levered, after interest and debt flows) is discounted at cost of equity and gives Equity Value directly.

## 8. WACC and CAPM (the cost of capital)

<div class="formula"><b>WACC</b><br> WACC = (E/V) x Re + (D/V) x Rd x (1 - tax) <div class="where">E = equity value, D = debt, V = E + D; Re = cost of equity, Rd = cost of debt</div></div>

<div class="formula"><b>CAPM (cost of equity)</b><br> Re = Rf + Beta x (Rm - Rf) <div class="where">Rf = risk-free rate, Beta = sensitivity to the market, (Rm - Rf) = equity risk premium</div></div>

WACC is the blended required return of all capital providers, and the discount rate for unlevered cash flows. Debt is multiplied by (1 - tax) because **interest is tax-deductible** (the tax shield), which makes debt cheaper than equity. Cost of equity comes from CAPM: a stock with beta above 1 is more volatile than the market and demands a higher return.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "Why is debt cheaper than equity?" Two reasons: debt holders are senior and bear less risk, so demand a lower return; and interest is tax-deductible. "What happens to WACC as you add debt?" It falls at first (cheap debt), then rises as financial-distress and bankruptcy risk push up both Rd and Re.</div>

## 9. Time Value of Money, NPV and IRR

A rupee today is worth more than a rupee tomorrow because it can be invested. Everything in finance discounts future cash to the present.

<div class="formula"><b>Net Present Value</b><br> NPV = sum of [ CF_t / (1 + r)^t ] - initial investment <div class="where">accept a project if NPV &gt; 0</div></div>

- **IRR** is the discount rate that makes NPV = 0, the project's implied annualized return. Accept if IRR > hurdle rate (cost of capital).
- **NPV vs IRR conflict:** for mutually exclusive projects of different size or timing they can disagree; **trust NPV**, because it measures value created in absolute terms and assumes reinvestment at the cost of capital, whereas IRR assumes reinvestment at the IRR itself.

## 10. Key Ratios and the DuPont Identity

| Group | Ratios | Reads |
|---|---|---|
| **Liquidity** | Current, Quick (acid-test) | Can it pay short-term bills |
| **Leverage** | Debt/Equity, Debt/EBITDA, Interest Coverage | How much debt, can it service it |
| **Profitability** | Gross, Operating, Net margin; ROE, ROA, ROIC | How efficiently it earns |
| **Efficiency** | Asset turnover, inventory turns, DSO | How hard assets work |

<div class="formula"><b>DuPont: decomposing ROE</b><br> ROE = Net Margin x Asset Turnover x Equity Multiplier <div class="where">= (profitability) x (efficiency) x (leverage). Shows whether a high ROE comes from real margins or just from debt</div></div>

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "Two firms have the same ROE, are they equal?" Not necessarily, use DuPont: one may earn it through strong margins, the other by loading on leverage, which is far riskier.</div>

## 11. Capital Structure: Debt vs Equity

Debt is cheaper (senior claim, tax-deductible interest) but adds fixed obligations and bankruptcy risk. Equity is flexible (no repayment, no mandatory dividend) but dilutive and more expensive. **Modigliani-Miller** says that in a perfect, tax-free world capital structure does not affect firm value; adding the real-world frictions of the **tax shield** (favors debt) and **financial-distress costs** (limits debt) gives the trade-off theory: an optimal structure balances the tax benefit of debt against the rising cost of distress.

## 12. Multiples: P/E vs EV/EBITDA

- **P/E** = Price / EPS: simple, equity-level, but distorted by different debt loads, tax rates, and depreciation policies.
- **EV/EBITDA**: capital-structure and depreciation neutral, so it is better for comparing companies with different leverage or for M&A. Preferred in most cross-company comparison.
- **PEG** = P/E / growth: adjusts the P/E for how fast earnings grow, so a high P/E can still be cheap if growth is high.

## 13. Risk and Return

- **Total risk = systematic + unsystematic.** Unsystematic (company-specific) risk is diversifiable, spread across many stocks and it washes out. Systematic (market) risk cannot be diversified away and is what investors are paid to bear, captured by **beta**.
- **Diversification** works because assets are not perfectly correlated; combining them lowers portfolio volatility for a given return (Markowitz).

<div class="formula"><b>Sharpe Ratio</b><br> Sharpe = (Return of portfolio - Rf) / Standard deviation of portfolio <div class="where">excess return per unit of total risk; higher is better risk-adjusted performance</div></div>

## 14. Bonds: the Essentials

- **Price and yield move inversely.** When market rates rise, existing bonds with lower coupons become less attractive, so their price falls.
- **Duration** measures interest-rate sensitivity: a duration of 5 means roughly a 5% price fall for a 1% rise in yield. Longer maturity and lower coupon mean higher duration.
- **Credit spread** is the extra yield over the risk-free rate that compensates for default risk; it widens when the economy weakens.

<div class="formula"><b>Price sensitivity</b><br> Percentage price change is approximately -Duration x change in yield <div class="where">convexity is the second-order correction for large yield moves</div></div>

## 15. Accretion / Dilution (M&A basics)

A deal is **accretive** if the acquirer's post-deal EPS rises, **dilutive** if it falls. Quick rule for an all-stock deal: if the acquirer's **P/E is higher than the target's P/E, the deal is accretive** (you are buying cheaper earnings with expensive shares), and dilutive if lower. Cash and debt financing are usually more accretive than stock because cash earns little and debt interest is cheap and tax-deductible, though they raise risk.

## 16. Non-Cash Items and Deferred Taxes

Depreciation and amortization, stock-based compensation, and impairments reduce accounting profit but are **added back** in cash flow because no cash left. **Deferred tax** arises when book and tax accounting differ in timing (for example, accelerated depreciation for tax): the company reports a tax expense it has not yet paid, creating a deferred tax liability that reverses later.

## 17. How to Pitch a Stock (structure to have ready)

Interviewers in any markets-facing role ask "pitch me a stock." Use this skeleton: **(1) Recommendation** (buy/sell and target), **(2) Business** in one line, **(3) Thesis**, two or three reasons the market is mispricing it, **(4) Valuation** (your DCF or multiple vs the market), **(5) Catalysts** (what closes the gap), **(6) Risks** and what would make you wrong. Have one long and one short idea prepared.

## 18. The Behavioral Core

Every interview also tests fit. Prepare crisp, structured answers for: "**Walk me through your resume**" (a 90-second narrative, not a list), "**Why finance / why this role**", "**Tell me about a time you...**" (use STAR: Situation, Task, Action, Result), and a few smart **questions for them**. Know your own resume and projects cold, every number on them is fair game.

<!-- secMORE -->
## 19. EBITDA: What It Is and Its Limits

**EBITDA = Earnings Before Interest, Taxes, Depreciation and Amortization.** It approximates core operating profitability while stripping out capital structure (interest), tax regimes, and non-cash D&A, so you can compare the raw operating performance of companies with different debt and asset bases. It drives EV/EBITDA and the leverage ratio Debt/EBITDA.

**Its limits (say these):** EBITDA is **not** cash flow. It ignores capex, working-capital needs, and cash taxes, all real costs. Capital-intensive businesses look deceptively strong on EBITDA because their heavy capex is invisible.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "Is EBITDA a good proxy for cash flow?" No, it excludes capex, working-capital changes, and taxes. That is the whole reason free cash flow exists.</div>

## 20. The Free Cash Flow Family

<div class="formula"><b>FCFF (unlevered)</b> = EBIT x (1 - t) + D&amp;A - Capex - Change in NWC &nbsp; &rarr; discount at <b>WACC</b> &rarr; Enterprise Value<br><b>FCFE (levered)</b> = Net Income + D&amp;A - Capex - Change in NWC + Net Borrowing &nbsp; &rarr; discount at <b>cost of equity</b> &rarr; Equity Value</div>

The one rule that trips people up: **match the cash flow to the discount rate.** Unlevered FCFF pairs with WACC and gives Enterprise Value; levered FCFE pairs with cost of equity and gives Equity Value directly. And FCF is not EBITDA, FCF subtracts the capex, working capital, and taxes that EBITDA leaves out.

## 21. Return on Capital and Value Creation

| Metric | Formula | Reads |
|---|---|---|
| **ROE** | Net Income / Equity | Return to shareholders (flattered by leverage) |
| **ROA** | Net Income / Assets | Asset efficiency, ignores capital structure |
| **ROIC** | NOPAT / Invested Capital | Return on all invested capital |

A company **creates value only when ROIC > WACC**, it earns more than the cost of the capital it deploys. If ROIC is below WACC, growth actually **destroys** value.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "How do you know a company is creating value?" The ROIC-minus-WACC spread. Positive and widening is the mark of a good business.</div>

## 22. Goodwill and Intangibles

**Goodwill = purchase price - fair value of net identifiable assets** acquired in an acquisition. It captures brand, customer relationships, and expected synergies that are not separately recorded. Under IFRS and US GAAP it is **not amortized** but tested annually for **impairment**; a write-down is a non-cash hit to the P&L. Other intangibles (patents, software, licences) are typically amortized over their useful life.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "A firm pays 100 for a business whose net assets are worth 70. What goes on the balance sheet?" 30 of goodwill.</div>

## 23. Revenue Recognition and Deferred Revenue

Revenue is recognized when it is **earned** (goods or services delivered), not when cash arrives, that is accrual accounting. Cash collected **before** delivery (an annual software subscription paid upfront) is **deferred revenue, a liability**, released to the P&L as the service is delivered. This is a classic divergence between cash and reported revenue and a common area for manipulation (premature recognition, channel stuffing).

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "Is deferred revenue an asset or a liability?" A liability, the company still owes the service it was paid for.</div>

## 24. Dividends vs Share Buybacks

Both return cash to shareholders. A **dividend** pays cash per share (cash and retained earnings fall). A **buyback** repurchases shares (cash and equity fall, share count shrinks, so EPS rises). Buybacks are more tax-efficient and flexible; dividends signal a stable, ongoing commitment. Neither creates value on its own, it depends on whether the company had higher-return uses for the cash.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "How does a buyback flow through the statements?" Cash down (financing outflow), treasury stock up so equity down, fewer shares outstanding so EPS up.</div>

## 25. LBO Basics

In a **leveraged buyout** a private-equity firm buys a company funded mostly with **debt** (often 60-70%), and the target's own cash flows service that debt. Returns come from three levers: **deleveraging** (cash flow pays down debt, growing the equity slice), **EBITDA growth**, and **multiple expansion** (exiting at a higher multiple). Measured by **IRR** and **MOIC** (money on money). A "Sources and Uses" table must balance.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "What makes a good LBO candidate?" Stable, predictable cash flows, low existing debt, modest capex, and strong assets to borrow against.</div>

## 26. Diluted Shares, the Treasury Stock Method and Net Debt

**Diluted shares** add the effect of in-the-money options, warrants, and convertibles. The **Treasury Stock Method** assumes option holders exercise and the company uses the proceeds to buy back shares at the market price; net new shares = options minus shares repurchased. **Net Debt = Total Debt - Cash.** Equity value uses the diluted share count; enterprise value uses net debt.

## 27. Levered vs Unlevered Beta

Observed (equity/levered) beta includes the company's financial risk from debt. To compare pure **business risk** across peers or to reweight for a target capital structure, you **unlever** it and then **relever**.

<div class="formula"><b>Hamada</b><br> Unlevered beta = Levered beta / [ 1 + (1 - t) x (D/E) ] <div class="where">strip out leverage to get asset beta, then relever at the target D/E for CAPM</div></div>

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "Why unlever beta?" To remove capital-structure differences so peers are comparable, then relever to the company you are valuing.</div>

## 28. Operating Leverage, Financial Leverage and Break-Even

**Operating leverage** is the fixed-vs-variable cost mix: high fixed costs make profit swing more for a given revenue change. **Financial leverage** is the use of debt, which amplifies both returns and risk.

<div class="formula"><b>Break-even (units)</b> = Fixed Costs / Contribution Margin per unit <div class="where">Contribution Margin = Price - Variable Cost per unit</div></div>

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "Two firms, same revenue, one with high fixed costs?" The high-fixed-cost firm has higher operating leverage, more profit upside when sales rise and more pain when they fall.</div>

## 29. Interest Rates and the Central Bank

The central bank (RBI in India, the Fed in the US) sets the **policy rate** to balance inflation and growth. **Higher rates raise discount rates (WACC), which lowers the present value of future cash flows**, so valuations fall, most sharply for long-duration growth stocks whose value sits far in the future. Higher rates also raise borrowing costs and cool demand. Rate cuts do the reverse.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "Why do rising rates hit growth stocks hardest?" Their cash flows are far out, so a higher discount rate compresses them most.</div>

## 30. Inflation: Real vs Nominal

**Nominal** figures include inflation; **real** figures strip it out.

<div class="formula"><b>Fisher relation</b><br> Real rate is approximately Nominal rate - Inflation <div class="where">more precisely: (1 + nominal) = (1 + real) x (1 + inflation)</div></div>

Inflation erodes purchasing power, pushes up nominal interest rates, helps borrowers (they repay in cheaper money) and hurts lenders and fixed-rate bondholders. Rising inflation expectations push bond prices down.

## 31. Credit Analysis Essentials

Lenders weigh the **5 Cs**: Character, Capacity, Capital, Collateral, Conditions. The core numbers:

<div class="formula"><b>Coverage &amp; serviceability</b><br> Interest Coverage = EBIT / Interest &nbsp;&bull;&nbsp; Leverage = Debt / EBITDA &nbsp;&bull;&nbsp; DSCR = Cash available for debt service / Debt service <div class="where">DSCR above 1 means the borrower generates enough to cover principal + interest</div></div>

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "How do you judge if a firm can take on more debt?" Compare leverage and coverage to peers and covenants, and test debt capacity under a stressed cash-flow case.</div>

## 32. Liquidity vs Solvency

**Liquidity** is the ability to meet short-term obligations (current and quick ratios, cash on hand). **Solvency** is the ability to meet long-term obligations (leverage, the equity cushion). They are different: a **profitable company can still go bankrupt** if it runs out of cash to pay bills as they fall due, a liquidity crisis, even while solvent on paper.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "Can a profitable company go bankrupt?" Yes, if it is illiquid. Profit is accrual; bankruptcy is about cash.</div>

## 33. Financial Modeling Best Practices

A clean, auditable model beats a clever one. The principles interviewers look for: separate **inputs/assumptions** from **calculations**; drive the model off a few clear **drivers**; keep each row's formula consistent across columns; build in **checks** (the balance sheet must balance, cash must tie to the cash-flow statement); handle **circularity** (interest depends on debt, which depends on cash, which depends on interest) with an iterative switch or a circularity breaker; label units; and make scenarios toggle-able.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "Where is the circular reference in a 3-statement model?" Interest expense &harr; debt balance &harr; cash. Solved with iterative calculation or a copy-paste breaker.</div>

## 34. Performance Metrics (Portfolio and Investing)

| Metric | Formula | Measures |
|---|---|---|
| **Alpha** | Return above CAPM / benchmark | Skill, value added |
| **Beta** | Sensitivity to the market | Systematic risk |
| **Sharpe** | (Return - Rf) / total volatility | Return per unit of total risk |
| **Treynor** | (Return - Rf) / beta | Return per unit of market risk |
| **Information Ratio** | Active return / tracking error | Consistency of outperformance |

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> Sharpe uses total risk (standard deviation); Treynor uses only systematic risk (beta). Use Treynor when the portfolio is one part of a diversified whole.</div>

<!-- secACCT -->
<div class="howto"><strong>Accounting deep-dive.</strong> Accounting is the language every finance interview is conducted in. These are the accounting mechanics that come up again and again, whatever the role, from a fund accountant to an equity analyst.</div>

## 35. The Accounting Equation and Double-Entry

<div class="formula"><b>The identity behind the balance sheet</b><br> Assets = Liabilities + Shareholders' Equity</div>

Every transaction has two equal sides (double-entry): a **debit** and a matching **credit**, which keeps the equation in balance. Debits increase assets and expenses; credits increase liabilities, equity, and revenue.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "A company buys 100 of inventory on credit, what happens?" Inventory (asset) up 100 and Accounts Payable (liability) up 100. Both sides rise, the equation still balances.</div>

## 36. Adjusting Entries: Accruals vs Deferrals

At period end, accrual accounting needs adjusting entries so revenue and expense fall in the right period. Four types:

| Type | Situation | Creates |
|---|---|---|
| **Accrued revenue** | Earned, not yet billed | An asset (receivable) |
| **Accrued expense** | Incurred, not yet paid (e.g. wages) | A liability (payable) |
| **Prepaid expense** | Paid in advance (e.g. insurance) | An asset, expensed over time |
| **Unearned (deferred) revenue** | Cash received before delivery | A liability |

## 37. Inventory: FIFO vs LIFO vs Weighted Average

The cost-flow assumption decides which costs go to COGS.

- **FIFO** (first in, first out): oldest costs to COGS. In inflation this gives **lower COGS, higher profit, higher tax**, and a higher ending inventory value.
- **LIFO** (last in, first out): newest costs to COGS. In inflation this gives **higher COGS, lower profit, lower tax**. LIFO is allowed under **US GAAP but banned under IFRS**.
- **Weighted average**: blends all unit costs.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "In an inflationary period, which method shows higher profit?" FIFO. "Which is not allowed under IFRS?" LIFO.</div>

## 38. Depreciation and Amortization Methods

Depreciation spreads a tangible asset's cost over its useful life; amortization does the same for intangibles; both are **non-cash**. **Straight-line** charges an equal amount each year. **Accelerated** methods (written-down-value, double-declining) charge more early. Firms often use straight-line for the books and an accelerated method for tax.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "Why use accelerated depreciation for tax but straight-line for books?" Higher tax depreciation early lowers taxable income now, deferring tax, a time-value benefit. The gap creates a deferred tax liability (topic 41).</div>

## 39. Capitalize vs Expense (Capex vs Opex)

A cost whose benefit extends beyond the current period is **capitalized** (recorded as an asset and depreciated over time). A cost consumed now is **expensed** immediately. Capitalizing lifts current profit (the cost is spread out) and creates an asset plus future depreciation. **Aggressively capitalizing costs that should be expensed is a classic fraud red flag** (WorldCom).

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "A company capitalizes a cost it should have expensed, what happens?" Current profit and assets are overstated; cash flow classification shifts from operating to investing.</div>

## 40. Accounts Receivable and the Allowance for Doubtful Accounts

Receivables are amounts customers owe. Because some will not pay, firms record an **allowance for doubtful accounts** (a contra-asset) and a matching **bad-debt expense**, recognizing the likely loss in the same period as the sale (prudence). **Net receivables = gross receivables - allowance.** Writing off a specific bad debt later reduces both gross AR and the allowance, with no further P&L hit because the expense was already taken.

## 41. Deferred Tax Assets and Liabilities

Because book accounting and tax rules differ in **timing**, the tax expense shown on the P&L differs from tax actually paid; the gap sits on the balance sheet.

- **Deferred Tax Liability (DTL):** book profit exceeds taxable profit now (e.g. accelerated tax depreciation), so more tax will be paid later.
- **Deferred Tax Asset (DTA):** more tax is paid now than the books imply (e.g. carryforward losses, warranty provisions), a future tax saving.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "Accelerated depreciation for tax creates what?" A deferred tax liability that reverses in later years.</div>

## 42. Provisions, Contingent Liabilities and Reserves

- A **provision** is a liability of uncertain timing or amount, recognized when an outflow is **probable and can be estimated** (warranty costs, restructuring).
- A **contingent liability** is possible but not probable or not estimable, so it is **disclosed in the notes, not booked** (a pending lawsuit).
- A **reserve** is an appropriation of retained earnings within equity, not an expense.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "A lawsuit you might lose, provision or disclosure?" If loss is probable and estimable, provide for it; if only possible, disclose it.</div>

## 43. Lease Accounting: Operating vs Finance

A **finance (capital) lease** transfers substantially all the risks and rewards of ownership, so the lessee records an asset and a liability and books depreciation plus interest. An **operating lease** was historically off balance sheet (just rent expense). Under **IFRS 16 / ASC 842**, lessees now bring almost all leases onto the balance sheet as a **right-of-use asset and a lease liability**.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "What changed with the new lease standards?" Operating leases moved onto the balance sheet, raising reported assets and debt-like liabilities.</div>

## 44. Stock-Based Compensation

Stock-based comp (options, RSUs) is a **real, non-cash expense** on the P&L, employees are paid in equity. On the cash flow statement it is **added back** (no cash left); on the balance sheet it increases paid-in capital; and it increases the **diluted share count**, so it dilutes existing shareholders.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "Is SBC a real expense?" Yes, it transfers value to employees and dilutes owners, even though no cash goes out. Ignoring it flatters adjusted earnings.</div>

## 45. Consolidation, Minority Interest and the Equity Method

Ownership drives the accounting:

| Stake | Treatment |
|---|---|
| Below 20% | Cost / fair value |
| 20-50% (significant influence) | **Equity method**: book your share of the investee's profit |
| Above 50% (control) | **Full consolidation**: combine 100% of the subsidiary, then subtract the share you do not own as **minority (non-controlling) interest** |

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "You own 30% of a company, how do you account for it?" Equity method. "Why does enterprise value add minority interest?" Because consolidated EBITDA includes 100% of the subsidiary, so EV must reflect the whole.</div>

## 46. Basic vs Diluted EPS

<div class="formula"><b>Basic EPS</b> = (Net Income - Preferred Dividends) / Weighted-average shares<br><b>Diluted EPS</b> also adds shares from in-the-money options, warrants and convertibles (via the treasury stock method) <div class="where">so diluted EPS is always less than or equal to basic EPS; analysts use diluted</div></div>

## 47. Impairment of Assets

When an asset's **carrying value exceeds its recoverable amount** (what it can earn or be sold for), it is written down. The impairment is a **non-cash expense** on the P&L that also reduces the asset and equity. Goodwill is tested for impairment annually. Under US GAAP most impairments are **not reversed**; IFRS allows reversal for some assets but never for goodwill.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "How does a goodwill impairment flow through?" Net income falls (P&L expense), the goodwill asset and equity fall, and it is added back on the cash flow as non-cash.</div>

## 48. GAAP vs IFRS: Key Differences

US GAAP is **rules-based**; IFRS is **principles-based**. The differences interviewers expect you to know:

| Area | US GAAP | IFRS |
|---|---|---|
| Inventory | LIFO allowed | **LIFO banned** |
| PP&E | Historical cost | Revaluation to fair value allowed |
| Development costs | Mostly expensed | Can be capitalized if criteria met |
| Impairment reversal | Not allowed | Allowed (except goodwill) |

## 49. Direct vs Indirect Cash Flow Statement

The operating section can be built two ways. The **indirect method** (used almost everywhere) starts from **Net Income** and adjusts for non-cash items and working-capital changes. The **direct method** lists actual cash receipts and payments. Both produce the **same operating cash figure**; indirect is preferred because it reconciles profit to cash and is easy to build from the ledger.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "Which cash-flow method is more common and why?" Indirect, because it ties net income to cash and is simpler to prepare.</div>

<!-- secMKTS -->
<div class="howto"><strong>Markets, macro and institutions.</strong> Beyond accounting and valuation, interviewers test whether you understand how markets, derivatives, the economy, and banks actually work. These are the broad must-knows for any markets-facing or finance role.</div>

## 50. Primary vs Secondary Markets and the IPO Process

The **primary market** is where securities are issued for the first time and the **company raises capital** (IPO, follow-on offer, rights issue, bond issuance). The **secondary market** is where investors trade existing securities among themselves on an exchange, the company receives no new money. An IPO runs: appoint bankers, file the prospectus (a DRHP with SEBI in India), price the shares via book-building, then list.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "When Reliance shares trade on the NSE, does Reliance get the cash?" No, that is the secondary market. The company only raised money at issuance.</div>

## 51. How Exchanges Work: Bid-Ask, Liquidity and Order Types

Buyers post **bids**, sellers post **asks**; the **bid-ask spread** between them is a cost of trading, a narrower spread means more **liquidity** (how easily you can trade without moving the price). Order types: a **market order** executes now at the best available price, a **limit order** only at a set price or better, and a **stop-loss** triggers once a level is hit. **Market makers** quote both sides to supply liquidity and earn the spread.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "What does a wide bid-ask spread tell you?" The security is illiquid and expensive to trade.</div>

## 52. Long, Short and Short Selling

Going **long** means buying, you profit if the price rises. Going **short** means selling borrowed shares hoping to buy them back cheaper; you profit if the price falls. A long's maximum loss is what you invested, but a **short's loss is theoretically unlimited** because the price can rise without bound. Shorts pay a borrow fee and can be caught in a short squeeze.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "What is the maximum loss on a short position?" Unlimited, versus a capped loss on a long.</div>

## 53. Futures vs Forwards

Both lock in a price today for a transaction in the future.

| | Forward | Future |
|---|---|---|
| Traded | Private, OTC | Exchange-traded |
| Terms | Customized | Standardized |
| Counterparty risk | Yes | Minimal (clearinghouse) |
| Settlement | At maturity | Marked-to-market daily, margin |

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> The three differences to name: standardization, daily mark-to-market, and a clearinghouse that removes counterparty risk.</div>

## 54. Options: Calls, Puts, Intrinsic vs Time Value

A **call** is the right (not the obligation) to **buy** at the strike; a **put** is the right to **sell**. The buyer pays a premium and can only lose that premium. Option value has two parts: **intrinsic value** (how in-the-money it is now, max(S - K, 0) for a call) plus **time value** (the extra paid for the chance it moves further before expiry). Time value decays to zero at expiration, that decay is **theta**.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "Most a call buyer can lose?" The premium. "An option with no intrinsic value still has a price, why?" Time value, the chance it finishes in the money.</div>

## 55. Hedging, Speculation and Arbitrage

- **Hedging:** taking an offsetting position to reduce risk (a farmer shorting wheat futures to lock a price).
- **Speculation:** taking on risk deliberately for expected profit (betting on direction).
- **Arbitrage:** a near risk-free profit from the same asset being priced differently in two markets, quickly competed away.

## 56. Interest Rate Swaps

Two parties exchange interest streams on a **notional** amount: typically one pays **fixed** and receives **floating**, the other the reverse. It is used to convert floating-rate debt to fixed (or vice versa) to manage interest-rate risk. Only the **net interest** changes hands; the notional itself is never exchanged.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "Why pay fixed in a swap?" To hedge floating-rate borrowing against rising rates by locking in a fixed cost.</div>

## 57. The Yield Curve

The yield curve plots yield against maturity for one issuer, usually government bonds. **Normal** is upward-sloping (longer maturities pay more for time and risk). An **inverted** curve, where short rates exceed long rates, has historically been a **recession signal**: the market expects the central bank to cut rates as growth slows.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "What does an inverted yield curve suggest?" A likely economic slowdown or recession ahead.</div>

## 58. Monetary vs Fiscal Policy

**Monetary policy** is run by the central bank, managing interest rates, open-market operations, and reserve requirements (CRR/SLR) to steer inflation and growth. **Fiscal policy** is run by the government, using taxation and public spending. The two can reinforce or offset each other.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> Know the owners: rates and money supply belong to the central bank; taxes and spending to the government.</div>

## 59. GDP, the Business Cycle and Leading Indicators

<div class="formula"><b>GDP (expenditure)</b> = Consumption + Investment + Government spending + Net exports</div>

Economies move through **expansion, peak, contraction (recession), trough**. **Leading indicators** turn before the economy (yield curve, PMI, new orders, building permits, equities); **lagging indicators** turn after (unemployment). The rule of thumb for recession is two consecutive quarters of falling real GDP.

## 60. Exchange Rates and the Current Account

An exchange rate is the price of one currency in another, driven by interest-rate differentials, inflation, and trade and capital flows. A **current-account deficit** (importing more than exporting) tends to weaken a currency. **Depreciation** helps exporters and hurts importers, and it raises imported inflation.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "A central bank hikes rates, what happens to its currency?" It usually appreciates, higher yields attract foreign capital.</div>

## 61. The Four Types of Financial Risk

| Risk | What it is |
|---|---|
| **Market** | Losses from moving prices: equity, interest-rate, FX, commodity |
| **Credit** | A borrower or counterparty defaults |
| **Liquidity** | Cannot trade or fund a position without a large loss |
| **Operational** | Failures of people, process, systems, or external events |

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "A bank cannot sell a bond without a steep price cut, which risk?" Liquidity risk.</div>

## 62. Working Capital Management

Managing the short-term operating cycle to stay liquid without trapping cash: collect receivables faster (lower DSO), turn inventory quicker (lower DIO), and stretch payables sensibly (higher DPO), which shortens the cash conversion cycle. Too little working capital risks a liquidity crunch; too much wastes capital that could earn a return.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "Levers to free up cash?" Cut DSO and DIO, extend DPO.</div>

## 63. Dividend Policy, Signaling and the Agency Problem

Firms decide how much profit to return versus reinvest. Starting or raising a dividend **signals** confidence in stable future earnings, and cutting it signals distress, which is why the market often punishes a cut sharply. The **agency problem** is the conflict between managers (agents) and shareholders (principals); returning cash through dividends and buybacks reduces the free cash managers might waste, helping align their interests alongside governance and incentive pay.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "Why does a dividend cut hit the stock hard?" It signals management expects weaker earnings, not just less cash returned.</div>

## 64. How Banks Make Money (and Their Key Ratios)

A bank's core profit is the **Net Interest Margin (NIM)** = interest earned on loans minus interest paid on deposits, plus fee income. **CASA** (current and savings account) deposits are cheap funding, so a high CASA ratio lifts margins. Health is judged by the **Capital Adequacy Ratio (CAR)** under Basel and by asset quality through **non-performing assets (the gross NPA ratio)**.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "How does a bank make money?" Mainly the net interest margin; a high CASA ratio and low NPAs mark a strong bank.</div>

<!-- secROUND -->
<div class="howto"><strong>Rounding out the toolkit.</strong> Statement analysis, deals, funds, statistics, behavioral finance, ESG, fintech and the Indian market, plus how to handle behavioral and guesstimate questions. This block covers the remaining ground interviewers probe across roles.</div>

## 65. Financial Statement Analysis

Three lenses beyond the raw numbers: **common-size (vertical)** analysis expresses each line as a percent of a base (revenue on the P&L, total assets on the balance sheet) so firms of different size compare; **horizontal (trend)** analysis tracks growth across years; and **ratio analysis** (topic 10). **Earnings-quality red flags:** profit rising while operating cash flow falls, receivables growing faster than sales, recurring "one-off" adjustments, and a rising DSO.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "Net income is up but operating cash flow is down, should you worry?" Yes, a classic red flag for aggressive revenue recognition or a working-capital build.</div>

## 66. Types of M&A, Synergies and Why Deals Fail

Deals are **horizontal** (same industry), **vertical** (up or down the supply chain), or **conglomerate** (unrelated). **Synergies** are cost (remove duplication, usually credible) or revenue (cross-sell, usually optimistic). Most acquisitions disappoint because of **overpayment** (a big control premium), **overestimated synergies**, and **integration and culture** problems.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "Why do most acquisitions destroy value?" Overpaying and over-promising synergies, then failing to integrate.</div>

## 67. Private Equity vs Venture Capital

| | Private Equity | Venture Capital |
|---|---|---|
| Stage | Mature, cash-generative firms | Early-stage startups |
| Stake | Majority / control | Minority |
| Leverage | Heavy (LBO) | None |
| Return driver | Cash flow, deleveraging, operations | A few big winners fund the losses |

## 68. Mutual Funds, ETFs and NAV

A **mutual fund** pools investors' money into a managed portfolio; you buy or redeem at the end-of-day **NAV = (assets - liabilities) / units**. An **ETF** is a similar basket but **trades intraday on an exchange** like a stock. **Active** funds try to beat a benchmark (higher fees); **passive / index** funds and most ETFs simply track it (low fees). A **SIP** invests a fixed amount regularly, averaging the entry price over time.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "ETF vs mutual fund?" ETFs trade intraday at market price; open-end mutual funds transact once a day at NAV.</div>

## 69. Credit Ratings and the Agencies

Agencies (S&P, Moody's, Fitch globally; CRISIL, ICRA in India) grade creditworthiness. **Investment grade** is BBB-/Baa3 and above; **speculative or "junk"** is below. A **downgrade raises borrowing costs and widens spreads**, and can force funds with rating mandates to sell.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> Know the investment-grade cutoff (BBB-) and that a downgrade widens credit spreads.</div>

## 70. Statistics for Finance

**Mean** is the average; **standard deviation** measures dispersion and is the standard proxy for **risk/volatility**; **variance** is its square. Under a **normal distribution** about 68% of outcomes fall within 1 standard deviation and 95% within 2. **Correlation** (-1 to +1) measures co-movement but is **not causation**. A **regression** fits a line to data, beta is the slope of a stock's returns on the market's. Real markets have **skew and fat tails**, so they are riskier than a normal assumes.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "What does standard deviation measure in finance?" Volatility, the usual proxy for total risk.</div>

## 71. Behavioral Finance: Common Biases

Markets are not fully rational. Key biases: **overconfidence**, **anchoring** (fixating on a reference number), **loss aversion** (losses hurt roughly twice as much as equal gains), **herding**, **recency bias**, **confirmation bias**, and **mental accounting**. They help explain bubbles, momentum, and why investors sell winners too early and cling to losers.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "Give an example of a bias in action." Loss aversion, holding a losing stock hoping to break even instead of cutting it.</div>

## 72. ESG and Sustainable Finance

**ESG = Environmental, Social, Governance** factors used to judge non-financial risk and impact. Investors integrate ESG to manage long-term risks (regulation, stranded assets, reputation) that standard financials miss. Instruments include **green bonds** and **sustainability-linked loans**. Live debates: **greenwashing** and inconsistent ratings. Disclosure is increasingly mandated (BRSR in India).

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "Why do investors care about ESG?" It flags long-term risks not visible in the financials and increasingly affects the cost of capital.</div>

## 73. Fintech, Digital Payments and Blockchain/Crypto

**Fintech** is technology reshaping finance: UPI and digital payments, neobanks, robo-advisors, digital lending, and open banking. **Blockchain** is a shared, tamper-evident ledger; **cryptocurrencies** (Bitcoin) are assets recorded on one; **stablecoins** peg to a fiat currency; **CBDCs** are central-bank digital currencies (the digital rupee). Know the promise (speed, access, lower cost) and the risks (volatility, fraud, unclear regulation).

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> Explain a blockchain in one line, "a shared, tamper-evident ledger," and UPI's impact on Indian payments.</div>

## 74. Indian Market Structure and Regulation

Regulators: **SEBI** (securities), **RBI** (banking and monetary policy), **IRDAI** (insurance). Exchanges: **NSE and BSE**, with benchmark indices **Nifty 50 and Sensex**. Shares are held in **demat** form through depositories (NSDL, CDSL). India settles trades at **T+1**, among the fastest in the world. Segments: cash, F&O, currency, and commodities.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> Know SEBI's role, the two exchanges and indices, demat, and T+1 settlement.</div>

## 75. Market Sizing and Guesstimates

Case-style questions ("how many credit cards are there in India?") test **structured thinking, not a precise number**. Approach: state your assumptions, break the problem down **top-down** (population to segments to penetration to per-capita) or bottom-up, do round-number arithmetic, then **sanity-check** the answer. Talk through the structure out loud.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> They grade the framework and assumptions, not the exact figure. Show clean structure and reasonable numbers.</div>

## 76. The Behavioral Interview: Fit and STAR

Every process tests fit. Prepare tight answers for: **walk me through your resume** (a 90-second narrative), **why finance / why this firm / why this role**, **strengths and weaknesses**, a **failure or conflict**, and **where you see yourself**. Use **STAR** (Situation, Task, Action, Result) for "tell me about a time" questions, and bring thoughtful questions to ask them.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> Structure and specificity beat generic enthusiasm. Tie every answer back to the role and know your own resume cold.</div>

## 77. Bond Types and Features

Core features: **face value, coupon** (fixed, floating, or zero), **maturity**, and **issuer**. Types: **government** (sovereign, safest), **corporate** (credit risk), **zero-coupon** (no coupon, issued at a discount), **convertible** (can convert to equity), **inflation-linked** (principal indexed), and **callable/putable**. Seniority and whether the bond is secured drive recovery in a default.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "What is a zero-coupon bond?" It pays no coupon, is issued below par, and redeems at par, the return is the discount.</div>

## 78. Corporate Governance

The system of rules and incentives that aligns management with shareholders and other stakeholders: an **independent board**, **audit and remuneration committees**, separation of chairman and CEO, transparent disclosure, and credible external auditors. Weak governance (dominant promoters, related-party abuse, rubber-stamp boards) is a major risk, especially in emerging markets. It is the practical answer to the agency problem (topic 63).

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> Name mechanisms that protect minority shareholders, independent directors, an audit committee, and full disclosure.</div>

## 79. Securitization and Asset-Backed Securities

Securitization pools illiquid loans (mortgages, auto loans, receivables) and sells **tradable securities backed by their cash flows**, usually sliced into **tranches** by risk (senior down to equity). It frees the originator's capital and transfers risk to investors. Opaque, mispriced mortgage-backed securities and CDOs were at the center of the **2008 financial crisis**.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> Securitization turns illiquid loans into tradable, tranched securities, know its role in 2008.</div>

<!-- secINSTN -->
<div class="howto"><strong>Institutional and specialist topics.</strong> Money markets, credit derivatives, alternatives, insurance, bank regulation, compliance, and corporate actions, the deeper, institution-specific ground that comes up for treasury, banking, risk, and markets roles.</div>

## 80. Money Market Instruments

Short-term (under one year), highly liquid, low-risk debt used to fund operations and park cash:

- **Treasury bills:** government paper, issued at a discount with no coupon.
- **Commercial paper:** unsecured short-term corporate note.
- **Certificates of deposit:** issued by banks.
- **Call / notice money:** interbank overnight lending.

Yields track the policy rate.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "What is a T-bill?" A short-term government security issued below face value; the return is the discount, not a coupon.</div>

## 81. Repo, Reverse Repo and the Central Bank's Liquidity Tools

A **repo** is a sale of securities with an agreement to buy them back shortly, effectively a **collateralized short-term loan**. The **repo rate** is the rate at which the RBI lends to banks (injecting liquidity); **reverse repo** absorbs it. **CRR** (cash reserve ratio) and **SLR** (statutory liquidity ratio) are reserves banks must hold. Together they steer money supply and short-term rates.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "The central bank raises the repo rate, what happens?" Borrowing gets costlier, liquidity tightens, aiming to cool inflation.</div>

## 82. Credit Default Swaps and Credit Derivatives

A **CDS** is insurance against a borrower defaulting: the buyer pays a periodic premium (the **spread**) and receives a payout if a credit event occurs. It is used to **hedge** credit exposure or to **speculate** on a name's creditworthiness. CDS spreads are a live market read on default risk. Naked CDS positions amplified the 2008 crisis (AIG).

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "What does a widening CDS spread tell you?" The market sees rising default risk for that reference entity.</div>

## 83. Hedge Funds and Their Strategies

Lightly regulated pooled vehicles for sophisticated investors, typically charging **"2 and 20"** (2% management + 20% performance) and often using leverage, short-selling, and derivatives. Strategies: **long/short equity, global macro, event-driven / merger arbitrage, relative-value arbitrage, and quant**. They target **absolute** returns, not benchmark-relative ones.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> Know the "2 and 20" fee model and that hedge funds can go short and chase absolute returns.</div>

## 84. Alternative Investments

Assets beyond stocks, bonds, and cash: **private equity, venture capital, hedge funds, real estate, infrastructure, commodities, private credit, and collectibles**. The appeal is **diversification** (low correlation with public markets) and higher return potential; the trade-offs are **illiquidity, high fees, opacity, and hard-to-verify valuations**.

## 85. REITs, InvITs and Real Estate / Infrastructure Finance

A **REIT** (real estate investment trust) pools capital to own income-producing property and must distribute most of its income as dividends, giving retail investors liquid access to real estate. An **InvIT** does the same for infrastructure (roads, power lines). **Project finance** funds large infrastructure on a **non-recourse** basis, repaid only from the project's own cash flows.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "What is a REIT?" A listed vehicle that owns income property and passes rental income to investors as dividends.</div>

## 86. Insurance: How Insurers Make Money

Two profit engines: **underwriting profit** (premiums minus claims and expenses) and **investment income on the float** (premiums held before claims are paid). Core concepts: pooling and the **law of large numbers**, **adverse selection**, **moral hazard**, and **reinsurance** (insurers insuring their own risk). Life and general (non-life) insurance differ in duration and reserving.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "What is insurance float?" Premiums the insurer holds and invests before paying claims, a major source of investment income.</div>

## 87. Treasury and Asset-Liability Management (ALM)

A **treasury** manages a firm's or bank's cash, funding, liquidity, and financial risk (rate and FX). **ALM** matches the maturity and rate profile of assets and liabilities to control interest-rate and liquidity risk. A bank funding long-dated loans with short-term deposits runs a **maturity mismatch**. Tools: gap analysis, duration matching, and hedging.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "What risk does a bank run borrowing short and lending long?" Interest-rate and liquidity risk from the maturity mismatch, the classic bank failure mode.</div>

## 88. Trade Finance

Instruments that de-risk cross-border trade. A **letter of credit (LC)** is a bank's guarantee that the seller is paid once correct shipping documents are presented. **Factoring** sells receivables to a financier for immediate cash; **forfaiting** does the same for medium-term export receivables. Bank guarantees and export credit round it out.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "What is a letter of credit?" A bank's promise to pay the seller on the buyer's behalf once documents are in order, bridging trust between distant parties.</div>

## 89. Basel III and Bank Capital / Liquidity

The global bank rulebook after 2008. **Capital:** banks hold minimum capital against **risk-weighted assets** (the CAR), with a core **CET1** (common equity tier 1) floor. **Liquidity:** the **LCR** (liquidity coverage ratio) ensures 30 days of high-quality liquid assets under stress; the **NSFR** (net stable funding ratio) pushes for stable funding. The goal is safer, better-capitalized banks.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "What does the LCR ensure?" Enough high-quality liquid assets to survive a 30-day funding stress.</div>

## 90. AML, KYC and Compliance

**Anti-money-laundering** rules require firms to **Know Your Customer** (verify identity), monitor for suspicious activity, and file suspicious-transaction reports. Laundering runs in three stages: **placement, layering, integration**. Failures bring heavy fines and reputational damage, so compliance is central in banking and operations roles.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> Know the three stages of money laundering and that KYC is the foundation of AML.</div>

## 91. Capital Budgeting Techniques

Choosing which long-term projects to fund. Beyond **NPV** (best, absolute value added) and **IRR** (topic 9):

- **Payback period:** years to recover the outlay, simple but **ignores the time value of money** and cash flows after payback.
- **Discounted payback:** payback using discounted cash flows.
- **Profitability index:** PV of inflows / initial outlay; accept if above 1.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "Weakness of the payback method?" It ignores the time value of money and everything that happens after the cutoff.</div>

## 92. Corporate Actions

Events that change a company's securities: **dividends**, **stock splits** (more shares at a lower price, no change in value), **bonus / scrip issues** (free shares from reserves), **rights issues** (existing holders can buy more, usually at a discount), and **buybacks**. They move the share count and per-share metrics but often leave total value unchanged.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "Does a 2-for-1 split create value?" No, twice the shares at half the price; total value is unchanged. It improves liquidity and affordability.</div>

## 93. Foreign Exchange, Deeper

**Spot** settles in two days; a **forward** locks a rate for a future date. The forward rate reflects the **interest-rate differential** (covered interest parity). A **carry trade** borrows a low-yield currency to invest in a high-yield one, earning the spread but bearing FX risk. Currencies can **float, be pegged, or be managed**; a **non-deliverable forward (NDF)** cash-settles restricted currencies.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "What drives the forward FX rate?" The interest-rate differential between the two currencies (interest-rate parity), not a forecast of the spot.</div>

## 94. Commodities and Gold

Real assets traded via **spot and futures**: energy (oil, gas), metals (gold, copper), and agriculture. Prices reflect supply and demand, inventories, and the dollar. **Gold** is a store of value and an inflation and uncertainty hedge, but it pays no cash flow, so it competes poorly when real rates are high. **Contango** (futures above spot) versus **backwardation** (futures below spot) drives the roll return on commodity futures.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "Why hold gold?" As a hedge against inflation and uncertainty. Its weakness is no yield, so high real rates hurt it.</div>

<!-- secADV -->
<div class="howto"><strong>Advanced and technical topics.</strong> Deeper valuation, capital-structure theory, market efficiency, factor models, risk measurement, and options and fixed-income math, the technical ground probed for quant, risk, research, and banking interviews.</div>

## 95. The Dividend Discount Model (DDM)

Values a stock as the present value of its future dividends.

<div class="formula"><b>Gordon growth (constant-growth DDM)</b><br> Price = D1 / (r - g) <div class="where">D1 = next year's dividend, r = cost of equity, g = perpetual dividend growth (g must be below r)</div></div>

A multi-stage DDM models a high-growth phase then a terminal Gordon phase. It suits **stable dividend payers** (banks, utilities, consumer staples) and is useless for firms that pay no dividend.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "When is DDM the right tool?" For mature companies with predictable dividends; use DCF or multiples otherwise.</div>

## 96. Economic Value Added (EVA) and Residual Income

<div class="formula"><b>EVA</b> = NOPAT - (Invested Capital x WACC) <div class="where">the profit left after charging for the cost of ALL capital employed</div></div>

Positive EVA means the firm earns **above its cost of capital**, the definition of value creation (the same idea as ROIC > WACC, topic 21). Residual-income valuation adds the present value of future residual income to today's book value.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "What does positive EVA tell you?" The company is earning more than the cost of the capital it deploys, so it is creating value.</div>

## 97. Capital Structure Theories

Building on Modigliani-Miller (topic 11):

- **Trade-off theory:** an optimal debt level balances the **tax shield** benefit against rising **financial-distress** costs.
- **Pecking order theory:** firms prefer **internal funds first, then debt, then equity last**, because issuing equity signals the stock may be overvalued.
- **Market timing:** firms issue equity when it looks richly priced.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "Why might a healthy firm avoid issuing equity?" Pecking-order signaling, an equity raise can suggest management thinks shares are overvalued.</div>

## 98. The Efficient Market Hypothesis (EMH)

Prices reflect available information, so you cannot consistently beat the market on a risk-adjusted basis. Three forms:

- **Weak:** prices reflect all past prices, so technical analysis should not work.
- **Semi-strong:** prices reflect all public information, so fundamental analysis on public data should not give an edge.
- **Strong:** prices reflect even private information.

Anomalies and behavioral biases (topic 71) are the standard challenges.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "If markets are semi-strong efficient, what stops working?" Trading on public news, it is already in the price.</div>

## 99. Multi-Factor Models: Fama-French and APT

CAPM uses one factor (the market). **Fama-French** adds **size** (small minus big) and **value** (high minus low book-to-market); the five-factor version adds profitability and investment. **Arbitrage Pricing Theory (APT)** allows several macro factors. These explain returns better than beta alone and underpin factor and smart-beta investing.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> Name the three Fama-French factors, market, size, and value, and note they extend CAPM.</div>

## 100. Value at Risk and Expected Shortfall

**VaR** is the maximum loss over a horizon at a confidence level, e.g. a 1-day 99% VaR of $10m means a 1% chance of losing more than $10m in a day. Methods: **historical, parametric (variance-covariance), and Monte Carlo**. Its flaw: VaR says nothing about **how bad** losses get beyond the threshold. **Expected Shortfall (CVaR)** fixes this by averaging the losses in the tail beyond VaR.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "What is the main weakness of VaR?" It ignores the severity of tail losses beyond the cutoff; expected shortfall captures them.</div>

## 101. Stress Testing, Scenario Analysis and Backtesting

**Stress testing** pushes a portfolio through extreme but plausible shocks (2008, a rate spike) to reveal losses beyond normal VaR. **Scenario analysis** models specific what-ifs. **Backtesting** checks whether a VaR model's actual breaches match its stated confidence over history, too many breaches means the model understates risk. Regulators require all three.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "Why stress test if you already compute VaR?" VaR assumes normal markets; stress tests capture the tail events it misses.</div>

## 102. Put-Call Parity and Black-Scholes Intuition

<div class="formula"><b>Put-call parity (European, no dividends)</b><br> Call - Put = Spot - PV(Strike) <div class="where">a no-arbitrage identity: break it and a riskless profit exists</div></div>

**Black-Scholes** prices a European option from spot, strike, time, the risk-free rate, and **volatility**. Volatility is the only unobservable input, so the market effectively quotes options in **implied-vol** terms.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "Which Black-Scholes input can't you observe?" Volatility, which is exactly why traders talk in implied volatility.</div>

## 103. Implied vs Realized Volatility and the Skew

**Realized** volatility is what actually happened; **implied** volatility is what option prices imply about the future. Selling options when implied exceeds realized is a common edge. The **volatility skew/smile** is that implied vol varies by strike, out-of-the-money puts usually cost more (crash protection), which contradicts Black-Scholes' constant-vol assumption.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "What is the volatility skew?" Implied vol differs across strikes, typically higher for downside puts, reflecting demand for crash protection.</div>

## 104. Duration, Convexity and Immunization

**Duration** (topic 14) is the first-order interest-rate sensitivity; **convexity** is the second-order correction, the curvature of the price-yield curve. Convexity **helps the bondholder**, prices rise more when yields fall than they drop when yields rise by the same amount. **Immunization** matches asset and liability duration so a portfolio is insulated from small rate moves (used by pensions and insurers).

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "Why is positive convexity good?" Gains from falling yields exceed losses from equal rising yields.</div>

## 105. The Yield Curve Deeper: Spot, Forward and Bootstrapping

The **spot (zero) rate** is the yield on a single cash flow at each maturity. **Forward rates** are the future rates between two dates implied by today's curve, via no-arbitrage. **Bootstrapping** derives spot rates step by step from coupon-bond prices. This zero curve is what actually discounts fixed-income and swap cash flows.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "What is a forward rate?" The rate for a future period implied today by the spot curve, locked in by no-arbitrage, not a forecast.</div>

## 106. Sum-of-the-Parts and Adjusted Present Value

**Sum-of-the-parts (SOTP)** values a diversified company by valuing each segment separately (its own multiple or DCF) and adding them, useful for conglomerates trading at a discount. **Adjusted Present Value (APV)** values the business as if all-equity, then adds the present value of financing effects (mainly the tax shield); it is cleaner than WACC when leverage changes over time.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "When use APV instead of a WACC-based DCF?" When capital structure shifts materially over the forecast, such as an LBO paying down debt.</div>

## 107. Counterparty Risk, CVA and Central Clearing

**Counterparty risk** is that the other side of a derivative defaults before settlement. **CVA (credit valuation adjustment)** is the market price of that risk, subtracted from a derivative's value. After 2008, reforms route standardized derivatives through **central counterparties (CCPs)** with margin, which mutualizes and reduces counterparty risk.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "What is CVA?" The adjustment to a derivative's value for the chance the counterparty defaults.</div>

## 108. Expected Credit Loss (IFRS 9) and Provisioning

IFRS 9 replaced the old "incurred loss" approach with a **forward-looking expected credit loss (ECL)** model: banks provision for expected losses from day one and move exposures through three stages as credit quality deteriorates.

<div class="formula"><b>Expected Loss</b> = PD x LGD x EAD <div class="where">probability of default x loss given default x exposure at default</div></div>

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> "How do banks size loan provisions now?" Forward-looking expected credit loss (PD x LGD x EAD), not just losses already incurred.</div>

## 109. Algorithmic Trading, HFT and Market Microstructure

**Algorithmic trading** executes orders by rules and code. **High-frequency trading (HFT)** is a subset competing on **speed** (co-location, ultra-low latency) for tiny per-trade edges; it adds liquidity but raises fairness and flash-crash concerns. **Market microstructure** studies how orders become prices, the order book, the bid-ask spread, market impact, and adverse selection.

<div class="keytake"><b>Interview angle:</b> Know that HFT competes on latency, and that microstructure explains how order flow actually moves prices.</div>

<div class="keytake"><b>Bottom line:</b> concepts 1, 2, 5, 6, 7, 8 are the backbone of technical finance interviews. If time is short, drill those six until you can explain each in under a minute, then use the 1,064 Q&amp;A below to go deep in your target area.</div>

<a name="secA"></a>

# A. Core Finance & Accounting

<a name="A1"></a>

## A1. Accounting & the Three Financial Statements  <span class="qrange">(Q1-Q40)</span>

**Q1.** What are the three primary financial statements and what does each one show?

The income statement (P&L) shows profitability over a period: revenue minus expenses equals net income. The balance sheet is a snapshot at a point in time showing Assets = Liabilities + Shareholders' Equity. The cash flow statement reconciles the change in cash over a period across operating, investing and financing activities. Together they answer: did the firm make money, what does it own/owe, and where did the cash actually go.

**Q2.** What is the fundamental accounting equation, and why must it always balance?

`Assets = Liabilities + Shareholders' Equity`. It must balance because every economic transaction has two sides under double-entry bookkeeping: anything a company owns (assets) is financed either by what it owes (liabilities) or by owners' capital (equity). If you buy a $100 machine with cash, one asset rises and another falls; if you buy it on credit, an asset and a liability both rise by $100, keeping the equation intact.

**Q3.** What is the difference between accrual accounting and cash accounting?

Accrual accounting records revenue when it is earned and expenses when they are incurred, regardless of when cash moves. Cash accounting records transactions only when cash is received or paid. Accrual gives a truer economic picture (and is required under IFRS, US GAAP and Ind-AS for most entities), while cash accounting is simpler and shows actual liquidity. The gap between the two is exactly what working-capital and accrual adjustments on the cash flow statement capture.

**Q4.** What is double-entry bookkeeping?

Double-entry means every transaction is recorded in at least two accounts with equal debits and credits, so the books always balance. For example, paying $500 rent debits rent expense (increasing expenses) and credits cash (decreasing an asset) by $500. This system underpins the accounting equation and makes errors detectable because total debits must equal total credits.

**Q5.** On the income statement, walk through the line items from revenue to net income.

Start with Revenue (sales), subtract Cost of Goods Sold (COGS) to get Gross Profit. Subtract operating expenses (SG&A, R&D, depreciation/amortization) to get Operating Income (EBIT). Subtract interest to get Pre-Tax Income (EBT), then subtract taxes to arrive at Net Income. Net income is the "bottom line" available to equity holders, from which dividends are paid and retained earnings are built.

**Q6.** What is the revenue recognition principle?

Revenue is recognized when it is earned and realizable, not necessarily when cash is received. Under the modern five-step model (IFRS 15 / Ind-AS 115 / ASC 606), you identify the contract and performance obligations, determine and allocate the transaction price, and recognize revenue as obligations are satisfied. For example, a SaaS firm billing $1,200 upfront for an annual subscription recognizes $100 per month, holding the rest as deferred (unearned) revenue, a liability.

**Q7.** What is the matching principle?

The matching principle says expenses should be recognized in the same period as the revenues they help generate. For instance, COGS for a product is recorded when the product is sold (matching the sale revenue), not when the inventory was purchased. Depreciation likewise spreads an asset's cost over the periods it generates revenue. This prevents profit distortion from timing mismatches between cost and benefit.

**Q8.** How are accounts receivable and accounts payable defined, and where do they sit?

Accounts receivable (AR) is money owed to the company by customers for goods/services already delivered on credit; it is a current asset. Accounts payable (AP) is money the company owes to suppliers for goods/services received; it is a current liability. Rising AR means revenue booked but cash not yet collected (a use of cash); rising AP means costs incurred but not yet paid (a source of cash).

**Q9.** What is the difference between a prepaid expense and an accrued expense?

A prepaid expense is cash paid in advance for a future benefit (e.g., paying 12 months' insurance upfront); it is a current asset that is expensed over time. An accrued expense is a cost incurred but not yet paid (e.g., salaries earned but unpaid at month-end); it is a current liability. Prepaid = pay now, expense later; accrued = expense now, pay later.

**Q10.** What is deferred (unearned) revenue and how does it differ from accrued revenue?

Deferred revenue is cash collected before the good/service is delivered, so it is a liability until earned (e.g., an airline ticket sold for a future flight). Accrued revenue is revenue earned but not yet billed or collected, so it is an asset (e.g., interest earned but not yet received). They are mirror images: one is "cash before work," the other is "work before cash."

**Q11.** What is depreciation, and why does it appear on the income statement?

Depreciation allocates the cost of a tangible fixed asset (plant, machinery, vehicles) over its useful life to match the expense against the revenue it helps produce. It is a non-cash expense on the income statement that reduces taxable income without an immediate cash outflow (the cash went out when the asset was bought). On the cash flow statement it is added back to net income in operating activities.

**Q12.** Explain straight-line depreciation versus the written-down value (declining balance) method.

Straight-line charges an equal amount each year: `(Cost - Salvage) / Useful Life`. Written-down value (WDV) / declining balance applies a fixed percentage to the reducing book value each year, so charges are higher early and lower later. Example: a $10,000 asset, 10-year life, no salvage, gives $1,000/year straight-line; at 20% WDV it gives $2,000 in year 1, $1,600 in year 2, and so on. WDV is common for tax in India (Income Tax Act uses block-of-assets WDV), while companies often use straight-line for book reporting.

**Q13.** What is amortization and how does it differ from depreciation?

Amortization spreads the cost of intangible assets (patents, software, licenses, customer lists) over their useful life, while depreciation does the same for tangible assets. Mechanically they are identical (usually straight-line for intangibles) and both are non-cash. A key nuance: indefinite-life intangibles like goodwill are not amortized under IFRS/Ind-AS/US GAAP but are tested for impairment instead.

**Q14.** How does a $100 increase in depreciation flow through the three statements (assume a 25% tax rate)?

Income statement: pre-tax income falls $100, tax falls $25, so net income falls $75. Cash flow: start with net income down $75, add back the $100 non-cash depreciation, so cash from operations rises $25 (the tax shield). Balance sheet: cash up $25 (assets), PP&E down $100 (assets), so total assets down $75; retained earnings down $75 (equity), and the balance sheet balances. This is the classic interview link question.

**Q15.** What is the capitalize-versus-expense decision and why does it matter?

If a cost provides benefits beyond the current period, it is capitalized as an asset and depreciated/amortized over time; if it only benefits the current period, it is expensed immediately. Capitalizing boosts near-term profit (cost is spread out) and creates an asset on the balance sheet, while expensing reduces current profit. The choice affects EBITDA, net income timing and cash flow classification (capex is investing, opex is operating), which is why aggressive capitalization is a common earnings-quality red flag.

**Q16.** What is working capital and how is it read off the balance sheet?

Working capital = Current Assets - Current Liabilities. Operating (net) working capital usually excludes cash and debt: `(AR + Inventory + Prepaids) - (AP + Accrued liabilities)`. It measures the short-term capital tied up in running the business; rising operating working capital consumes cash, while falling working capital releases cash. It is the bridge between accrual profit and operating cash flow.

**Q17.** How do changes in working capital affect the cash flow statement?

In the indirect method, an increase in an operating current asset (e.g., AR or inventory) is a use of cash (subtracted), and an increase in an operating current liability (e.g., AP) is a source of cash (added). Intuition: if AR grows, you booked revenue but did not collect cash; if AP grows, you booked an expense but did not pay cash. So fast-growing firms often show strong profit but weak operating cash flow because working capital absorbs cash.

**Q18.** What are the three sections of the cash flow statement?

Operating activities: cash from core business operations (net income adjusted for non-cash items and working-capital changes). Investing activities: cash for buying/selling long-term assets and investments (capex, acquisitions, asset sales). Financing activities: cash from/to capital providers (issuing or repaying debt, issuing or buying back equity, paying dividends). The three net to the change in cash for the period, which ties to the balance sheet cash line.

**Q19.** What is the difference between the direct and indirect methods of presenting operating cash flow?

The direct method lists actual cash inflows and outflows (cash from customers, cash paid to suppliers and employees). The indirect method starts from net income and adjusts for non-cash items (depreciation, etc.) and working-capital changes to arrive at operating cash flow. Both yield the same operating cash figure; the indirect method is far more common in practice because it reconciles easily to the income statement, though IFRS/Ind-AS and US GAAP encourage (but rarely see) the direct method.
