**Q20.** What is EBITDA and how does it differ from net income?

EBITDA = Earnings Before Interest, Taxes, Depreciation and Amortization, roughly `Operating Income + D&A`. It strips out financing (interest), tax regime and non-cash D&A to approximate operating cash generation and enable cross-company comparison. Net income is the bottom line after all of those, reflecting actual returns to equity. EBITDA is not a GAAP/Ind-AS line item and ignores capex and working-capital needs, so it can overstate true cash flow.

**Q21.** Why is EBITDA a poor proxy for free cash flow?

EBITDA excludes three real cash drains: capital expenditure (capex), changes in working capital, and cash taxes and interest. A capital-intensive business can show high EBITDA yet generate little free cash flow once it reinvests in PP&E. Free cash flow to firm is roughly `EBIT*(1-tax) + D&A - capex - change in working capital`, which is why analysts move from EBITDA toward FCF for valuation.

**Q22.** What are the main inventory costing methods?

FIFO (First-In, First-Out) assumes the oldest inventory is sold first. LIFO (Last-In, First-Out) assumes the newest is sold first. Weighted average cost assigns the average per-unit cost to all units. Each yields different COGS and ending-inventory values when prices change, which directly affects gross profit, taxes and the balance sheet inventory figure.

**Q23.** In a period of rising prices, how do FIFO and LIFO affect profit and taxes?

Under rising prices, FIFO charges older (cheaper) costs to COGS, giving lower COGS, higher gross profit, higher taxes, and a higher (closer-to-current) inventory value on the balance sheet. LIFO charges newer (more expensive) costs to COGS, giving higher COGS, lower profit, lower taxes, and an understated inventory balance. That tax advantage is the main reason US firms use LIFO.

**Q24.** Do IFRS and Ind-AS permit LIFO?

No. IFRS (IAS 2) and Indian Ind-AS 2 prohibit LIFO; only FIFO and weighted-average cost are allowed. LIFO is permitted under US GAAP, which is a notable GAAP-vs-IFRS difference. Consequently Indian and most non-US companies report inventory on FIFO or weighted-average, improving comparability and reducing balance-sheet understatement.

**Q25.** How is inventory valued under "lower of cost or net realizable value"?

Under IFRS/Ind-AS (IAS 2 / Ind-AS 2), inventory is carried at the lower of cost and net realizable value (NRV = estimated selling price minus costs to complete and sell). If NRV falls below cost, a write-down is recognized as an expense. US GAAP uses "lower of cost or market" for LIFO/retail method and lower of cost or NRV otherwise. IFRS/Ind-AS also allow reversal of prior write-downs if NRV recovers, which US GAAP generally does not.

**Q26.** What is goodwill and when does it arise?

Goodwill is the premium paid in an acquisition over the fair value of the acquired company's identifiable net assets. It is calculated as `Purchase Price - Fair Value of Identifiable Net Assets Acquired` and is recorded as an intangible asset on the acquirer's consolidated balance sheet. It represents unidentifiable value such as brand, customer relationships, synergies and workforce. Goodwill only appears via acquisitions, never for internally generated goodwill.

**Q27.** How is goodwill treated after acquisition under IFRS/Ind-AS and US GAAP?

Goodwill is not amortized; instead it is tested for impairment at least annually (and whenever there are indicators). If its recoverable/fair value falls below carrying value, an impairment loss is recognized on the income statement and goodwill is written down. Under IFRS/Ind-AS impairment testing is at the cash-generating-unit level using a one-step recoverable-amount test; US GAAP tests at the reporting-unit level. Goodwill impairments are non-cash and cannot be reversed once recognized.

**Q28.** What is asset impairment and how does it differ from depreciation?

Impairment is a sudden, often one-time write-down when an asset's carrying value exceeds its recoverable amount (the higher of fair value less costs to sell and value in use). Depreciation is a planned, systematic allocation of cost over an asset's life. Impairment reflects an unexpected loss of value (technology obsolescence, market decline), is recognized as an expense, and under IFRS/Ind-AS can be reversed for assets other than goodwill if conditions improve, whereas US GAAP prohibits reversals.

**Q29.** What is a deferred tax liability (DTL) and what commonly creates one?

A DTL is tax that a company will owe in the future due to a temporary timing difference between book and tax accounting, where book profit currently exceeds taxable profit. The classic cause is using straight-line depreciation for books but accelerated/WDV depreciation for tax: tax depreciation is higher early, so taxable income (and tax paid) is lower now but will be higher later. The deferred amount sits as a liability until the timing difference reverses.

**Q30.** What is a deferred tax asset (DTA) and what commonly creates one?

A DTA is a future tax benefit arising when a company has paid more tax (or recognized more taxable income) now than book accounting implies, so it will pay less later. Common sources are carried-forward tax losses, provisions/warranty expenses recognized for books but deductible only when paid, and revenue taxed before it is booked. A DTA is only recognized to the extent future taxable profits are probable; otherwise a valuation allowance reduces it.

**Q31.** Why might effective tax rate differ from the statutory tax rate?

The effective tax rate (tax expense / pre-tax income) differs from the statutory rate because of permanent differences (non-deductible expenses, tax-exempt income), tax credits, different jurisdictional rates, changes in deferred tax balances, and valuation allowances on DTAs. For example, in India the headline corporate rate may be 25% but generous exemptions, MAT credits or non-deductible items can push the effective rate above or below that. Reconciling the two is a standard earnings-quality check.

**Q32.** What is a provision and how does it differ from an accrual?

A provision is a liability of uncertain timing or amount, recognized when a present obligation exists, an outflow is probable, and the amount can be reliably estimated (IAS 37 / Ind-AS 37), e.g., warranty provisions, restructuring or litigation provisions. An accrual is a known, near-certain liability with only minor estimation (e.g., accrued wages). Provisions involve genuine measurement uncertainty; accruals are essentially timing entries for known amounts.

**Q33.** What is a contingent liability and how is it disclosed?

A contingent liability is a possible obligation depending on an uncertain future event, or a present obligation that is not probable or cannot be reliably measured (e.g., a pending lawsuit or guarantee). Under IAS 37 / Ind-AS 37 it is not recognized on the balance sheet but is disclosed in the notes; it is recorded as a provision only if an outflow becomes probable and estimable. If the outflow is remote, no disclosure is required.

**Q34.** How do you distinguish recognizing a provision from disclosing a contingent liability?

It hinges on probability and measurability. If a present obligation is probable (more likely than not) and reliably estimable, you recognize a provision as a liability and an expense. If the obligation is only possible, or probable but not reliably measurable, you disclose it as a contingent liability in the notes. If remote, you do nothing. This decision tree from IAS 37 / Ind-AS 37 prevents both premature liability recognition and hidden obligations.

**Q35.** What are the key differences between US GAAP and IFRS?

US GAAP is rules-based; IFRS is principles-based. Major differences: LIFO is allowed under US GAAP but banned under IFRS; IFRS permits revaluation of PP&E and reversal of impairments (except goodwill), US GAAP generally does not; development costs can be capitalized under IFRS (IAS 38) but are mostly expensed under US GAAP; and presentation/terminology differs. These cause real comparability issues for cross-listed firms and analysts.

**Q36.** What is Ind-AS and how does it relate to IFRS?

Ind-AS (Indian Accounting Standards) is India's set of accounting standards converged with IFRS, notified under the Companies Act and applicable to larger listed and unlisted companies based on net-worth thresholds. It is "converged with" rather than identical to IFRS, with carve-outs (e.g., certain treatments for foreign-exchange differences and specific exemptions). Smaller Indian companies still follow the older Indian GAAP (AS). Like IFRS, Ind-AS bans LIFO and does not amortize goodwill.

**Q37.** How do interest paid and dividends paid get classified on the cash flow statement under IFRS/Ind-AS versus US GAAP?

Under US GAAP, interest paid and interest/dividends received are operating, and only dividends paid are financing. Under IFRS/Ind-AS there is a policy choice: interest and dividends paid can be classified as either operating or financing, and interest/dividends received as either operating or investing, applied consistently. This flexibility means operating cash flow is not always directly comparable across IFRS and US GAAP reporters.

**Q38.** A company reports strong net income but negative operating cash flow. What could explain it, and is it a red flag?

It often signals a working-capital drain: surging accounts receivable (sales booked but uncollected), inventory build-up, or shrinking payables. It can also reflect aggressive revenue recognition or large non-cash gains inflating profit without cash backing. While rapid growth can legitimately consume working capital, a persistent gap between accrual profit and operating cash flow is a classic earnings-quality red flag worth investigating in the cash flow reconciliation.

**Q39.** How does issuing $100 of new equity flow through the three statements?

Cash flow: financing activities rise $100, increasing total cash by $100. Balance sheet: cash (asset) rises $100, and on the other side common stock/share capital and additional paid-in capital (equity) rise $100, so it balances. The income statement is unaffected because raising capital is not revenue or expense. This illustrates that financing transactions hit the balance sheet and cash flow but bypass the P&L.

**Q40.** How do the three statements link together into one connected model?

Net income from the income statement flows into the top of the cash flow statement (operating section) and into retained earnings on the balance sheet. The cash flow statement reconciles net income to the change in cash, and its ending cash becomes the balance sheet's cash line. Non-cash items (D&A) and working-capital changes bridge the income statement and balance sheet through the cash flow statement. If all links are correct, the balance sheet balances: this interconnection is the backbone of any three-statement financial model.

<a name="A7"></a>

## A7. Advanced Financial Accounting  <span class="qrange">(Q41-Q73)</span>

**Q41.** Under the old standards, what was the difference between an operating lease and a finance (capital) lease, and why did it matter?

A finance lease transferred substantially all risks and rewards of ownership, so the lessee capitalized the asset and recognized a corresponding liability on the balance sheet, splitting payments into depreciation plus interest. An operating lease was treated as a simple rental: the entire payment hit the income statement as a single lease/rent expense (above EBIT) and nothing appeared on the balance sheet. The classification mattered because operating leases kept large lease obligations off-balance-sheet, understating leverage and overstating returns on assets.

**Q42.** What changed under IFRS 16 / Ind-AS 116, and why was the old model considered abused?

IFRS 16 (and Ind-AS 116, effective 1 April 2019 in India) introduced a single lessee model that puts almost all leases on the balance sheet, eliminating the operating-vs-finance distinction for lessees. The old split-model was criticized because companies (airlines, retailers, telecoms) structured leases to qualify as "operating" to hide debt; investors had to manually capitalize lease commitments from the footnotes. Now the lessee recognizes a right-of-use (ROU) asset and a lease liability for nearly every lease, with only short-term (<=12 months) and low-value leases exempted.

**Q43.** Walk through the initial recognition of a lease under IFRS 16 / Ind-AS 116.

The lease liability is recognized at the present value of remaining lease payments, discounted at the interest rate implicit in the lease (or the lessee's incremental borrowing rate if that is not readily determinable). The ROU asset starts equal to the lease liability, plus initial direct costs, prepaid lease payments, and estimated restoration costs, less any lease incentives received. Example: 5 annual payments of INR 100 discounted at 8% give a liability of roughly INR 399, so both the ROU asset and lease liability are booked at INR 399 on day one.

**Q44.** How does on-balance-sheet lease accounting affect EBITDA and key ratios?

Under IFRS 16/Ind-AS 116 the old single rent expense is replaced by depreciation of the ROU asset plus interest on the liability, both of which sit below EBITDA, so EBITDA rises (the rent that used to depress it is gone). Total assets and total debt both increase, so debt/EBITDA can move either way but leverage ratios and gross debt clearly rise, while ROA and asset turnover fall because the asset base is larger. Note US GAAP (ASC 842) also capitalizes operating leases but keeps a straight-line single lease expense in operating costs, so under US GAAP EBITDA is NOT boosted the way it is under IFRS.

**Q45.** Why is the income statement expense front-loaded for a lease under IFRS 16?

Because depreciation of the ROU asset is typically straight-line while interest is calculated on a declining liability balance, total expense (depreciation + interest) is higher in early years and lower later, even though the cash payment is level. This contrasts with the old operating lease's flat rent expense. Over the full lease term total expense is identical; only the timing differs.

**Q46.** State the IFRS 15 / Ind-AS 115 / ASC 606 five-step revenue recognition model.

(1) Identify the contract with a customer; (2) identify the distinct performance obligations in the contract; (3) determine the transaction price; (4) allocate the transaction price to the performance obligations based on relative standalone selling prices; (5) recognize revenue as (or when) each performance obligation is satisfied, i.e., when control transfers to the customer. The core principle is that revenue reflects the consideration the entity expects to be entitled to in exchange for transferring goods or services.

**Q47.** What is the difference between recognizing revenue "over time" versus "at a point in time"?

Revenue is recognized over time if any of three criteria are met: the customer simultaneously receives and consumes the benefits (e.g., a SaaS subscription), the entity creates/enhances an asset the customer controls, or the asset has no alternative use and the entity has an enforceable right to payment for work done. If none apply, revenue is recognized at a point in time, typically when control transfers (delivery, title, acceptance). A construction contract usually qualifies as over-time using a percentage-of-completion measure of progress.

**Q48.** How does the 5-step model handle a bundled contract, e.g., a phone sold with a 24-month plan?

The transaction price is allocated to each distinct performance obligation (the handset and the network service) based on their relative standalone selling prices, not the contractual invoicing pattern. So even if the handset is "free" up front, a portion of the monthly fees is reallocated to the handset and recognized as revenue at delivery, with the rest recognized over 24 months. This often shifts revenue earlier than the cash-based view and creates a contract asset on the balance sheet.

**Q49.** What are contract assets and contract liabilities under IFRS 15?

A contract asset arises when the entity has satisfied a performance obligation (earned revenue) but does not yet have an unconditional right to payment (which would be a receivable) — for example, unbilled revenue. A contract liability (deferred/unearned revenue) arises when the customer has paid, or payment is due, before the entity has performed. Example: an annual SaaS plan billed upfront for INR 1,200 creates a contract liability that is released to revenue at INR 100 per month.

**Q50.** How is stock-based compensation (SBC) accounted for, and why is it a real expense?

Under IFRS 2 / Ind-AS 102 (and ASC 718), equity-settled SBC is measured at the grant-date fair value of the award (often via Black-Scholes or a binomial model for options) and expensed over the vesting period, with a credit to equity (additional paid-in capital). It is a genuine expense because the company is transferring value to employees in lieu of cash compensation, diluting existing shareholders. It is non-cash, so it is added back in the operating section of the cash flow statement.

**Q51.** Why do analysts often dislike adding SBC back to free cash flow even though it is "non-cash"?

Because SBC is a recurring economic cost: it compensates employees and, if the company wants to avoid dilution, it must spend cash buying back shares to offset the new grants. Treating it as a costless add-back (as in "adjusted EBITDA" or FCF) flatters cash generation and ignores the dilution borne by shareholders. A rigorous valuation either expenses SBC or models the share-count growth it causes.

**Q52.** Explain the treasury stock method (TSM) for diluted EPS.

The TSM assumes in-the-money options and warrants are exercised, the company receives the exercise proceeds, and uses those proceeds to buy back shares at the average market price. The net new shares added to the diluted count = options outstanding − (options * strike / average price). Example: 1,000 options at a INR 50 strike with a INR 100 average price raise INR 50,000, which repurchases 500 shares, so net dilution is 1,000 − 500 = 500 shares.

**Q53.** What is the difference between a defined benefit (DB) and a defined contribution (DC) pension plan, and who bears the risk?

In a DC plan the employer pays a fixed contribution (e.g., into a provident/pension fund) and the employee bears all investment and longevity risk; the expense equals the contribution and there is no ongoing balance-sheet obligation. In a DB plan the employer promises a defined future benefit (e.g., a formula on final salary and years of service), so the employer bears investment and actuarial risk and must recognize a pension obligation. DB plans are far more complex because they require actuarial assumptions about discount rates, salary growth, and mortality.

**Q54.** What is the PBO and how is funded status determined?

The Projected Benefit Obligation (PBO) is the actuarial present value of pension benefits earned to date, measured using expected future salary levels and discounted at a high-quality corporate bond rate. Funded status = fair value of plan assets − PBO; a negative figure is an underfunded plan, shown as a net pension liability on the balance sheet. Under IAS 19 the net interest cost is computed by applying the discount rate to this net liability/asset.

**Q55.** Under IAS 19 / Ind-AS 19, how are the components of pension cost split between P&L and OCI?

Service cost (current and past) and net interest on the net defined benefit liability go through profit or loss. Remeasurements — actuarial gains and losses from changes in assumptions and the difference between actual and expected return on plan assets — are recognized in Other Comprehensive Income (OCI) and are not recycled to P&L. Routing volatile remeasurements through OCI keeps earnings from swinging with market and actuarial noise.

**Q56.** Distinguish temporary from permanent differences in deferred tax accounting.

A temporary difference is a timing gap between the book carrying value and the tax base of an asset or liability that will reverse in the future, creating a deferred tax asset (DTA) or liability (DTL) — e.g., accelerated tax depreciation vs straight-line book depreciation. A permanent difference never reverses and so creates no deferred tax — e.g., tax-exempt income, fines/penalties that are not deductible, or expenses permanently disallowed. Only temporary differences hit deferred taxes; permanent differences instead change the effective tax rate.

**Q57.** How does accelerated tax depreciation create a deferred tax liability? Give a quick number.

Faster depreciation for tax purposes means lower taxable income (and lower tax paid) now, but higher taxable income later when book depreciation exceeds tax depreciation, so the company owes more tax in the future — a DTL. Example: an asset is depreciated INR 300 for tax but only INR 100 for books in year 1; the INR 200 temporary difference at a 25% tax rate creates a DTL of INR 50 that reverses in later years. The DTL represents tax deferred, effectively an interest-free loan from the government.

**Q58.** What is a deferred tax valuation allowance, and when is it recorded?

A valuation allowance (US GAAP term) reduces a DTA to the amount that is "more likely than not" to be realized, recognizing that future taxable profits may be insufficient to use the asset. It is recorded when there is doubt — recent losses, expiring carryforwards, or a weak earnings outlook. Under IFRS/Ind-AS there is no separate allowance account; instead the DTA is recognized only to the extent realization is probable, achieving the same conservatism through a recognition test rather than a contra account.

**Q59.** How do net operating losses (NOLs) create deferred tax assets?

An NOL can be carried forward to offset future taxable income, reducing future cash taxes, so it is recognized as a DTA equal to the NOL times the enacted tax rate (subject to recognition/valuation-allowance tests). Example: a USD 100m NOL at a 21% rate is a USD 21m DTA. If the company is not expected to generate enough future profit before the NOL expires (where limits apply), the DTA is written down or only partially recognized.

**Q60.** In purchase accounting, how is goodwill created? Give the mechanics.

Goodwill = purchase consideration − fair value of identifiable net assets acquired (assets at fair value minus liabilities assumed, including any newly recognized intangibles and the related deferred taxes). It is the premium paid over the fair value of what is separately identifiable, capturing synergies, assembled workforce, and going-concern value. Example: pay INR 1,000 for a target whose identifiable net assets are fair-valued at INR 700, and goodwill is INR 300.

**Q61.** What is purchase price allocation (PPA) and what intangibles get recognized that weren't on the target's books?

PPA steps up the acquired assets and liabilities to fair value and recognizes identifiable intangibles the target never capitalized — customer relationships, brands/trademarks, patents, developed technology, and order backlog — with the residual going to goodwill. These intangibles are then amortized (except indefinite-lived ones like some brands), creating extra post-deal amortization that depresses GAAP earnings. The write-up also creates a deferred tax liability when the step-up is not deductible for tax.

**Q62.** What is a bargain purchase and how is it treated?

A bargain purchase (negative goodwill) occurs when the consideration paid is less than the fair value of identifiable net assets acquired, e.g., a forced or distressed sale. Under IFRS 3 / Ind-AS 103 the acquirer first reassesses whether all assets and liabilities were correctly identified and measured, then recognizes any remaining excess immediately as a gain in profit or loss. This contrasts with goodwill, which is capitalized; negative goodwill is never carried as a liability.

**Q63.** Is goodwill amortized or tested for impairment? Note the India nuance.

Under IFRS 3 / Ind-AS 103 and US GAAP, goodwill is not amortized but tested for impairment at least annually (or on a triggering event); impairment is recognized when the recoverable amount of the cash-generating unit falls below its carrying amount and cannot be reversed. Note that Indian AS (the old standard for unlisted/SME entities) did amortize goodwill, and US private companies may elect amortization — but under Ind-AS, listed Indian companies follow the impairment-only model.

**Q64.** When do you consolidate a subsidiary, use the equity method, or just hold it at fair value?

Control (generally >50% of voting rights, or power to direct relevant activities) triggers full consolidation, where 100% of the subsidiary's assets, liabilities, revenue and expenses are combined and a non-controlling interest is shown. Significant influence (typically 20–50%, no control) triggers the equity method, where the investment is carried at cost plus the investor's share of post-acquisition profits as a one-line item. Below significant influence, the stake is a financial asset measured at fair value (FVTPL or FVOCI) under IFRS 9 / Ind-AS 109.

**Q65.** What is non-controlling (minority) interest and how does it flow through the statements?

When a parent owns less than 100% of a consolidated subsidiary, non-controlling interest (NCI) represents the equity in that subsidiary not owned by the parent. On the balance sheet NCI appears within equity (separate from parent equity); on the income statement, total consolidated net income is split into the portion attributable to the parent and the portion attributable to NCI. Example: own 80% of a sub earning INR 100, and INR 20 of consolidated net income is allocated to NCI.

**Q66.** Contrast the equity method with proportionate consolidation for a joint arrangement.

Under the equity method (required by IFRS 11 / Ind-AS 28 for joint ventures), the investor records a single line "investment in associate/JV" and picks up its share of profit as one line, with no gross-up of the investee's assets and liabilities. Proportionate consolidation (no longer permitted under IFRS for joint ventures) would instead bring the investor's percentage share of each individual asset, liability, revenue and expense onto the face of the statements. The equity method keeps leverage and revenue understated relative to the economic exposure, which is why analysts sometimes "look through" to the gross figures.

**Q67.** Explain functional vs presentation currency and the resulting cumulative translation adjustment (CTA).

The functional currency is the currency of the primary economic environment in which an entity operates; the presentation (reporting) currency is the one the financials are presented in. Under IAS 21 / Ind-AS 21, a foreign subsidiary's assets and liabilities are translated at the closing rate and its income at average rates, and the resulting exchange differences accumulate in OCI as a cumulative translation adjustment (CTA) within equity. The CTA is recycled to profit or loss only when the foreign operation is disposed of.

**Q68.** What is the difference between the current-rate (translation) method and the temporal (remeasurement) method?

The current-rate method (used when the subsidiary's functional currency differs from the parent's) translates all assets/liabilities at the closing rate, equity at historical rates, and income at average rates, with differences going to CTA in OCI. The temporal/remeasurement method (used when the books are kept in a currency other than the functional currency, e.g., a hyperinflationary or integrated operation) translates monetary items at the closing rate but non-monetary items at historical rates, and the resulting remeasurement gain/loss flows through net income. The key distinction is OCI (translation) vs P&L (remeasurement).

**Q69.** What are off-balance-sheet items and special purpose entities (SPEs), and why are they a red flag?

Off-balance-sheet items are obligations or assets not shown on the face of the balance sheet — historically operating leases, certain guarantees, factored receivables, and interests in unconsolidated SPEs/VIEs. SPEs (special purpose vehicles) are legal entities created to isolate assets/liabilities, often to move debt off the sponsor's books, as infamously abused by Enron. They are a red flag because they can hide leverage and risk; modern standards (IFRS 10/Ind-AS 110's control model and IFRS 16's lease capitalization) have pulled much of this back on-balance-sheet.

**Q70.** How is R&D treated under US GAAP versus IFRS / Ind-AS, and what is the effect on the financials?

Under US GAAP, R&D is generally expensed as incurred (with narrow exceptions like certain software costs). Under IAS 38 / Ind-AS 38, research is expensed but development costs MUST be capitalized once technical and commercial feasibility, intention, and ability to use/sell are demonstrated, then amortized over the asset's useful life. The result is that an IFRS firm can show higher near-term earnings and an intangible asset, while an otherwise identical US GAAP firm expenses the same spend immediately — a real comparability issue when benchmarking R&D-heavy companies.

**Q71.** How does Ind-AS 37 / IAS 37 distinguish a provision, a contingent liability, and a contingent asset?

A provision is recognized (a liability is booked) when there is a present obligation from a past event, an outflow of resources is probable (>50% likely), and the amount can be reliably estimated — e.g., warranty costs or restructuring. A contingent liability is only disclosed, not recognized, when the outflow is possible but not probable, or the amount cannot be reliably measured. A contingent asset is never recognized and is only disclosed when an inflow is probable, recognized only when realization becomes virtually certain — reflecting accounting conservatism's asymmetry.

**Q72.** Explain the fair value hierarchy under IFRS 13 / Ind-AS 113 (Level 1, 2, 3).

Level 1 inputs are unadjusted quoted prices in active markets for identical assets/liabilities (e.g., a listed equity's closing price) — the most reliable. Level 2 inputs are observable but not Level 1, such as quoted prices for similar items or model inputs like yield curves and benchmark rates (e.g., an interest-rate swap valued off market curves). Level 3 inputs are unobservable, relying on the entity's own assumptions and models (e.g., a private equity stake or a bespoke illiquid derivative), and carry the highest measurement uncertainty and disclosure burden.

**Q73.** What is the difference between basic and diluted EPS, and how are convertibles handled?

Basic EPS = (net income − preferred dividends) / weighted average shares outstanding. Diluted EPS adjusts the denominator (and sometimes the numerator) for all dilutive potential shares — options/warrants via the treasury stock method, and convertible bonds/preferreds via the "if-converted" method, which adds the conversion shares to the denominator and adds back the after-tax interest (or preferred dividend) to the numerator. Anti-dilutive securities (those that would raise EPS) are excluded, so diluted EPS is always less than or equal to basic EPS.

<a name="A2"></a>

## A2. Financial Ratios & Analysis  <span class="qrange">(Q74-Q98)</span>

**Q74.** What is the current ratio and what does it measure?

The current ratio measures short-term liquidity: a firm's ability to cover liabilities due within a year using assets convertible to cash within a year. `Current Ratio = Current Assets / Current Liabilities`. Example: current assets of INR 600 cr and current liabilities of INR 300 cr give a ratio of 2.0, meaning INR 2 of current assets per INR 1 of near-term obligations. A ratio above 1 is generally safe, though the "ideal" level is industry-specific.

**Q75.** How does the quick (acid-test) ratio differ from the current ratio?

The quick ratio strips out inventory (and often prepaid expenses) because inventory is the least liquid current asset and may not convert to cash quickly. `Quick Ratio = (Current Assets - Inventory) / Current Liabilities`, or equivalently `(Cash + Marketable Securities + Receivables) / Current Liabilities`. If current assets are 600 with 200 of inventory and current liabilities are 300, the quick ratio is (600-200)/300 = 1.33 versus a current ratio of 2.0. It is a stricter test favored for inventory-heavy businesses.

**Q76.** What is the cash ratio and when is it most relevant?

The cash ratio is the most conservative liquidity measure, counting only cash and cash equivalents (plus marketable securities) against current liabilities. `Cash Ratio = (Cash + Marketable Securities) / Current Liabilities`. It assumes receivables and inventory may not materialize, so it matters most in distress, banking stress tests, or for assessing a firm that cannot rely on collections. A cash ratio of 0.5 means half of short-term debts could be paid from cash on hand immediately.

**Q77.** Define gross, operating, and net profit margins.

These are profitability ratios scaled to revenue. `Gross Margin = Gross Profit / Revenue` (revenue minus COGS), `Operating Margin = Operating Income (EBIT) / Revenue`, and `Net Margin = Net Income / Revenue`. Each strips out progressively more costs: gross margin reflects production economics, operating margin adds overheads/SG&A and D&A, and net margin includes interest and taxes. For a firm with revenue 1000, COGS 600, EBIT 200, net income 120: margins are 40%, 20%, and 12% respectively.

**Q78.** What is Return on Assets (ROA) and what does it tell you?

ROA measures how efficiently a company uses its total assets to generate profit. `ROA = Net Income / Total Assets` (often using average total assets). A 10% ROA means each INR 100 of assets produces INR 10 of net income. It is most useful for comparing asset-intensive businesses and is independent of the financing mix only when computed on a net-income basis; some analysts use `ROA = EBIT / Total Assets` to remove financing effects.

**Q79.** What is Return on Equity (ROE) and why do investors watch it?

ROE measures the return generated on shareholders' invested capital. `ROE = Net Income / Shareholders' Equity` (often average equity). An ROE of 18% means the firm earns INR 18 per INR 100 of equity. Investors watch it because, over the long run, a company compounding book value at a high ROE creates substantial value, but ROE can be inflated by leverage, so it must be read alongside the debt load.

**Q80.** What is ROCE and how does it differ from ROE?

Return on Capital Employed measures pre-financing operating returns on all long-term capital. `ROCE = EBIT / Capital Employed`, where `Capital Employed = Total Assets - Current Liabilities` (equivalently equity plus long-term debt). Unlike ROE, which is an after-tax, after-interest equity return, ROCE uses EBIT and the full capital base, so it is unaffected by capital structure and is a cleaner gauge of operating efficiency. It is widely cited in India for comparing capital-intensive sectors like cement, metals, and infrastructure.

**Q81.** What is ROIC and why is it considered a high-quality metric?

Return on Invested Capital measures the after-tax operating return on capital actively invested in the business. `ROIC = NOPAT / Invested Capital`, where `NOPAT = EBIT * (1 - tax rate)` and `Invested Capital = Total Debt + Equity - Cash` (or net working capital plus net fixed assets). It is considered high-quality because it isolates the productivity of operating capital from financing and excess cash, making it the right lens for judging whether management allocates capital well.

**Q82.** Why does comparing ROIC to WACC matter?

A firm creates economic value only when its return on invested capital exceeds its cost of that capital: value is created when `ROIC > WACC` and destroyed when `ROIC < WACC`. The spread `ROIC - WACC` times invested capital approximates economic profit (EVA). For example, a company earning 14% ROIC against a 10% WACC earns a 4% spread; growth then adds value, whereas if ROIC were 8%, growth would actually destroy shareholder value.

**Q83.** What is the debt-to-equity ratio and how is it interpreted?

D/E is a leverage/solvency ratio comparing borrowed funds to owners' funds. `Debt-to-Equity = Total Debt / Shareholders' Equity`. A D/E of 1.5 means INR 1.50 of debt per INR 1 of equity. Higher ratios mean greater financial risk and fixed interest obligations but can amplify equity returns; acceptable levels vary by industry, with utilities and banks tolerating much higher leverage than asset-light tech firms.

**Q84.** What does the debt-to-EBITDA ratio measure?

Debt-to-EBITDA gauges how many years of current operating cash earnings would be needed to repay total debt, and is a core metric in credit analysis and loan covenants. `Debt-to-EBITDA = Total Debt / EBITDA`. A ratio of 3.0 means debt equals three years of EBITDA; lenders often set covenants around 3.0-4.0x, and rating agencies use it heavily. It is preferred over D/E for credit because EBITDA proxies the cash available to service debt.

**Q85.** What is the interest coverage ratio (TIE) and what level signals risk?

Times Interest Earned measures how comfortably operating profit covers interest expense. `Interest Coverage (TIE) = EBIT / Interest Expense`. A ratio of 6 means EBIT covers interest six times over. A coverage below roughly 1.5-2.0x is a warning sign that a downturn could leave the firm unable to service debt; some analysts use EBITDA or (EBIT + lease)/interest variants for a fuller picture.

**Q86.** What is the financial leverage (equity) multiplier?

The equity multiplier captures how much of a firm's assets are financed by equity versus debt. `Equity Multiplier = Total Assets / Shareholders' Equity`. A multiplier of 2.5 means assets are 2.5 times equity, implying 60% of assets are debt-financed. It is the leverage component of the DuPont decomposition, and a higher multiplier mechanically raises ROE while also raising financial risk.

**Q87.** What is asset turnover and what does it indicate?

Asset turnover measures how efficiently a company converts its asset base into sales. `Asset Turnover = Revenue / Total Assets` (often average total assets). A turnover of 1.5 means each INR 1 of assets generates INR 1.50 of revenue. Low-margin, high-volume businesses (retailers, distributors) typically run high asset turnover, while capital-intensive firms run low turnover; it pairs with margin to explain ROA.

**Q88.** What is inventory turnover and how do you convert it to Days Inventory Outstanding (DIO)?

Inventory turnover shows how many times a firm sells and replaces its inventory in a period. `Inventory Turnover = COGS / Average Inventory`. To express it in days: `DIO = 365 / Inventory Turnover = (Average Inventory / COGS) * 365`. If COGS is 600 and average inventory is 100, turnover is 6x and DIO is about 61 days, meaning inventory sits roughly two months before being sold. Higher turnover (lower DIO) generally signals efficient inventory management.

**Q89.** What is receivables turnover and Days Sales Outstanding (DSO)?

Receivables turnover measures how quickly a firm collects credit sales. `Receivables Turnover = Revenue / Average Accounts Receivable`, and `DSO = 365 / Receivables Turnover = (Average Receivables / Revenue) * 365`. With revenue of 1000 and average receivables of 150, turnover is 6.7x and DSO is about 55 days, the average time to collect from customers. Rising DSO can signal weaker collections or looser credit terms and ties up working capital.

**Q90.** What is Days Payable Outstanding (DPO) and is a higher value good or bad?

DPO measures how long a firm takes to pay its suppliers. `DPO = (Average Accounts Payable / COGS) * 365`, or `365 / Payables Turnover` where payables turnover is COGS/Average Payables. A higher DPO means the firm holds onto cash longer, effectively obtaining free supplier financing, which benefits working capital, but stretching it too far can strain supplier relationships or forfeit early-payment discounts. With payables of 80 and COGS 600, DPO is about 49 days.

**Q91.** What is the cash conversion cycle and how is it computed?

The cash conversion cycle (CCC) measures the number of days cash is tied up in operations between paying suppliers and collecting from customers. `CCC = DIO + DSO - DPO`. Using earlier figures (DIO 61, DSO 55, DPO 49), CCC is about 67 days. A shorter or even negative CCC is ideal: firms like large retailers and e-commerce platforms can collect from customers before paying suppliers, funding growth with supplier capital.

**Q92.** Walk through the 3-step DuPont decomposition of ROE.

The 3-step DuPont breaks ROE into profitability, efficiency, and leverage: `ROE = Net Margin * Asset Turnover * Equity Multiplier`, i.e. `(Net Income/Revenue) * (Revenue/Assets) * (Assets/Equity)`, which telescopes back to Net Income/Equity. For example, 10% net margin * 1.5 asset turnover * 2.0 equity multiplier = 30% ROE. It reveals whether a firm's ROE is driven by fat margins, efficient asset use, or heavy leverage.

**Q93.** What does the 5-step (extended) DuPont decomposition add?

The 5-step DuPont further splits net margin to isolate the drags from interest and taxes: `ROE = (EBIT/Revenue) * (Revenue/Assets) * (Assets/Equity) * (EBT/EBIT) * (Net Income/EBT)`. Here the first term is operating margin, the fourth (EBT/EBIT) is the interest burden, and the fifth (Net Income/EBT) is the tax burden. It lets an analyst see whether a falling ROE stems from operations, rising interest costs from leverage, or a higher effective tax rate.

**Q94.** Using DuPont, explain how leverage inflates ROE.

In the DuPont identity, ROE = ROA * Equity Multiplier, so for a fixed ROA, raising the equity multiplier (more debt-funded assets) mechanically raises ROE. If ROA is 8% and the firm levers up from a multiplier of 1.5 to 3.0, ROE rises from 12% to 24% with no operational improvement. The catch: leverage also magnifies downside, raises interest expense (cutting the interest-burden term), and increases bankruptcy risk, so a high ROE built purely on debt is lower quality.

**Q95.** When is a high ROE actually a red flag rather than a strength?

A high ROE is a red flag when it is manufactured by excessive leverage, a shrinking equity base (e.g., large buybacks or accumulated losses), or one-off gains rather than durable operating returns. Because `ROE = ROA * Equity Multiplier`, two firms can show identical 20% ROE while one earns it through 18% ROA and the other through 6% ROA plus 3.3x leverage. DuPont decomposition and a check on ROIC versus WACC separate genuine quality from financial engineering.

**Q96.** What is common-size analysis and why is it useful?

Common-size analysis restates financial statements as percentages of a base figure so firms of different sizes and time periods can be compared. On the income statement every line is expressed as a percent of revenue; on the balance sheet every line is a percent of total assets. For example, showing COGS at 60% of revenue this year versus 55% last year instantly flags margin compression. It is the backbone of trend (vertical) analysis and peer benchmarking.

**Q97.** What are the main limitations of ratio analysis?

Ratios are only as good as the accounting behind them and lack meaning in isolation. Key limitations: they rely on historical book values that may not reflect market reality; differing accounting policies (e.g., Ind-AS vs IFRS vs US GAAP, FIFO vs weighted-average inventory, lease capitalization) hurt comparability; window-dressing and one-off items distort them; and "good" levels are industry-specific, so cross-sector comparison misleads. Ratios should be read as trends and against peers, not as standalone verdicts.

**Q98.** How do accounting standards and seasonality complicate ratio comparisons in practice, with an India angle?

Two firms can look very different purely due to standards and timing rather than economics. Under Ind-AS 116 / IFRS 16, operating leases are capitalized onto the balance sheet, inflating both assets and debt and thus changing D/E, asset turnover, and EBITDA versus older or US-GAAP-treated peers; depreciation methods and revaluations similarly shift ROA and ROCE. Seasonality matters too: a retailer's quarter-end inventory and receivables around Diwali can distort turnover and liquidity ratios, which is why analysts use average (not period-end) balances and compare same-quarter year-over-year.

<a name="A8"></a>

## A8. Earnings Quality & Forensic Accounting  <span class="qrange">(Q99-Q128)</span>

**Q99.** What is "quality of earnings" and why do analysts care more about it than the headline EPS?

Quality of earnings measures how repeatable, cash-backed and conservatively-stated a company's reported profit is, versus how much it depends on aggressive accruals, one-offs or accounting choices. High-quality earnings are supported by operating cash flow, recur period after period, and are not flattered by reserve releases or capitalization tricks. Two firms can report identical EPS but the one whose profit converts cleanly into cash is worth more because the number is more likely to persist.

**Q100.** Why is a persistent gap between operating cash flow (OCF) and net income a warning sign?

Over time, accrual accounting profit should largely convert into cash; if net income keeps rising while OCF stagnates or falls, profit is being driven by non-cash accruals (booked revenue not yet collected, deferred costs, etc.) rather than real cash generation. A simple screen is the cash conversion ratio `OCF / Net Income` — sustainably below ~0.8-1.0 invites scrutiny. The divergence often precedes write-downs or restatements because accruals eventually reverse.

**Q101.** Define the accruals ratio and explain how to read it.

The balance-sheet-free version is `Accruals Ratio = (Net Income - CFO) / Total Assets`, or using both operating and investing cash flow `(NI - CFO - CFI) / Average Total Assets`. It captures the share of earnings NOT backed by cash. A high positive value means earnings are accrual-heavy (lower quality and, per Sloan, likely to mean-revert); a low or negative value means earnings are cash-rich. Example: NI = 100, CFO = 40, Total Assets = 1,000, so accruals ratio = (100-40)/1000 = 6%, signalling 60 of the 100 profit is non-cash.

**Q102.** What is the Sloan accrual anomaly?

Richard Sloan (1996) showed that firms with high accruals (low cash content of earnings) systematically underperform, while firms with low accruals outperform, because investors naively fixate on bottom-line earnings and fail to discount the lower persistence of the accrual component. A long-low-accruals / short-high-accruals strategy historically earned abnormal returns. The intuition: the accrual part of earnings reverses faster than the cash part, so high-accrual earnings are "borrowed from the future."

**Q103.** What is the Beneish M-score and what is it used for?

The Beneish M-score is an eight-variable statistical model that estimates the probability that a company has manipulated its earnings. A score greater than -1.78 flags a likely manipulator (the model is built so that less negative / higher is worse). It was notably the model a group of students used to flag Enron before its collapse. It is a screening tool, not proof of fraud — it raises a red flag for deeper forensic work.

**Q104.** Name the eight inputs to the Beneish M-score and the intuition behind the key ones.

The eight ratios are: DSRI (Days Sales in Receivables Index), GMI (Gross Margin Index), AQI (Asset Quality Index), SGI (Sales Growth Index), DEPI (Depreciation Index), SGAI (SG&A Index), LVGI (Leverage Index), and TATA (Total Accruals to Total Assets). Intuitively, a jump in DSRI suggests inflated/uncollected revenue; deteriorating GMI pressures management to manage earnings; rising AQI hints at capitalizing costs; high SGI creates pressure; falling DEPI suggests slowing depreciation to lift profit; and TATA captures the degree to which earnings are accrual-driven.

**Q105.** In the M-score, which two variables typically carry the most weight and why?

DSRI and TATA (and SGI) tend to be the most influential. DSRI spikes when receivables grow faster than sales, the classic signature of channel stuffing or premature/fictitious revenue; TATA directly measures how much of earnings is accruals rather than cash, the core of the accrual-manipulation story. A manipulator usually shows up as inflated receivables plus high non-cash accruals simultaneously.

**Q106.** What is channel stuffing and how does it show up in the financials?

Channel stuffing is shipping more product to distributors/retailers than they can sell, recognizing the revenue now to hit targets, while real end-demand is unchanged. It inflates current-period revenue and receivables and borrows from future periods, so it shows up as a sharp rise in DSO and receivables outpacing sales, often followed by elevated returns/allowances and a revenue "air pocket" next period. Auto, pharma and FMCG companies near quarter-end are classic venues.

**Q107.** Explain "cookie-jar reserves."

Cookie-jar reserves are over-provisions (for warranties, bad debts, restructuring, litigation) deliberately built up in good years and released into income in weak years to smooth earnings. The reserve acts as a savings jar that management dips into to manufacture steady, beating-expectations profit. Watch for reserves rising when business is strong and falling exactly when an earnings miss looms — the smoothing pattern is the tell.

**Q108.** What is a "big bath" and why do managers take one?

A big bath is deliberately overstating losses/write-offs in an already-bad period (often around a CEO change, recession, or restructuring) to clear the decks. By kitchen-sinking every conceivable charge now, future periods are relieved of those costs and depreciation, making the subsequent recovery look stronger and easier to beat. It is the mirror image of cookie-jar reserves — both move earnings across time.

**Q109.** How does capitalizing operating costs inflate earnings, and how do you detect it?

Recording a cost that should be an expense (e.g., routine maintenance, software, or in WorldCom's case line/network costs) as a capital asset moves it off the income statement into the balance sheet, boosting current profit and only bleeding through slowly as depreciation. Detect it via rising AQI (more "other assets" or capitalized costs), capex growing far faster than revenue, a widening gap between net income and CFO, and unusually long or lengthening asset lives. WorldCom's USD 3.8bn fraud was exactly this.

**Q110.** What is bill-and-hold revenue and when is it legitimate vs abusive?

Bill-and-hold is invoicing a customer and booking revenue while the goods remain physically in the seller's warehouse. It is legitimate only under strict conditions: the buyer (not seller) requested it, has a substantive business reason, the goods are segregated and complete, and risks/rewards have genuinely transferred. It is abusive when used to pull future sales into the current quarter without real transfer of control — a frequent revenue-recognition fraud (Sunbeam under "Chainsaw Al" Dunlap).

**Q111.** What is round-tripping (or "lazy Susan") revenue?

Round-tripping is two companies selling roughly equal amounts of goods/services to each other, or one selling and simultaneously buying back, to inflate reported revenue (and sometimes both sides' top lines) with no real economic substance and no net cash or profit. It was rampant among dot-com and energy-trading firms (e.g., swaps of network capacity). Red flag: revenue grows but margins, cash flow and net income do not, and there are large offsetting purchases from the same counterparties.

**Q112.** Why is a rising Days Sales Outstanding (DSO) a revenue red flag?

`DSO = (Accounts Receivable / Revenue) * 365`. Rising DSO means receivables are growing faster than sales — cash is being collected more slowly, which can signal channel stuffing, premature/fictitious revenue, looser credit terms to buy sales, or deteriorating customer quality. Example: revenue up 10% but receivables up 40% is a strong signal that reported sales are not converting to cash. Always compare DSO trend against peers since some seasonality and industry norms differ.

**Q113.** What do unbilled receivables (contract assets) tell you about earnings quality?

Unbilled receivables arise when revenue is recognized (e.g., percentage-of-completion or over-time contracts) before the customer can be invoiced. A fast-growing unbilled balance means profit is being booked on management's estimates of progress, not on billings or cash — highly subjective and easy to inflate. IT services and EPC/construction firms (in India, infra and IT) warrant extra scrutiny here; spiking unbilled revenue relative to sales is a classic aggressive-recognition flag.

**Q114.** How can swings in deferred revenue signal earnings management?

Deferred (unearned) revenue is a liability for cash collected but not yet earned; its change is a leading indicator of future revenue and of recognition policy. A sudden drop in deferred revenue that boosts current sales can mean the company is accelerating recognition or that bookings are slowing. Conversely, manipulators may also use it as a reserve to smooth — releasing or building it to hit targets. Track the deferred-revenue balance and its growth alongside billings, not just recognized revenue.

**Q115.** Describe a cash-flow classification game and why it matters.

Companies can flatter operating cash flow (the metric investors prize) by shifting outflows out of the operating section: e.g., classifying recurring expenditures as investing (capex), securitizing/factoring receivables to pull collections into CFO, stretching payables, or capitalizing interest. Because OCF is the headline "real cash" number, dressing it up while reported earnings hold can mask deteriorating quality. Always reconcile OCF to net income, read the cash-flow notes, and check whether free cash flow (OCF minus capex) tells a worse story.

**Q116.** Under IFRS/Ind-AS vs US GAAP, how does interest classification create comparability issues in the cash-flow statement?

Under US GAAP, interest paid and received and dividends received are all operating; dividends paid are financing. Under IFRS/Ind-AS there is policy choice — interest paid can be operating or financing, interest/dividends received operating or investing — so two otherwise-identical firms can report different OCF. This is a classification game that can legitimately or aggressively boost reported operating cash flow, so for cross-border comparison reclassify everyone to a consistent definition before comparing.

**Q117.** Why is segment and geographic analysis useful for spotting earnings-quality issues?

Consolidated numbers can hide a struggling core business behind a strong segment or a one-off. Segment disclosures (Ind-AS 108 / IFRS 8) let you see which units drive margin, where growth is slowing, where capital is being parked, and whether profit is concentrated in opaque geographies (often low-tax or hard-to-audit jurisdictions). Sudden margin divergence between segments, profits booked in tax havens, or "corporate/unallocated" line items that absorb losses are red flags worth probing.

**Q118.** What is the abuse of non-GAAP / adjusted EBITDA, and what should you watch for?

Companies present "adjusted" metrics excluding items they deem non-recurring — but serial add-backs of stock-based compensation, "restructuring" that happens every year, "one-off" legal costs, or capitalized software amortization inflate the number that gets multiplied in valuations. The tell is a persistently widening gap between adjusted and GAAP earnings, and add-backs that are clearly recurring cash costs (especially stock comp, which is a real expense). Always rebuild EBITDA yourself and judge each add-back's legitimacy rather than trusting the company's bridge.

**Q119.** Distinguish pro-forma figures from reported (GAAP/Ind-AS) figures.

Reported figures follow the accounting standards and are audited; pro-forma figures are management-constructed hypotheticals — e.g., "as if" an acquisition had occurred all year, or earnings excluding chosen items. Pro-forma can aid genuine comparability (combining merged entities) but is unregulated and easily slanted to flatter. Treat pro-forma as a starting point, always reconcile it back to reported numbers, and never value a company purely off management-defined metrics.

**Q120.** Why are related-party transactions (RPTs) a forensic red flag, and what governs them in India?

RPTs are dealings with entities connected to promoters/management (subsidiaries, promoter firms, key-personnel relatives) where prices may not be arm's-length, enabling tunneling of cash/profits out of the listed company or fabricating revenue. They were central to Satyam and to many promoter-driven frauds. In India, the Companies Act 2013 (Sec 188) and SEBI LODR Regulation 23 mandate audit-committee approval, shareholder approval for material RPTs, and disclosure; a heavy or growing RPT load relative to revenue warrants deep scrutiny.

**Q121.** What are the main types of audit opinion and what does each imply?

Unqualified/clean: statements fairly presented. Qualified ("except for"): a specific, material but non-pervasive issue. Adverse: misstatements are material AND pervasive — the statements as a whole are unreliable. Disclaimer: the auditor could not obtain enough evidence to form an opinion. Anything other than a clean opinion is a serious signal; an adverse or disclaimer opinion effectively means you cannot trust the reported numbers.

**Q122.** What is a going-concern qualification and how should an analyst react?

A going-concern emphasis/qualification is the auditor flagging material uncertainty about the company's ability to continue operating for the next ~12 months (e.g., recurring losses, negative working capital, debt covenant breaches, inability to refinance). It does not mean imminent bankruptcy but materially raises the risk and usually triggers covenant, rating and liquidity concerns. Treat it as a trigger to stress-test liquidity, debt maturities and refinancing capacity before any investment.

**Q123.** What is a restatement and why does it matter for earnings quality?

A restatement is a formal correction of previously issued financial statements because they contained material errors or were not prepared per GAAP/Ind-AS. It directly destroys the reliability of historical trends, often signals weak internal controls or fraud, and is empirically associated with sharp negative stock reactions and elevated future risk. Distinguish "little r" (immaterial, revised in current filings) from "Big R" (material, requiring reissuance and an 8-K-type disclosure) — the latter is far more serious.

**Q124.** How does off-balance-sheet leverage distort the picture, and how do you adjust?

Obligations kept off the balance sheet — historically operating leases, SPEs/VIEs, factored receivables, take-or-pay contracts, and guarantees — understate true leverage and overstate returns on capital. To compare fairly, capitalize them: bring the debt-like obligation and a corresponding asset onto an adjusted balance sheet and recompute leverage and coverage. Enron's special-purpose entities were the textbook abuse, hiding billions of debt off-book until the structures collapsed.

**Q125.** How did IFRS 16 / Ind-AS 116 change lease accounting, and how do you adjust pre-2019 comparables?

IFRS 16 / Ind-AS 116 (effective ~2019) require lessees to capitalize nearly all leases — recognizing a right-of-use asset and a lease liability — so rent that was once a single operating expense now splits into depreciation plus interest, lifting reported EBITDA and gross debt. To compare a post-2019 company with older data or with US GAAP (which keeps operating leases largely off-balance-sheet in P&L geography), either capitalize old leases (a common rule of thumb is debt-equivalent ≈ 8x annual rent, or PV of lease commitments) or strip the lease effect out, so the comparison is apples-to-apples.

**Q126.** Why must pensions and other post-employment obligations be adjusted for comparability?

A material unfunded defined-benefit pension or gratuity obligation is economically debt, and pension expense can be flattered by aggressive assumptions — a high expected return on plan assets, a high discount rate, or low wage-growth assumptions all reduce reported expense and the liability. For comparison, add the net unfunded obligation to debt, and normalize the assumed return/discount rate to a realistic level. In India, gratuity and leave-encashment provisions under Ind-AS 19 deserve the same scrutiny, especially the discount-rate choice.

**Q127.** What does it mean to "normalize" earnings, and how do you do it?

Normalizing strips out non-recurring, non-operating and accounting-distortion items to estimate sustainable, repeatable earnings power for valuation. Steps: remove genuine one-offs (asset-sale gains, litigation settlements, restructuring), reverse obvious accrual manipulation, adjust for cyclicality (use mid-cycle margins), put leases/pensions on a consistent basis, normalize the tax rate to a sustainable level, and exclude unsustainable reserve releases. Example: reported PBT 120 including a 30 one-time land-sale gain and a 10 unusual litigation charge normalizes toward 120 - 30 + 10 = 100 of recurring pre-tax profit.

**Q128.** Give a quick worked example of working-capital manipulation and tie together the cash-fraud lesson from Satyam.

Near year-end a firm can stretch payables, factor receivables and defer inventory purchases to flatter both reported profit and operating cash flow, then reverse it next period — so always look at multi-quarter working-capital trends, not a single snapshot. The deeper lesson from Satyam (2009), where ~INR 7,000 crore of "cash and bank balances" were simply fictitious, is that even the asset analysts trust most can be fabricated: independently verify cash via bank confirmations, reconcile interest income to the cash balance (fake cash earns no real interest), and treat unverifiable cash, related-party flows and auditor red flags as central to forensic analysis rather than back-office detail.

<a name="A3"></a>

## A3. Corporate Finance  <span class="qrange">(Q129-Q169)</span>

**Q129.** What is the Net Present Value (NPV) of a project and what is the decision rule?

NPV is the sum of all cash flows discounted to present value at the project's required rate of return, minus the initial outlay: `NPV = -CF0 + sum( CFt / (1+r)^t )`. Accept if NPV > 0 (it adds value), reject if NPV < 0. Example: outlay 100, cash flows of 60 and 70 at r=10% give NPV = -100 + 60/1.1 + 70/1.21 = -100 + 54.5 + 57.9 = +12.4, so accept.

**Q130.** What is the Internal Rate of Return (IRR)?

IRR is the discount rate that makes NPV exactly zero: it solves `0 = -CF0 + sum( CFt / (1+IRR)^t )`. The rule is to accept a project if IRR > cost of capital (hurdle rate). It is a percentage measure of return, which managers find intuitive, but it implicitly assumes interim cash flows are reinvested at the IRR itself.

**Q131.** What is the payback period and its main weakness?

Payback period is the time taken for cumulative cash flows to recover the initial investment. If a project costs 100 and returns 40 per year, payback is 2.5 years. Its weaknesses are that it ignores the time value of money and ignores all cash flows occurring after the payback point, so it can reject high-NPV long-dated projects.

**Q132.** How does discounted payback differ from ordinary payback?

Discounted payback first discounts each cash flow to present value, then measures how long the cumulative discounted cash flows take to recover the outlay. It fixes the time-value flaw of simple payback but still ignores cash flows beyond the cutoff. It is always longer than ordinary payback because discounting shrinks each inflow.

**Q133.** What is the Profitability Index (PI) and its decision rule?

PI is the ratio of the present value of future cash flows to the initial investment: `PI = PV(future cash flows) / Initial Investment = 1 + NPV/Investment`. Accept if PI > 1. It is useful for ranking projects under capital rationing because it measures value created per rupee (or dollar) invested.

**Q134.** What is the difference between independent and mutually exclusive projects?

Independent projects can all be accepted if each meets the criterion; the decision on one does not affect the others. Mutually exclusive projects compete, so accepting one means rejecting the rest (e.g. two designs for the same factory). For independent projects NPV and IRR usually agree; for mutually exclusive projects they can conflict, and NPV should be used to rank.

**Q135.** Why is NPV generally preferred over IRR?

NPV measures the actual rupee/dollar value added to shareholders, assumes reinvestment at the realistic cost of capital, and works correctly even with non-conventional cash flows. IRR can mislead because it ignores project scale, assumes reinvestment at the IRR, and can produce multiple or no solutions. When NPV and IRR conflict on mutually exclusive projects, NPV wins.

**Q136.** What causes the NPV-versus-IRR conflict on mutually exclusive projects?

Conflicts arise from differences in scale (size of investment) or in the timing pattern of cash flows. IRR favours projects with early cash flows or smaller scale because it is a rate, whereas NPV captures total value. A large project may have a lower IRR but a much higher NPV; since the goal is to maximise value, NPV should decide.

**Q137.** What is the crossover rate?

The crossover rate is the discount rate at which two projects have equal NPV — i.e. the IRR of the differential cash flows (Project A minus Project B). Below the crossover rate one project has higher NPV; above it, the ranking flips. It tells you the cost of capital at which your preference between two mutually exclusive projects changes.

**Q138.** When can a project have multiple IRRs?

Multiple IRRs occur with non-conventional (unconventional) cash flows — when the sign of cash flows changes more than once (e.g. outflow, inflows, then a large outflow for cleanup/decommissioning). By Descartes' rule of signs there can be as many IRRs as sign changes. In such cases IRR is unreliable and NPV or MIRR should be used instead.

**Q139.** What is MIRR and how does it fix IRR's reinvestment problem?

Modified IRR (MIRR) compounds all positive cash flows forward to the terminal date at the cost of capital (the reinvestment rate) and discounts all negative flows to time zero at a finance rate, then finds the single rate linking them: `MIRR = (FV of inflows / PV of outflows)^(1/n) - 1`. Because reinvestment is at the realistic cost of capital and there is one unique solution, MIRR avoids both the optimistic-reinvestment and multiple-IRR problems.

**Q140.** A project costs 1000, returns 600 and 700 over two years; cost of capital is 12%. Compute MIRR.

FV of inflows at year 2 = 600*(1.12) + 700 = 672 + 700 = 1372. PV of outflow = 1000. MIRR = (1372/1000)^(1/2) - 1 = 1.1713 - 1 = 0.1713, about 17.1%. Since MIRR (17.1%) exceeds the 12% hurdle, accept.

**Q141.** How do you choose between mutually exclusive projects with unequal lives?

Use the Equivalent Annual Annuity (EAA) or the replacement-chain method to put projects on a comparable annual basis. EAA converts each project's NPV into a level annual cash flow: `EAA = NPV / annuity_factor(r, n)`. Choose the project with the higher EAA, since over repeated cycles it generates more value per year.

**Q142.** Why should sunk costs be ignored but opportunity costs included in capital budgeting?

Sunk costs are already incurred and unrecoverable, so they do not change with the accept/reject decision and must be excluded from incremental cash flows. Opportunity costs — the value forgone by using an asset you already own (e.g. land that could be sold) — are real economic costs of the project and must be included. Capital budgeting evaluates only incremental, forward-looking cash flows.

**Q143.** What is CAPM and what does each term mean?

The Capital Asset Pricing Model gives the required return on equity: `Re = Rf + beta*(Rm - Rf)`. Rf is the risk-free rate, beta measures the stock's sensitivity to market moves, and (Rm - Rf) is the equity risk premium. Example: Rf=7% (Indian 10-yr G-sec), beta=1.2, premium=6%, so Re = 7% + 1.2*6% = 14.2%.

**Q144.** What does beta measure and what does a beta of 1.5 mean?

Beta measures the systematic (non-diversifiable) risk of a stock relative to the overall market; it is the covariance of the stock with the market divided by the market variance. A beta of 1.5 means the stock tends to move 1.5% for every 1% move in the market — more volatile than the index (Nifty or S&P 500). The market portfolio has beta 1.0 and the risk-free asset has beta 0.

**Q145.** What is the after-tax cost of debt and why use the after-tax figure?

After-tax cost of debt is `Rd*(1 - Tc)`, where Rd is the yield/interest rate on debt and Tc is the marginal corporate tax rate. Interest is tax-deductible, so debt provides a tax shield that lowers its effective cost. Example: Rd=9%, Tc=25%, after-tax cost = 9%*(1-0.25) = 6.75%.

**Q146.** Write the WACC formula and explain why it is the discount rate for a project.

`WACC = (E/V)*Re + (D/V)*Rd*(1-Tc)`, where E and D are the market values of equity and debt, V = E+D, Re is cost of equity and Rd*(1-Tc) is after-tax cost of debt. It is the blended required return of all capital providers, so it is the minimum return a project of average risk must earn to satisfy both shareholders and lenders. Discounting at WACC values cash flows at the firm's true opportunity cost of capital.

**Q147.** Compute WACC: equity 600, debt 400, Re=15%, Rd=10%, tax 30%.

V = 600 + 400 = 1000, so E/V = 0.6 and D/V = 0.4. WACC = 0.6*15% + 0.4*10%*(1-0.30) = 9.0% + 0.4*7% = 9.0% + 2.8% = 11.8%. This 11.8% is the hurdle rate for projects of similar risk to the firm.

**Q148.** Why should WACC use market values and target weights rather than book values?

Market values reflect the actual current opportunity cost of capital that investors demand, whereas book values are historical accounting figures that can be badly out of date. Using market (or the firm's target) capital-structure weights gives a forward-looking cost consistent with how the firm intends to finance itself. Book weights can materially distort WACC, especially for firms whose equity trades far above book value.

**Q149.** What is the equity risk premium and roughly what value is used in India versus the US?

The equity risk premium (ERP) is the extra return investors require for holding the market portfolio over the risk-free asset, (Rm - Rf). In the US it is commonly estimated around 4.5-5.5%; for India practitioners typically use a higher mature-market premium plus an additional country risk premium, giving an effective ERP often in the 7-9% range. The exact figure depends on whether a historical, implied, or survey approach is used.

**Q150.** What is country risk premium and how is it applied for valuing Indian companies?

Country risk premium (CRP) is extra return demanded for the political, economic and currency risk of investing in a given country relative to a mature market like the US. A common approach (Damodaran) is CRP = sovereign default spread * (equity volatility / bond volatility), using India's rating-based spread. It is added into the cost of equity, e.g. `Re = Rf + beta*(mature ERP) + CRP`, raising the discount rate for Indian cash flows.

**Q151.** What is the difference between levered and unlevered beta?

Levered beta (equity beta) reflects both business risk and the extra risk from financial leverage, so it rises with debt. Unlevered beta (asset beta) strips out leverage to capture pure business risk. You unlever a comparable firm's beta to isolate business risk, then relever it at your own firm's capital structure to estimate your equity beta.

**Q152.** State the Hamada equation for levering and unlevering beta.

Hamada: `beta_levered = beta_unlevered * [1 + (1 - Tc)*(D/E)]`. To unlever, rearrange: `beta_unlevered = beta_levered / [1 + (1 - Tc)*(D/E)]`. Example: comparable beta_L=1.2, its D/E=0.5, Tc=25% gives beta_U = 1.2 / (1 + 0.75*0.5) = 1.2/1.375 = 0.87 — the pure business-risk beta.

**Q153.** Why does a firm's equity beta increase as it takes on more debt?

More debt means fixed interest obligations that must be paid before equity holders, magnifying the volatility of residual cash flows to shareholders. This financial leverage adds financial risk on top of business risk, so the equity beta rises. The Hamada relation shows beta_levered increasing with D/E, holding business risk (asset beta) constant.

**Q154.** State Modigliani-Miller Proposition I without taxes and its core intuition.

MM Proposition I (no taxes) states that in perfect markets the value of a firm is independent of its capital structure: `V_levered = V_unlevered`. The "pie" of firm value is fixed by its assets and cash flows; slicing it differently between debt and equity does not change the total. Investors can replicate or undo corporate leverage through homemade leverage, so they will not pay a premium for it.

**Q155.** State MM Proposition II without taxes.

MM Proposition II says the cost of equity rises linearly with leverage: `Re = Ra + (Ra - Rd)*(D/E)`, where Ra is the unlevered (asset) cost of capital. As cheaper debt is added, the increasing financial risk raises the required equity return by exactly enough to keep WACC constant. So WACC stays flat and firm value is unchanged.

**Q156.** How does MM Proposition I change when corporate taxes are introduced?

With taxes, interest is deductible, creating a tax shield that adds value: `V_levered = V_unlevered + Tc*D`, where Tc*D is the present value of the perpetual debt tax shield. This implies firm value rises with leverage and, taken literally, that 100% debt is optimal. The unrealistic all-debt conclusion is what motivates the trade-off theory.

**Q157.** What is the trade-off theory of capital structure?

Trade-off theory says firms balance the tax-shield benefit of debt against the rising present value of financial distress and bankruptcy costs. The optimal capital structure is where the marginal tax benefit of an extra rupee of debt equals the marginal expected distress cost: `V_levered = V_unlevered + PV(tax shield) - PV(distress costs)`. It explains why profitable, stable firms borrow more and why an interior optimum exists rather than 100% debt.

**Q158.** What is the pecking order theory and how does it differ from trade-off theory?

Pecking order theory (Myers-Majluf) says, because of asymmetric information, firms prefer internal funds first, then debt, and issue equity only as a last resort, since issuing equity signals managers think shares are overvalued. Unlike trade-off theory, it implies no well-defined target leverage ratio — observed leverage simply reflects the cumulative gap between internal cash and investment needs. It explains why highly profitable firms often carry little debt (they fund internally).

**Q159.** What are the costs of financial distress?

Direct costs are legal, accounting and administrative fees of bankruptcy/restructuring. Indirect costs are larger and include lost sales, supplier and customer flight, key-employee departures, fire-sale asset disposals, and agency problems such as underinvestment and asset substitution. These costs rise with leverage and cap how much debt a firm can usefully take on.

**Q160.** How does WACC behave as a firm increases leverage under the trade-off view?

Initially WACC falls as cheap, tax-shielded debt replaces costly equity. Beyond an optimal point, rising costs of debt and equity (from financial distress and default risk) outweigh the tax benefit, so WACC turns up, giving a U-shaped curve. The optimal capital structure is the leverage that minimises WACC, which also maximises firm value.

**Q161.** What is the agency cost of debt and what conflicts does it create?

Agency costs of debt arise from conflicts between shareholders and bondholders once debt is in place. Examples are asset substitution (taking on riskier projects that benefit equity at lenders' expense) and the underinvestment/debt-overhang problem (skipping positive-NPV projects because gains accrue mainly to bondholders). Lenders anticipate this and impose covenants and higher rates, which are a real cost of leverage.

**Q162.** State the Dividend Irrelevance theorem and its assumptions.

Miller and Modigliani's dividend irrelevance theorem says that, in perfect markets (no taxes, no transaction costs, fixed investment policy), dividend policy does not affect firm value; only the firm's investment and earning power matter. A shareholder wanting cash can sell shares (homemade dividends), and one wanting to reinvest can buy more, so the payout split is irrelevant. Real-world frictions — taxes, signaling, transaction costs — are what make dividend policy matter.

**Q163.** What is dividend signaling?

Dividend signaling theory holds that, because managers have inside information, changes in dividends convey information about expected future earnings. Raising the dividend signals management's confidence in sustainable cash flows; cutting it signals trouble and usually triggers a sharp price drop. This explains why firms are reluctant to cut dividends and tend to smooth them over time.

**Q164.** Compare a cash dividend with a share buyback.

Both return cash to shareholders, but a dividend pays everyone proportionally and tends to be sticky, whereas a buyback reduces share count, boosts EPS, and is more flexible/one-off. Buybacks let shareholders self-select on timing and (in many regimes) offer tax advantages by converting income into capital gains. In an idealised no-tax world the two are equivalent in value; differences arise from taxes, signaling and flexibility.

**Q165.** What is the Gordon Growth (dividend discount) model and how does it link price to payout?

The Gordon Growth Model values a share as the present value of dividends growing at a constant rate g: `P0 = D1 / (Re - g)`, where D1 is next year's dividend and Re the cost of equity. It links payout, growth and value because `g = retention ratio * ROE` and D1 = EPS*(1 - retention). Example: D1=10, Re=12%, g=4% gives P0 = 10/(0.12-0.04) = 125.

**Q166.** Does paying a higher dividend (lower retention) always raise the share price in the Gordon model?

No. Raising the payout increases D1 but lowers g (since g = retention*ROE), so the net effect depends on whether the firm's ROE exceeds its cost of equity Re. If ROE > Re, retaining and reinvesting creates value and a higher payout lowers price; if ROE < Re, paying out is better. Only when ROE = Re is dividend policy value-neutral, consistent with MM irrelevance.

**Q167.** What is the clientele effect in dividend policy?
