The clientele effect is the tendency for different investor groups to prefer different payout policies based on their tax status and income needs — e.g. tax-exempt pension funds and retirees favour high dividends, while high-tax investors prefer low-payout, growth/buyback stocks. Firms attract a clientele matched to their policy, so changing policy can disturb that base. At the margin this makes a stable, predictable policy valuable.

**Q168.** Explain the history of the Dividend Distribution Tax (DDT) in India and the current dividend tax regime.

Historically India levied a Dividend Distribution Tax (DDT) paid by the company on dividends declared (effectively around 20.36% grossed-up), making dividends tax-free in investors' hands up to limits. The Finance Act 2020 abolished DDT effective 1 April 2020 and shifted to the classical system: dividends are now taxed in the shareholder's hands at their applicable slab/rate, with companies deducting TDS (10% above the threshold for residents). This made dividends less tax-efficient for high-income investors and increased the relative appeal of buybacks.

**Q169.** How are share buybacks taxed in India and how does that compare with dividends?

After DDT's abolition, buybacks of listed shares were subject to a buyback distribution tax paid by the company (around 20% under Section 115QA), making proceeds tax-free for shareholders. From 1 October 2024 the regime changed so that buyback proceeds are taxed in the shareholder's hands as deemed dividend income at their slab rate, aligning buyback and dividend taxation. This removed the earlier tax arbitrage that had made buybacks markedly more attractive than dividends for Indian investors.

<a name="A4"></a>

## A4. Time Value of Money & Fixed Income  <span class="qrange">(Q170-Q189)</span>

**Q170.** What is the time value of money, and how do you compute present value and future value?

Money today is worth more than the same amount later because it can be invested to earn a return. Future value compounds a present sum forward: `FV = PV*(1+r)^n`, while present value discounts a future sum back: `PV = FV/(1+r)^n`. For example, $100 invested at r=8% for n=3 years grows to FV = 100*1.08^3 = $125.97; conversely, $125.97 received in 3 years is worth $100 today at an 8% discount rate.

**Q171.** What is the difference between an ordinary annuity and an annuity due, and how do you value each?

An ordinary annuity pays at the end of each period (most bonds, loans), while an annuity due pays at the beginning (rent, leases, some insurance premiums). The PV of an ordinary annuity is `PV = CF*[1 - (1+r)^-n]/r`; an annuity due is simply that multiplied by (1+r) because every cash flow arrives one period earlier. So an annuity due is always worth more than an otherwise identical ordinary annuity.

**Q172.** Work out the present value of a 5-year annuity paying INR 10,000 per year at 9%.

Using `PV = CF*[1 - (1+r)^-n]/r` = 10,000*[1 - 1.09^-5]/0.09. Here 1.09^-5 = 0.6499, so the annuity factor = (1 - 0.6499)/0.09 = 3.8897. PV = 10,000*3.8897 = INR 38,897. If it were an annuity due, multiply by 1.09 to get INR 42,398.

**Q173.** What is a perpetuity and how is it valued?

A perpetuity is a stream of equal cash flows that continues forever, valued as `PV = CF/r`. For example, a console-style bond paying $50 per year at a 5% discount rate is worth 50/0.05 = $1,000. The intuition: even though payments never stop, distant cash flows discount to near-zero, so the sum converges.

**Q174.** What is a growing perpetuity and where is the formula used?

A growing perpetuity has cash flows that grow at a constant rate g forever: `PV = CF1/(r-g)`, where CF1 is next period's cash flow. It requires r > g or the value is meaningless (infinite). This is the engine behind the Gordon Growth dividend discount model and the terminal value in a DCF; e.g., a dividend of $2 growing at 3% with r=8% is worth 2/(0.08-0.03) = $40.

**Q175.** What is the effective annual rate and why does compounding frequency matter?

The stated (nominal) rate understates the true return when interest compounds more than once a year. The effective annual rate is `EAR = (1+r/m)^m - 1`, where r is the nominal annual rate and m is compounding periods per year. A 12% nominal rate compounded monthly gives EAR = (1+0.12/12)^12 - 1 = 12.68%, so the borrower actually pays more than 12%.

**Q176.** How does continuous compounding work and what is its formula?

Continuous compounding is the limit as compounding frequency goes to infinity, using the exponential: `FV = PV*e^(rT)` and `EAR = e^r - 1`. For r=12%, the continuously compounded EAR = e^0.12 - 1 = 12.75%, the maximum possible for a 12% nominal rate. Continuous compounding is standard in derivatives pricing (e.g., Black-Scholes discounting) for mathematical convenience.

**Q177.** Define the key terms of a bond: par/face value, coupon, and maturity.

Face (par) value is the principal repaid at maturity, typically $1,000 in the US or INR 100/INR 1,000 for Indian G-Secs. The coupon is the periodic interest, quoted as an annual coupon rate times face value (e.g., a 6% coupon on $1,000 pays $60/year, often $30 semi-annually). Maturity is the date the principal is returned and coupons stop.

**Q178.** What is the difference between the clean price and the dirty price of a bond?

The dirty (full) price is what the buyer actually pays and includes accrued interest since the last coupon; the clean price is the quoted price excluding accrued interest. The relationship is `Dirty = Clean + Accrued Interest`, where accrued = coupon*(days since last coupon/days in period). Markets quote clean prices so the figure does not saw-tooth up and down purely due to coupon accrual.

**Q179.** Why do bond prices and yields move inversely?

A bond's cash flows are fixed, so its price is just the PV of those flows; when market yields rise, the discount rate goes up and the PV (price) falls, and vice versa. If you hold a 6% bond and new bonds yield 8%, yours is less attractive and must trade below par to compensate. This inverse, convex relationship is the foundation of all fixed income risk.

**Q180.** What is yield to maturity (YTM)?

YTM is the single discount rate that sets the PV of all a bond's future coupons and principal equal to its current market price; it is the bond's internal rate of return if held to maturity and all coupons are reinvested at that same rate. It is solved iteratively from `Price = sum(Coupon/(1+y)^t) + Face/(1+y)^n`. A bond trading at a discount has YTM above its coupon rate; at a premium, YTM is below the coupon.

**Q181.** Distinguish current yield, YTM, and yield to call.

Current yield = annual coupon / current price, a simple income measure that ignores capital gain/loss and reinvestment. YTM captures total return including the pull-to-par and the timing of all cash flows. Yield to call (YTC) is the same calculation but assuming the issuer redeems the bond at the first call date and call price; for a callable premium bond, prudent investors quote the lower of YTM and YTC (yield-to-worst).

**Q182.** What is a zero-coupon bond and how is it priced and taxed?

A zero-coupon bond pays no coupons; it is issued at a deep discount and redeems at face value, with all return coming from price appreciation. Price = `Face/(1+y)^n`; e.g., a 10-year zero at 7% yield is worth 100/1.07^10 = INR 50.83 per 100 face. Note that even with no cash coupon, imputed interest is typically taxed annually as accrued (phantom income) in the US and India.

**Q183.** What is Macaulay duration?

Macaulay duration is the weighted-average time, in years, until a bond's cash flows are received, where each weight is that cash flow's PV as a fraction of price. It is expressed as `MacDur = sum(t*PV(CFt))/Price`. A zero-coupon bond's Macaulay duration equals its maturity exactly, while coupon bonds have duration less than maturity because some value is returned earlier.

**Q184.** What is modified duration and how does it estimate price changes?

Modified duration converts Macaulay duration into a price-sensitivity measure: `ModDur = MacDur/(1+y/m)`, where y/m is the periodic yield. The first-order price approximation is `%change in Price = -ModDur*change in yield`. For example, a bond with ModDur = 7 facing a +50bp yield move (delta_y = 0.005) loses roughly -7*0.005 = -3.5% of its price.

**Q185.** What is convexity and why does it matter?

Convexity measures the curvature of the price-yield relationship that linear duration misses; because the curve is convex, duration overestimates price falls when yields rise and underestimates price gains when yields drop. The improved estimate is `%change in Price = -ModDur*delta_y + 0.5*Convexity*delta_y^2`. Higher convexity is desirable (more upside, less downside for a given yield move), which matters most for large rate shifts and barbell portfolios.

**Q186.** What are DV01 and PV01 and how are they used?

DV01 (dollar value of a basis point), also called PV01, is the absolute price change in money terms for a 1bp (0.01%) move in yield: `DV01 = ModDur*Price*0.0001`. A bond with ModDur = 7 priced at $1,000 has DV01 = 7*1000*0.0001 = $0.70 per basis point. Traders use DV01 to size hedges so that the DV01s of the long and short positions offset, neutralizing rate risk.

**Q187.** What do the shapes of the yield curve signal, and what does inversion mean?

The yield curve plots yield against maturity: normal (upward-sloping) reflects expectations of growth and a term premium; flat suggests uncertainty or transition; inverted (short rates above long rates) means investors expect rate cuts amid slowing growth. An inverted curve, especially the 10y-2y or 10y-3m spread, has historically been one of the most reliable recession predictors in the US. In India the focus is on the G-Sec curve, e.g., the 10-year benchmark yield versus shorter T-bills.

**Q188.** What is a credit spread and what drives it?

A credit spread is the extra yield a corporate (or other risky) bond pays over a comparable-maturity risk-free government bond: `Credit Spread = Corporate Yield - Risk-free Yield`. It compensates investors for default risk, liquidity risk, and uncertainty; e.g., a BBB bond yielding 9.5% against a 7% G-Sec has a 250bp spread. Spreads widen sharply in stress (flight to quality) and narrow in risk-on conditions, making them a key gauge of credit-market sentiment.

**Q189.** What are G-Secs and what is the RBI's role in the Indian bond market?

Government Securities (G-Secs) are sovereign debt issued by the Government of India, including dated bonds and short-term Treasury bills, considered risk-free in INR terms and used as the benchmark for the rupee yield curve. The RBI acts as the government's debt manager and banker, conducting primary auctions, managing the borrowing program, and using open market operations (OMO) and the repo rate to steer liquidity and yields. Unlike the US where the Treasury issues and the Fed conducts monetary policy separately, in India the RBI both manages public debt and sets policy rates, and banks must hold G-Secs to meet their SLR (Statutory Liquidity Ratio).

<a name="A5"></a>

## A5. Economics & Statistics for Finance  <span class="qrange">(Q190-Q204)</span>

**Q190.** What is GDP and what are its components under the expenditure approach?

GDP (Gross Domestic Product) is the total market value of all final goods and services produced within a country's borders in a given period. Under the expenditure approach: `GDP = C + I + G + (X - M)`, where C is private consumption, I is investment (capex + inventory change), G is government spending, and (X - M) is net exports. For example, if consumption is INR 140 lakh cr, investment 60, government 35, exports 25 and imports 30, then GDP = 140 + 60 + 35 + (25 - 30) = INR 230 lakh cr.

**Q191.** What is the difference between real and nominal GDP, and what is the GDP deflator?

Nominal GDP is measured at current prices, so it rises with both output and inflation; real GDP holds prices fixed at a base year, isolating actual changes in output. The link is the GDP deflator: `GDP Deflator = (Nominal GDP / Real GDP) * 100`. If nominal GDP grows 11% but the deflator shows 5% inflation, real growth is roughly 11% - 5% = 6%, which is the figure that reflects genuine economic expansion.

**Q192.** How is inflation measured via CPI and WPI, and how do they differ in India?

CPI (Consumer Price Index) tracks a basket of goods and services bought by households (heavily weighted to food and fuel), while WPI (Wholesale Price Index) tracks prices at the wholesale/producer level and excludes services. Inflation is the percent change: `Inflation = (Index_now - Index_year_ago) / Index_year_ago * 100`. In India the RBI's official inflation target is based on CPI (Combined), not WPI, because CPI better reflects the cost of living faced by consumers.

**Q193.** Explain India's inflation-targeting framework and the RBI's mandate.

Since 2016 the RBI follows a flexible inflation-targeting regime set jointly with the government, aiming for CPI inflation of 4% with a tolerance band of +/- 2% (i.e., 2% to 6%). The Monetary Policy Committee (MPC), a six-member body, sets the policy repo rate to keep inflation within this band while supporting growth. If CPI breaches the band for three consecutive quarters, the RBI must formally explain the failure and remedial steps to the government.

**Q194.** What are the RBI's main monetary policy tools: repo rate, CRR, SLR, and OMO?

The repo rate is the rate at which the RBI lends short-term funds to banks; raising it makes credit costlier and cools demand/inflation. CRR (Cash Reserve Ratio) is the share of deposits banks must park with the RBI as cash (no interest), and SLR (Statutory Liquidity Ratio) is the share they must hold in approved liquid assets like G-secs; both control how much banks can lend. OMO (Open Market Operations) is the RBI buying or selling government securities to inject or absorb durable liquidity, e.g., buying G-secs pushes money into the system and softens rates.

**Q195.** How does monetary policy transmit through interest rates to bonds and equities?

When a central bank raises policy rates, bond yields rise and existing bond prices fall (inverse relationship), because `Bond Price = sum of CF / (1+y)^t` falls as y increases. Equities typically fall too: higher rates raise the discount rate in DCF valuations and increase borrowing costs, compressing the present value of future cash flows. Rate-sensitive sectors (banks, real estate, autos) and high-duration growth/tech stocks react most sharply to rate changes.

**Q196.** What is fiscal policy, and how does the fiscal deficit differ from a budget surplus?

Fiscal policy is the government's use of taxation and spending to influence aggregate demand; expansionary policy (more spending or tax cuts) stimulates growth, while contractionary policy restrains an overheating economy. The fiscal deficit is the shortfall when total expenditure exceeds total revenue (excluding borrowings) and is usually expressed as a percentage of GDP. India targets a glide path toward a fiscal deficit around 4.5% of GDP; large deficits are financed by borrowing, which can crowd out private investment and pressure bond yields.

**Q197.** What moves the USD/INR exchange rate, and what do appreciation and depreciation mean?

USD/INR is the number of rupees per US dollar; if it rises from 83 to 86, the rupee has depreciated (weakened) and the dollar appreciated. Key drivers include interest-rate differentials, inflation gaps, the current account/trade deficit, FII/FPI capital flows, crude oil prices (India is a big importer), and RBI intervention via forex reserves. A weaker rupee helps exporters and IT/pharma firms but raises the cost of imports and dollar-denominated debt, feeding into imported inflation.

**Q198.** What is the business cycle, and what are its phases?

The business cycle is the recurring pattern of fluctuations in economic activity around a long-run growth trend, with four phases: expansion, peak, contraction (recession), and trough. A common rule-of-thumb defines a recession as two consecutive quarters of negative real GDP growth. Policymakers use countercyclical tools, cutting rates and boosting spending in downturns and tightening during overheating booms, to smooth the cycle.

**Q199.** Explain demand-supply equilibrium and price elasticity of demand.

Market equilibrium occurs where the quantity demanded equals quantity supplied, setting the clearing price; a shift in either curve moves price and quantity. Price elasticity of demand measures responsiveness: `PED = (% change in quantity demanded) / (% change in price)`. If a 10% price rise cuts quantity demanded by 20%, PED = -2 (elastic); essentials like staples or fuel tend to be inelastic (|PED| < 1), so revenue rises when their price increases.

**Q200.** Define mean, variance, and standard deviation, and why standard deviation matters in finance.

The mean is the average, `mean = (sum of x_i) / n`; variance is the average squared deviation from the mean, `var = sum((x_i - mean)^2) / n`; and standard deviation is `sigma = sqrt(var)`. Standard deviation is the workhorse measure of risk/volatility in finance because it is in the same units as returns. For returns of 2%, -1%, 4%, 3%, the mean is 2%, and sigma quantifies how widely actual returns scatter around that 2%.

**Q201.** What is the normal distribution and how do z-scores work?

The normal (Gaussian) distribution is the symmetric bell curve fully described by its mean and standard deviation, where about 68% of outcomes fall within +/-1 sigma, 95% within +/-2 sigma, and 99.7% within +/-3 sigma. A z-score standardizes a value: `z = (x - mean) / sigma`, telling you how many standard deviations x lies from the mean. If a stock's monthly return is 8% with mean 2% and sigma 3%, then z = (8 - 2) / 3 = 2, an unusually high outcome (top ~2.5% of the distribution).

**Q202.** Explain covariance and correlation, including the correlation formula.

Covariance measures whether two variables move together: `cov(x,y) = sum((x_i - mean_x)*(y_i - mean_y)) / n`; its sign indicates direction but its magnitude is unbounded and scale-dependent. Correlation normalizes it to a -1 to +1 range: `corr = cov(x,y) / (sigma_x * sigma_y)`. A correlation of +1 means perfect co-movement, 0 means no linear relationship, and -1 means they move exactly opposite; low or negative correlations between assets are what make diversification reduce portfolio risk.

**Q203.** How are regression slope, beta, and R-squared defined and interpreted?

In a simple linear regression `y = alpha + beta*x + error`, the slope is `beta = cov(x,y) / var(x)`. In finance, beta from regressing a stock's returns on the market's returns measures systematic risk: beta = 1.2 means the stock tends to move 1.2% for every 1% market move. R-squared is the fraction of the dependent variable's variance explained by the model (`R^2 = 1 - SS_residual / SS_total`); for a stock-vs-market regression, R-squared shows how much of the stock's movement is driven by the market versus stock-specific factors.

**Q204.** What is hypothesis testing with a p-value, and why do skewness and kurtosis matter for fat tails in finance?

In hypothesis testing you assume a null hypothesis (e.g., a strategy's true alpha is zero), compute a test statistic, and the p-value is the probability of seeing a result this extreme if the null were true; p < 0.05 conventionally lets you reject the null as statistically significant. Skewness measures asymmetry of returns (negative skew = frequent small gains but occasional large losses), and kurtosis measures tail-heaviness, with the normal distribution having kurtosis of 3 (excess kurtosis 0). Financial returns typically show negative skew and high (leptokurtic) kurtosis, meaning extreme crashes occur far more often than a normal distribution predicts, which is exactly why VaR models that assume normality understate true tail risk.

<a name="A6"></a>

## A6. Indian Markets, Structure & Regulation  <span class="qrange">(Q205-Q224)</span>

**Q205.** What is SEBI and what are its core functions?

SEBI (Securities and Exchange Board of India), established as a statutory body under the SEBI Act 1992, is the regulator of the Indian securities market. Its mandate is threefold: protect the interests of investors, promote development of the market, and regulate it. In practice it registers and supervises intermediaries (brokers, mutual funds, RAs, merchant bankers), frames regulations (disclosure norms, insider-trading rules, takeover code), and has quasi-legislative, quasi-executive and quasi-judicial powers including investigation, search-and-seizure, penalties and disgorgement.

**Q206.** What is the difference between the primary market and the secondary market?

The primary market is where securities are issued for the first time and capital flows directly from investors to the issuer — e.g., an IPO, FPO, rights issue, or fresh bond issue. The secondary market is where already-issued securities trade between investors (on exchanges like NSE/BSE), providing liquidity and price discovery but no new capital to the issuer. A company raises money only in the primary market; subsequent share-price moves on the exchange do not affect its cash balance.

**Q207.** What is the difference between NSE and BSE?

Both are SEBI-regulated stock exchanges. The BSE (Bombay Stock Exchange), founded 1875, is Asia's oldest exchange and lists the most companies; its benchmark is the Sensex (30 stocks). The NSE (National Stock Exchange), launched in 1994, pioneered fully electronic screen-based trading and dominates cash-equity and especially derivatives volumes in India; its benchmark is the Nifty 50. For most liquid stocks the NSE has higher turnover, so traders default to it, but listed shares are fungible across both.

**Q208.** What are the Nifty 50 and the Sensex, and how do they differ?

Both are large-cap benchmark indices. The Sensex (BSE) tracks 30 large, financially-sound companies; the Nifty 50 (NSE) tracks 50 large-caps across sectors. Both are free-float market-capitalization weighted, so a stock's weight reflects only the shares freely available for public trading, not promoter/locked holdings. They move closely together since they share most heavyweight constituents (e.g., Reliance, HDFC Bank, ICICI Bank).

**Q209.** What is free-float market-cap weighting and why is it used?

Free-float market cap = price * (shares outstanding * free-float factor), where the free-float factor excludes promoter holdings, strategic/government stakes, and locked-in shares. An index weights each stock by its free-float market cap divided by the index's total, so larger and more freely-traded companies move the index more. It is preferred over full-market-cap because it reflects only investable shares, reduces distortion from closely-held companies, and makes the index easier to replicate by index funds.

**Q210.** What are Bank Nifty and Nifty Next 50?

Bank Nifty (Nifty Bank) is a sectoral index of the most liquid and large-cap banking stocks on the NSE (around 12 banks), heavily used for trading and derivatives due to its volatility. Nifty Next 50 comprises the 50 stocks ranked just below the Nifty 50 by free-float market cap — effectively the "on-deck" large-caps from which future Nifty 50 entrants are often drawn. Both are free-float market-cap weighted like the headline indices.

**Q211.** Walk through the book-building IPO process in India.

In book building, the issuer and merchant bankers (lead managers) set a price band, and investors bid for quantities at prices within that band during the offer period. The cut-off (final issue) price is determined from the demand book, and shares are allotted at that price. Key documents are the DRHP (Draft Red Herring Prospectus, filed with SEBI for review) and the RHP (Red Herring Prospectus, filed with the RoC before the issue opens). Retail and other categories have reserved quotas, and listing follows on a T+3 basis from issue close under current SEBI norms.

**Q212.** What is a Red Herring Prospectus?

A Red Herring Prospectus (RHP) is the offer document filed by an issuer ahead of a book-built IPO that contains most disclosures — business, risk factors, financials, use of proceeds — but omits the final price and exact number of shares (hence "red herring," because price is not yet fixed). It is filed with the Registrar of Companies before the issue opens; the final prospectus with price and size is filed after the book closes. The earlier draft version reviewed by SEBI is the DRHP.

**Q213.** What is ASBA and how does it work in IPO applications?

ASBA (Application Supported by Blocked Amount) is the mandatory mechanism for IPO applications in India whereby the application money is merely blocked (lien-marked) in the investor's own bank account rather than debited upfront. The funds keep earning interest and are debited only to the extent of actual allotment; the blocked amount on unallotted shares is released. This protects investors and improves cash efficiency versus the old system of paying the full amount in advance. UPI-based ASBA is used by retail investors.

**Q214.** What are NSDL and CDSL, and what is dematerialization?

NSDL (National Securities Depository Limited) and CDSL (Central Depository Services Limited) are India's two depositories — institutions that hold securities in electronic (book-entry) form, analogous to a "bank for shares." Dematerialization is the process of converting physical paper share certificates into electronic holdings in a demat account, which is accessed via a Depository Participant (DP) such as a broker or bank. SEBI mandates demat form for trading listed securities, which eliminated forgery, bad deliveries and physical transfer delays.

**Q215.** What is the NISM Series-XV exam and what do the SEBI RA Regulations require?

The SEBI (Research Analysts) Regulations, 2014 require anyone issuing research reports or making buy/sell recommendations on securities to register with SEBI as a Research Analyst and meet certification and qualification norms. The relevant certification is the NISM Series-XV: Research Analyst certification (NISM = National Institute of Securities Markets, SEBI's educational arm), which must be obtained and kept valid. The rules also mandate disclosure of conflicts of interest, separation of research from other business lines, and limits on the analyst trading against their own recommendations.

**Q216.** What is a clearing corporation, what does NSCCL do, and what is T+1 settlement?

A clearing corporation novates and guarantees settlement, acting as the central counterparty (CCP) — buyer to every seller and seller to every buyer — so neither side bears the other's default risk; NSCCL (National Securities Clearing Corporation, now NSE Clearing) does this for the NSE, netting obligations, running the settlement guarantee fund, collecting margins and managing pay-in/pay-out. Settlement happens on a T+1 cycle, meaning a trade on day T settles the next business day. India fully adopted T+1 in early 2022 — ahead of the US, which moved only in 2024 — shortening the trade-to-settlement gap, freeing capital faster and cutting counterparty risk; SEBI has also piloted optional same-day (T+0) settlement.

**Q217.** How do index options differ from stock options in India regarding settlement and style?

Indian index options (Nifty, Bank Nifty) are European-style — exercisable only at expiry — and are cash-settled against the index level, since you cannot deliver an index. Single-stock options are also European-style but are physically settled: open positions at expiry result in delivery/receipt of the underlying shares rather than cash. This physical-settlement rule for stock F&O, introduced by SEBI, was designed to curb excessive speculation and align derivative prices with the cash market.

**Q218.** What are SPAN and exposure margins in F&O?

SPAN (Standard Portfolio Analysis of Risk) margin is the core initial margin computed by simulating the portfolio's worst-case loss across a grid of price and volatility scenarios; it covers the largest likely one-day move. The exposure (or extreme-loss) margin is an additional buffer levied on top of SPAN to cover tail events beyond SPAN's scenarios. Together SPAN + exposure form the upfront margin a trader must keep; mark-to-market losses are settled daily on top of this.

**Q219.** Explain lot sizes and weekly vs monthly expiry in Indian derivatives.

Indian F&O trades in standardized "lots" (a fixed number of underlying units per contract), with lot sizes set by the exchange so that contract value stays around a SEBI-prescribed minimum — you cannot trade a single share in F&O. Monthly contracts expire on a designated day of the last week of each month, while index options also have weekly expiries that settle every week, giving short-dated trading opportunities. SEBI has been rationalizing the number of weekly expiries (limiting each exchange to one weekly-expiry index) to temper retail speculation.

**Q220.** What is Securities Transaction Tax (STT)?

STT is a tax levied on the value of transactions in listed securities executed on Indian exchanges, collected at source by the broker. Rates differ by segment — e.g., it applies to both buy and sell legs of delivery equity, only to the sell side of intraday and futures, and on the sell (premium/settlement) side of options — and is generally low (fractions of a percent). Because equity trades are subject to STT, they qualify for the concessional equity capital-gains tax regime under Sections 111A/112A.

**Q221.** How are LTCG and STCG taxed on listed equity in India?

For listed equity shares and equity mutual funds (which are subject to STT), the holding-period threshold is 12 months: gains on holdings sold within 12 months are Short-Term Capital Gains (STCG) taxed at a flat concessional rate, while gains held beyond 12 months are Long-Term Capital Gains (LTCG) taxed at a lower rate with an annual exemption threshold on aggregate LTCG. (Following the 2024 Budget, equity STCG is taxed at 20 percent and LTCG at 12.5 percent above the exemption, but the candidate should state the rule conceptually since rates change.) This contrasts with debt and other assets, which carry longer holding thresholds and different rates.

**Q222.** How is income from F&O trading taxed in India?

Profits and losses from futures and options are treated as non-speculative business income (not capital gains), reported under "Profits and Gains from Business or Profession" and taxed at the individual's slab rate. Because it is business income, the trader can deduct related expenses (brokerage, internet, data subscriptions), and losses can be set off and carried forward under business-loss rules. Significant turnover may trigger tax-audit requirements under the Income Tax Act.

**Q223.** What are circuit breakers and price bands on Indian exchanges?

Price bands are stock-level limits (e.g., 5/10/20 percent) beyond which an individual security cannot move in a day, preventing runaway single-stock moves. Market-wide circuit breakers halt trading across the entire market when a benchmark index (Nifty/Sensex) moves by 10, 15 or 20 percent, with halt durations escalating by the trigger level and time of day — coordinated across exchanges. Their purpose is to give the market a cooling-off period, curb panic-driven cascades, and let information disseminate during extreme volatility.

**Q224.** How do the jurisdictions of RBI and SEBI differ, and what are FPI/FII flows?

SEBI regulates the securities market — exchanges, listed companies, brokers, mutual funds, and capital-market intermediaries — while the RBI (Reserve Bank of India) is the central bank governing monetary policy, banks, the rupee, government securities, and foreign-exchange management under FEMA; there is overlap on areas like corporate bonds and the G-sec market. Mutual funds are SEBI-regulated AMCs (Asset Management Companies) whose industry body is AMFI (Association of Mutual Funds in India), which sets self-regulatory standards. FPI/FII (Foreign Portfolio/Institutional Investor) flows are overseas capital invested in Indian listed securities — registered with SEBI and routed under RBI/FEMA limits — and large net inflows or outflows are a major driver of Nifty/Sensex movements and the rupee.

<a name="A9"></a>

## A9. Indian Financial System & Products (Advanced)  <span class="qrange">(Q225-Q249)</span>

**Q225.** Map the four main financial regulators in India and what each one supervises.

RBI regulates banks, NBFCs, the money market, government securities, payment systems and monetary policy. SEBI regulates the securities markets — stock exchanges, listed companies, mutual funds, AIFs, brokers, RIAs and research analysts. IRDAI regulates the insurance sector (life, general, reinsurance), and PFRDA regulates the National Pension System (NPS) and pension funds. A useful boundary case: ULIPs (insurance-linked investment) sit with IRDAI, mutual funds with SEBI, and corporate/government bonds straddle RBI (issuance, primary dealers for G-Secs) and SEBI (listed corporate bonds, secondary trading on exchanges).

**Q226.** What are the main money market instruments in India and their typical tenors?

The money market handles short-term debt up to one year. Key instruments: Treasury Bills (T-bills, issued by RBI for the government at 91, 182 and 364 days, sold at a discount, zero coupon); Commercial Paper (CP, unsecured promissory notes by corporates/PDs, 7 days to 1 year); Certificates of Deposit (CDs, issued by banks/FIs, 7 days to 1 year); and call/notice/term money (interbank borrowing — call money is overnight, notice is 2-14 days). All are designed for liquidity management rather than long-term financing.

**Q227.** How is a 91-day T-bill priced and what yield does it imply if it is issued at Rs 98.50 per Rs 100 face value?

T-bills are zero-coupon and sold at a discount to face value; the return is the difference between purchase price and the Rs 100 redemption. The implied yield (simple, money-market convention) is `Yield = ((Face - Price)/Price)*(365/Days)`. For Rs 98.50 over 91 days: `((100 - 98.50)/98.50)*(365/91) = 0.01523 * 4.011 = 6.11%` annualised. There is no periodic coupon — all return comes from the discount.

**Q228.** Explain repo, reverse repo, the LAF corridor and the MSF.

Repo is RBI lending to banks against G-Sec collateral at the repo rate (the policy rate); reverse repo / the Standing Deposit Facility (SDF) is banks parking surplus funds with RBI at a lower rate. The Liquidity Adjustment Facility (LAF) corridor is the band: the repo rate sits in the middle, the SDF rate forms the floor, and the Marginal Standing Facility (MSF) rate forms the ceiling. MSF lets banks borrow overnight against SLR securities (even dipping into SLR) at a penal rate above repo — an emergency liquidity backstop. The corridor width (e.g. SDF and MSF each typically 25 bps either side of repo) keeps overnight rates anchored near the policy rate.

**Q229.** What is the G-Sec market and what role do Primary Dealers play?

Government Securities (G-Secs) are sovereign debt — dated securities (long tenor, coupon-bearing) and T-bills — issued by RBI on the government's behalf through auctions on the E-Kuber/NDS-OM platform. Primary Dealers (PDs) are RBI-registered entities obligated to bid in every primary auction (underwriting/market-making), ensuring the auction is fully subscribed, and to make two-way quotes in the secondary market to provide liquidity. They are the backbone of price discovery in the sovereign curve; retail access has been opened via the RBI Retail Direct scheme.

**Q230.** Why is India's corporate bond market considered underdeveloped relative to its equity market?

Despite a deep sovereign market, the corporate bond market is shallow because issuance is dominated by private placements to a few institutions (concentrated in AAA/AA names), secondary-market liquidity is thin, and a large share is held to maturity by insurers/pension funds and banks. Demand-side constraints (limited retail participation, FPI investment limits in debt) and supply concentration in top-rated issuers crowd out lower-rated paper, so smaller/riskier firms rely on banks instead. SEBI reforms — electronic bidding platforms, a Request-for-Quote (RFQ) platform, and a backstop/limited-purpose Corporate Debt Market Development Fund — aim to deepen liquidity.

**Q231.** What are AIFs and how do SEBI's Category I, II and III differ?

Alternative Investment Funds (AIFs) are privately pooled vehicles registered under SEBI's AIF Regulations, 2012, with a minimum investor commitment of Rs 1 crore and capped at 1,000 investors per scheme (250 for angel funds). Category I invests in socially/economically desirable areas — VC funds, SME funds, infrastructure, angel funds (incentivised, no leverage). Category II is the residual catch-all — private equity, debt funds, fund-of-funds — that uses no leverage except for day-to-day needs. Category III employs complex/diverse strategies including leverage and derivatives — hedge funds and long-short equity funds — and is the only category that can trade actively for short-term returns.

**Q232.** How are Category III AIFs taxed differently from Category I and II?

Category I and II AIFs enjoy "pass-through" status under Section 115UB — income (other than business income) is taxed in the investors' hands as if they invested directly, so the fund itself is not taxed on it. Category III AIFs do NOT have full pass-through status; the fund is generally taxed at the fund level (often at maximum marginal rate on certain income), which is a key structural consideration when choosing between categories. This is why long-short/hedge strategies in Cat III carry a heavier tax-administration burden.

**Q233.** What is a REIT, why was it created, and how is it structured in India?

A Real Estate Investment Trust (REIT) is a SEBI-regulated trust that pools investor money to own income-generating commercial real estate (offices, malls), letting retail investors access real estate without buying property directly and giving developers an exit/monetisation route. Structure: Sponsor, Trustee, Manager and Special Purpose Vehicles (SPVs) holding the assets. SEBI mandates at least 80% of assets in completed, rent-generating property and requires distribution of at least 90% of net distributable cash flows to unitholders, paid at least semi-annually — making it a yield instrument akin to a property-backed dividend stock.

**Q234.** What is an InvIT and how does it differ from a REIT?

An Infrastructure Investment Trust (InvIT) is the infrastructure analogue of a REIT — it pools capital to own operating infrastructure assets (roads, power transmission lines, gas pipelines, telecom towers) and is used by developers/governments to recycle capital out of completed projects. The structure (Sponsor, Trustee, Investment Manager, Project SPVs) and the 90% distribution rule mirror REITs; the difference is the underlying asset class (infrastructure vs commercial real estate) and InvITs can be publicly listed or privately placed. Both distribute via a mix of interest, dividend and capital-return components, each taxed differently in the unitholder's hands.

**Q235.** How is income from REIT/InvIT units taxed for an Indian investor?

Distributions come in components, each taxed differently: the interest component is taxable at the investor's slab rate; the dividend component is generally tax-free to the investor if the SPV did not opt for the concessional corporate tax regime (otherwise taxable at slab); and the return-of-capital/amortisation component reduces the cost of acquisition (deferring tax until sale). Capital gains on the units follow listed-security rules — short-term if held under the threshold, long-term beyond it. This component split is why a single REIT payout can have multiple tax treatments in one financial year.

**Q236.** Explain SEBI's mutual fund scheme categorization and why it was introduced.

In 2017-18 SEBI introduced scheme categorization to stop AMCs running dozens of overlapping schemes; it defined five broad groups (Equity, Debt, Hybrid, Solution-oriented, Other/Index-FoF) with standardised sub-categories and only one scheme per category per AMC (with exceptions like index funds/ETFs). Equity sub-categories are defined by market-cap rules: large-cap = top 100 stocks by full market cap, mid-cap = 101st-250th, small-cap = 251st onwards. The goal was comparability and true-to-label investing, so a "large-cap fund" must actually hold a minimum quota (e.g. 80%) in large-caps.

**Q237.** What is the Total Expense Ratio (TER), and how does it affect returns?

TER is the annual percentage of a fund's assets charged to investors for management, administration, distribution and other costs; it is deducted daily and already reflected in the NAV, so investors never pay it separately. SEBI caps TER on a sliding scale that falls as AUM rises (e.g. higher slabs for the first few hundred crores, lower for larger funds), and equity funds are allowed higher TER than debt/index funds. On a 12% gross return, a 1.5% TER nets the investor ~10.5%; compounded over decades the drag is large, which is the core argument for low-cost index funds.

**Q238.** What is the difference between a direct plan and a regular plan?

Both invest in the identical portfolio of the same scheme, but a regular plan pays a trail commission to the distributor/agent, which is embedded in a higher TER, while a direct plan (bought straight from the AMC/RTA/SEBI-registered platform) carries no distribution commission and thus a lower TER and higher NAV. The difference (often 0.5-1.0% per year) compounds significantly over time — a direct plan can leave an investor materially richer over 15-20 years purely from the cost gap, with no difference in the underlying holdings.

**Q239.** Define NAV and explain how SIP rupee-cost averaging works.

Net Asset Value (NAV) is the per-unit value of a fund: `NAV = (Total assets - liabilities) / Units outstanding`, struck once daily (for most funds) on a forward-pricing basis. A Systematic Investment Plan (SIP) invests a fixed rupee amount at regular intervals, so it automatically buys more units when NAV is low and fewer when NAV is high — "rupee-cost averaging" — lowering the average cost per unit over a volatile period versus a single lump-sum timed badly. It does not guarantee profit or protect against loss in a falling market, but it removes timing risk and instils discipline.

**Q240.** How do index funds and ETFs differ, especially in the Indian context?

Both passively track an index (e.g. Nifty 50, Sensex), but an index fund is a mutual fund bought/sold at end-of-day NAV directly from the AMC, while an ETF trades intraday on the exchange like a stock at a live price that can deviate from its iNAV. ETFs need a demat account and a broker, and rely on Authorised Participants/market makers and a creation-redemption mechanism to keep price near NAV; liquidity and tracking error (and bid-ask spread) are the practical concerns. In India, EPFO inflows and government disinvestment (e.g. CPSE/Bharat 22 ETFs) have boosted ETF AUM, though many retail investors prefer index funds via SIP for simplicity.

**Q241.** What happens to share price on a bonus issue versus a stock split?

In a bonus issue the company capitalises reserves to issue free shares (e.g. 1:1), increasing the share count while face value stays the same; in a stock split the face value itself is divided (e.g. Rs 10 to Rs 2, a 5-for-1 split), also increasing share count. In both the share price drops proportionally on the ex-date so total market cap and an investor's wealth are unchanged — a 1:1 bonus roughly halves the price, doubling units. Neither creates value; they improve liquidity/affordability and reset the per-share optics.

**Q242.** How does a rights issue work and what is the theoretical ex-rights price (TERP)?

A rights issue lets existing shareholders buy new shares in proportion to holdings at a discount to market price, raising fresh capital while protecting them from dilution if they subscribe. The post-issue price settles at the theoretical ex-rights price: `TERP = (Old shares*Cum-price + New shares*Issue price) / (Old + New shares)`. Example: holding at Rs 100 cum-rights, a 1:1 rights at Rs 60 gives `TERP = (1*100 + 1*60)/2 = Rs 80`; the right itself is renounceable and trades on the exchange during the offer.

**Q243.** Explain a buyback and how it differs from a dividend, including the Indian tax angle.

A buyback is the company repurchasing its own shares (via tender offer or open market), reducing share count and returning cash; it boosts EPS and signals that management sees shares as undervalued, whereas a dividend distributes cash without changing share count. Tax treatment in India has shifted: previously the company paid buyback tax and proceeds were tax-free to shareholders, but post the October 2024 change, buyback proceeds are taxed in the shareholder's hands as deemed dividend at slab rates — narrowing the historic tax arbitrage that made buybacks attractive over dividends for promoters.

**Q244.** What are ASBA and UPI in the IPO application process?

ASBA (Application Supported by Blocked Amount) means the application money stays in the investor's bank account and is only blocked (not debited) until allotment — it is debited only for shares actually allotted, and the block is released otherwise, so the investor keeps earning interest and avoids refund delays. ASBA is mandatory for IPO applications. Retail investors up to a value limit (Rs 5 lakh) can apply via the UPI mechanism, where the bank block is triggered by approving a UPI mandate in their payment app, integrating the broker, exchange, sponsor bank and RTA into one fast workflow.

**Q245.** What are NSDL and CDSL, and what does "demat" really involve?

NSDL and CDSL are India's two depositories — institutions that hold securities in electronic (dematerialised) form, eliminating physical certificates. Investors don't deal with them directly; they open a demat account with a Depository Participant (DP), typically a broker or bank, which is the agent of the depository. Demat converts paper shares into book entries so that ownership transfer on trade settlement is a simple electronic debit/credit; corporate actions (dividends, bonus, splits), pledges and ISIN-level identification all flow through the depository, making settlement fast, safe and irreversible.

**Q246.** Explain India's T+1 settlement cycle and the role of clearing corporations.

India moved to a T+1 rolling settlement (full rollout completed January 2023), meaning a trade executed today settles — funds and securities exchanged — the next business day, faster than the global T+2 norm; SEBI has since piloted optional T+0 same-day settlement. The clearing corporations — NSE Clearing (NSCCL/NCL) for NSE and Indian Clearing Corporation (ICCL) for BSE — step in as the central counterparty (CCP), novating each trade so they become buyer to every seller and seller to every buyer, guaranteeing settlement even if one side defaults. This novation removes counterparty risk between the two original parties.

**Q247.** What is the Settlement Guarantee Fund and why does it matter?

The Settlement Guarantee Fund (SGF, also called the Core SGF) is a pool of capital maintained by the clearing corporation, funded by contributions from the CC, the exchange and clearing members, that is used to complete settlement if a member defaults. It is the financial backbone of the CCP guarantee — when a clearing member fails to pay or deliver, the CC draws on the defaulter's margins and contributions first, then the SGF, in a defined "default waterfall." This is what makes the settlement guarantee credible rather than just a promise, underpinning systemic trust in exchange-traded markets.

**Q248.** Describe India's margin framework: SPAN + exposure, upfront and peak margin.

For F&O, total initial margin is SPAN margin (a portfolio risk-based margin computed by simulating worst-case price/volatility moves across a scenario grid) plus an Exposure margin (an additional ad-hoc buffer over SPAN). SEBI mandates upfront margin collection — brokers must collect the full initial margin before/at the time of the trade, not at end of day. The peak margin rule requires margins to be met based on the highest of multiple intraday snapshots (clearing corporations take random snapshots through the day), curbing the old practice of brokers offering excessive intraday leverage; shortfalls attract penalties.

**Q249.** Summarise SEBI's PIT (Prohibition of Insider Trading) Regulations and the NISM Research Analyst obligations.

Under SEBI (PIT) Regulations 2015, an "insider" (connected person or anyone with Unpublished Price Sensitive Information, UPSI) is barred from trading in securities while in possession of UPSI or from communicating it (tipping), unless for legitimate purposes; companies must maintain structured digital databases, codes of conduct, and trading windows/closure around results. A SEBI-registered Research Analyst (passing NISM Series XV) must disclose conflicts of interest, any financial interest or holding in the subject company, and whether they/relatives received compensation; they cannot trade contrary to their own recommendation within prescribed windows, must keep records, separate research from other business, and present price targets with assumptions and risk factors. FPIs (Foreign Portfolio Investors) bring portfolio/secondary-market money into listed securities subject to SEBI registration and investment limits, whereas FDI (Foreign Direct Investment) is a lasting controlling stake (typically 10%+) in a business with sector caps and RBI/government routes — and for the same investor, debt is taxed largely at slab/applicable rates with no STCG concession while listed equity enjoys concessional STCG (15%, with grandfathered LTCG taxed at 10%/12.5% above a threshold), driving very different after-tax outcomes.

<a name="secB"></a>

# B. Equity Research

<a name="B1"></a>

## B1. DCF & Intrinsic Valuation  <span class="qrange">(Q250-Q278)</span>

**Q250.** What is a discounted cash flow (DCF) valuation and why is it used?

A DCF values a business as the present value of the cash flows it is expected to generate, discounted back at a rate that reflects their risk and the time value of money: `Value = sum of CF_t / (1+r)^t`. It is used because it is the most direct way to estimate intrinsic value from fundamentals (cash, growth, risk) rather than relying on what the market is paying for comparable companies. Its strength is that it forces explicit assumptions; its weakness is that it is highly sensitive to those assumptions, especially the discount rate and terminal value.

**Q251.** What is the core difference between FCFF and FCFE?

Free cash flow to the firm (FCFF) is the cash available to all capital providers (both debt and equity holders) before financing flows; free cash flow to equity (FCFE) is the cash available only to equity holders after interest and net debt repayments. FCFF is "unlevered" (independent of capital structure), while FCFE is "levered" because it is affected by interest and borrowing. Roughly, `FCFE = FCFF - Interest*(1-t) + Net Borrowing`.

**Q252.** Write out the formula for FCFF starting from EBIT.

`FCFF = EBIT*(1-t) + D&A - CapEx - change in NWC`. You start with EBIT (operating profit), tax it at the marginal rate to get unlevered after-tax operating profit (NOPAT), add back non-cash D&A, subtract reinvestment in fixed assets (CapEx) and subtract the increase in net working capital. For example, EBIT 200, t 25%, D&A 40, CapEx 60, change in NWC 20 gives FCFF = 150 + 40 - 60 - 20 = 110.

**Q253.** Why do we add back D&A and subtract CapEx and change in NWC in the FCFF bridge?

D&A is a non-cash expense already deducted to get EBIT, so it is added back to convert accounting profit to cash. CapEx is a real cash outflow for long-lived assets that the income statement only recognises gradually, so it is subtracted in full. An increase in net working capital (more receivables/inventory than payables) ties up cash, so a rise in NWC reduces free cash flow while a fall releases cash.

**Q254.** What is the difference between an unlevered DCF and a levered DCF?

An unlevered DCF discounts FCFF (pre-financing cash flows) at the WACC to arrive at enterprise value, and is the standard approach because it isolates operating performance from capital structure. A levered DCF discounts FCFE (post-financing cash flows) at the cost of equity to arrive directly at equity value. The unlevered version is preferred in practice because WACC changes less than FCFE does when leverage shifts, making it more stable.

**Q255.** Which discount rate matches FCFF, and what value does it produce?

FCFF is discounted at the WACC because FCFF belongs to all capital providers and WACC is the blended cost of all capital. Discounting FCFF at WACC produces enterprise value (the value of the operating business). Matching the cash flow to the right rate is the cardinal rule: an all-investor cash flow needs an all-investor discount rate.

**Q256.** Which discount rate matches FCFE, and what value does it produce?

FCFE is discounted at the cost of equity (Re), typically from CAPM `Re = Rf + beta*(equity risk premium)`, because FCFE is the residual cash belonging only to shareholders. Discounting FCFE at the cost of equity produces equity value directly, with no enterprise-to-equity bridge needed. Mixing them up (e.g., discounting FCFF at the cost of equity) is a classic and serious error.

**Q257.** Walk through the enterprise-to-equity value bridge.

Start with enterprise value (EV) from the FCFF/WACC DCF, then `Equity Value = EV - Total Debt - Preferred - Minority Interest + Cash & equivalents`. You subtract claims that rank ahead of common equity and add non-operating assets like cash. For example, EV 1,000, debt 300, cash 50 gives equity value = 1,000 - 300 + 50 = 750; dividing by shares outstanding gives intrinsic value per share.

**Q258.** What is terminal value and why is it needed?

Terminal value (TV) captures the value of all cash flows beyond the explicit forecast horizon, because you cannot forecast individual years to infinity. It is estimated at the end of the explicit period and then discounted back to today like any other cash flow. TV typically accounts for 60-80% of total DCF value, which is why its assumptions deserve heavy scrutiny.

**Q259.** State the Gordon growth (perpetuity growth) terminal value formula.

`TV = FCF_(n+1) / (WACC - g) = FCF_n*(1+g) / (WACC - g)`, where FCF_n is the final-year free cash flow, g is the perpetual growth rate, and WACC is the discount rate. For example, final-year FCFF 110, g 3%, WACC 9% gives TV = 110*1.03 / (0.09 - 0.03) = 113.3 / 0.06 = 1,888. This TV is then discounted back from year n to the present.

**Q260.** What is the exit multiple method for terminal value?

The exit multiple method estimates TV by applying a market-based multiple (most commonly EV/EBITDA) to the final-year metric: `TV = exit multiple * final-year EBITDA`. It anchors the terminal value to where comparable businesses currently trade rather than to a growth assumption. The drawback is that it imports current market sentiment into an intrinsic valuation, so it is less "pure" than perpetuity growth.

**Q261.** How do you sanity-check the Gordon growth TV against the exit multiple TV?

Compute TV both ways and back out the implied figure each method produces: derive the implied exit multiple from your perpetuity-growth TV, and back out the implied perpetuity growth rate from your exit-multiple TV. If the perpetuity method implies a 30x EV/EBITDA exit (versus 8x for peers) or the exit multiple implies 6% perpetual growth (above GDP), your assumptions are inconsistent and need revisiting. The two methods should land in a similar range for a credible valuation.

**Q262.** What is the maximum sensible value for the perpetual growth rate g, and why?

The perpetual growth rate must be at or below the long-run nominal GDP growth rate of the economy, because no company can grow faster than the whole economy forever (it would eventually become the entire economy). For developed markets this is roughly 2-3%; for India a higher 4-6% nominal can be justified given faster trend growth, but should still be tied to long-run GDP and inflation. A g that exceeds long-run GDP is the single most common terminal-value abuse.

**Q263.** What is the mid-year convention and why does it raise value?

The mid-year convention assumes cash flows arrive on average in the middle of each year rather than at year-end, so each period is discounted using exponents like 0.5, 1.5, 2.5 instead of 1, 2, 3. This shortens the effective discounting period, raising the present value modestly (typically a few percent). It is more realistic because firms generate cash continuously through the year rather than in a single year-end lump.

**Q264.** How do you choose the length of the explicit forecast horizon?

The explicit period should run until the business reaches a steady state where growth, margins and reinvestment have stabilised to sustainable levels, so the perpetuity assumption is reasonable; this is usually 5-10 years. High-growth or cyclical firms need longer horizons (10-15 years) so the company can mature before terminal value kicks in. Too short a horizon forces an unrealistically high terminal growth onto a company still growing fast, distorting the valuation.

**Q265.** What does a WACC-versus-g sensitivity table show and why is it standard?

A sensitivity table arrays implied value (or per-share price) across a grid of WACC on one axis and terminal growth g on the other, showing how value responds to the two assumptions that drive it most. It is standard because point estimates give false precision; the grid communicates a value range and reveals how fragile the conclusion is. If a 0.5% change in WACC or g swings value by 30%, the model is telling you the answer is highly assumption-dependent.

**Q266.** What is a reverse DCF and what does it tell you?

A reverse DCF takes the current market price as given and solves for the assumptions (usually the growth rate or operating margin) that the market is implicitly pricing in. Instead of asking "what is the company worth," it asks "what must you believe for today's price to be fair." For example, if a stock's price only makes sense at 18% revenue growth for 10 years, you can judge whether that expectation is plausible, which is often more useful than a forward DCF's single output.

**Q267.** Why is a standard DCF inappropriate for banks and insurance companies?

For banks and insurers, debt (deposits, policy reserves) is raw material in their operations rather than just financing, so FCFF, CapEx and net working capital are not meaningful and enterprise value is ill-defined. The interest spread is the core business, so you cannot separate "operating" from "financing." Instead, analysts use equity-based methods: a dividend discount model, an FCFE/excess-capital model, or relative valuation on P/B and return-on-equity.

**Q268.** What does it mean to normalise or use through-cycle FCF, and when does it matter?

Normalising means basing the forecast (and especially the terminal year) on mid-cycle or sustainable cash flows rather than a peak or trough year, smoothing out temporary distortions. It matters most for cyclical businesses (commodities, metals, autos, real estate) where a peak-year FCF capitalised into perpetuity wildly overstates value. The fix is to use average margins/returns over a full cycle, or to ensure the terminal year reflects a normalised steady state.

**Q269.** How are share buybacks treated in a DCF?

In an enterprise-value DCF, buybacks are a financing/distribution decision and do not change FCFF or enterprise value; they reroute cash that is already captured in the model to shareholders. They matter at the equity stage: a buyback reduces both cash and the share count, so per-share intrinsic value (equity value / shares) can rise even though total equity value is unchanged. Double-counting buybacks as both reinvestment and a return source is a mistake.

**Q270.** How should stock-based compensation (SBC) be handled in a DCF?

SBC is a real economic cost that transfers value from shareholders to employees, so it should not simply be added back as a "non-cash" item the way naive cash-flow templates do. Best practice is either to treat SBC as a cash operating expense (do not add it back) or to add back the cash effect but then increase the diluted share count for the new shares issued. Ignoring SBC overstates free cash flow and double-favours the company by also inflating value before dilution.

**Q271.** List several of the most common DCF mistakes.

Common errors include: mismatching cash flow and discount rate (FCFF with cost of equity, or FCFE with WACC); a terminal growth rate above long-run GDP; inconsistent forecasts (revenue growth without matching CapEx/working-capital investment); double-counting items like SBC or buybacks; using a too-short horizon that dumps unstabilised growth into the terminal value; and forgetting to adjust the EV-to-equity bridge for items like minority interest, leases or pensions. Each can swing the valuation materially.

**Q272.** How does a change in WACC move the valuation?

Value moves inversely to WACC: a higher discount rate lowers every present value and, because WACC appears in the denominator `(WACC - g)` of the terminal value, the effect is amplified in the terminal portion. As WACC approaches g, the terminal value explodes toward infinity, so small WACC changes produce large value swings when the spread (WACC - g) is small. This convexity is exactly why the sensitivity grid and a sensible WACC are critical.

**Q273.** How does a change in the terminal growth rate g move the valuation?

A higher g raises value both by increasing the numerator `FCF*(1+g)` and by shrinking the denominator `(WACC - g)`, so the impact is non-linear and concentrated in the terminal value. The closer g is to WACC, the more violently value responds to small changes in g. This is why a disciplined cap on g (at or below long-run GDP) protects the model from runaway terminal values.

**Q274.** How can you value a company that currently has negative free cash flow?

Forecast forward to the point where the business turns FCF-positive as it scales and margins improve, so the explicit period captures the path from cash burn to maturity; the early negative years simply enter as negative present values. The value then rests heavily on the later years and terminal value, so the model must justify the route to profitability (unit economics, margin trajectory, reinvestment). A longer explicit horizon is essential so that terminal value is anchored on a normalised, profitable steady state rather than on a loss-making year.

**Q275.** What special care does a DCF for a high-growth firm require?

High-growth firms need a longer explicit horizon (often 10-15 years), an explicit fade of growth and margins toward mature, sustainable levels by the terminal year, and reinvestment (CapEx, working capital, R&D) that genuinely supports the assumed growth. The terminal growth must still respect the GDP ceiling no matter how fast the firm grows today. The biggest risk is capitalising a high growth/high margin terminal year that the company will never sustain, so a sanity check against the implied exit multiple is vital.

**Q276.** Why is enterprise value (not equity value) the natural output of an unlevered DCF?

Because FCFF is the cash available to all capital providers, discounting it at WACC values the entire operating enterprise, independent of how it is financed, so the result is enterprise value. Equity is then derived by subtracting net debt and other senior claims in the bridge. This separation is useful because it lets you value the operating business cleanly and then handle the capital structure explicitly.

**Q277.** How do you build WACC for use in an FCFF DCF, and is it constant?

`WACC = (E/V)*Re + (D/V)*Rd*(1-t)`, where E and D are the market values of equity and debt, V = E+D, Re is the cost of equity (often CAPM), Rd is the pre-tax cost of debt, and t is the marginal tax rate; the `(1-t)` captures the tax deductibility of interest. WACC is not necessarily constant: if leverage is expected to change over the forecast (e.g., an LBO deleveraging), WACC should be re-estimated each period, or you should switch to APV (adjusted present value). For Indian companies, use the local risk-free rate (10-year G-Sec) and an India equity risk premium so the discount rate is currency-consistent with INR cash flows.

**Q278.** Provide a compact end-to-end worked DCF example.

Assume FCFF of 100, 110, 121 over years 1-3 (10% growth), WACC 10%, terminal g 3%. Terminal value at year 3 = 121*1.03 / (0.10 - 0.03) = 124.6 / 0.07 = 1,781; discount the three FCFFs and the TV at 10%: PV(FCFF) = 100/1.1 + 110/1.21 + 121/1.331 = 90.9 + 90.9 + 90.9 = 272.7, PV(TV) = 1,781/1.331 = 1,338. Enterprise value = 272.7 + 1,338 = 1,611; if net debt is 300, equity value = 1,311, and dividing by shares gives intrinsic value per share. Note the terminal value is ~83% of EV here, underscoring how dominant the terminal assumptions are.

<a name="B2"></a>

## B2. Relative Valuation & Multiples  <span class="qrange">(Q279-Q308)</span>

**Q279.** What is relative valuation, and what is its core underlying assumption?

Relative valuation prices an asset by comparing it to similar assets using standardized multiples (e.g., P/E, EV/EBITDA), rather than by discounting its own cash flows. The core assumption is that the market prices comparable companies correctly on average, so applying a peer multiple to the target's metric yields a fair value. If the entire peer set is mispriced (a bubble or a depressed sector), relative valuation simply inherits that mispricing.

**Q280.** What is a "multiple" and why are multiples standardized?

A multiple is a ratio of value (price or enterprise value) to a fundamental driver (earnings, EBITDA, sales, book value), e.g., P/E = Price per share / EPS. Standardizing by a per-unit driver lets you compare a 500 crore company with a 50,000 crore company on the same scale. The implicit logic is that two firms with the same fundamentals (growth, risk, returns) should trade at the same multiple.

**Q281.** What is the difference between trading comps and precedent transaction comps?

Trading comps (comparable company analysis) use multiples of currently listed peers trading in the public market, reflecting standalone, minority-stake values. Precedent transaction comps use multiples paid in actual M&A deals, which embed a control premium and expected synergies. Transaction comps therefore usually give higher multiples and set a ceiling/acquisition value, while trading comps give a "no-control" market value.

**Q282.** What is the difference between an enterprise-value multiple and an equity multiple?

EV multiples (EV/EBITDA, EV/EBIT, EV/Sales) value the whole firm available to all capital providers (debt + equity), so the denominator must be a pre-interest, pre-financing metric. Equity multiples (P/E, P/B, P/S) value only the shareholders' claim, so the denominator must be post-interest (net income, book equity). The cardinal rule is consistency: never put an equity-side numerator over a firm-side denominator (e.g., Price/EBITDA is wrong; EV/Net income is wrong).

**Q283.** Write out the EV bridge: how do you get from equity market cap to enterprise value?

EV = Equity market cap + Total debt + Minority (non-controlling) interest + Preferred stock - Cash and cash equivalents. The logic: an acquirer buying the equity must also assume the debt, preferred, and minority claims, but gets the cash which can repay debt. Example: market cap 800, debt 300, minority 20, preferred 0, cash 100 gives EV = 800 + 300 + 20 + 0 - 100 = 1,020.

**Q284.** Why do we add minority interest and subtract cash when computing enterprise value?

Minority interest is added because the income statement consolidates 100% of a partly-owned subsidiary's EBITDA, so EV must include 100% of the claim against it for consistency. Cash is subtracted because it is a non-operating asset that the acquirer can use to pay down the purchase price, and EV is meant to capture only the operating (core) business value.

**Q285.** Why is EV/EBITDA called "capital-structure neutral" and often preferred over P/E?

EBITDA is computed before interest expense, so it is unaffected by how the firm splits its financing between debt and equity; EV (the numerator) also covers all capital providers, so the ratio is independent of leverage. P/E, by contrast, uses net income (after interest) and price (equity only), so two identical businesses with different debt loads show different P/Es. This makes EV/EBITDA better for comparing peers with very different leverage and for cross-border peers.

**Q286.** Beyond leverage neutrality, what other advantages does EV/EBITDA have over P/E?

EBITDA strips out depreciation and amortization, reducing distortion from different depreciation policies and accounting choices, and it is usually positive even when net income is negative, so loss-making but cash-generative firms remain comparable. EV/EBITDA is also less affected by one-off below-the-line items and differing tax rates. It is the workhorse multiple in M&A, LBOs, and capital-intensive sectors.

**Q287.** What are the main weaknesses of EV/EBITDA?

EBITDA ignores capital expenditure and changes in working capital, so it overstates the cash generation of capex-heavy businesses (a steel plant and a software firm at the same EV/EBITDA are not equally cheap). It also ignores the depreciation that proxies for real asset consumption — Warren Buffett's critique that "EBITDA pretends capex doesn't exist." For capex-heavy firms, EV/EBIT or EV/(EBITDA - capex) is often more honest.

**Q288.** When is P/E a perfectly fine multiple, and when does it mislead?

P/E works well for mature, profitable companies with similar capital structures and stable, comparable accounting — for example comparing two FMCG majors. It misleads when earnings are negative or near zero (the ratio becomes meaningless or huge), when leverage differs sharply across peers, or when one-off items, different tax regimes, or aggressive accounting distort net income. In cyclical firms, a low trailing P/E at the peak of the cycle can be a value trap.

**Q289.** What is P/B (price-to-book) and why does it suit banks and financials?

P/B = Market cap / Book value of equity (or Price per share / Book value per share). It suits banks, insurers, and NBFCs because their balance sheets are marked largely to financial assets, so book value is a meaningful, near-economic measure of net worth, and their "earnings power" is closely tied to equity capital. For these firms earnings are volatile and EBITDA is not meaningful (interest is their core revenue), so P/B and P/E on normalized earnings dominate. In India, banks like HDFC Bank are routinely valued on P/B.

**Q290.** How does P/B link to ROE, and why does that matter for justified valuation?

A firm's justified P/B is driven by how its return on equity compares to its cost of equity: P/B = (ROE - g) / (r - g) in a Gordon-style framework. A bank earning ROE above its cost of equity should trade above 1x book; one earning below should trade below book. So when comparing two banks at the same P/B, the one with higher sustainable ROE is the cheaper, better value.

**Q291.** What is P/S (price-to-sales), and when is it useful?

P/S = Market cap / Revenue (the equity-side version; EV/Sales is the firm-side analogue). It is useful for early-stage or loss-making companies with no meaningful earnings (e.g., young SaaS or e-commerce firms), and because revenue is harder to manipulate than earnings. Its weakness is that it ignores profitability entirely — a high-revenue, low-margin firm can look cheap on P/S while being a poor business.

**Q292.** What is the PEG ratio and how do you interpret it?

PEG = (P/E) / (annual EPS growth rate in %), designed to adjust P/E for growth so high-growth stocks don't automatically look expensive. A PEG around 1.0 is often taken as fairly valued; below 1.0 may indicate undervaluation relative to growth, above 1.0 overvaluation. Example: a stock with P/E of 30 growing earnings at 30% has PEG = 30/30 = 1.0, while P/E 30 growing at 15% has PEG = 2.0.

**Q293.** What are the pitfalls of the PEG ratio?

PEG assumes a linear, proportional relationship between P/E and growth, which is theoretically wrong — value rises faster than linearly with growth and also depends on risk and payout. It relies on a single forecast growth number that is easily gamed or optimistic, ignores differences in risk/quality between firms, and breaks down for low-growth or negative-growth companies. It is a quick screen, not a rigorous valuation.

**Q294.** What is the difference between trailing and forward multiples, and which is generally preferred?

A trailing multiple uses the last reported (LTM/historical) metric; a forward multiple uses a forecast (e.g., next-twelve-months or next-fiscal-year EBITDA/EPS). Forward multiples are generally preferred because valuation is about the future and they reflect expected growth, but they depend on the reliability of estimates. For a growing company, the forward P/E is lower than the trailing P/E; mixing trailing for one peer and forward for another is a classic comparability error.

**Q295.** How do you choose a good peer set for trading comps?

Select companies similar on the value-relevant dimensions: same industry/business model, comparable size, similar growth and margin profile, similar geography/regulatory regime, and similar capital intensity. The goal is firms whose fundamentals (growth, risk, returns on capital) are close enough that they "deserve" the same multiple. A tight set of 5-8 true comparables beats a long list of loosely related names; you then screen for outliers and accounting comparability.

**Q296.** Why might a stock legitimately trade at a premium or discount to its peers?

Differences in fundamentals justify multiple differences: higher expected growth, higher and more sustainable ROIC/ROE, lower risk (beta, leverage, earnings volatility), better management/governance, or stronger competitive moat all warrant a premium. Discounts arise from cyclicality, governance concerns, customer concentration, lower liquidity, or holding-company/conglomerate structures. A premium is only "expensive" if it exceeds what the superior fundamentals justify.

**Q297.** A peer trades at 12x EV/EBITDA. Your target has EBITDA of 500 crore, debt of 800 crore, and cash of 200 crore. What is the implied equity value?

Implied EV = 12 x 500 = 6,000 crore. Then equity value = EV - net debt (and any preferred/minority): 6,000 - (800 - 200) = 6,000 - 600 = 5,400 crore. Divide by shares outstanding to get the implied price per share. This is the standard "apply multiple, then walk down the EV bridge to equity" workflow.

**Q298.** State the Gordon Growth (single-stage DDM) formula and the meaning of each term.

P0 = D1 / (r - g), where P0 is the fair price today, D1 is next year's expected dividend (D1 = D0 x (1+g)), r is the cost of equity, and g is the constant perpetual dividend growth rate. It assumes dividends grow forever at a constant rate g, with r > g required for a finite value. Example: D1 = 20, r = 12%, g = 4% gives P0 = 20 / (0.12 - 0.04) = 250.

**Q299.** Why is the Gordon Growth model so sensitive, and when is it appropriate?

Because the denominator is (r - g), small changes in either input swing the value dramatically — if g moves from 4% to 5% with r = 12%, value jumps from 250 to 285 (+14%) on the same D1. It is appropriate only for stable, mature firms paying predictable dividends with growth at or below the long-run economy rate (g cannot exceed r, and realistically not the GDP growth rate forever). It is widely used for regulated utilities and large banks.

**Q300.** What is a multi-stage DDM and why use it?

A multi-stage DDM models an explicit high-growth period with year-by-year dividends, then applies a Gordon-growth terminal value for the stable phase: P0 = sum of D_t/(1+r)^t for the explicit years + [D_(n+1)/(r - g_stable)] / (1+r)^n. It is used when a firm cannot grow at a constant rate forever — e.g., a fast-growing company that will mature. The two-stage (and H-model) variants let growth fade from high to sustainable, which is more realistic than assuming a single perpetual g.

**Q301.** What is sum-of-the-parts (SOTP) valuation and when is it used?

SOTP values a diversified company by valuing each business segment separately with the multiple or method appropriate to that segment, then summing the parts and adjusting for net debt, corporate costs, and any holding-company discount. It is used for conglomerates and multi-segment firms where a single blended multiple hides value — e.g., valuing a group's IT arm on EV/EBIT, its NBFC on P/B, and its telecom on EV/EBITDA. Indian conglomerates like Reliance or the Tata/Aditya Birla groups are common SOTP candidates.

**Q302.** What is a holding-company discount and why does it appear in SOTP?

A holding-company (or conglomerate) discount is the gap between the market value of a holding company and the sum of its stakes in listed/unlisted subsidiaries. It arises from double taxation on dividends passing up, lack of control over subsidiary cash flows, capital-allocation concerns, and reduced transparency. In SOTP for Indian holdcos, analysts often apply a 20-50% discount to the underlying NAV to reflect this.

**Q303.** What is a control premium, and why do precedent transaction multiples include it?

A control premium is the extra amount an acquirer pays above the unaffected market price to obtain control — the ability to change management, capital structure, and strategy, and to capture synergies. Because precedent transactions are completed acquisitions, their multiples bake in this premium (often 20-40% over the pre-deal share price). That is why transaction comps typically exceed trading comps and are used to estimate acquisition (takeover) value rather than standalone value.

**Q304.** How do synergies affect transaction multiples, and should you pay for them?

Synergies (cost savings + revenue gains) raise the post-deal earnings/EBITDA the acquirer expects, so a buyer may justify a higher multiple on the target's standalone metric. But standard practice is that the seller should not capture all synergy value — the buyer pays a control premium partly funded by synergies but ideally retains a share, otherwise the deal destroys value for the acquirer. When using transaction comps you should be aware their multiples are inflated by deal-specific synergies that may not apply to your target.

**Q305.** What is a "football field" chart and what is it used for?

A football field is a horizontal bar chart that displays the valuation range for a company from each method side by side — 52-week trading range, trading comps, precedent transactions, DCF, LBO analysis — so you can see where the ranges overlap.
