It is the standard summary exhibit in valuation/M&A decks because it conveys that valuation is a range, not a point, and shows which methods agree. The overlap zone often guides the negotiation or fairness-opinion range.

**Q306.** How do you reconcile a DCF value that is much higher (or lower) than what comps imply?

First check that both are internally consistent — same forecast assumptions, leverage, and metric definitions — then identify the source of divergence: comps reflect current market sentiment for the sector, while DCF reflects your explicit cash-flow and terminal-value assumptions. If DCF is far above comps, either the market is pricing the sector pessimistically (an opportunity) or your DCF growth/terminal assumptions are too aggressive; reverse-engineering the implied growth/multiple from each helps locate the disagreement. Analysts typically present both and triangulate rather than forcing them to match.

**Q307.** Why are sector-specific multiples used, and give examples across sectors?

Each industry has a value driver that standard accounting multiples capture poorly, so practitioners use a metric tied to the real economics. Examples: banks and insurers on P/B (and P/E on normalized earnings); telecom and cable on EV/EBITDA and EV per subscriber; hotels on EV per room; oil & gas on EV/EBITDAX or EV per barrel of reserves; real estate/REITs on price-to-NAV and EV per square foot; internet/SaaS on EV/Sales, EV/ARR, and EV per user/MAU. The chosen multiple should correlate with the cash the asset ultimately produces.

**Q308.** Why are EV/Subscriber and similar "per-unit operating" multiples sometimes used despite ignoring profitability?

Per-unit metrics (EV per subscriber, per user, per room, per ton of capacity) are used when current earnings are negative or volatile but the operational base is the true long-run value driver, allowing comparison of firms not yet profitable — common for early-stage telecom, streaming, and internet companies. They normalize by scale of the customer/asset base, which is more stable than fluctuating early earnings. The danger is that they ignore the quality, monetization, and cost of serving each unit, so a high EV/subscriber can flatter a business with poor ARPU or churn; they are best used alongside profitability and lifetime-value analysis.

<a name="B3"></a>

## B3. Research Process, Modeling & Technical Analysis  <span class="qrange">(Q309-Q339)</span>

**Q309.** What is the difference between sell-side and buy-side equity research?

Sell-side research is produced by brokerages and investment banks (e.g., Morgan Stanley, Kotak Institutional Equities) and is distributed to clients to generate trading commissions and corporate-access revenue; it publishes ratings, target prices and detailed models. Buy-side research sits inside asset managers, mutual funds, hedge funds and insurers (e.g., a fund house's internal analyst) and is kept private to drive the firm's own portfolio decisions. Sell-side analysts cover more names and write more publicly; buy-side analysts go deeper on fewer names and answer only to their portfolio managers.

**Q310.** What are the core deliverables of a sell-side equity research analyst?

The four staples are: (1) an earnings/financial model forecasting revenue, margins, EPS and cash flow; (2) a rating (Buy/Hold/Sell or Overweight/Neutral/Underweight); (3) a 12-month target price derived from a valuation method; and (4) written notes — an initiating-coverage report plus ongoing updates on results, management meetings and news. The analyst also fields client calls and arranges corporate access. In India, sell-side research must carry SEBI Research Analyst (RA) registration and disclosures.

**Q311.** What does it mean to "initiate coverage" on a stock?

Initiating coverage is the first formal research report an analyst publishes on a company, establishing a rating, target price and full investment thesis. It is typically long and comprehensive — business overview, industry context, model, valuation, risks — because it sets the baseline against which all later updates are measured. After initiation the analyst is expected to maintain the stock with regular updates.

**Q312.** How is a target price typically derived and what does it represent?

A target price is the analyst's estimate of fair value over a stated horizon, usually 12 months, and the rating is set by the implied upside/downside versus the current price. It is commonly derived from a forward multiple (e.g., target P/E times next-year EPS), a DCF, a sum-of-the-parts, or a blend. Example: if FY+1 EPS is INR 50 and the justified P/E is 20x, the target price is 50 * 20 = INR 1,000.

**Q313.** What is the difference between top-down and bottom-up revenue forecasting?

Top-down starts from a large aggregate and narrows: total addressable market (TAM) times an assumed market share, or GDP/industry growth scaled to the company. Bottom-up builds revenue from the company's own operational drivers — units * price, stores * sales-per-store, subscribers * ARPU — and sums them. Bottom-up is usually more defensible and auditable; analysts often build bottom-up and sanity-check it against a top-down TAM number.

**Q314.** What are "revenue drivers" and why model revenue by drivers instead of a single growth rate?

Revenue drivers are the underlying volume and price levers that mechanically produce sales, e.g., `Revenue = Volume * Price`, or for a telco `Revenue = Subscribers * ARPU`, for retail `Stores * Avg sales per store`, for SaaS `Customers * ACV` with churn. Driver-based modeling makes assumptions explicit and testable, lets you flex one lever at a time for scenarios, and ties forecasts to operating reality rather than an arbitrary single growth percentage that hides the logic.

**Q315.** How do you forecast operating margins in a model?

Forecast each cost line either as a percentage of revenue or via its own driver, then derive margin as the residual. Distinguish variable costs (scale with volume — raw materials, freight) from largely fixed costs (rent, core salaries, depreciation) so the model captures operating leverage: as revenue grows, fixed costs spread over more units and EBIT margin expands. Anchor assumptions to historical trends, management guidance and capacity, and check that gross, EBITDA and EBIT margins move coherently.

**Q316.** What makes a good investment thesis?

A good thesis is a clear, differentiated and falsifiable view on why a stock will outperform: what the market is mispricing, why you are right (variant perception), the catalyst and timeframe that closes the gap, and the valuation upside if you are correct. It states explicit assumptions and the specific data points that would prove it wrong. "Good company" is not a thesis; "good company whose margin recovery the Street is underestimating, re-rating on H2 results" is.

**Q317.** What is a catalyst, and why does it matter to a research call?

A catalyst is a specific, datable event expected to force the market to re-price a stock toward fair value — earnings beats, new product launch, regulatory approval, capacity coming online, margin inflection, M&A, or index inclusion. A cheap stock can stay cheap indefinitely (a "value trap") without a catalyst, so identifying one gives the call a timeframe and converts a valuation gap into an actionable trade. Catalysts are central to differentiating a Buy from a "fairly valued."

**Q318.** What is a bull case and bear case in a research report?

The bull case and bear case are scenario valuations bracketing the base-case target: the bull case stacks optimistic but plausible assumptions (faster growth, margin expansion, higher multiple) and the bear case the pessimistic ones (share loss, margin compression, de-rating). Together they frame the risk/reward — e.g., base INR 1,000, bull INR 1,400, bear INR 700 against a price of 950 gives roughly +47% / -26% skew. A favorable skew (more upside than downside) strengthens a Buy.

**Q319.** What is "scuttlebutt" or primary research in equity analysis?

Coined by Philip Fisher, scuttlebutt is gathering insight from primary, ground-level sources rather than filings alone — talking to customers, suppliers, distributors, ex-employees, competitors and channel checks at stores. It helps verify demand, pricing power and management claims, and often surfaces variant perception before it shows in reported numbers. The constraint is legality and ethics: it must not cross into material non-public information (insider trading), a SEBI/SEC violation.

**Q320.** Outline Porter's Five Forces and their use in industry analysis.

The five forces that determine industry profitability are: (1) threat of new entrants, (2) bargaining power of suppliers, (3) bargaining power of buyers, (4) threat of substitutes, and (5) rivalry among existing competitors. An industry where all five are weak (high entry barriers, fragmented suppliers and buyers, few substitutes, benign rivalry) tends to earn structurally high returns. Analysts use the framework to judge whether a company's margins are durable or will be competed away.

**Q321.** What is an economic moat, and what are Morningstar's moat sources?

An economic moat is a durable structural advantage that lets a firm earn returns above its cost of capital for an extended period while fending off competitors. Morningstar identifies five sources: (1) intangible assets (brands, patents, regulatory licenses), (2) switching costs, (3) the network effect, (4) cost advantages (scale, process, location), and (5) efficient scale (a niche market profitably served by a few). Morningstar rates moats as none, narrow, or wide.

**Q322.** What is the Competitive Advantage Period (CAP) and why does it matter for valuation?

CAP (also called the fade period) is the length of time a company is expected to earn returns on invested capital above its WACC before competition erodes them to the cost of capital. A wider moat implies a longer CAP, which justifies a higher multiple because value-creating growth persists longer. In a DCF it shows up as how many years you let ROIC exceed WACC before fading spreads to zero in the terminal value.

**Q323.** What are the main red flags of poor earnings quality?

Key red flags: net income growing while operating cash flow stagnates or falls (earnings not converting to cash), rising Days Sales Outstanding (DSO) signaling aggressive or uncollectible sales, rising inventory days, recurring "one-time" or exceptional items, frequent restatements, large gaps between reported and tax accounting, capitalizing costs that should be expensed, and bill-and-hold or end-of-quarter revenue spikes. Each suggests reported profits may be overstated or unsustainable.

**Q324.** Why is rising Days Sales Outstanding (DSO) a warning sign?

DSO measures how long it takes to collect receivables: `DSO = (Accounts Receivable / Revenue) * 365`. If DSO rises faster than revenue, the company may be booking sales it hasn't collected — through looser credit terms, channel stuffing, or premature revenue recognition — inflating reported revenue and earnings while cash lags. Example: receivables up 40% on revenue up 10% means DSO is climbing sharply and the quality of that growth is suspect.

**Q325.** What is the cash conversion ratio and what does a falling ratio indicate?

Cash conversion compares cash generation to accounting profit, commonly `OCF / Net Income` (or FCF / Net Income), with a healthy figure near or above 1.0 over time. A persistently falling ratio means profits are increasingly tied up in working capital or driven by non-cash accruals rather than real cash — a classic earnings-quality concern. It can flag aggressive revenue recognition, ballooning receivables/inventory, or capitalized expenses propping up reported income.

**Q326.** What is channel stuffing and how would you detect it?

Channel stuffing is shipping more product to distributors/retailers than end-demand warrants, often near quarter-end, to inflate current-period revenue at the expense of future periods. Detect it via spiking DSO and receivables, rising distributor inventory, a sales surge followed by returns or a sharp next-quarter drop, and lengthening payment terms or generous incentives. Channel checks (scuttlebutt with distributors) are often the fastest way to confirm it.

**Q327.** What is the accruals ratio and how is it used to assess earnings quality?

The accruals ratio isolates the non-cash portion of earnings; the balance-sheet version is `Accruals Ratio = (NOA_end - NOA_begin) / Avg NOA`, where NOA is net operating assets, or equivalently `(Net Income - OCF - FCF...) ` via the cash-flow version `(NI - CFO - CFI) / Avg NOA`. High positive accruals mean earnings are driven by accounting estimates rather than cash, and academic evidence (Sloan, 1996) shows high-accrual firms tend to see earnings reverse and underperform. Lower, stable accruals signal higher-quality, cash-backed earnings.

**Q328.** How is ESG incorporated into modern equity research?

ESG analysis assesses environmental, social and governance factors as drivers of risk and long-term value rather than just ethics screens — e.g., carbon-transition risk to a fossil producer, labor and supply-chain risk, and especially governance (board independence, related-party transactions, promoter pledging). Analysts integrate material ESG factors into the discount rate, growth/margin assumptions, or the valuation multiple, and may flag controversies as thesis risks. In India, SEBI mandates BRSR (Business Responsibility and Sustainability Reporting) for the top 1,000 listed firms, giving analysts standardized ESG data.

**Q329.** How do you value a loss-making, high-growth company?

Because near-term earnings are negative, anchor on revenue and the path to profitability: use EV/Sales or EV/Gross Profit versus growth-adjusted peers, a DCF built to a normalized steady-state margin in the terminal year, or unit economics (LTV/CAC, contribution margin, cohort retention) to prove the model works at scale. Test whether the implied terminal margin and market share are realistic, and stress the cash runway and dilution risk. Example: a SaaS firm at INR 500 cr revenue growing 60% with 80% gross margin and clear operating leverage can justify a high EV/Sales if the path to ~25% EBIT margin is credible.

**Q330.** What defines an uptrend and a downtrend in technical analysis?

An uptrend is a sequence of higher highs and higher lows — each rally peak and each pullback trough is above the prior one — reflecting net buying pressure; a downtrend is the mirror, lower highs and lower lows. The trend is presumed intact until that structure breaks (e.g., an uptrend makes a lower low). "The trend is your friend" captures the bias to trade in the direction of the prevailing structure rather than against it.

**Q331.** What are support and resistance levels?

Support is a price zone where buying interest has repeatedly halted declines; resistance is a zone where selling has repeatedly capped advances. They form at prior swing highs/lows, round numbers, and high-volume nodes, and reflect clustered orders and trader memory. A key principle is polarity: once decisively broken, support tends to become resistance and vice versa, which is why breakouts and retests are watched closely.

**Q332.** What is the difference between a Simple and an Exponential Moving Average?

A Simple Moving Average (SMA) is the equal-weighted mean of the last N closes: `SMA = (P1 + P2 + ... + PN) / N`. An Exponential Moving Average (EMA) weights recent prices more heavily: `EMA_today = Price * k + EMA_yesterday * (1 - k)`, where `k = 2 / (N + 1)`. The EMA therefore reacts faster to new price action (less lag) but is more prone to whipsaws; SMA is smoother but slower.

**Q333.** What are the golden cross and death cross?

A golden cross is a bullish signal where a shorter moving average crosses above a longer one — classically the 50-day SMA crossing above the 200-day SMA — suggesting upward momentum is building. A death cross is the bearish opposite: the 50-day crossing below the 200-day. Both are lagging signals confirming a trend already underway rather than predicting turns, and work best filtered with volume and broader context.

**Q334.** How is the RSI calculated and how is it interpreted?

The Relative Strength Index is a momentum oscillator bounded 0-100: `RSI = 100 - 100 / (1 + RS)`, where `RS = average gain / average loss` over a lookback (default 14 periods). Readings above 70 are conventionally overbought and below 30 oversold, though in strong trends RSI can stay extended. A widely watched signal is divergence — price makes a new high but RSI does not — hinting the move is losing momentum.

**Q335.** How does the MACD indicator work?

MACD measures momentum via the gap between two EMAs: `MACD line = EMA(12) - EMA(26)`, the `signal line = EMA(9) of the MACD line`, and the histogram = MACD minus signal. A bullish trigger is the MACD line crossing above the signal line; bearish is crossing below; crossing the zero line marks a shift in the underlying trend. As with RSI, MACD/price divergence is used to anticipate weakening momentum.

**Q336.** What are Bollinger Bands and how are they used?

Bollinger Bands plot a middle band (typically a 20-period SMA) with upper and lower bands at plus/minus two standard deviations of price: `Upper = SMA(20) + 2*sigma`, `Lower = SMA(20) - 2*sigma`. The bands widen when volatility rises and contract ("squeeze") when it falls, often before a large move. Price tagging a band is not itself a buy/sell signal — in a trend price can "walk the band" — so it is read alongside trend and volume.

**Q337.** Why is volume important for confirming price moves?

Volume gauges conviction behind a move: a breakout or trend advance on rising volume is more credible, while a move on thin volume is suspect and prone to failure. Classic interpretation — rising price on rising volume confirms an uptrend; rising price on falling volume warns of weakening demand and possible reversal. Volume confirmation is also key for validating breakouts from support/resistance or chart patterns.

**Q338.** Describe the doji, engulfing, and hammer candlestick patterns.

A doji has open and close nearly equal (a tiny body), signaling indecision and a possible turning point. A bullish engulfing is a large up-candle whose body fully engulfs the prior down-candle, indicating a momentum shift to buyers (bearish engulfing is the inverse). A hammer is a small body with a long lower wick appearing after a decline, showing buyers rejected lower prices — a potential bottom. All gain reliability when confirmed by the next candle, support/resistance, and volume.

**Q339.** What are the head-and-shoulders and double top/bottom patterns, and how does technical analysis complement fundamental analysis?

Head-and-shoulders is a bearish reversal pattern — three peaks with a higher middle "head" — that signals a top when price breaks the neckline; the inverse pattern signals a bottom. A double top (two failed highs at resistance) is bearish and a double bottom (two failed lows at support) is bullish, confirmed on a break of the intervening level. TA does not replace fundamentals: fundamentals (valuation, thesis, catalysts) answer *what* and *why* to own, while TA helps with *when* — timing entries/exits, sizing, setting stops near support, and gauging crowd momentum around the fundamental call.

<a name="secC"></a>

# C. Risk - Market & Credit

<a name="C1"></a>

## C1. Market Risk: VaR, Volatility & Backtesting  <span class="qrange">(Q340-Q374)</span>

**Q340.** What is Value at Risk (VaR) and what three parameters define it?

VaR is the maximum loss on a portfolio that is not expected to be exceeded over a given holding period, at a given confidence level, under normal market conditions. It is defined by three parameters: the horizon (e.g. 1 day, 10 days), the confidence level (e.g. 95%, 99%), and the base currency/value of the position. Formally, VaR is the loss quantile: P(Loss > VaR) = 1 - confidence.

**Q341.** How do you read the statement "1-day 99% VaR = $1m"?

It means that on a normal trading day there is a 99% probability the loss will be no more than $1m, or equivalently a 1% chance (roughly 1 day in 100, about 2-3 days a year) the loss will exceed $1m. Crucially, VaR tells you the threshold but says nothing about how bad the loss is on those breach days — it could be $1.1m or $50m.

**Q342.** What is the difference between absolute VaR and relative VaR?

Absolute VaR measures the loss relative to zero (the worst loss in money terms), while relative VaR measures the loss relative to the expected return or a benchmark. Over short horizons like 1 day the expected return (drift) is tiny, so the two are nearly identical and drift is usually ignored; over longer horizons the distinction matters more.

**Q343.** What are the three main methods for calculating VaR?

(1) Historical simulation — re-price the portfolio using actual historical returns and read off the loss quantile; (2) Parametric / variance-covariance — assume a distribution (usually normal) and compute VaR = z*sigma*V from estimated volatility and correlations; (3) Monte Carlo simulation — generate thousands of random scenarios from an assumed model and read off the quantile. Each trades off realism, speed, and ease of implementation.

**Q344.** What is the parametric (variance-covariance) VaR formula and what are the standard z-values?

VaR = z * sigma * V, where z is the standard-normal quantile, sigma is the return volatility over the horizon, and V is the portfolio value. The z-multipliers are 1.65 for 95% and 2.33 for 99% (one-tailed). Example: a $10m portfolio with 1-day sigma = 2% has 99% VaR = 2.33 * 0.02 * 10m = $466,000.

**Q345.** Work a quick parametric VaR: 95% 1-day VaR on a 50 crore INR equity book with daily volatility of 1.5%.

VaR = z * sigma * V = 1.65 * 0.015 * 50,00,00,000 = roughly 1.24 crore INR. So on 95 days out of 100 the one-day loss should stay below about 1.24 crore. Bumping to 99% replaces 1.65 with 2.33, giving about 1.75 crore.

**Q346.** What are the pros and cons of historical simulation VaR?

Pros: no distributional assumption (it captures fat tails and actual correlations as they occurred), easy to explain, and handles non-linear instruments like options by full revaluation. Cons: it assumes the past window represents the future, the result is bounded by the worst event in the window, it reacts slowly to regime changes, and a short window gives a noisy tail while a long window mixes stale data.

**Q347.** What are the pros and cons of parametric / variance-covariance VaR?

Pros: fast, analytic, low data needs (just volatilities and a correlation matrix), and easy to decompose into marginal/component contributions. Cons: it assumes returns are normal (or elliptical), so it understates tail risk for fat-tailed assets, and it handles option-like non-linearity (gamma, vega) poorly unless you add delta-gamma approximations.

**Q348.** What are the pros and cons of Monte Carlo VaR?

Pros: very flexible — it can use any distribution, capture non-linear payoffs through full revaluation, and model path dependence and complex correlations. Cons: it is computationally expensive (thousands of full repricings), results depend entirely on the chosen model and parameters (model risk), and convergence in the tail is slow, requiring many simulations for a stable 99% number.

**Q349.** What is Expected Shortfall (ES) / Conditional VaR (CVaR)?

Expected Shortfall is the average loss conditional on being in the tail beyond the VaR level — i.e. the mean of losses worse than VaR. ES at 97.5% answers "if we breach VaR, how bad is it on average?" Formally ES_alpha = E[Loss | Loss >= VaR_alpha], so ES is always at least as large as the corresponding VaR.

**Q350.** Why does Expected Shortfall give more information than VaR?

VaR is a single threshold and is blind to the shape and severity of the tail beyond it — two portfolios with identical VaR can have wildly different worst-case losses. ES averages over the entire tail, so it "sees" how heavy the tail is and penalises portfolios with catastrophic but rare payoffs (like short deep-out-of-the-money options) that VaR can hide.

**Q351.** What are the four properties of a coherent risk measure?

A risk measure rho is coherent (Artzner et al., 1999) if it satisfies: (1) Monotonicity — if portfolio A always loses more than B, rho(A) >= rho(B); (2) Translation invariance — adding cash c reduces risk by c, rho(X + c) = rho(X) - c; (3) Positive homogeneity — rho(lambda*X) = lambda*rho(X) for lambda >= 0 (scaling); (4) Subadditivity — rho(A + B) <= rho(A) + rho(B), so merging never increases risk.

**Q352.** Why is VaR not a coherent risk measure?

VaR satisfies monotonicity, homogeneity, and translation invariance but can violate subadditivity: for non-elliptical or fat-tailed/discrete distributions, the VaR of a combined portfolio can exceed the sum of the standalone VaRs. This is perverse because it implies diversification "increases" measured risk, and it can be gamed by portfolios with payoffs concentrated just beyond the quantile.

**Q353.** Why is Expected Shortfall coherent while VaR is not?

ES satisfies all four axioms including subadditivity for any distribution, because it averages over the whole tail rather than picking a single quantile point. By integrating the tail, ES properly rewards diversification (ES of a merged book is never more than the sum) and cannot be hidden by lumping losses just beyond the VaR cutoff.

**Q354.** Give a simple example where VaR violates subadditivity.

Take two independent corporate bonds each with a 4% default probability and a loss of 100 on default, zero otherwise. Each bond's 95% VaR is 0 (default sits in the 4% tail, beyond the 5% point). Combined, the chance at least one defaults is about 7.84%, so the 95% VaR of the two-bond portfolio is 100 — greater than 0 + 0. Diversifying made VaR worse, violating subadditivity.

**Q355.** What is the square-root-of-time rule for scaling VaR?

To convert a 1-day VaR to an h-day VaR you scale volatility by sqrt(h): VaR_h = VaR_1 * sqrt(h). Example: a 1-day 99% VaR of $1m becomes a 10-day VaR of 1m * sqrt(10) = about $3.16m. Basel's old market-risk framework used exactly this to scale a 1-day figure to the 10-day regulatory horizon.

**Q356.** What assumptions underlie square-root-of-time scaling, and when does it break down?

It assumes returns are independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) with zero autocorrelation and constant volatility, so variances add linearly across days. It breaks down under volatility clustering (GARCH effects), mean reversion (which makes it overstate risk), or momentum/autocorrelation and fat tails (which make it understate risk). For options and path-dependent positions it is also unreliable because risk is non-linear in time.

**Q357.** What is backtesting of VaR and why is it required?

Backtesting compares the number of days actual losses exceeded the predicted VaR (exceptions/breaches) against the number expected from the confidence level. For a 99% 1-day VaR over 250 trading days you expect about 2.5 breaches; far more suggests the model understates risk, far fewer suggests it is too conservative. Regulators (Basel) require banks to backtest internal VaR models to validate them.

**Q358.** What is the Kupiec POF (proportion of failures) test?

Kupiec's POF test checks whether the observed breach frequency matches the expected rate p = 1 - confidence using a likelihood-ratio statistic. LR_POF = -2*ln[ ((1-p)^(N-x) * p^x) / ((1-x/N)^(N-x) * (x/N)^x) ], where N is the number of observations and x the number of breaches; it is compared to a chi-squared with 1 degree of freedom. It tests unconditional coverage only — whether the total count of breaches is right.

**Q359.** What is the Christoffersen independence test and conditional coverage test?

Christoffersen's independence test checks whether breaches are clustered in time (a breach today predicting a breach tomorrow) versus being independent — clustering signals the model fails to react to volatility regimes. His conditional coverage test combines Kupiec's coverage test with the independence test: LR_cc = LR_POF + LR_ind, compared to chi-squared with 2 degrees of freedom. A good model needs both the right number of breaches and breaches that are independent over time.

**Q360.** Describe Basel's traffic-light approach to backtesting.

Basel evaluates breaches of the 99% 1-day VaR over the most recent 250 days and assigns a zone: Green (0-4 exceptions) — model fine, no penalty; Yellow (5-9) — possible problem, a graduated capital multiplier add-on applies; Red (10+) — model rejected, maximum add-on and supervisory action. The multiplier scales the capital charge from a base of 3 up to 4 as breaches rise.

**Q361.** What is realized (historical) volatility and how is it computed?

Realized/simple volatility is the standard deviation of past returns, sigma = sqrt( (1/(n-1)) * sum((r_t - mean)^2) ), often computed over a rolling window. It is then annualised by multiplying the daily figure by sqrt(252). Equal-weighting all observations is its weakness — an old extreme return stays in and then drops out abruptly, causing "ghosting" jumps in the estimate.

**Q362.** What is EWMA / RiskMetrics volatility and why is it preferred over a simple rolling window?

EWMA (Exponentially Weighted Moving Average) weights recent returns more heavily: sigma_t^2 = lambda*sigma_{t-1}^2 + (1-lambda)*r_{t-1}^2. RiskMetrics uses lambda = 0.94 for daily data. It responds faster to volatility changes and avoids the abrupt ghosting jumps of equal-weighted windows, while needing only the previous variance and return to update.

**Q363.** In EWMA, what does the decay factor lambda control?

Lambda sets how fast old observations lose weight: a high lambda (e.g. 0.97) gives a smooth, slowly-adapting estimate with a long memory, while a low lambda (e.g. 0.90) reacts quickly but is noisier. The weight on the return k days ago is (1-lambda)*lambda^k, so with lambda = 0.94 the effective memory is roughly 1/(1-lambda) = about 17 days.

**Q364.** What is the GARCH(1,1) model for volatility?

GARCH(1,1) models conditional variance as sigma_t^2 = omega + alpha*r_{t-1}^2 + beta*sigma_{t-1}^2, where omega is a constant, alpha weights the latest squared return (the "news"), and beta weights yesterday's variance (persistence). It captures volatility clustering and mean reversion to a long-run level, and EWMA is just the special case omega = 0, alpha + beta = 1.

**Q365.** In GARCH(1,1), what are persistence and the long-run variance?

Persistence is alpha + beta; the closer it is to 1, the longer volatility shocks take to decay (typical equity estimates are around 0.98-0.99). The long-run (unconditional) variance is omega / (1 - alpha - beta), to which the model mean-reverts. Example: omega = 0.000002, alpha = 0.08, beta = 0.91 gives long-run variance 0.000002/0.01 = 0.0002, i.e. a long-run daily vol of about 1.41%.

**Q366.** What is the GJR-GARCH model and the leverage effect?

GJR-GARCH adds an asymmetry term so negative returns raise future volatility more than positive ones: sigma_t^2 = omega + (alpha + gamma*I)*r_{t-1}^2 + beta*sigma_{t-1}^2, where the indicator I = 1 when r_{t-1} < 0. This captures the leverage effect — empirically, equity markets become more volatile after falls than after equal-sized rallies, which symmetric GARCH and EWMA miss.

**Q367.** What is volatility clustering and why does it matter for VaR?

Volatility clustering is the empirical fact that large moves tend to be followed by large moves and calm by calm — high and low volatility come in bursts (Mandelbrot's observation). It violates the i.i.d. assumption, so static volatility estimates lag reality; this is exactly why EWMA and GARCH models, which let volatility evolve, give more responsive and accurate VaR than equal-weighted methods.

**Q368.** What are fat tails (leptokurtosis) and why do they matter for VaR?

Financial return distributions have more probability mass in the extremes than the normal distribution (excess kurtosis > 0), meaning large moves happen far more often than a bell curve predicts. This is why normal-based parametric VaR systematically understates the probability and size of extreme losses, and why ES, historical simulation, or t-distribution assumptions are used to capture the tail better.

**Q369.** Why does normal-distribution VaR understate tail risk?

The normal distribution assigns vanishingly small probability to large moves, but real markets show fat tails and clustering, so 4-5 sigma events occur far more often than "once in millennia." A 99% normal VaR uses z = 2.33, but the true 1% loss quantile of a fat-tailed series is larger, so the model under-reserves precisely in the crashes that matter. This is a core reason regulators supplement VaR with stressed measures and ES.

**Q370.** What is Stressed VaR (sVaR) and why was it introduced?

Stressed VaR computes VaR using model inputs (volatilities, correlations) calibrated to a continuous 12-month period of significant financial stress, such as 2007-2009, rather than the recent calm window. Basel 2.5 introduced it after 2008 because ordinary VaR estimated on benign recent data badly understated risk going into the crisis; total capital became roughly VaR + sVaR, sharply raising charges.

**Q371.** Define marginal, incremental, and component VaR.

Marginal VaR is the change in portfolio VaR per unit change in a position's size (the sensitivity/derivative). Incremental VaR is the actual change in total VaR from adding or removing an entire position or trade. Component VaR allocates the total VaR across positions such that the components sum exactly to portfolio VaR, showing each position's true risk contribution including diversification — useful for risk budgeting.

**Q372.** What is the diversification benefit in VaR and how is it measured?

Because assets are not perfectly correlated, portfolio VaR is less than the sum of standalone VaRs; the gap is the diversification benefit. Diversification benefit = sum(standalone VaRs) - portfolio VaR. For two positions, portfolio sigma = sqrt(w1^2*s1^2 + w2^2*s2^2 + 2*w1*w2*rho*s1*s2), so the lower the correlation rho, the bigger the benefit — and it can vanish in a crisis when correlations spike toward 1.

**Q373.** What are basis risk and gap risk in a market-risk context?

Basis risk is the risk that a hedge and the hedged item do not move perfectly together — e.g. hedging a Nifty cash portfolio with Nifty futures still leaves the cash-futures basis, or hedging a corporate bond with a government bond leaves spread risk. Gap risk is the risk of a sudden discontinuous price jump (overnight or on a news shock) that prevents trading out at intermediate levels, so a "delta-neutral" book or stop-loss is overrun — VaR built on continuous moves understates both.

**Q374.** What are the key limitations of VaR, and why did Basel move to Expected Shortfall under FRTB?

VaR ignores tail severity beyond the quantile, is not subadditive (penalising diversification), can be gamed, assumes stable distributions, and gives a false sense of a single number. Under the Fundamental Review of the Trading Book (FRTB), Basel replaced 99% VaR with a 97.5% Expected Shortfall measure — coherent, tail-sensitive, and calibrated to a stress period with liquidity-horizon scaling — to better capture extreme and illiquid risks exposed in 2008.

<a name="C2"></a>

## C2. Credit Risk  <span class="qrange">(Q375-Q404)</span>

**Q375.** What is credit risk, and what are its main forms?

Credit risk is the risk of loss arising because a borrower or counterparty fails to meet its contractual obligations. It has three broad forms: default risk (the borrower fails to pay interest or principal), downgrade/migration risk (the borrower's credit rating deteriorates, raising the required yield and lowering bond price even without default), and spread risk (the market-wide credit spread widens, repricing the exposure). All three reduce the value of a credit-exposed position.

**Q376.** What are the three key parameters of credit risk: PD, LGD and EAD?

PD (Probability of Default) is the likelihood the borrower defaults over a given horizon, usually one year, expressed as a percentage. LGD (Loss Given Default) is the fraction of exposure lost if default occurs, after recoveries (e.g., 45%). EAD (Exposure at Default) is the expected amount owed at the time of default, in currency (e.g., the drawn loan plus a portion of undrawn commitments). Together they drive the Expected Loss of an exposure.

**Q377.** Define recovery rate and its relationship to LGD.

Recovery rate is the fraction of the exposure that the lender recovers after a default, through collateral sale, restructuring or bankruptcy proceeds. It is simply the complement of LGD: `Recovery Rate = 1 - LGD`. So if LGD is 40%, the recovery rate is 60%; a senior secured loan with strong collateral has high recovery (low LGD), while subordinated unsecured debt has low recovery (high LGD).

**Q378.** What is Expected Loss and how is it calculated? Give a worked number.

Expected Loss (EL) is the average credit loss a lender anticipates over a horizon and is treated as a cost of doing business (covered by provisions/pricing). The formula is `EL = PD * LGD * EAD`. Example: a loan with EAD = INR 100 crore, PD = 2% and LGD = 45% gives `EL = 0.02 * 0.45 * 100 = INR 0.9 crore` (INR 90 lakh) of expected loss per year.

**Q379.** What is Unexpected Loss and how does it differ from Expected Loss?

Unexpected Loss (UL) is the volatility of credit loss around the expected level, i.e., the potential for losses to exceed EL in a bad year. EL is covered by provisions and loan pricing, whereas UL must be absorbed by economic capital. For a single exposure, `UL = EAD * sqrt(PD*(1-PD)*LGD^2 + PD*sigma_LGD^2)`; capital is sized to cover UL up to a high confidence level (e.g., 99.9%), which is the basis of credit VaR.

**Q380.** How do PD, LGD and EAD map to the Basel regulatory capital framework?

Under the Basel Internal Ratings-Based (IRB) approach, banks estimate PD, LGD and EAD as inputs to a supervisory risk-weight formula that computes capital for Unexpected Loss. The Foundation IRB lets banks estimate PD only (LGD/EAD prescribed by the regulator), while Advanced IRB lets them estimate all three. Expected Loss is covered separately by provisions, and any shortfall of provisions versus EL is deducted from capital.

**Q381.** What is the Altman Z-score and what does it measure?

The Altman Z-score is a multivariate discriminant model (Edward Altman, 1968) that predicts the probability of corporate bankruptcy within about two years from five financial ratios. It blends profitability, leverage, liquidity, solvency and activity into a single score. A higher Z indicates greater financial health; it remains a popular quick screen for distress despite being decades old.

**Q382.** State the five-ratio Altman Z-score formula and its cut-offs.

For public manufacturers: `Z = 1.2*X1 + 1.4*X2 + 3.3*X3 + 0.6*X4 + 1.0*X5`, where X1 = working capital/total assets, X2 = retained earnings/total assets, X3 = EBIT/total assets, X4 = market value of equity/total liabilities, X5 = sales/total assets. Interpretation: Z > 2.99 = "safe" zone, Z < 1.81 = "distress" zone, and 1.81-2.99 = "grey" zone. A score below 1.81 flags a high bankruptcy risk.

**Q383.** What are the Z' and Z'' variants of the Altman model?

The Z' model is for private manufacturers: it replaces market value of equity in X4 with book value of equity and re-estimates the coefficients (safe > 2.90, distress < 1.23). The Z'' model drops X5 (sales/total assets) to suit non-manufacturers and is also applied to emerging-market corporates: `Z'' = 6.56*X1 + 3.26*X2 + 6.72*X3 + 1.05*X4`, with a constant of +3.25 added for the EM scoring (EMS) version to align it with US bond rating equivalents.

**Q384.** Walk through a quick Altman Z-score calculation.

Take a public manufacturer with X1 = 0.15, X2 = 0.20, X3 = 0.10, X4 = 0.8, X5 = 1.5. Then `Z = 1.2*0.15 + 1.4*0.20 + 3.3*0.10 + 0.6*0.8 + 1.0*1.5 = 0.18 + 0.28 + 0.33 + 0.48 + 1.5 = 2.77`. A Z of 2.77 sits in the grey zone (1.81-2.99), suggesting caution but not imminent distress.

**Q385.** What is the Merton structural model of default?

The Merton (1974) model treats a firm's equity as a call option on the value of its assets, struck at the face value of debt D maturing at time T. If at maturity asset value V exceeds D, equityholders repay debt and keep the residual; if V < D, they default and hand the firm to creditors. Default is therefore an endogenous event driven by the firm's asset value falling below its debt, and equity = max(V - D, 0).

**Q386.** Why is equity modelled as a call option in the Merton framework?

Equityholders have limited liability: their downside is capped at zero (they walk away in bankruptcy) but their upside in the firm's assets is unlimited, exactly the payoff of a call option on assets V with strike D. This lets you price equity with Black-Scholes and, crucially, back out the unobservable asset value V and asset volatility sigma from the observable equity value and equity volatility. Debt is correspondingly a risk-free bond minus a put option (the default option) held by equityholders.

**Q387.** Define distance-to-default and how PD is derived in Merton.

Distance-to-default (DD) measures how many standard deviations the firm's asset value is above its default point: `DD = (ln(V/D) + (mu - 0.5*sigma^2)*T) / (sigma*sqrt(T))`, where V is asset value, D the debt/default point, mu the asset drift and sigma the asset volatility. The model's default probability is `PD = N(-DD)`, the normal CDF of the negative distance-to-default. A larger DD means the firm is further from the default boundary and has a lower PD.

**Q388.** Work through a simple distance-to-default and PD calculation.

Suppose V = 120, D = 100, mu = 5%, sigma = 25%, T = 1. Then `ln(120/100) = 0.1823`, and the drift term `(0.05 - 0.5*0.25^2)*1 = 0.0188`, so numerator = 0.2011; denominator = `0.25*sqrt(1) = 0.25`; `DD = 0.2011/0.25 = 0.80`. Hence `PD = N(-0.80) = 21.2%`. A higher asset cushion or lower volatility would push DD up and PD sharply down.

**Q389.** Why do structural-model PDs for investment-grade firms collapse to nearly zero?

Because PD = N(-DD) and the normal distribution has very thin tails, a large distance-to-default maps to an almost vanishing probability. For a well-capitalised IG firm with DD around 5-7 standard deviations, N(-DD) is on the order of 1e-7 or smaller, far below observed IG default rates. This is a known weakness of basic Merton: real short-horizon credit spreads imply non-trivial default risk, so practitioners use jumps, stochastic volatility, or calibrate to market spreads (e.g., Moody's KMV's empirical EDF mapping) rather than the raw normal CDF.

**Q390.** What are reduced-form (intensity) models and how do they differ from structural models?

Reduced-form models treat default as an unpredictable event governed by a stochastic hazard rate (intensity) lambda, without modelling the firm's assets or capital structure. Default arrives as the first jump of a Poisson process, so the survival probability to time T is `S(T) = exp(-integral of lambda dt)`, and for constant intensity `PD over T = 1 - exp(-lambda*T)`. Unlike structural models (where default is a predictable boundary crossing), default here is a "surprise," which produces realistic non-zero short-term spreads and lets parameters be calibrated directly to market credit spreads and CDS quotes.

**Q391.** What is a credit rating and what scale do agencies use?

A credit rating is an independent opinion of a borrower's or instrument's creditworthiness, i.e., its relative probability of default and expected loss. S&P and Fitch use AAA (highest) down through AA, A, BBB, BB, B, CCC, CC, C to D (default), with +/- modifiers; Moody's uses Aaa, Aa, A, Baa, Ba, B, Caa, Ca, C with 1/2/3 modifiers. Ratings drive borrowing cost, investor eligibility and regulatory capital.

**Q392.** What distinguishes investment grade from speculative grade?

Investment grade (IG) is BBB-/Baa3 and above: relatively low default risk, eligible for many regulated/institutional portfolios. Speculative grade (also "high yield" or "junk") is BB+/Ba1 and below: meaningfully higher default risk and yields. The IG/HY boundary matters because many mandates, indices and capital rules force selling when an issuer is downgraded from BBB- to BB+ (a "fallen angel"), which can trigger forced selling and spread widening.

**Q393.** Who are the major credit rating agencies globally and in India?

Globally the "Big Three" are S&P Global Ratings, Moody's Investors Service and Fitch Ratings, which dominate sovereign and corporate ratings. In India, SEBI-registered domestic agencies include CRISIL (majority-owned by S&P), ICRA (associate of Moody's), CARE Ratings, India Ratings (a Fitch group company), Acuité and Brickwork. Indian issuers map to national-scale symbols such as CRISIL AAA, "[ICRA]AA", or "CARE A" with similar gradations.

**Q394.** What is the difference between through-the-cycle (TTC) and point-in-time (PIT) ratings?

A through-the-cycle rating reflects a borrower's creditworthiness across an entire economic cycle, deliberately stable and "stressed," so it does not jump with every macro swing; agency ratings are broadly TTC. A point-in-time rating/PD reflects current conditions at the assessment date and so rises and falls with the cycle. TTC ratings give stable capital and lending decisions; PIT estimates (used in IFRS 9 / Ind-AS 109 expected loss) are more responsive and react quickly to downturns.

**Q395.** What is a rating transition matrix and what does it tell you?

A rating transition (migration) matrix gives the probabilities that an issuer in a given rating moves to each other rating (or defaults) over a horizon, typically one year. Rows are the starting rating, columns the ending rating, each row summing to 100%; the diagonal (staying put) is usually the largest entry, and the last column is the PD for that grade. For example, a one-year AAA-to-default probability is essentially 0%, while a B-rated issuer might show a 4-6% default column, so the matrix feeds migration-based credit VaR and multi-year cumulative PD estimates.

**Q396.** What is a credit spread and what does it compensate investors for?

A credit spread is the extra yield a risky bond pays over a comparable risk-free benchmark (e.g., a US Treasury or, in India, a G-Sec of similar tenor): `Credit Spread = Corporate Yield - Risk-Free Yield`. It compensates investors for expected default loss plus several premia: a risk premium for uncertain/unexpected losses, a liquidity premium, and tax/structural factors. As a rough first approximation, `Spread is approximately PD * LGD`, the annualised expected loss.

**Q397.** How is a credit spread decomposed?

A credit spread can be decomposed into: (1) an expected-loss component, approximately PD * LGD, the compensation for average default losses; (2) a credit-risk premium for unexpected losses and the systematic, non-diversifiable nature of default risk; (3) a liquidity premium for the difficulty of trading the bond; and (4) tax and other technical effects. Empirically the expected-loss piece explains only a minority of IG spreads, a puzzle known as the "credit spread puzzle," with risk and liquidity premia making up the rest.

**Q398.** What is a credit default swap (CDS)?

A CDS is a bilateral derivative that transfers the credit risk of a reference entity from a protection buyer to a protection seller. The buyer pays a periodic premium (the CDS spread, in basis points on notional) and, if a defined credit event occurs (default, failure to pay, restructuring), the seller compensates the buyer for the loss, typically `(1 - Recovery) * Notional`. It functions like insurance on a bond and lets investors hedge or take a synthetic long/short credit position without owning the underlying.

**Q399.** Explain the premium leg versus the protection leg of a CDS.

The premium (or fee) leg is the stream of periodic spread payments the protection buyer makes to the seller until maturity or a credit event, plus any accrued premium at default. The protection (or contingent) leg is the single payment the seller makes to the buyer if a credit event occurs, equal to `(1 - Recovery Rate) * Notional`. A CDS is fair when the present value of the premium leg equals the present value of the expected protection leg; solving that equality gives the par CDS spread.

**Q400.** Why is the CDS spread approximately equal to PD times LGD?

Setting the PV of the protection leg equal to the PV of the premium leg, the par spread is approximately `Spread is approximately lambda * (1 - Recovery) = PD * LGD` per year, where lambda is the default intensity. Intuitively, the annual premium must cover the annual expected loss: probability of default times loss given default. Example: if the market quotes a 5-year CDS at 300 bps (3%) and recovery is assumed at 40% (LGD 60%), the implied annual PD is roughly `0.03 / 0.60 = 5%`.

**Q401.** What is credit VaR and how does it differ from market VaR?

Credit VaR is the worst credit loss over a horizon (often 1 year) at a given confidence level (e.g., 99.9%), measured as the difference between that quantile loss and the expected loss; capital is held against this unexpected loss. Unlike market VaR, the credit loss distribution is highly skewed and fat-tailed: most years see small losses near EL, but rare clustered defaults create a long right tail. Models like CreditMetrics (rating migration) and CreditRisk+ (actuarial intensity) are used to generate this distribution.

**Q402.** Why do default correlation and concentration matter in a credit portfolio?

Because credit losses are driven by common factors (the economy, sector, region), defaults cluster rather than occur independently, fattening the tail of the loss distribution and raising the capital needed for a given confidence level. Higher default correlation means diversification benefits shrink and unexpected loss rises. Concentration, large single-name exposures or heavy sector/geographic tilts, further amplifies tail risk, which is why regulators impose large-exposure limits and why portfolio managers monitor name and sector concentration (e.g., via a Herfindahl index).

**Q403.** What are the RBI IRAC norms and how do NPAs get classified in India?

RBI's Income Recognition and Asset Classification (IRAC) norms govern how Indian banks recognise bad loans. A loan becomes a Non-Performing Asset (NPA) when interest/principal is overdue for more than 90 days. NPAs are then graded: Sub-Standard (NPA up to 12 months), Doubtful (NPA beyond 12 months), and Loss assets (uncollectible), with rising minimum provisioning at each stage (e.g., 15% for secured sub-standard, scaling up to 100% for loss assets). Accounts overdue 31-90 days are flagged as Special Mention Accounts (SMA-1/SMA-2) as an early-warning stage.

**Q404.** How does Expected Credit Loss under Ind-AS 109 / IFRS 9 work, and how does it relate to RBI provisioning?

Ind-AS 109 (India's version of IFRS 9) uses a forward-looking Expected Credit Loss (ECL) model with three stages: Stage 1 (performing) provides 12-month ECL; Stage 2 (significant increase in credit risk) and Stage 3 (credit-impaired/default) provide lifetime ECL. The core computation is `ECL = PD * LGD * EAD`, discounted, and incorporating macro forecasts, so provisions are recognised before an account actually turns NPA. This differs from RBI's rule-based IRAC provisioning (fixed percentages by classification); Indian banks still follow IRAC, while NBFCs report under Ind-AS ECL, and the RBI has proposed moving banks to an expected-loss framework, with the higher of the two often maintained.

<a name="C3"></a>

## C3. Risk Frameworks, Regulation & Other Risks  <span class="qrange">(Q405-Q435)</span>

**Q405.** What are the main categories of financial risk a bank or trading firm manages?

The standard taxonomy is: market risk (losses from moves in prices, rates, FX, equities), credit risk (a borrower or issuer defaulting), liquidity risk (inability to fund or to sell without large price concessions), operational risk (failures of people, process, systems or external events), counterparty credit risk (a trading counterparty defaulting before settlement), model risk (losses from using a wrong or misused model), and reputational risk (loss of franchise value from damaged perception). Regulators (Basel) explicitly capitalize market, credit and operational risk; the others are managed but harder to capital-charge.

**Q406.** What is the difference between market risk and credit risk?

Market risk is the risk of loss from adverse movements in market prices or rates (equity, interest rate, FX, commodity, volatility) on positions you hold. Credit risk is the risk that a counterparty or borrower fails to meet contractual obligations, causing loss of principal and/or interest. A bond portfolio has both: market risk if yields move and the price falls, and credit risk if the issuer defaults.

**Q407.** What is liquidity risk, and what are its two main types?

Liquidity risk is the risk of being unable to meet obligations or unwind positions in a timely way without incurring unacceptable losses. The two types are funding liquidity risk (you cannot raise cash to meet liabilities as they fall due) and market (asset) liquidity risk (you cannot sell an asset quickly near its fair value because the market is thin). In a crisis the two reinforce each other: fire-sales widen bid-ask spreads, which worsens both.

**Q408.** What is operational risk? Give examples.

Operational risk is the risk of loss from inadequate or failed internal processes, people and systems, or from external events (Basel definition); it explicitly includes legal risk but excludes strategic and reputational risk. Examples: rogue trading (Nick Leeson at Barings, Kerviel at Societe Generale), internal/external fraud, settlement and system failures, cyber-attacks, and natural disasters. Unlike market and credit risk, it is not taken deliberately to earn return.

**Q409.** What is model risk?

Model risk is the risk of adverse consequences from decisions based on models that are incorrect, misspecified, fed bad data, or used outside their intended scope. Examples include mispricing exotic options with a flawed volatility surface, or VaR understating tail risk because it assumes normality. It is managed through independent model validation, back-testing, and governance (e.g. the US SR 11-7 guidance).

**Q410.** What is reputational risk?

Reputational risk is the risk that negative perception by clients, counterparties, investors or regulators erodes a firm's revenue, funding access or franchise value. It is often a second-order consequence of another failure (a fraud, a mis-selling scandal, a data breach) and is hard to quantify or capitalize. Banks manage it through conduct standards, disclosure and crisis communication rather than a capital charge.

**Q411.** Briefly trace the evolution from Basel I to Basel II to Basel III.

Basel I (1988) introduced a simple risk-weighted capital framework focused almost entirely on credit risk, requiring 8% capital against risk-weighted assets. Basel II (2004) added a three-pillar structure, more risk-sensitive credit approaches (standardized and internal ratings-based), and an explicit operational risk charge. Basel III (post-2008, phased from 2013) kept the pillars but sharply raised the quality and quantity of capital, and added liquidity standards (LCR, NSFR), a leverage ratio, and capital buffers.

**Q412.** What are the three pillars of Basel II?

Pillar 1 is minimum capital requirements (the quantitative rules for credit, market and operational risk capital). Pillar 2 is the supervisory review process, where regulators assess a bank's internal capital adequacy (ICAAP) and can require capital above the minimum for risks not fully captured in Pillar 1. Pillar 3 is market discipline, achieved through public disclosure of risk and capital so the market can assess the bank.

**Q413.** What is the Capital Adequacy Ratio (CAR), and what is the minimum?

CAR (also called CRAR in India) measures a bank's capital against its risk-weighted assets: `CAR = Eligible Capital / Risk-Weighted Assets`. The Basel minimum total CAR is 8%; the RBI requires Indian banks to hold a minimum of 9%, plus buffers on top. Example: a bank with INR 90 crore of eligible capital and INR 1,000 crore of RWA has CAR = 90/1000 = 9%.

**Q414.** What are risk-weighted assets (RWA)?

RWA scale each asset by a risk weight reflecting its credit (and other) riskiness, so safer assets need less capital. For example a sovereign exposure might carry 0% weight, a residential mortgage 35%, and an unrated corporate loan 100%: `RWA = sum(exposure_i * risk_weight_i)`. A USD 100 loan at 100% weight is USD 100 of RWA needing USD 8 of capital at 8%; the same USD 100 in a 20% asset is only USD 20 RWA needing USD 1.60.

**Q415.** What is the difference between Tier 1 and Tier 2 capital?

Tier 1 is going-concern capital that absorbs losses while the bank operates: it comprises Common Equity Tier 1 (CET1 - common shares, retained earnings) plus Additional Tier 1 (AT1 - perpetual instruments like contingent convertibles). Tier 2 is gone-concern capital that absorbs losses in liquidation, such as subordinated debt and certain loan-loss reserves. Tier 1 is higher quality and more loss-absorbing than Tier 2.

**Q416.** What is CET1 capital and what is its minimum requirement under Basel III?

Common Equity Tier 1 is the highest-quality capital: common shares, share premium, retained earnings and disclosed reserves, net of regulatory deductions like goodwill. Under Basel III the minimum CET1 ratio is 4.5% of RWA, the minimum Tier 1 is 6%, and minimum total capital is 8% - before buffers. CET1 is the first line of loss absorption and the focus of stress tests.

**Q417.** What is the capital conservation buffer?

The capital conservation buffer is an extra 2.5% of CET1 above the minimum, designed to be built up in good times and drawn down in stress. With it, effective CET1 rises to 7% and total capital to 10.5%. If a bank's capital falls into the buffer, it faces automatic restrictions on dividends, buybacks and bonuses until capital is rebuilt.

**Q418.** What is the countercyclical capital buffer (CCyB)?

The CCyB is a discretionary buffer of 0-2.5% CET1 that national regulators switch on when they judge that excessive system-wide credit growth is building up risk, and release in downturns. Its aim is macroprudential - to lean against the credit cycle and protect the banking system, not just the individual bank. In India the RBI has the framework in place but has so far kept the CCyB at 0%.

**Q419.** What is the leverage ratio and why was it added in Basel III?

The leverage ratio is a non-risk-based backstop: `Leverage Ratio = Tier 1 Capital / Total Exposure (on- plus off-balance-sheet)`, with a 3% minimum. It was added because risk-weighting can be gamed or mis-calibrated (pre-2008 banks looked well-capitalized on RWA but were hugely levered), so a simple, hard cap on total leverage constrains balance-sheet growth regardless of model-assigned risk weights.

**Q420.** What is the Liquidity Coverage Ratio (LCR)?

The LCR ensures a bank holds enough high-quality liquid assets (HQLA) to survive a 30-day stressed cash outflow: `LCR = HQLA / Total Net Cash Outflows over 30 days >= 100%`. HQLA are assets that can be sold or pledged quickly with little loss (cash, central-bank reserves, high-grade government bonds). It is a short-term, acute-stress liquidity measure introduced by Basel III.

**Q421.** What is the Net Stable Funding Ratio (NSFR)?

The NSFR addresses structural, longer-term liquidity over a one-year horizon: `NSFR = Available Stable Funding / Required Stable Funding >= 100%`. Available stable funding weights liabilities and capital by how reliable they are (equity and long-term deposits score high; overnight wholesale funding scores low), while required stable funding reflects the illiquidity and tenor of assets. It discourages over-reliance on short-term wholesale funding to hold illiquid assets.

**Q422.** What is stress testing, and how does it differ from VaR?

Stress testing estimates losses under specific severe-but-plausible scenarios (e.g. a 30% equity crash, a 200 bps rate shock, a 2008-style event), whether historical or hypothetical. Unlike VaR, which gives a probabilistic loss threshold under normal conditions and tends to underrepresent tails, stress tests are scenario-driven and deliberately probe extreme conditions. They are central to Pillar 2 / ICAAP and to supervisory exercises like the Fed's CCAR and the RBI's macro stress tests.

**Q423.** What is reverse stress testing?

Reverse stress testing starts from the outcome and works backwards: it asks "what scenarios would cause the firm to fail or breach viability?" rather than "what is the loss under scenario X?" This identifies hidden vulnerabilities and concentrations that forward scenarios might miss, and informs recovery and resolution planning. Regulators increasingly require it as a complement to conventional stress tests.

**Q424.** What are the Basel approaches for operational risk capital?

Under Basel II banks could use the Basic Indicator Approach (capital as a fixed percentage, 15%, of average gross income), the Standardized Approach (different percentages by business line), or the Advanced Measurement Approach (AMA, internal loss models). Basel III replaces all of these with a single Standardized Measurement Approach (SMA), which combines a Business Indicator (a proxy for size from income statement items) with an Internal Loss Multiplier based on the bank's own historical operational losses.

**Q425.** What is counterparty credit risk (CCR) in derivatives?

CCR is the risk that the counterparty to a derivative or securities-financing trade defaults before the final settlement of cash flows, when the contract has positive value to you (so the default creates a replacement loss). Unlike a loan, the exposure is uncertain and bilateral - it changes with market moves and can be positive or negative. It is measured through metrics like Expected Exposure (EE), Potential Future Exposure (PFE) and Exposure at Default (EAD).

**Q426.** What are CVA and DVA?

Credit Valuation Adjustment (CVA) is the adjustment to a derivative's value reflecting the expected loss from the counterparty defaulting - effectively the market price of counterparty credit risk: roughly `CVA ~ LGD * sum(EE(t) * PD(t))` over future times t. Debit Valuation Adjustment (DVA) is the mirror image: the gain from your own default risk, which reduces the value of your liabilities. DVA is controversial because a firm's own credit deterioration produces an accounting gain.

**Q427.** What is wrong-way risk?

Wrong-way risk arises when a counterparty's credit quality is positively correlated with the exposure to it, so that exposure rises just as the counterparty becomes more likely to default. A classic example: buying credit protection on a company from a counterparty closely tied to that company. Right-way risk is the favorable opposite, where exposure falls as default risk rises.

**Q428.** How do netting and collateral/margin reduce counterparty exposure?

Close-out netting under an ISDA Master Agreement lets you offset positive and negative mark-to-market across all trades with a counterparty, so exposure is the net rather than gross amount. Collateral (margin) further reduces it: variation margin is posted to cover current mark-to-market moves, while initial margin covers potential future exposure over the close-out period. Together they sharply cut the loss-given-default, which is why a CSA (Credit Support Annex) governs collateral terms.

**Q429.** What is a central counterparty (CCP), and why did central clearing expand after 2008?

A CCP interposes itself between the two sides of a trade through novation, becoming buyer to every seller and seller to every buyer, and manages risk via multilateral netting, daily margining and a default fund/waterfall. After the 2008 crisis (where opaque bilateral OTC exposures, e.g. around AIG and Lehman, amplified contagion), the G20 mandated central clearing of standardized OTC derivatives to increase transparency and reduce systemic interconnectedness. The trade-off is concentration of risk in the CCPs themselves, making them systemically critical.

**Q430.** What is hedging, and why do firms hedge?

Hedging is taking an offsetting position whose value moves opposite to an existing exposure, so that gains on one roughly cancel losses on the other, reducing the variability of outcomes. Firms hedge to protect margins and cash flows from risks outside their core competence (FX, rates, commodity prices) - for example an Indian IT exporter selling USD forward to lock in the INR value of dollar receivables. The goal is to reduce risk, not to make a profit, and a perfect hedge eliminates the targeted risk entirely.

**Q431.** What is basis risk in hedging?

Basis risk is the residual risk that the hedge and the underlying exposure do not move in perfect lockstep, because they differ in instrument, maturity, location or quality. For instance, hedging jet fuel with crude oil futures leaves the crude-to-jet-fuel spread (the basis) unhedged. The basis is `Basis = Spot price of asset hedged - Futures price used`, and unexpected changes in it are the source of basis risk.

**Q432.** What is the difference between over-hedging and under-hedging?

Under-hedging means the hedge covers less than the full exposure (hedge ratio below 1), leaving part of the original risk live. Over-hedging means the hedge notional exceeds the exposure (hedge ratio above 1), so the "hedge" itself creates a net speculative position in the opposite direction. The optimal minimum-variance hedge ratio is `h = correlation * (sigma_spot / sigma_futures)`, and deviations from it leave residual or added risk.

**Q433.** What is the difference between economic capital and regulatory capital?

Regulatory capital is the minimum capital prescribed by supervisors (Basel/RBI rules) using standardized formulas, regardless of the bank's own view. Economic capital is the bank's internal estimate of the capital needed to absorb unexpected losses to a chosen confidence level and horizon (e.g. 99.9% over one year), tailored to its actual risk profile and used for risk-based pricing and performance (RAROC). They can diverge: a bank may hold economic capital above the regulatory floor where it judges its risks to be greater than the standardized rules assume.

**Q434.** What is risk appetite, and how do limits operationalize it?

Risk appetite is the board-approved statement of the amount and type of risk a firm is willing to take to meet its objectives, expressed through a risk appetite framework (RAF). It cascades into concrete, monitored limits - VaR limits, notional or position limits, stop-losses, concentration limits, stress-loss limits - that traders and desks must operate within. Limits convert a high-level appetite into enforceable controls, with escalation and breach procedures when they are exceeded.

**Q435.** How are risk and capital oversight split between the RBI and SEBI in India?

The RBI is the banking regulator: it sets capital adequacy (CRAR minimum 9% plus buffers), implements Basel III, prescribes liquidity (LCR/NSFR), runs supervisory stress tests, and oversees bank credit, market and operational risk. SEBI regulates securities markets - exchanges, brokers, mutual funds, clearing corporations - and governs market risk controls such as margining, position limits, circuit breakers and disclosure. So a bank's solvency sits with the RBI, while conduct and risk in the capital markets (including F&O margins and the clearing corporations that act as CCPs) sit with SEBI.

<a name="secD"></a>

# D. Financial Analyst / FP&A

<a name="D1"></a>

## D1. Budgeting, Forecasting & Variance Analysis  <span class="qrange">(Q436-Q465)</span>

**Q436.** What is FP&A, and what is the purpose of a budget?

FP&A (Financial Planning & Analysis) is the corporate finance function that builds budgets and forecasts, analyzes performance against plan, and advises management on resource allocation. A budget is a quantified plan for a period (usually a fiscal year) that sets revenue and cost targets, allocates resources, and creates a benchmark to measure actual results against. Its purposes are planning, coordination across departments, communication of targets, motivation, and control (variance analysis).

**Q437.** Walk through the typical annual budgeting cycle.

A typical cycle: (1) management sets high-level assumptions and targets (growth, margins, headcount), (2) departments build bottom-up budgets, (3) FP&A consolidates into a master budget, (4) iterate and negotiate to close gaps to targets, (5) board/CFO approval, (6) load the approved budget into the GL/planning system, then (7) monitor actuals vs budget monthly and reforecast. In India the fiscal year runs Apr-Mar, so the cycle typically finalizes in Jan-Mar; US/global firms commonly use a Jan-Dec calendar.

**Q438.** What is incremental budgeting and what is its main drawback?

Incremental budgeting starts from the prior year's actuals or budget and adjusts by a percentage or fixed amount for the new period (e.g., last year's INR 100 lakh spend +5% = INR 105 lakh). It is fast and simple but tends to perpetuate past inefficiencies ("budget creep") because existing spend is never re-justified, and it can encourage "use it or lose it" year-end spending.

**Q439.** What is zero-based budgeting (ZBB) and when is it used?

In ZBB every line item starts from a "zero base" and must be justified from scratch each cycle, rather than carrying forward prior amounts. It forces managers to defend every cost against its value, which can strip out legacy waste, but it is time-consuming and analytically heavy. It is often deployed for cost-transformation programs (e.g., consumer-goods firms cutting SG&A) rather than every year.

**Q440.** What is activity-based budgeting (ABB)?

ABB budgets costs based on the activities that drive them and their expected volumes, rather than on departmental line items. You identify cost drivers (e.g., number of purchase orders, machine setups, customer calls), estimate the activity volume, then multiply by the cost per activity. Budget = sum over activities of (expected driver volume * cost per driver unit). It links spending to operational demand and is the budgeting counterpart of activity-based costing.

**Q441.** What is a rolling (continuous) budget and how does it differ from a static annual budget?

A rolling budget always maintains a fixed horizon (e.g., 12 or 18 months) by adding a new period as each one elapses, so a month-end re-plan continuously extends the view. A static annual budget is set once and remains the fixed benchmark for the whole year. Rolling budgets keep the plan current and reduce the "January is detailed, December is stale" problem, at the cost of more frequent planning effort.

**Q442.** Compare top-down and bottom-up budgeting.

Top-down: senior management sets targets and pushes them down to departments; it is fast and strategically aligned but can be unrealistic and demotivating if disconnected from operations. Bottom-up: departments build estimates from operational detail and roll them up; it improves ownership and accuracy but risks padding and may not hit corporate targets. Most firms use a hybrid ("goal-setting top-down, build bottom-up, then reconcile").

**Q443.** Distinguish the operating budget, capital budget, and cash budget.

The operating budget covers day-to-day revenues and expenses (sales, COGS, opex) and feeds the budgeted P&L. The capital budget covers long-term asset investments (plant, equipment, IT) evaluated via NPV/IRR/payback. The cash budget projects cash inflows and outflows and timing to ensure liquidity; it is distinct from the P&L because of accruals, capex timing, and working-capital changes.

**Q444.** What is the master budget and what are its main components?

The master budget is the consolidated, integrated set of all sub-budgets producing the budgeted financial statements. It starts with the sales budget, then production/purchases, direct materials, direct labour, overhead, and SG&A budgets (the operating budgets), plus the capital and cash budgets, culminating in the budgeted income statement, balance sheet, and cash flow statement. The sales forecast is the keystone because nearly everything else depends on volume.

**Q445.** Define forecast, budget, and actual, and how they relate.

The budget is the fixed target approved at the start of the period; the forecast is the current best estimate of how the period will actually end (updated through the year); the actual is what was realized. FP&A reports all three: actuals vs budget show performance against the original plan, while forecast vs budget signals whether the year is tracking to land on, above, or below target.

**Q446.** What is run-rate, and how do you annualize it?

Run-rate annualizes recent performance as if it continued unchanged. Simple form: Annualized = latest period figure * number of periods per year (e.g., monthly revenue of INR 50 lakh => INR 6 crore run-rate). A smoother version uses a trailing average, e.g., Annualized = (sum of last 3 months / 3) * 12. It is a quick sanity check but ignores seasonality and one-offs, so it can mislead for cyclical businesses.

**Q447.** Describe trend-based forecasting and its limitation.

Trend forecasting extrapolates the historical growth pattern forward, e.g., next period = current * (1 + historical growth rate), or fitting a line/CAGR through past data. CAGR = (Ending / Beginning)^(1/n) - 1. It is simple and works when the past is a good guide to the future, but it breaks at inflection points (new products, recessions, regime changes) because it assumes the trend persists.

**Q448.** How is regression used in forecasting?

Regression fits a statistical relationship between a dependent variable (e.g., sales) and one or more drivers, e.g., y = a + b*x, where b is the slope (sales per unit of driver) and a the intercept. For example, Sales = 200 + 1.5*MarketingSpend predicts INR 1.5 of sales per INR 1 of marketing. Quality is judged by R-squared and the significance of coefficients; the risk is over-fitting and assuming correlation implies a stable causal driver.

**Q449.** What is driver-based forecasting and why is it preferred for planning?

Driver-based forecasting models outputs as a function of operational drivers rather than as a single top-line growth number, e.g., Revenue = units * average price, or = traffic * conversion rate * average order value. It makes assumptions explicit and lets managers test "what if conversion improves 1 point," improving transparency and scenario flexibility versus a blunt percentage-growth forecast.

**Q450.** What does "favorable" vs "unfavorable" mean in variance analysis, and what is the sign convention?

A variance is favorable (F) when it increases profit versus plan, and unfavorable (U) when it decreases profit. For revenue, Actual > Budget is favorable; for costs, Actual > Budget is unfavorable (you spent more). A common convention reports cost variance as Budget - Actual so a positive number is favorable, while revenue variance is Actual - Budget so positive is favorable; always state direction explicitly to avoid sign confusion.

**Q451.** Define a flexible (flex) budget and explain why it is useful.

A flexible budget recalculates budgeted revenues and costs at the actual activity (volume) level, using the original per-unit standards and budgeted fixed costs. For example, if budgeted variable cost was INR 100/unit and actual volume was 1,200 units, the flex budget variable cost is INR 1,20,000 regardless of the 1,000-unit static budget. It separates volume effects from price/efficiency effects so you compare like-for-like at the achieved volume.

**Q452.** How do the flex-budget variance and the sales-volume variance decompose the total static-budget variance?

Total static-budget variance = flexible-budget variance + sales-volume variance. The sales-volume variance = (actual units - budgeted units) * budgeted contribution margin per unit, isolating the effect of selling more/fewer units. The flexible-budget variance = actual results - flexible budget (at actual volume), isolating price and efficiency effects. Example: static op income variance of INR 50,000 might split into INR 30,000 volume + INR 20,000 flex (price/cost) effects.

**Q453.** Decompose a revenue variance into price and volume variances with a worked example.

Price variance = (Actual price - Budget price) * Actual quantity; Volume variance = (Actual quantity - Budget quantity) * Budget price. Example: budget 1,000 units at INR 100; actual 1,100 units at INR 105. Price variance = (105-100)*1,100 = +INR 5,500 F; Volume variance = (1,100-1,000)*100 = +INR 10,000 F. Total revenue variance = 1,100*105 - 1,000*100 = 1,15,500 - 1,00,000 = +INR 15,500 F = 5,500 + 10,000. (The cross term is conventionally absorbed into the price variance by using actual quantity.)

**Q454.** What is mix variance, and how does total variance split into price, volume (quantity), and mix?

With multiple products, total variance = price variance + pure quantity (volume) variance + mix variance. Mix variance captures the effect of selling a different proportion of products than budgeted, at constant total units: Mix variance = sum over products of [(actual mix % - budget mix %) * total actual units * budgeted contribution per unit]. The quantity variance uses budgeted mix on the change in total units, so mix isolates the shift toward higher- or lower-margin products independent of total volume.

**Q455.** Work a price/volume/mix decomposition for a two-product example.

Budget: A 600u at CM 10, B 400u at CM 20 (total 1,000u; budget mix 60/40; avg CM = (600*10+400*20)/1000 = 14). Actual total 1,100u, mix 50/50 (550 A, 550 B), CMs as budget. Quantity variance = (1,100-1,000)*14 = +1,400 F. Mix variance = sum[(actual mix% - budget mix%)*actual total units*budget CM] = (0.50-0.60)*1,100*10 + (0.50-0.40)*1,100*20 = -1,100 + 2,200 = +1,100 F. Total volume+mix = 2,500 F (matches: actual CM 550*10+550*20=16,500 vs budget 14,000). Price variance would add on top if CMs changed.

**Q456.** Explain the direct material price and usage (quantity) variances.

Material price variance = (Actual price - Standard price) * Actual quantity purchased; it measures buying efficiency. Material usage variance = (Actual quantity used - Standard quantity for actual output) * Standard price; it measures consumption efficiency. Example: standard 2 kg at INR 50/kg for output; actual 2.2 kg at INR 48/kg for one unit. Price = (48-50)*2.2 = -4.4 (F); Usage = (2.2-2.0)*50 = +10 (U).

**Q457.** Explain the direct labour rate and efficiency variances.

Labour rate variance = (Actual rate - Standard rate) * Actual hours; it reflects wage differences.
