Labour efficiency variance = (Actual hours - Standard hours for actual output) * Standard rate; it reflects productivity. Example: standard 5 hrs at INR 200/hr; actual 5.4 hrs at INR 210/hr. Rate = (210-200)*5.4 = +54 (U); Efficiency = (5.4-5.0)*200 = +80 (U).

**Q458.** What are the overhead spending and volume variances?

For variable overhead: spending variance = actual VOH - (actual hours * standard VOH rate); efficiency variance = (actual hours - standard hours)*standard VOH rate. For fixed overhead: spending (budget) variance = actual fixed OH - budgeted fixed OH; volume variance = budgeted fixed OH - (standard hours allowed * fixed OH rate), arising because fixed costs are absorbed on a per-unit basis and actual volume differs from the level used to set the rate. The fixed-OH volume variance is purely an absorption artifact, not a cash over/underspend.

**Q459.** How do you set a materiality threshold for investigating variances?

Firms set a rule combining absolute size and percentage, e.g., investigate any variance over INR 5 lakh OR over 10% of the line, whichever is breached, to avoid chasing tiny noise or ignoring large small-percentage items. Thresholds may be tighter for controllable/high-value lines and looser for volatile ones. The principle is management by exception: focus analyst time where the variance is both material and actionable.

**Q460.** What is a rolling reforecast and how often is it done?

A rolling reforecast updates the full-year (or rolling-horizon) estimate using actuals to date plus a revised view of the remaining periods, typically monthly or quarterly. A common structure: Full-year forecast = actuals YTD + reforecast of remaining months. It keeps guidance and resource decisions current and is the practical bridge between the static budget and reality, often paired with run-rate checks for the back half.

**Q461.** What KPIs does FP&A typically track in budgeting and performance reporting?

Common KPIs: revenue growth, gross and operating margin, EBITDA, budget vs actual variance %, forecast accuracy (e.g., absolute % error), cost per unit, headcount and cost per FTE, working-capital metrics (DSO, DPO, DIO), cash burn/runway (for startups), and operational drivers like units, ARPU, churn, or utilization. Forecast accuracy = 1 - |Actual - Forecast| / Actual, tracked over time to gauge planning quality.

**Q462.** What is budget padding (sandbagging) and how do you detect and mitigate it?

Padding/sandbagging is deliberately overstating costs or understating revenue so targets are easy to beat. Detection: persistent favorable variances in the same departments, hockey-stick spending near year-end, and benchmarking against drivers/peers. Mitigation: zero-based or driver-based reviews, holding managers to forecast accuracy (not just hitting target), stretch goals, and decoupling the budget used for incentives from the forecast used for planning.

**Q463.** Describe the FP&A monthly close and reporting loop.

After accounting closes the books (typically business days 3-7), FP&A pulls actuals, computes variances vs budget and prior forecast, investigates material items with business owners, prepares the management/board reporting pack with commentary, and updates the rolling reforecast and KPIs. The loop is: close -> analyze variances -> explain -> reforecast -> report -> act, repeating each month so the organization steers off current information rather than the stale annual budget.

**Q464.** What is scenario and sensitivity analysis in planning, and how do they differ?

Sensitivity analysis flexes one variable at a time to see its impact on an output (e.g., "a 1% price cut reduces EBITDA by INR 20 lakh"), revealing which assumptions matter most. Scenario analysis changes a coherent set of assumptions together to describe a whole state of the world (base, bull, bear), each with internally consistent revenue, cost, and cash figures. Sensitivity isolates drivers; scenarios stress-test the plan against plausible futures and inform contingency planning.

**Q465.** A static budget showed operating income of INR 1,00,000 (1,000 units at INR 1,000, variable cost INR 600, fixed INR 3,00,000); actuals were 1,100 units, price INR 980, variable cost INR 620/unit, fixed INR 3,10,000. Reconcile via the flexible budget.

Budget CM/unit = 1,000-600 = 400. Sales-volume variance = (1,100-1,000)*400 = +40,000 F. Flexible budget (at 1,100u, budget prices): CM = 1,100*400 = 4,40,000, less fixed 3,00,000 = 1,40,000 op income. Actual: revenue 1,100*980 = 10,78,000; variable 1,100*620 = 6,82,000; CM = 3,96,000; less fixed 3,10,000 = 86,000. Flex-budget variance = 86,000 - 1,40,000 = -54,000 U (price -22,000, variable cost -22,000, fixed -10,000). Total vs static (1,00,000) = 86,000 - 1,00,000 = -14,000 U = +40,000 volume - 54,000 flex.

<a name="D2"></a>

## D2. 3-Statement Financial Modeling  <span class="qrange">(Q466-Q490)</span>

**Q466.** What are the three financial statements and what does each one tell you?

The income statement shows profitability over a period (revenue minus expenses down to net income). The balance sheet is a snapshot at a point in time of what the company owns and owes (Assets = Liabilities + Equity). The cash flow statement reconciles net income to actual cash movement across operating (CFO), investing (CFI), and financing (CFF) activities, explaining the change in the cash balance between two balance sheet dates. Under Ind-AS the structure mirrors IFRS, so the same three-statement logic applies for Indian listed companies.

**Q467.** At a high level, how do the three statements link together?

Net income from the bottom of the income statement flows to the top of the cash flow statement (as the starting point of CFO) and into retained earnings on the balance sheet. CapEx sits in CFI, debt and equity issuance/repayment and dividends sit in CFF, and changes in working capital sit in CFO. The single ending cash figure produced by the cash flow statement (CFO + CFI + CFF + beginning cash) becomes the cash line on the balance sheet, and depreciation links the IS expense, the CF add-back, and the reduction of PP&E on the balance sheet.

**Q468.** Where does net income show up across all three statements?

Net income is the last line of the income statement, the first line of the cash flow statement (CFO), and it increases retained earnings within equity on the balance sheet (Ending RE = Beginning RE + Net Income - Dividends). This is why a single net income number ties the whole model together. If you change one assumption that moves net income, all three statements should move consistently.

**Q469.** Walk me through how a $10 increase in depreciation flows through all three statements (assume a 25% tax rate).

Income statement: pre-tax income falls by 10, so taxes fall by 2.50 (10 * 25%) and net income falls by 7.50. Cash flow statement: net income is down 7.50 but you add back the full 10 of non-cash depreciation, so cash from operations rises by 2.50; total cash increases by 2.50. Balance sheet: cash up 2.50 on the asset side, PP&E down 10 (net assets down 7.50), while retained earnings fall by 7.50 on the equity side, so the balance sheet stays balanced. The net 2.50 cash gain is exactly the tax shield from depreciation.

**Q470.** Why does extra depreciation actually increase a company's cash, even though it is an expense?

Depreciation is a non-cash expense, so the only real cash effect is that it lowers taxable income and therefore the cash taxes paid. With a 25% tax rate, 10 of extra depreciation saves 2.50 of taxes (10 * 0.25), and that 2.50 is the "depreciation tax shield." Without taxes, extra depreciation would have zero cash effect because you subtract it on the IS and add the identical amount back on the CF.

**Q471.** Walk me through a $10 increase in inventory across the three statements (purchased with cash, no tax effect).

Income statement: no immediate impact, because buying inventory is not an expense until it is sold (it sits in current assets). Cash flow statement: inventory is a use of cash, so CFO falls by 10 and total cash falls by 10. Balance sheet: inventory (asset) up 10, cash (asset) down 10, so total assets are unchanged and the statement still balances with no change to liabilities or equity.

**Q472.** Walk me through $10 of CapEx across all three statements.

Income statement: no immediate impact, since CapEx is capitalized, not expensed (only the future depreciation hits the IS). Cash flow statement: CapEx is an outflow in CFI of 10, so total cash falls by 10. Balance sheet: cash (asset) down 10 and gross PP&E (asset) up 10, so total assets are unchanged and the balance sheet balances. In later periods the depreciation of that asset then flows through the IS.

**Q473.** Walk me through what happens when a company raises $100 of debt.

Income statement: no immediate impact at the moment of issuance (interest expense only accrues over time). Cash flow statement: a financing inflow (CFF) of 100, so cash rises by 100. Balance sheet: cash (asset) up 100 and debt (liability) up 100, so Assets = Liabilities + Equity still holds. In subsequent periods, interest expense reduces net income and the debt schedule tracks principal repayments.

**Q474.** Walk me through a $10 inventory write-down across the three statements (25% tax rate).

Income statement: the 10 write-down is an expense (often in COGS or as an impairment), so pre-tax income falls 10, taxes fall 2.50, and net income falls 7.50. Cash flow statement: net income down 7.50 but the write-down is non-cash, so add back 10; CFO and total cash rise by 2.50 (the tax shield). Balance sheet: inventory down 10, cash up 2.50 (net assets down 7.50), retained earnings down 7.50 - balanced. The mechanics are identical to extra depreciation because both are non-cash charges.

**Q475.** How does the balance sheet "balance," and what do we mean by the plug?

The balance sheet balances when total assets equal total liabilities plus equity, and in a working model this happens automatically only if every line item is correctly linked to the IS and CF. The "plug" is the line that absorbs the residual flow of cash so the model stays internally consistent - typically the cash balance (and a revolver/short-term debt line when cash would otherwise go negative). If your statements are all correctly linked, the balance sheet balances by construction; you never manually force it.

**Q476.** What is the revolver (cash-sweep) mechanism and why is it used in a model?

The revolver is a short-term line of credit that acts as the financing plug: if the model projects a cash shortfall (cash below a minimum required balance), the revolver is automatically drawn to cover it; if there is excess cash above the minimum, the model uses it to pay the revolver down (the "cash sweep"). This keeps cash from going negative and keeps the balance sheet balanced under any operating scenario. The revolver balance sits in CFF and on the liability side, and its interest feeds back into the income statement.

**Q477.** Explain the circular reference created by interest on debt and why it arises.

Interest expense depends on the debt balance, but interest reduces net income, which reduces cash, which (via the cash sweep) changes how much revolver is drawn or repaid - which changes the debt balance and therefore interest again. This loop (interest -> net income -> cash -> debt -> interest) is a genuine circularity because each variable depends on the others within the same period. It is most acute when you calculate interest on the average of beginning and ending debt balances.

**Q478.** How do you resolve a circular reference from interest in a model?

Three common approaches: (1) enable iterative calculation in Excel (File > Options > Formulas > Enable iterative calculation) so the spreadsheet converges on a solution; (2) use a "circularity switch" - a toggle cell that, when flipped, replaces the live interest formula with a hardcoded zero or value to break the loop if a #REF or #DIV/0 error corrupts the model, then flip it back; or (3) compute interest on the beginning balance only, which removes the circularity entirely at the cost of slight accuracy. Copy-paste-special-values of the interest figure is a quick manual break but it freezes the link.

**Q479.** Why is a circularity switch considered best practice when using iterative calc?

Iterative calculation can "blow up" - if any single cell in the loop briefly returns an error (#DIV/0, #REF), the error propagates around the circle and the whole model fills with errors that iteration cannot clear on its own. A circularity switch lets you set interest to 0 (breaking the loop), let the model recalculate cleanly, then turn the switch back on to restore the live calculation. It is essentially a circuit breaker that makes the circular model recoverable.

**Q480.** How is a working capital schedule built using days-based drivers?

You forecast the major working capital items from turnover ratios expressed in days: DSO (Days Sales Outstanding) = Accounts Receivable / Revenue * 365 drives receivables; DIO (Days Inventory Outstanding) = Inventory / COGS * 365 drives inventory; DPO (Days Payable Outstanding) = Accounts Payable / COGS * 365 drives payables. You set forecast assumptions for each days metric, then back into the balance, e.g. forecast AR = DSO / 365 * forecast Revenue. The period-over-period change in each item then flows into CFO.

**Q481.** How does a change in working capital flow into the cash flow statement?

An increase in an operating asset (receivables, inventory) is a use of cash and reduces CFO; a decrease is a source of cash. An increase in an operating liability (payables, accrued expenses) is a source of cash and increases CFO; a decrease is a use. The cash flow change equals the negative of the change in net working capital (excluding cash and debt). For example, if receivables rise 10 and payables rise 4, the net WC use of cash is 6.

**Q482.** Walk me through a debt schedule and how interest is calculated.

A debt schedule tracks, for each tranche: beginning balance, mandatory amortization/repayments, optional prepayments or drawdowns, and ending balance (Ending = Beginning - Repayments + Drawdowns). Interest expense is typically calculated as the interest rate times the average of beginning and ending balance, or sometimes on the beginning balance to avoid circularity. The ending balances feed the balance sheet liabilities, the interest feeds the income statement, and principal flows feed CFF. The cash sweep determines optional revolver paydown from excess free cash.

**Q483.** How is a PP&E and depreciation schedule constructed?

Roll forward the net PP&E balance: Ending PP&E = Beginning PP&E + CapEx - Depreciation - Disposals. CapEx is often forecast as a percentage of revenue, and depreciation as a percentage of beginning PP&E or of revenue, or built bottom-up from a depreciation waterfall on each asset vintage. The ending PP&E goes to the balance sheet, CapEx is an outflow in CFI, and depreciation is a non-cash add-back in CFO and an expense on the IS. This single schedule keeps the three depreciation/CapEx touchpoints consistent.

**Q484.** What are deferred taxes and how do they arise in a model?

Deferred taxes arise from timing differences between book (financial-reporting) income and taxable income - most commonly because tax depreciation (e.g. accelerated/written-down value under the Income Tax Act in India, or MACRS in the US) differs from straight-line book depreciation. When tax depreciation exceeds book depreciation, the company pays less cash tax now and books a Deferred Tax Liability (DTL), which reverses later. The book tax expense stays on the IS, while the DTL change reconciles book vs cash taxes.

**Q485.** How do deferred taxes flow through the cash flow statement and balance sheet?

The income statement shows the book tax provision, but cash taxes actually paid differ; the difference is the change in deferred tax balances. An increase in a Deferred Tax Liability is a non-cash add-back in CFO (you expensed more tax on the books than you paid in cash), and it grows the DTL on the balance sheet liability side. A Deferred Tax Asset works in reverse. Modeling DTL/DTA changes is how you reconcile book tax expense to actual cash tax outflow.

**Q486.** What are the core modeling best practices around inputs and formulas?

No hardcoded numbers buried inside formulas - every assumption should live in a clearly labeled input cell so it can be flexed and audited. Convention is to color inputs/assumptions blue and formulas/calculations black (and links to other sheets often green), so anyone can instantly see what is an assumption versus a calculation. Build error checks (e.g. a balance check row showing Assets - Liabilities - Equity = 0), avoid circular links except where intended, and keep one consistent sign convention. Clean structure and consistency matter as much as the math.

**Q487.** What is the balance check and how should it be implemented?

The balance check is a dedicated row that computes Total Assets minus (Total Liabilities + Total Equity) for every period; it should equal exactly 0 in all columns. You usually highlight it (e.g. conditional formatting that flags any non-zero, or a single master cell that sums the absolute checks). If it shows any non-zero value, the model is internally inconsistent and you must fix the linkage before trusting any output. It is the single most important integrity check in a three-statement model.

**Q488.** What are the most common reasons a model does not balance?

Typical culprits: net income not flowing to both retained earnings and the top of the cash flow; a sign error on a cash flow line (treating a use of cash as a source); CapEx or depreciation linked in only two of the three statements; the change in a balance sheet item not reflected in CFO; dividends deducted from retained earnings but not from CFF (or vice versa); and the ending cash from the CF not being linked into the balance sheet cash line. A disciplined approach is to isolate the period where the check first breaks and trace each balance sheet item's change to its cash flow counterpart.

**Q489.** What is driver-based forecasting and why is it preferred over straight-line growth?

Driver-based forecasting builds line items from underlying operational drivers (units * price, customers * ARPU, capacity * utilization, store count * sales per store) rather than just applying a flat growth rate to last year's number. It makes assumptions transparent and testable, lets you run sensitivities on the real levers, and ties the financials to business reality. For example, revenue = number of stores * average revenue per store, so you can flex each driver independently for scenario analysis.

**Q490.** At a high level, how do you model an acquisition or a share buyback in a three-statement model?

For an acquisition, the cash (or debt) used is an outflow in CFI/CFF, the target's assets and liabilities are added to the balance sheet, any premium over net assets creates goodwill (an asset), and you consolidate the target's IS going forward; new acquisition debt adds interest expense. For a buyback, cash leaves via CFF (a financing outflow), treasury stock/equity is reduced on the balance sheet, share count falls (boosting EPS), and there is no income statement impact except the lower share count in the EPS denominator. Both must keep the balance check at zero - cash down on the asset side matches the offsetting reduction or increase in equity/liabilities.

<a name="D3"></a>

## D3. Cost Accounting, Unit Economics & Business Partnering  <span class="qrange">(Q491-Q515)</span>

**Q491.** What is the difference between fixed, variable and semi-variable costs?

Fixed costs stay constant in total within a relevant range regardless of output (rent, salaries, depreciation), so per-unit fixed cost falls as volume rises. Variable costs change in total in proportion to output (raw materials, packaging, sales commissions) but stay constant per unit. Semi-variable (mixed) costs have both a fixed base and a variable component, e.g. an electricity bill with a fixed connection charge plus a per-kWh usage charge, or a mobile plan with a base rental plus per-GB overage.

**Q492.** How do you split a semi-variable cost into its fixed and variable parts?

The simplest method is high-low: variable cost per unit = (cost at highest activity - cost at lowest activity) / (highest units - lowest units), then fixed cost = total cost - (variable per unit * units) at either point. Example: at 10,000 units cost is 70,000 and at 4,000 units cost is 40,000, so variable per unit = (70,000-40,000)/(10,000-4,000) = 5, and fixed = 70,000 - 5*10,000 = 20,000. Regression analysis is the more accurate alternative because it uses all data points, not just two extremes.

**Q493.** What is the difference between direct and indirect costs?

Direct costs can be traced economically to a single cost object (the wood in a chair, the wages of the worker building it). Indirect costs (overheads) are shared across multiple cost objects and must be allocated, such as factory rent, the plant manager's salary, or electricity. The same cost can be direct or indirect depending on the chosen cost object: a supervisor's salary is indirect to a single unit but direct to the department they run.

**Q494.** What is the difference between product costs and period costs?

Product (inventoriable) costs are incurred to make the product (direct materials, direct labour, manufacturing overhead) and sit on the balance sheet as inventory until the goods are sold, when they flow to COGS. Period costs are expensed in the period incurred regardless of sales, and include selling, general and administrative (SG&A) costs like marketing, office rent and the CFO's salary. Under both Ind-AS 2 and IAS 2, inventory is valued at production cost; selling and admin costs are excluded from inventory and expensed as period costs.

**Q495.** Define contribution margin and the contribution margin ratio.

Contribution margin per unit = selling price - variable cost per unit; it is the amount each unit contributes toward covering fixed costs and then profit. The CM ratio = CM per unit / price = total CM / total sales, expressed as a percentage. Example: price 100, variable cost 60, so CM = 40 per unit and CM ratio = 40/100 = 40%, meaning 40 paise of every rupee of sales is available to cover fixed costs and profit.

**Q496.** How do you compute the break-even point in units and in revenue? Give a worked example.

BEP (units) = fixed costs / CM per unit; BEP (revenue) = fixed costs / CM ratio. Example: fixed costs 200,000, price 100, variable cost 60, so CM = 40 and CM ratio = 40%. BEP units = 200,000/40 = 5,000 units, and BEP revenue = 200,000/0.40 = 500,000 (which equals 5,000 * 100, confirming consistency).

**Q497.** How do you find the volume needed to hit a target profit?

Treat the desired profit like an extra fixed cost: target units = (fixed costs + target profit) / CM per unit. Using fixed costs 200,000, CM 40 and a target profit of 100,000, required units = (200,000+100,000)/40 = 7,500 units. For an after-tax target, gross it up first: pre-tax profit = after-tax profit / (1 - tax rate).

**Q498.** What is the margin of safety and why does it matter?

Margin of safety = actual (or budgeted) sales - break-even sales, often expressed as a percentage: MoS% = (actual sales - BEP sales) / actual sales. It measures how far sales can fall before the firm hits a loss, so it is a cushion against demand risk. Example: if BEP is 5,000 units and you sell 8,000, MoS = 3,000 units or 3,000/8,000 = 37.5%.

**Q499.** What is operating leverage and how is the degree of operating leverage (DOL) calculated?

Operating leverage measures how sensitive operating income is to a change in sales, driven by the proportion of fixed costs in the cost structure. DOL = contribution margin / operating income (EBIT); a DOL of 4 means a 10% rise in sales produces a roughly 40% rise in operating income. Example: CM 400,000 and operating income 100,000 gives DOL = 4.

**Q500.** Why do high fixed-cost firms have more volatile profits?

Because fixed costs do not move with volume, a larger fixed-cost base means a higher DOL, so each percentage change in sales is magnified into a larger percentage change in profit, in both directions. Airlines, telecom and software firms have high fixed costs and low variable costs, so they swing sharply between large losses below break-even and high margins above it. The trade-off is operational: high operating leverage rewards growth but punishes downturns.

**Q501.** What is the difference between variable (marginal) costing and absorption costing?

Under variable (marginal) costing, only variable manufacturing costs are inventoried and all fixed manufacturing overhead is expensed as a period cost. Under absorption (full) costing, fixed manufacturing overhead is allocated to each unit and stays in inventory until sold. Absorption costing is required for external reporting under Ind-AS 2 / IAS 2 / US GAAP, while variable costing is used internally for CVP and decision-making because it isolates contribution margin.

**Q502.** How can the choice between absorption and variable costing change reported profit in a period?

The difference equals the fixed manufacturing overhead trapped in or released from inventory: when production exceeds sales, absorption costing defers some fixed overhead into ending inventory, so it reports higher profit than variable costing. When sales exceed production, the reverse happens and absorption shows lower profit. Example: 1,000 units produced, 800 sold, fixed overhead 50 per unit; absorption defers 200*50 = 10,000 of fixed cost into inventory, so absorption profit is 10,000 higher than variable that period. This is also why absorption costing can incentivise overproduction to inflate short-term profit.

**Q503.** What is activity-based costing (ABC) and how does it differ from traditional overhead allocation?

ABC assigns overhead to products based on the activities that actually drive cost, using multiple cost pools and cost drivers, rather than spreading all overhead on one volume measure like direct labour hours. It gives more accurate product costs when overhead is large and products consume resources unevenly, exposing that low-volume, complex products are often under-costed by traditional methods. Steps: identify activities, pool their costs, pick a cost driver for each pool, compute a driver rate, then assign cost to products by their driver usage.

**Q504.** What is a cost driver? Give examples.

A cost driver is the factor that causes the cost of an activity to change, used as the allocation base in ABC. Examples: number of machine setups drives setup cost, number of purchase orders drives procurement cost, number of inspections drives quality cost, and number of customer support tickets drives support cost. Choosing a driver that genuinely correlates with resource consumption is what makes ABC more accurate than a single volume-based rate.

**Q505.** What is the difference between standard costing and actual costing, and what is a variance?

Standard costing records inventory and production at predetermined (standard) costs set from engineering estimates and budgets, while actual costing uses costs actually incurred. The variance is the difference between standard and actual, split into price/rate variances (paid more or less per unit of input) and quantity/efficiency variances (used more or less input than standard). Variance analysis turns the gap into management signals; for example an unfavourable material price variance points to procurement, while an unfavourable efficiency variance points to the shop floor.

**Q506.** What is cost-volume-profit (CVP) analysis and what assumptions underlie it?

CVP analysis models how profit changes with volume using the relationship Profit = (price - variable cost)*units - fixed costs, and underpins break-even, target-profit and margin-of-safety calculations. Key assumptions: selling price and variable cost per unit are constant, total fixed costs are constant within the relevant range, costs split cleanly into fixed and variable, and product mix is stable for multi-product firms. These assumptions make CVP a short-run planning tool rather than a precise long-run forecast.

**Q507.** What is a relevant cost for decision-making, and what makes a cost a sunk cost?

A relevant cost is a future cash flow that differs between the alternatives being considered; only relevant costs and revenues belong in a decision. A sunk cost is a cost already incurred that cannot be changed by any future choice, so it is never relevant, no matter how large. For example, money already spent on R&D for a product is sunk and should be ignored when deciding whether to launch; only incremental future costs and revenues matter.

**Q508.** How do you make a make-vs-buy (outsourcing) decision on a relevant-cost basis?

Compare the incremental cost to buy from a supplier against the costs you would actually avoid by not making it in-house, which are the variable production costs plus any avoidable (escapable) fixed costs. Unavoidable fixed costs like factory rent that continues regardless are irrelevant. Example: making costs 50 variable + 20 fixed per unit but only 8 of that fixed is avoidable; relevant make cost is 58, so buying at 55 is cheaper, but at 60 it is not. Also weigh qualitative factors: quality control, supply reliability and any opportunity use of freed capacity.

**Q509.** How should a company evaluate a one-off special order priced below normal price?

If there is spare capacity, accept the order when the price exceeds the incremental (mostly variable) cost per unit, because fixed costs are already covered by regular business and any contribution is extra profit. Example: normal price 100, variable cost 60; a special order at 70 still adds 10 of contribution per unit if capacity is idle. Be careful that the order does not cannibalise full-price sales, breach price-discrimination rules, or set a precedent that erodes the regular price.

**Q510.** How do you decide whether to drop a product line or segment?

Drop it only if the contribution margin lost is less than the fixed costs actually saved (avoidable fixed costs); a segment showing an accounting loss after allocated common costs can still be worth keeping if it covers its own variable costs and contributes to shared overhead. Example: a line with 80,000 contribution but 100,000 of allocated fixed cost looks like a 20,000 loss, yet if only 30,000 of those fixed costs disappear on closure, dropping it worsens profit by 80,000-30,000 = 50,000. Allocated common costs that continue after closure are irrelevant to the decision.

**Q511.** What is transfer pricing and why does the chosen method matter?

A transfer price is the internal price one division charges another for goods or services, and it shifts measured profit between divisions, affecting performance evaluation, manager incentives and, across borders, taxable income in each jurisdiction. Common methods: market-based (use the external market price), cost-based (variable or full cost, sometimes plus a markup), and negotiated. Tax authorities require cross-border transfers to follow the arm's-length principle; in India this is enforced under the Income-tax Act transfer-pricing rules (Sections 92 to 92F) aligned to OECD guidelines, with documentation and penalties for non-compliance.

**Q512.** Define the core SaaS unit-economics KPIs: CAC, LTV and the LTV/CAC ratio.

CAC (customer acquisition cost) = total sales and marketing spend / number of new customers acquired in the period. LTV (lifetime value) = average revenue per account * gross margin % / churn rate, i.e. the gross profit a customer generates over their lifetime. LTV/CAC compares value created to cost to acquire; a ratio around 3x or higher is considered healthy, while below 1x means you lose money on every customer and well above 5x may signal underinvestment in growth.

**Q513.** What are CAC payback period, MRR/ARR and gross margin, and how do they fit together?

CAC payback = CAC / (monthly gross-margin revenue per customer); it tells you how many months of margin recover the acquisition cost, with under 12 months generally considered good. MRR is monthly recurring revenue and ARR = MRR * 12; both count only recurring subscription revenue, excluding one-off services. SaaS gross margin = (recurring revenue - cost of delivering the service, e.g. hosting and support) / recurring revenue, typically 70-85% for software; high gross margin is what makes LTV and payback attractive.

**Q514.** Explain churn and net revenue retention (NRR), and why NRR can exceed 100%.

Churn is the rate at which customers or revenue are lost; logo churn counts customers, revenue churn counts lost MRR. Net revenue retention = (starting MRR + expansion - contraction - churn) / starting MRR, measured for an existing cohort and excluding new customers. NRR can exceed 100% when expansion (upsells, seat growth, price increases) outweighs churn and downgrades, meaning the existing base grows even without new logos; best-in-class SaaS firms run NRR of 120%+, a powerful compounding engine.

**Q515.** What is the Rule of 40, and what is the FP&A business-partnering role including driver-based planning?

The Rule of 40 says a healthy software company's revenue growth rate plus profit margin (often EBITDA or free-cash-flow margin) should be at least 40%, e.g. 30% growth + 12% margin = 42% passes; it captures the growth-versus-profitability trade-off. FP&A business partnering means finance works alongside operating teams to turn strategy into forecasts, challenge assumptions and inform decisions rather than just report numbers. Driver-based planning builds the model from operational drivers (units, price, headcount, conversion rate, churn) rather than simply growing last year's totals by a percentage; a simple cost model links volume drivers to variable costs, adds step-fixed costs as capacity thresholds are crossed, and layers in fixed overhead, so a change in a driver flows transparently through to cost, margin and cash.

<a name="secE"></a>

# E. Quant / Derivatives

<a name="E1"></a>

## E1. Options & Futures Fundamentals  <span class="qrange">(Q516-Q546)</span>

**Q516.** What is a call option versus a put option, and who holds the right versus the obligation?

A call gives the buyer the RIGHT (not obligation) to BUY the underlying at the strike K; a put gives the buyer the right to SELL at K. The option BUYER holds the right and pays a premium; the option SELLER (writer) receives the premium and takes on the OBLIGATION to deliver (call) or buy (put) if exercised. So buyers have limited loss (premium) and sellers have limited gain (premium) but potentially large loss.

**Q517.** What does "long" versus "short" a position mean, and what are the four basic option positions?

Long means you bought/own it; short means you sold/wrote it. The four positions are: long call (bullish, pay premium), short call (bearish/neutral, receive premium), long put (bearish, pay premium), short put (bullish/neutral, receive premium). Long positions have the rights; short positions carry the obligations and the margin requirement.

**Q518.** What is the payoff of a long call at expiry, and write the formula?

Payoff = max(S_T - K, 0), where S_T is the spot at expiry and K the strike. Profit = max(S_T - K, 0) - premium. Example: Nifty call K = 22000 bought for 150; if Nifty expires at 22300, payoff = 300, profit = 300 - 150 = 150 per unit.

**Q519.** What is the payoff of a long put at expiry?

Payoff = max(K - S_T, 0). Profit = max(K - S_T, 0) - premium. Example: a TCS 3800 put bought for 60; if TCS expires at 3700, payoff = 100 and profit = 100 - 60 = 40 per share. A put gains value as the underlying falls, capped because price cannot go below zero.

**Q520.** What are the payoffs to the option WRITER (short call and short put)?

Short call payoff = -max(S_T - K, 0); short put payoff = -max(K - S_T, 0). The writer keeps the premium if the option expires worthless. Profit (short call) = premium - max(S_T - K, 0); a naked short call has theoretically unlimited loss, while a short put's max loss is K - premium (if the stock goes to zero).

**Q521.** Define intrinsic value and time value of an option.

Intrinsic value is the in-the-money amount if exercised now: call = max(S - K, 0), put = max(K - S, 0). Time value = option premium - intrinsic value, reflecting the chance of moving further ITM before expiry. Example: stock 105, call K = 100 priced 8: intrinsic = 5, time value = 3. Time value decays to zero at expiry (theta decay).

**Q522.** Explain moneyness (ITM/ATM/OTM) for calls and puts.

For a call: ITM when S > K, ATM when S ~ K, OTM when S < K. For a put it reverses: ITM when S < K, OTM when S > K. ITM options have intrinsic value; ATM/OTM options are pure time value. Example: spot 100 — the 95 call is ITM, the 100 call ATM, the 105 call OTM; the 105 put is ITM and the 95 put OTM.

**Q523.** What is the difference between a payoff diagram and a profit diagram?

A payoff diagram plots the value at expiry (ignoring cost), so a long call's payoff is a hockey stick that is flat at zero up to K then slopes up at 45 degrees. A profit diagram subtracts the premium paid (or adds premium received), shifting the line down by the premium, so breakeven for a long call is at K + premium, not K. Profit accounts for the upfront cost; payoff does not.

**Q524.** What is the breakeven point for a long call and a long put?

Long call breakeven = K + premium (stock must rise enough to recover the premium). Long put breakeven = K - premium. Example: 22000 Nifty call at 150 breaks even at 22150; 22000 put at 130 breaks even at 21870. Below/above breakeven respectively the buyer is still net negative even if the option is ITM.

**Q525.** What are the main drivers of an option premium?

Six drivers (Black-Scholes inputs): underlying price S, strike K, time to expiry T, volatility sigma, risk-free rate r, and dividends/yield q. Calls rise with S, T, sigma and r, and fall with q; puts rise with K, T, sigma and q, and fall with r. Volatility and time are the key "time value" drivers — higher sigma or more time means more premium for both calls and puts.

**Q526.** Why does higher volatility increase BOTH call and put premiums?

Options have asymmetric payoffs (limited downside, open upside), so a wider distribution of S_T raises the expected payoff of the optionality without a matching penalty. Higher sigma fattens the tails, increasing the probability of finishing deep ITM, while the OTM side is already floored at zero. This is captured by positive vega for both calls and puts.

**Q527.** What is the difference between American and European exercise style?

American options can be exercised any time up to expiry; European options only at expiry. American options are worth at least as much as European (more flexibility). In India, both index options (Nifty, Bank Nifty) AND single-stock options on NSE are European-style; US equity options are typically American, while US index options like SPX are European.

**Q528.** When is it ever optimal to early-exercise an American option?

For a non-dividend-paying stock, an American CALL is never optimally exercised early (you forgo time value and interest on K) — so it equals a European call. American PUTS can be optimal to exercise early (deep ITM, to receive K and earn interest). Early call exercise becomes rational just before a large dividend, to capture the dividend.

**Q529.** State the put-call parity relationship and explain each term.

For European options: C - P = S - K*e^(-r*T), where C and P are call and put prices on the same K and T, S is spot, r the risk-free rate. It says a long call plus short put (same strike) replicates a forward on the stock. Rearranged: C + K*e^(-r*T) = P + S (fiduciary call = protective put). With a dividend yield q, replace S with S*e^(-q*T).

**Q530.** Use put-call parity to value a missing option: S = 100, K = 100, r = 6%, T = 0.5, call = 7. Find the put.

P = C - S + K*e^(-r*T) = 7 - 100 + 100*e^(-0.06*0.5) = 7 - 100 + 100*0.9704 = 7 - 100 + 97.04 = 4.04. The put is worth about 4.04. The call exceeds the put here because the present-valued strike (97.04) is below spot, reflecting the interest carry benefit to the call holder.

**Q531.** How do you build a synthetic forward (or synthetic long stock) using options?

Synthetic long forward = long call + short put at the same strike K and expiry; its payoff is S_T - K, identical to a forward. Synthetic long stock today = long call + short put + invest K*e^(-r*T) in bonds. Conversely synthetic short stock = short call + long put. These follow directly from put-call parity C - P = S - K*e^(-r*T).

**Q532.** What does Indian regulation say about exercise style and how does it affect parity?

SEBI/NSE moved all equity (single-stock) options to European style years ago, and index options were always European, so standard European put-call parity C - P = S - K*e^(-r*T) holds cleanly in India. Because Indian stock options are physically settled, the parity should also account for any expected dividend (use S*e^(-q*T)) and the cost of holding/borrowing the stock to deliver.

**Q533.** What is a covered call versus a naked call?

A covered call is writing a call while owning the underlying stock — the stock covers the delivery obligation, capping upside but earning premium income. A naked (uncovered) call is writing a call without owning the stock, exposing the writer to theoretically unlimited loss and requiring large margin. Covered calls are an income/yield strategy; naked calls are pure short-volatility bets.

**Q534.** What is the difference between exercise and assignment?

Exercise is the option holder (long) choosing to invoke their right to buy/sell at K. Assignment is when the clearing corporation (e.g., NSE Clearing / OCC in the US) selects a short option holder to fulfil that obligation. Assignment is usually random across writers; an in-the-money option at expiry is auto-exercised, triggering assignment to a writer who must then deliver or take delivery.

**Q535.** What is the difference between open interest and volume?

Volume is the number of contracts traded during the day (resets daily). Open interest (OI) is the total number of contracts currently open/outstanding (not yet closed or expired). OI rises when a new buyer and new seller create a contract and falls when both close. Rising price with rising OI suggests a strengthening trend; rising price with falling OI suggests short covering.

**Q536.** How do futures differ from forwards?

Futures are exchange-traded, standardized, marked-to-market daily, and guaranteed by a clearinghouse with margin — minimizing counterparty risk. Forwards are OTC, customized, settled at maturity, and carry counterparty/credit risk with no daily cash flows. Because of daily settlement, futures have small convexity differences from forwards, but for short maturities and low rate-correlation they are treated as equal in price.

**Q537.** What is the cost-of-carry fair value of a future/forward? Give the formula.

F = S*e^((r - q)*T) for continuous compounding, where r is the risk-free rate, q the dividend yield (or convenience yield for commodities), and T the time to expiry. For commodities with storage cost u: F = S*e^((r + u - y)*T), y = convenience yield. Example: S = 22000 Nifty, r = 6%, q = 1.5%, T = 0.25: F = 22000*e^(0.045*0.25) = 22000*1.0113 = 22249.

**Q538.** What is contango versus backwardation?

Contango is when futures price is above spot (F > S), the normal state when carry cost (r) exceeds yield (q) — the curve slopes upward. Backwardation is F < S, common when convenience yield or dividends exceed financing cost, or with supply tightness. As expiry approaches, the basis converges to zero, so futures pull toward spot.

**Q539.** Define basis and basis risk.

Basis = spot price - futures price (some texts use futures - spot; be consistent). At expiry basis converges to zero. Basis risk is the risk that the basis changes unpredictably before you close a hedge, so the hedge does not perfectly offset the cash position — it arises when the hedging instrument, location, or maturity does not exactly match the exposure. Example: hedging jet fuel with crude futures leaves basis risk from the fuel-crude spread.

**Q540.** How do you hedge an equity portfolio with index futures using beta? Give the contract formula.

Number of contracts = beta * (Portfolio value) / (Futures price * lot multiplier). Going SHORT that many index futures neutralizes market risk. Example: INR 1 crore portfolio, beta 1.2, Nifty future 22000, lot 50, contract value = 22000*50 = 11,00,000; contracts = 1.2*1,00,00,000 / 11,00,000 = 10.9, so short about 11 contracts.

**Q541.** How do you change a portfolio's beta (not fully hedge) using index futures?

Contracts = (beta_target - beta_current) * Portfolio value / (Futures price * lot multiplier). A positive result means buy futures (raise beta), negative means sell (lower beta). Example: move beta from 1.0 to 0.5 on a INR 1 crore book, Nifty future contract value 11,00,000: contracts = (0.5 - 1.0)*1,00,00,000/11,00,000 = -4.5, so short ~5 contracts.

**Q542.** What is the minimum-variance hedge ratio for a futures hedge?

h* = correlation(spot, futures) * (sigma_spot / sigma_futures) = covariance(spot returns, futures returns) / variance(futures returns). It is the slope of regressing spot price changes on futures price changes, minimizing variance of the hedged position. Number of contracts = h* * (size of exposure) / (futures contract size); for a portfolio this h* effectively equals the beta versus the index.

**Q543.** What are initial margin and maintenance margin, and what triggers a margin call?

Initial margin is the deposit required to open a futures position; maintenance margin is the lower threshold the balance must stay above. As positions are marked-to-market daily, losses reduce the margin account; if it falls below maintenance, a margin call requires topping up back to the initial margin. Example: initial 1,00,000, maintenance 75,000 — if MTM losses drop the balance to 70,000, you must add 30,000.

**Q544.** What is SPAN margin in the Indian F&O market?

SPAN (Standard Portfolio Analysis of Risk) is the system used by NSE/exchanges to compute the worst-case one-day loss of a portfolio across price and volatility scenarios, setting the initial margin. Total upfront margin = SPAN margin + Exposure margin (an additional buffer). SPAN nets risk across a portfolio (e.g., a hedged spread needs far less margin than two outright positions).

**Q545.** What are typical lot sizes and expiries for Nifty and Bank Nifty derivatives?

Lots are exchange-defined and revised periodically — Nifty is commonly 25 (revised from 50/75 over time) and Bank Nifty around 15-35; always check the current NSE circular. Index options had weekly and monthly expiries, though SEBI has rationalized to fewer weekly expiries (one benchmark weekly per exchange) plus monthly contracts that expire on a designated weekday. Stock F&O are monthly only.

**Q546.** What is physical versus cash settlement, and how does India apply it?

Cash settlement pays the net difference in cash at expiry; physical settlement requires actual delivery of the underlying. In India, all single-stock F&O (futures and options) are PHYSICALLY settled — ITM stock options and stock futures held to expiry result in delivery obligations and full contract-value funds/shares. Index derivatives (Nifty, Bank Nifty) are CASH settled against the settlement value, since you cannot deliver an index.

<a name="E2"></a>

## E2. Black-Scholes-Merton & the Greeks  <span class="qrange">(Q547-Q581)</span>

**Q547.** What does the Black-Scholes-Merton (BSM) model do, and who is it for?

BSM gives a closed-form fair value for a European option on a non-dividend-paying stock, derived in 1973 by Black, Scholes and Merton. Its central insight is that an option can be perfectly replicated by a continuously rebalanced portfolio of the stock and a risk-free bond, so by no-arbitrage the option must cost the same as that replicating portfolio. This removes any need to know investor risk preferences or the stock's expected return, which is why it became the foundation of modern derivatives pricing.

**Q548.** List the core assumptions behind BSM.

The key assumptions are: (1) stock prices follow geometric Brownian motion, so log returns are normally distributed and prices are lognormal; (2) volatility sigma is constant and known; (3) the risk-free rate r is constant and known; (4) no arbitrage opportunities exist; (5) trading is continuous and the stock is perfectly divisible; (6) no transaction costs or taxes; (7) the option is European (exercise only at expiry); and (8) no dividends in the base model (Merton later relaxed this). Most real-world deviations from BSM trace back to one of these assumptions failing.

**Q549.** Write the BSM formula for a European call.

C = S*N(d1) - K*e^(-r*T)*N(d2), where d1 = (ln(S/K) + (r + 0.5*sigma^2)*T) / (sigma*sqrt(T)) and d2 = d1 - sigma*sqrt(T). Here S is spot, K is strike, T is time to expiry in years, r is the continuously-compounded risk-free rate, sigma is annualized volatility, and N() is the standard normal cumulative distribution function (CDF). The call value is the discounted expected payoff under the risk-neutral measure.

**Q550.** What is the BSM formula for a European put?

P = K*e^(-r*T)*N(-d2) - S*N(-d1), using the same d1 and d2 as the call. Equivalently you can get it from put-call parity: P = C - S + K*e^(-r*T). Both routes give the identical price, since BSM is internally consistent with no-arbitrage parity.

**Q551.** What does "lognormal prices" mean and why does BSM assume it?

It means the stock price S_T at any future date is lognormally distributed, i.e. ln(S_T) is normally distributed. This follows from modeling returns as dS/S = mu*dt + sigma*dW, geometric Brownian motion, where percentage changes (not absolute changes) are random and normally distributed. Lognormality conveniently keeps prices strictly positive and makes the expected payoff integral solvable in closed form.

**Q552.** Interpret N(d2) in the BSM call formula.

N(d2) is the risk-neutral probability that the call finishes in-the-money, i.e. the probability that S_T > K under the risk-neutral measure. The term K*e^(-r*T)*N(d2) is therefore the present value of paying the strike, weighted by the chance you actually exercise. It is a risk-neutral probability, not the real-world probability, because it uses r rather than the true expected return.

**Q553.** Interpret N(d1) in the BSM call formula.

N(d1) is the option's delta — the sensitivity of the call price to the stock — and the term S*N(d1) is the present value of receiving the stock conditional on exercise. More technically N(d1) is the risk-neutral expected value of S_T given finishing ITM, discounted and divided by S. Loosely, N(d1) tells you how many shares to hold to hedge, while N(d2) is the exercise probability.

**Q554.** Why does BSM use the risk-free rate r instead of the stock's real expected return?

Because the option is priced by replication/hedging, not by forecasting. A delta-hedged option position is locally riskless, so it must earn the risk-free rate to avoid arbitrage; the stock's true drift mu cancels out of the pricing equation entirely. This is the essence of risk-neutral valuation: we discount the expected payoff computed under a measure where every asset drifts at r.

**Q555.** What is risk-neutral valuation in one sentence?

Price any derivative as the expected value of its payoff computed under the risk-neutral probability measure (where all assets grow at r), discounted back at the risk-free rate: Price = e^(-r*T) * E_Q[payoff]. It works because of no-arbitrage and replication, and it sidesteps the need to estimate risk premia or real-world drift.

**Q556.** How does Merton's dividend adjustment change the formula?

For a stock paying a continuous dividend yield q, replace S with S*e^(-q*T) everywhere. So C = S*e^(-q*T)*N(d1) - K*e^(-r*T)*N(d2), with d1 = (ln(S/K) + (r - q + 0.5*sigma^2)*T)/(sigma*sqrt(T)). Intuitively dividends leak value out of the stock you would receive on exercise, so the call is worth less and the put more. The same q-adjustment is used to price index options (where q is the index dividend yield) and, via cost-of-carry, FX and futures options.

**Q557.** Define delta and give the BSM delta for a call and a put.

Delta is the rate of change of option value per unit change in the underlying: delta = dV/dS. For a (non-dividend) European call, delta = N(d1), ranging from 0 (deep OTM) to 1 (deep ITM); for a put, delta = N(d1) - 1, ranging from -1 to 0. With a dividend yield q the call delta is e^(-q*T)*N(d1). An ATM call has delta around 0.5.

**Q558.** Why is delta also called the hedge ratio?

Because to neutralize the directional risk of one short call you hold delta shares of the underlying (delta shares per option), making the combined position locally insensitive to small spot moves. If you are short a call with delta 0.55 on a lot, you buy 0.55*lot_size shares to be delta-neutral. As spot moves, delta changes, so the hedge must be rebalanced — that is dynamic delta hedging.

**Q559.** Why is delta a rough approximation of the probability of finishing ITM?

For a call, delta = N(d1) while the true risk-neutral ITM probability is N(d2), and since d1 > d2 these differ only by the sigma*sqrt(T) gap. For short-dated, low-vol options that gap is small, so traders use delta as a quick proxy for "chance of expiring ITM." A 25-delta option is loosely treated as having roughly a 25% chance of finishing ITM, though strictly the probability is N(d2), a bit lower.

**Q560.** Define gamma and state where it is largest.

Gamma is the second derivative of value with respect to spot, gamma = d(delta)/dS = d2V/dS2; it measures how fast delta changes. Gamma is identical for a call and the corresponding put (parity), is always positive for long options, and is largest for at-the-money options close to expiry, where delta swings most violently around the strike. The BSM formula is gamma = N'(d1) / (S*sigma*sqrt(T)), where N'() is the normal PDF.

**Q561.** Why do traders care about gamma even though they hedge delta?

Because delta hedging is only exact for infinitesimal moves; gamma measures the hedging error from large moves. High positive gamma means your delta hedge becomes stale quickly, forcing frequent rebalancing, but it also means a delta-neutral long-option book profits from big moves in either direction. Gamma is the curvature that a single delta number cannot capture, so a full hedge tracks both.

**Q562.** Define vega and its units.

Vega is the sensitivity of option value to a change in volatility: vega = dV/dsigma. By convention it is quoted per 1 percentage-point (0.01) change in vol. The BSM formula is vega = S*sqrt(T)*N'(d1) (times e^(-q*T) with dividends); it is the same for calls and puts, always positive for long options. Note "vega" is not a real Greek letter, but the name stuck.

**Q563.** Where is vega largest, and how does it differ from gamma's profile?

Vega is largest for at-the-money options that are longer-dated, because more time means more room for volatility to move the terminal price distribution. This contrasts with gamma, which is also largest ATM but peaks for short-dated options. So a long-dated ATM option is a big vega / small gamma bet, while a near-expiry ATM option is a big gamma / small vega bet.

**Q564.** Define theta and explain its usual sign.

Theta is the rate of change of value with the passage of time: theta = dV/dt, usually quoted per day (per calendar or trading day). For most long options theta is negative — the option loses time value as expiry approaches ("time decay") — and decay is fastest for at-the-money options near expiry. Deep ITM European puts (and some dividend cases) can have positive theta, but the typical long-option holder is fighting theta.

**Q565.** What is the relationship between theta, gamma and vega for an option's time value?

They are linked through the BSM PDE: theta + 0.5*sigma^2*S^2*gamma + r*S*delta - r*V = 0. The key intuition is the gamma-theta tradeoff: positive gamma (good — you profit from moves) is paid for by negative theta (bad — you bleed time value). A delta-neutral long-gamma book earns from realized volatility but pays theta; it is profitable only if realized vol exceeds the implied vol it paid.

**Q566.** Define rho and say when it matters.

Rho is the sensitivity of value to the risk-free rate: rho = dV/dr, quoted per 1 percentage-point change. For a call rho = K*T*e^(-r*T)*N(d2) > 0; for a put rho = -K*T*e^(-r*T)*N(-d2) < 0. Rho matters most for long-dated options (LEAPS) and in high-rate environments; for typical short-dated equity options it is the least important Greek.

**Q567.** Walk through delta hedging and why it must be dynamic.

To hedge a sold call you buy delta shares; the position is then insensitive to small spot moves. But because gamma is non-zero, delta changes as spot moves and as time passes, so you must continuously rebalance — buy more shares as spot rises, sell as it falls. In a perfect BSM world this continuous re-hedging exactly replicates the option, and the cost of replication equals the BSM premium.

**Q568.** What is gamma scalping?

Gamma scalping is the trading strategy of holding a long-gamma, delta-neutral position (e.g. long a straddle, delta-hedged) and profiting from realized volatility. As spot moves, your delta drifts; you rebalance by selling shares after rallies and buying after dips — mechanically "buy low, sell high" — locking in small profits. These scalping gains must beat the theta you pay; you win when realized vol > implied vol.

**Q569.** Spell out the gamma-theta tradeoff with a quick example.

Long gamma earns money from movement but bleeds theta; short gamma collects theta but loses on big moves. Roughly, the daily P&L of a delta-hedged book is about 0.5*gamma*(dS)^2 - theta_per_day. If a stock with gamma giving a 0.5*gamma*S^2 of, say, a 0.01 daily variance equivalent earns more from the squared move than the day's theta cost, the long-gamma trader profits; otherwise theta wins.

**Q570.** Summarize the sign of each Greek for a long call versus a long put.

Long call: delta + (0 to 1), gamma +, vega +, theta - (usually), rho +. Long put: delta - (-1 to 0), gamma +, vega +, theta - (usually), rho -. Gamma and vega are positive for any long option (call or put); shorting any option flips all signs. The asymmetry is in delta and rho, which carry the directional and rate-direction information.

**Q571.** What is vanna?

Vanna is a second-order Greek measuring how delta changes when volatility changes (or equivalently how vega changes when spot changes): vanna = d2V/(dS dsigma). It is important for risk-managing skew, because it links your directional exposure to vol moves. Books with significant vanna can see their delta shift purely from a change in implied vol, even with spot unchanged.

**Q572.** What is volga (vomma)?

Volga, also called vomma, is the second derivative of value with respect to volatility: volga = d2V/dsigma2, i.e. how vega changes as vol changes. It captures the convexity of the option in vol and is central to pricing volatility-of-volatility and the smile. Long options that are away-from-the-money tend to have high positive volga, which benefits from large swings in implied vol.

**Q573.** What is charm?

Charm (delta decay) measures how delta changes with the passage of time: charm = d(delta)/dt = d2V/(dS dt). It tells a hedger how their delta will drift overnight or over a weekend even if spot does not move, which matters for setting hedges before non-trading periods. ATM options near expiry have the largest charm, so hedges can go stale quickly as time passes.

**Q574.** How do the main Greeks change as an option approaches expiry?

Gamma and theta both spike sharply for at-the-money options as T -> 0 (delta flips between 0 and 1 over a tiny spot range, and time value decays fastest). Vega shrinks toward zero because there is little time left for vol to act. Delta tends toward 0 or 1 (a step function), and rho also shrinks as the discounting horizon collapses.

**Q575.** How do Greeks change as volatility rises?

Higher sigma widens the terminal price distribution: vega is positive so option values rise, and OTM options gain the most in relative terms. Higher vol pushes deltas of OTM/ITM options back toward 0.5 (more uncertainty about ending side of the strike), spreads gamma over a wider spot range (lowering the ATM peak), and increases time value, making theta (in absolute terms) larger. In short, vol "smears" the Greek profiles across strikes.

**Q576.** Describe the Cox-Ross-Rubinstein (CRR) binomial model.

CRR discretizes time into N steps where each step the stock moves up by factor u = e^(sigma*sqrt(dt)) or down by d = 1/u, with risk-neutral up-probability p = (e^(r*dt) - d)/(u - d). You build the price tree forward, then discount expected payoffs backward step by step at the risk-free rate. It is flexible enough to price American options and discrete dividends, which closed-form BSM cannot handle directly.

**Q577.** How does the binomial model relate to BSM?

As the number of steps N goes to infinity (dt -> 0), the CRR binomial price converges to the BSM price for a European option — the discrete lognormal tree approaches continuous geometric Brownian motion. CRR is essentially a numerical scheme that, in the limit, reproduces the closed-form BSM value. This convergence is why binomial trees are taught as the intuitive bridge to BSM.

**Q578.** Why can't BSM price American options directly, and how is it handled?

BSM assumes European exercise (only at expiry), but American options can be exercised early, adding an optimal-stopping decision BSM's formula ignores. American puts (and calls on dividend-paying stocks) can be worth exercising early, so they need numerical methods — binomial/trinomial trees, finite differences, or least-squares Monte Carlo. Note an American call on a non-dividend stock is never optimally exercised early, so it equals its European value (and BSM applies).

**Q579.** What is the volatility smile/skew, and why does it contradict BSM?

BSM assumes a single constant sigma, which would imply identical implied vol across all strikes. In reality, plotting implied vol against strike shows a smile or (for equity indices) a downward skew, with OTM puts priced at higher implied vols. This reflects fat tails and crash fear — markets price more probability into extreme down-moves than the lognormal assumption allows — directly violating BSM's constant-vol and lognormal assumptions.

**Q580.** Name the main situations where BSM breaks down in practice.

BSM struggles with: (1) American early exercise (needs trees/numerical methods); (2) fat tails and jumps — real returns have more extreme moves than lognormal predicts; (3) the volatility smile/skew — vol is not constant across strikes; (4) stochastic and time-varying volatility; (5) transaction costs and discrete (not continuous) hedging; and (6) discrete dividends and changing rates. Practitioners patch these with local/stochastic-vol models, jump-diffusion, and implied-vol surfaces.

**Q581.** State put-call parity and how it constrains the Greeks of calls and puts.

Put-call parity is C - P = S*e^(-q*T) - K*e^(-r*T) (or C - P = S - K*e^(-r*T) with no dividends). Differentiating gives Greek relationships: call_delta - put_delta = e^(-q*T) (so for q=0, delta_call - delta_put = 1); gamma and vega are identical for a matched call and put; and theta/rho differ only by the derivatives of the deterministic parity terms. This is why a call and put on the same strike/expiry share their gamma and vega exposures exactly.

<a name="E3"></a>

## E3. Implied Volatility, the Smile & Vol Trading  <span class="qrange">(Q582-Q601)</span>

**Q582.** What is the difference between implied volatility and realized (historical) volatility?

Realized (historical) volatility is backward-looking: it is the actual standard deviation of past returns, e.g. computed as the annualized stdev of daily log returns. Implied volatility (IV) is forward-looking: it is the volatility input embedded in the current market price of an option, i.e. the market's expectation of future volatility over the option's life. They often differ — IV typically trades above realized vol (a "variance risk premium") because option sellers demand compensation for taking on risk.

**Q583.** What exactly is implied volatility?

Implied volatility is the value of sigma that, when plugged into the Black-Scholes-Merton (BSM) formula, makes the model price equal the observed market price of the option. All other BSM inputs (spot S, strike K, time T, rate r, dividend q) are observable, so the option's market price can be "inverted" to back out a single unknown — sigma. It is the market's consensus forecast of annualized volatility expressed in BSM's language, not a directly observed quantity.

**Q584.** Why can't you solve for implied volatility with a closed-form formula?

Because the BSM price is a non-linear, monotonic-but-not-invertible function of sigma — the option price depends on sigma through the cumulative normal N(d1) and N(d2), which cannot be algebraically rearranged to isolate sigma. So IV must be found numerically using iterative root-finding such as Newton-Raphson or bisection. The good news is that price is strictly increasing in sigma (vega > 0), so a unique solution exists for any arbitrage-free price.

**Q585.** How does Newton-Raphson solve for implied volatility?

You iterate IV_{n+1} = IV_n - (model_price(IV_n) - market_price) / vega(IV_n), where vega is the derivative of price with respect to sigma. Starting from a guess (say 20%), each step uses the pricing error divided by vega to correct the estimate, and it converges quadratically — usually 3-5 iterations to high precision. Example: if model price is 4.80 vs market 5.00 and vega is 0.40 (per 1.00 = 100% vol), then IV_{n+1} = IV_n + 0.20/0.40 = IV_n + 0.50 vol points adjustment.

**Q586.** What is the volatility smile?

The volatility smile is the empirical pattern that implied volatility varies with strike (or moneyness) rather than being constant as BSM assumes. When you plot IV against strike, out-of-the-money puts and calls often show higher IV than at-the-money options, producing a U or "smile" shape. It exists because real return distributions have fatter tails and are not lognormal, so the market prices tail strikes richer than a flat-vol BSM would.

**Q587.** What is volatility skew and how does it differ from a smile?

Skew refers to an asymmetric smile where IV is systematically higher on one side of the strike range than the other. Equity indices show a downward (negative) skew: low strikes (downside puts) carry much higher IV than high strikes. A symmetric smile implies the market sees roughly equal fat-tail risk on both sides, whereas skew implies asymmetric crash/tail expectations.

**Q588.** Why do equity index options exhibit a pronounced downward skew?

Three reinforcing reasons: (1) crash-o-phobia — after 1987, markets price a higher probability of sudden large down moves than up moves; (2) the leverage effect — as equity prices fall, firms' debt/equity ratios rise, increasing volatility, so down moves and higher vol are correlated; and (3) supply/demand — institutions persistently buy downside puts for portfolio insurance, bidding up low-strike IV. The Nifty and Bank Nifty options on the NSE show this same negative skew.

**Q589.** Why do some assets (like FX or certain commodities) show a more symmetric smile?

For currencies, a large move in either direction is roughly equally likely and equally feared — a sharp appreciation or depreciation both create tail risk — so demand for OTM puts and calls is more balanced, producing a near-symmetric smile. Equities skew because the downside is the feared, leverage-amplified direction; FX has no such asymmetry between "up" and "down." Some commodities can even show a reverse (upward) skew when supply shocks make spikes the feared tail.

**Q590.** What is the term structure of volatility?

It is how implied volatility varies with time to maturity for a fixed strike or moneyness (typically ATM). In calm markets it usually slopes upward (longer maturities price more vol as uncertainty accumulates and mean-reverts toward a long-run level); in stress it inverts, with near-term IV spiking above long-dated IV because the shock is expected to be acute but temporary. It reflects the market's expected path of volatility over time.

**Q591.** What is the implied volatility surface?

The IV surface is a 3D plot of implied volatility as a function of two axes — strike (or moneyness/delta) and time to maturity. A cross-section at fixed maturity gives the smile/skew; a cross-section at fixed strike gives the term structure. Traders use the whole surface to price and risk-manage exotic and vanilla books consistently, and a well-behaved surface must be free of arbitrage in both the strike and time directions.

**Q592.** What is a 25-delta risk reversal and what does it measure?

A 25-delta risk reversal is the IV of the 25-delta call minus the IV of the 25-delta put (RR = IV(25d call) - IV(25d put)). It quantifies the skew/asymmetry of the smile: a negative risk reversal means puts are bid over calls (typical for equities), signalling downside fear. It is the standard FX-market measure of skew and is quoted directly as a tradable structure (long call / short put at equal delta).

**Q593.** What is a 25-delta butterfly and what does it measure?

The 25-delta butterfly is BF = 0.5*(IV(25d call) + IV(25d put)) - IV(ATM), the average of the two 25-delta wing vols minus the at-the-money vol. It measures the convexity (curvature) of the smile — how much the wings are bid relative to the center, i.e. how fat the market prices both tails. Together, ATM vol, the risk reversal, and the butterfly fully parameterize the smile at a given maturity.

**Q594.** What does the VIX measure and why is it called the "fear gauge"?

The VIX is the CBOE Volatility Index: the market's expected 30-day implied volatility of the S&P 500, expressed in annualized percentage points. It is computed model-free from a strip of OTM SPX option prices (a variance-swap-style weighted sum across strikes), not from a single BSM inversion. It is called the fear gauge because it spikes when investors rush to buy protection during sell-offs — VIX above ~30 signals stress, below ~15 signals calm.

**Q595.** What is India VIX and how does it relate to the VIX?

India VIX is the NSE's volatility index, measuring the expected 30-day implied volatility of the Nifty 50 from near- and next-month Nifty options, using the same CBOE-style model-free methodology. It is widely watched as India's fear gauge: it jumps around events like Union Budget, RBI policy, and election results. Like the VIX, it is annualized in percent and tends to be negatively correlated with the underlying index.

**Q596.** What is the difference between IV rank and IV percentile?

IV rank measures where current IV sits between its 52-week low and high: IV Rank = (IV_now - IV_low) / (IV_high - IV_low) * 100. IV percentile measures the fraction of days over the lookback during which IV was below the current level. IV rank is sensitive to a single extreme spike (it anchors on the min/max), while IV percentile better reflects the typical distribution; an IV rank of 50 with a percentile of 80 tells you IV is mid-range but has rarely been this high day-to-day.

**Q597.** What is vega and how do volatility traders use it to express views?

Vega is the option's sensitivity to a 1-percentage-point change in implied volatility (dPrice/dsigma); for example a vega of 0.12 means the option gains about 0.12 if IV rises by 1 point. A trader who thinks vol is too cheap buys options (long vega); one who thinks vol is too rich sells options (short vega). Vega is largest for at-the-money, longer-dated options, so vol views are often expressed with ATM straddles or variance swaps to get clean vega exposure with minimal directional delta.

**Q598.** Explain sticky-strike versus sticky-delta (sticky-moneyness) regimes.

Sticky-strike assumes each strike keeps its IV as the spot moves, so an option's IV stays put while its moneyness changes — common in quiet, range-bound markets. Sticky-delta (sticky-moneyness) assumes the IV attached to a given delta/moneyness stays fixed, so the whole smile slides with the spot — common in trending markets. The assumption matters for hedging because it changes the effective delta: under sticky-delta the skew adds to the BSM delta, altering how many shares you hedge with.

**Q599.** What no-arbitrage constraints must a volatility surface satisfy?

Two key families: (1) the calendar-spread (term) constraint — total implied variance sigma^2*T must be non-decreasing in maturity for a fixed moneyness, otherwise a calendar arbitrage exists; and (2) the butterfly constraint — the implied risk-neutral density from the strike dimension must be non-negative, which requires call prices to be convex and monotonic in strike. Violations (e.g. IV curving too sharply, or near-dated variance exceeding far-dated) imply free lunches, so quants fit arbitrage-free parameterizations like SVI.

**Q600.** Why is selling options described as "short vol" and "short gamma"?

When you sell an option you are short vega (you lose if IV rises) — hence short vol. You are also short gamma: a delta-hedged short option position must buy as the underlying rises and sell as it falls (buy high, sell low), so realized movement bleeds money via negative gamma P&L. The short option earns positive theta (time decay) to compensate; the position profits when realized volatility comes in below the IV you sold, and loses when the market moves more than priced.

**Q601.** What is the difference between variance and volatility, and why does volatility mean-revert?

Volatility is sigma (standard deviation of returns); variance is sigma^2. The distinction matters because variance is additive over time and is what variance swaps and the VIX actually trade — payoffs are linear in variance but non-linear (concave) in vol, so a vol position has convexity. Implied (and realized) volatility mean-reverts: it oscillates around a long-run average, spiking in crises and decaying back, which is why models like Heston and GARCH include a mean-reversion term and why traders fade extreme IV rank readings expecting normalization.

<a name="E4"></a>

## E4. Option Strategies & Quant Math  <span class="qrange">(Q602-Q616)</span>

**Q602.** What is a covered call, and what market view does it express?

A covered call means you hold the underlying (long stock or futures) and sell one call against it, usually slightly out-of-the-money. The view is mildly bullish to neutral: you expect the stock to stay flat or rise modestly, and you collect the premium as extra yield. Max profit = (strike - purchase price) + premium received, capped if the stock rallies above the strike; max loss is large (stock can fall to zero, cushioned only by the premium); breakeven = purchase price - premium. On NSE this is a common Nifty/Bank Nifty F&O overlay where investors write monthly calls on a held position to earn premium income.

**Q603.** What is a protective put and when would you use it?

A protective put is long underlying plus a long put, acting like an insurance policy on your position. The view is bullish but nervous: you want upside exposure while capping downside, so you pay a premium to floor your losses at the strike. Max loss = (purchase price - put strike) + premium paid; max profit is unlimited (stock can keep rising) minus the premium cost; breakeven = purchase price + premium. Indian portfolio managers buy Nifty puts as a hedge before events like the Union Budget or RBI policy to protect against a market drop.

**Q604.** Construct a bull call spread and state its risk profile.

A bull call spread buys a lower-strike call and sells a higher-strike call of the same expiry, financing part of the cost with the short call. The view is moderately bullish with defined risk: you profit if the underlying rises toward the higher strike but you cap both gain and loss. Max profit = (K_high - K_low) - net premium paid; max loss = net premium paid; breakeven = K_low + net premium. Example on Nifty: buy 24000 call at 200, sell 24200 call at 120, net debit 80; max profit = 200 - 80 = 120, max loss = 80, breakeven = 24080.

**Q605.** How does a bear put spread work and what is its payoff?

A bear put spread buys a higher-strike put and sells a lower-strike put of the same expiry, a defined-risk way to play a moderate fall. The view is moderately bearish: you profit as the underlying declines toward the lower strike, with capped gain and capped loss. Max profit = (K_high - K_low) - net premium paid; max loss = net premium paid; breakeven = K_high - net premium. It is the mirror image of the bull call spread and is cheaper than buying a naked put because the short put offsets premium.

**Q606.** What is a long straddle, and when is it profitable?

A long straddle buys a call and a put at the same strike (usually at-the-money) and expiry. The view is long volatility / direction-agnostic: you expect a big move either way but are unsure of direction, common around earnings or binary events. Max loss = total premium paid (if the stock pins the strike at expiry); max profit is unlimited on the upside and large on the downside; there are two breakevens at strike +/- total premium. You need the realized move to exceed the combined premium, so a long straddle loses if implied volatility was already rich and the stock stays quiet.

**Q607.** How does a long strangle differ from a long straddle?

A long strangle buys an out-of-the-money call and an out-of-the-money put (different strikes, same expiry), so it is cheaper than a straddle but needs a larger move to pay off. The view is still long volatility expecting a big swing. Max loss = total premium paid; max profit is unlimited up / large down; breakevens are at K_call + total premium and K_put - total premium. Because both legs start OTM the upfront cost (and max loss) is lower, but the no-profit zone between the strikes is wider than a straddle's.

**Q608.** Describe a short straddle/strangle and its risk.

Selling a straddle (short ATM call + short ATM put) or a strangle (short OTM call + short OTM put) collects premium and profits when the underlying stays range-bound and implied volatility falls. The view is short volatility / neutral. Max profit = premium received (when the stock pins the strikes); max loss is theoretically unlimited on a large move in either direction; breakevens are strike +/- premium (straddle) or each strike +/- premium (strangle). This is high-risk: a sharp gap (e.g., a Bank Nifty move on RBI surprise) can blow past the premium, which is why SEBI margins for short option positions are heavy.

**Q609.** What is a long butterfly spread and what view does it express?
