A long call butterfly buys one lower-strike call, sells two middle-strike calls, and buys one higher-strike call (equally spaced strikes, same expiry) for a small net debit. The view is range-bound / short volatility: you expect the underlying to expire near the middle strike. Max profit = (wing width) - net premium, achieved at the middle strike; max loss = net premium paid; two breakevens sit at K_low + net premium and K_high - net premium. It is a cheap, fully defined-risk way to bet on a pin, with the lost premium being the only downside.

**Q610.** Explain the iron condor and the risk reversal as two range / skew strategies.

An iron condor sells an OTM put spread and an OTM call spread simultaneously (four legs), collecting net premium; the view is range-bound short volatility, with max profit = net premium received if the underlying stays between the short strikes, and max loss = (spread width - net premium) capped by the long wings. A risk reversal is directional-plus-skew: sell an OTM put and buy an OTM call (bullish version), often for near-zero cost, giving long-stock-like exposure financed by the put premium; max loss is large if the stock falls below the put strike, max profit is unlimited above the call strike. A collar is the conservative cousin of the risk reversal applied to a held position: long stock + long protective put + short covered call, which caps both downside and upside for little or no net premium and is widely used by Indian PMS/HNI clients to lock in a band around a concentrated holding.

**Q611.** What is geometric Brownian motion and why is it the standard model for stock prices?

Geometric Brownian motion (GBM) models the stock as the SDE `dS = mu*S*dt + sigma*S*dW`, where mu is the drift (expected return), sigma the volatility, and dW the increment of a Wiener process. It is "geometric" because returns rather than absolute price levels are random: dividing by S gives `dS/S = mu*dt + sigma*dW`, so percentage moves are stationary. This is preferred because it keeps prices strictly positive and produces lognormal prices with proportional volatility, matching how equity returns actually behave; it is the engine behind Black-Scholes.

**Q612.** What are the defining properties of a Wiener process (standard Brownian motion)?

A Wiener process W(t) satisfies: W(0) = 0; it has independent increments; each increment is normally distributed with `W(t) - W(s) ~ Normal(0, t-s)` (variance grows linearly with time); and its paths are continuous but nowhere differentiable. A key scaling consequence is that `dW` has standard deviation `sqrt(dt)`, i.e., E[dW] = 0 and E[dW^2] = dt, which is why volatility scales with `sqrt(T)` (the square-root-of-time rule). These properties make W the canonical source of continuous-time randomness in finance.

**Q613.** Apply Ito's lemma to S and explain why d(ln S) carries a -0.5*sigma^2 term.

Ito's lemma is the stochastic chain rule: for f(S) it adds a second-order term, `df = (df/dS) dS + 0.5*(d2f/dS2) (dS)^2`, where crucially `(dW)^2 = dt`. Applying it to f = ln S with `dS = mu*S*dt + sigma*S*dW` gives `d(ln S) = (mu - 0.5*sigma^2) dt + sigma*dW`. The -0.5*sigma^2 (the Ito correction) appears because ln is concave and `(dS)^2 = sigma^2 * S^2 * dt` is non-zero in stochastic calculus; intuitively, volatility drags down the growth rate of the log because gains and losses are not symmetric in compounded returns.

**Q614.** Why are stock prices lognormal while log-returns are normal?

From `d(ln S) = (mu - 0.5*sigma^2) dt + sigma*dW`, the log of the price is normally distributed, so the continuously-compounded return `ln(S_T/S_0)` is `Normal((mu - 0.5*sigma^2)T, sigma^2 * T)`. Because the price itself is the exponential of a normal variable, `S_T = S_0 * exp((mu - 0.5*sigma^2)T + sigma*sqrt(T)*Z)`, S_T follows a lognormal distribution: skewed right and bounded below at zero. This is economically sensible, prices cannot go negative and a stock can multiply many-fold but only fall to zero, which is exactly the asymmetry a lognormal captures.

**Q615.** How does Monte-Carlo simulation price a derivative, and how fast does it converge?

Monte-Carlo simulates many price paths under the risk-neutral GBM `S_T = S_0 * exp((r - 0.5*sigma^2)T + sigma*sqrt(T)*Z)`, computes the payoff on each path (e.g., max(S_T - K, 0) for a call), averages them, and discounts: `price = exp(-r*T) * (1/N) * sum(payoff_i)`. Its statistical error shrinks as `1/sqrt(N)`, so cutting the error in half requires roughly 4x the paths, slow but dimension-independent, which makes it the go-to method for path-dependent and high-dimensional options (Asian, basket, barrier) where closed forms do not exist. Variance-reduction tricks like antithetic variates and control variates are used to tighten the estimate without simply quadrupling N.

**Q616.** What is the difference between the real-world and risk-neutral measures, and why price under risk-neutral?

Under the real-world (physical) measure P the stock drifts at its true expected return mu, reflecting investors' risk premium; under the risk-neutral measure Q the drift is replaced by the risk-free rate r, so `dS = r*S*dt + sigma*S*dW` and all assets earn r on average. We price derivatives under Q because, by the fundamental theorem of asset pricing, the absence of arbitrage means a discounted price is a martingale under Q, giving `price = exp(-r*T) * E_Q[payoff]`, a value that does not depend on anyone's risk appetite. The real-world measure is used for risk management, forecasting and VaR, while the risk-neutral measure is used purely for consistent, arbitrage-free valuation; only the drift changes between them, the volatility sigma is the same.

<a name="E5"></a>

## E5. Advanced Derivatives & Quantitative Finance  <span class="qrange">(Q617-Q646)</span>

**Q617.** What distinguishes a path-dependent option from a path-independent one, and give examples of each?

A path-independent option's payoff depends only on the underlying's level at expiry (or exercise), not the route taken - a vanilla European call paying max(S_T - K, 0) is the canonical example. A path-dependent option's payoff depends on the trajectory: Asian options use the average price over the life, barrier options depend on whether a level was touched, and lookbacks use the running max/min. Path dependence usually rules out simple closed forms and pushes you toward trees, PDEs, or Monte Carlo.

**Q618.** What is an Asian (average) option, why is it cheaper than a vanilla, and who uses it?

An Asian option's payoff uses the average underlying over a set of dates rather than the single expiry price - an average-rate call pays max(A - K, 0) where A is the arithmetic mean of observed prices. Averaging lowers effective volatility, so the option is cheaper than a vanilla and is robust to a single-day spike or expiry-day manipulation. Treasurers and commodity desks use them to hedge a stream of regular transactions (e.g. a fuel buyer hedging monthly purchases) where the average cost is what they actually pay; note the geometric-average version has a closed form while the arithmetic one needs Monte Carlo.

**Q619.** Define knock-in versus knock-out barrier options, the parity linking them to vanillas, and why their Greeks are dangerous.

A knock-out starts alive and dies if the underlying touches the barrier (an up-and-out call dies if S hits H above spot); a knock-in only springs into existence once the barrier is touched, and for the same terms knock-in + knock-out = the vanilla. Barriers are cheaper than vanillas because you forgo payoff in some scenarios, which is why FX desks and structured notes love them. Near the barrier the value can jump to zero over a tiny move, so delta and gamma spike and flip sign, making delta-hedging extremely costly - dealers manage this with barrier shifting or vanilla over-hedges.

**Q620.** What is a lookback option and why is it expensive?

A lookback's payoff references the best price reached over the life: a floating-strike lookback call pays S_T - min(S), effectively letting you buy at the low, while a fixed-strike version pays max(max(S) - K, 0). Because you always capture the optimal historical price, lookbacks embed perfect market-timing you could never achieve, so they are expensive and mostly a structuring/teaching tool rather than a liquid product.

**Q621.** Explain a digital (binary) option and how desks actually hedge its blow-up risk near the strike.

A cash-or-nothing digital pays a fixed amount (say 1) if S_T > K and zero otherwise, so its payoff is a step function. As expiry nears, delta becomes a tall narrow spike at K and gamma flips sign violently around it. Dealers therefore replicate and hedge a digital with a tight vanilla call spread - long a call at K-eps, short at K+eps, scaled by 1/(2*eps) - which caps the local risk and is what really gets traded.

**Q622.** What is a chooser option and how does it decompose?

A chooser lets the holder decide at a future date t whether the option becomes a call or a put (same strike K, expiry T). At the choice date a rational holder takes max(C, P), so via put-call parity a simple chooser equals a call expiring at T plus a put expiring at the earlier choice date t. It suits a buyer expecting a big move of uncertain direction and is cheaper than buying an outright straddle.

**Q623.** What is a compound option and where do compound options arise naturally?

A compound option is an option on an option - e.g. a call-on-a-call gives the right to buy a call for a preset premium at the first expiry, so it has two strikes and two dates. They appear in real-options analysis (a phased capital project is a sequence of options on options), in hedging contingent deals where the underlying hedge may or may not be needed, and in installment-premium structures. They are very sensitive to volatility, exhibiting strong gamma-of-vega.

**Q624.** What is a basket option and why is correlation its key risk?

A basket option pays off on a weighted portfolio of underlyings, e.g. max(w1*S1 + w2*S2 + ... - K, 0). Its price hinges on pairwise correlations because basket variance = sum over i,j of w_i*w_j*sigma_i*sigma_j*rho_ij; higher correlation raises basket vol and the price. Buyers use baskets to express a view on a group (an index, an FX basket) more cheaply than buying options on each name, precisely because diversification lowers basket vol.

**Q625.** Derive the risk-neutral up-probability in a binomial tree and state the CRR choice of u and d.

Risk-neutral pricing requires the discounted expected stock to equal today's price: S = e^(-r*dt)*(p*S*u + (1-p)*S*d), giving p = (e^(r*dt) - d) / (u - d), which must satisfy d < e^(r*dt) < u so 0 < p < 1. Cox-Ross-Rubinstein sets u = e^(sigma*sqrt(dt)) and d = 1/u so the tree recombines and matches lognormal variance as dt -> 0. Example: S=100, sigma=20%, dt=1y, r=5% gives u=1.221, d=0.819, p=(1.0513-0.819)/(1.221-0.819)=0.578; p is a pricing measure, not the real-world probability.

**Q626.** How does a binomial tree price an American option, and when is early exercise actually optimal?

You roll backward from expiry taking value = max(immediate exercise payoff, e^(-r*dt)*(p*V_up + (1-p)*V_down)) at every node, comparing intrinsic value to continuation value - backward induction is required because a node's continuation value needs its children's already-computed values. This early-exercise test is exactly what Black-Scholes cannot capture. Early exercise is never optimal for an American call on a non-dividend stock (you would forfeit time value and interest on the strike), but can pay for a deep in-the-money American put or a call just before a large dividend.

**Q627.** What does a trinomial tree add over a binomial tree?

A trinomial tree allows three moves per step (up, unchanged, down), giving an extra degree of freedom that improves stability and convergence and maps directly onto an explicit finite-difference scheme. The middle branch lets you align nodes with specific levels - invaluable for barrier options and for interest-rate models like Hull-White - and fit the local drift/vol more flexibly than a binomial lattice.

**Q628.** Why is Monte Carlo natural for exotics, and why does its error shrink only like 1/sqrt(N)?

Monte Carlo simulates many risk-neutral paths, averages the payoffs, and discounts: price = e^(-r*T)*mean(payoff); it handles path dependence and high dimensions (baskets, many dates) trivially where trees suffer the curse of dimensionality. By the Central Limit Theorem the standard error is sigma_payoff/sqrt(N), so error falls as 1/sqrt(N) - halving it needs 4x the paths and one extra decimal needs 100x. Its weakness is early exercise (needs Longstaff-Schwartz regression) and this slow convergence, which is why variance reduction matters.

**Q629.** Explain antithetic and control variates as variance-reduction techniques.

Antithetic variates pair each random draw Z with -Z; the two payoffs are negatively correlated, so Var((X+Y)/2) = (Var(X)+Var(Y)+2Cov(X,Y))/4 shrinks when Cov is negative - essentially free and best for monotonic payoffs. A control variate uses a correlated quantity with a known analytic price: estimator = MC(target) - b*(MC(control) - TrueValue(control)), with optimal b = Cov(target,control)/Var(control). For an arithmetic Asian the geometric Asian is the classic control because it is highly correlated and has a closed form, often cutting variance by an order of magnitude.

**Q630.** What are finite-difference methods conceptually, and how do explicit and implicit schemes differ?

Finite-difference methods solve the Black-Scholes PDE directly by discretizing price and time onto a grid, approximating derivatives with differences, and stepping backward from the payoff boundary. The explicit scheme is simple but only stable under a tight time-step condition (like a trinomial tree); implicit and Crank-Nicolson schemes solve a linear system each step but are unconditionally stable and more accurate. FDM excels for low-dimensional American and barrier problems where you want the full price surface and clean Greeks.

**Q631.** Why do models beyond Black-Scholes exist - what does the volatility smile prove?

Black-Scholes assumes one constant volatility, which would imply a flat implied-vol curve across strikes; the market instead shows a smile/skew (out-of-the-money equity puts priced richer). This proves the real return distribution has fatter tails and negative skew than lognormal, so a single sigma cannot price all strikes consistently. Hence local-vol, stochastic-vol, and jump models exist to reproduce the observed smile and price exotics consistently with vanillas.

**Q632.** Contrast local volatility (Dupire) with stochastic volatility models.

Local volatility makes sigma a deterministic function sigma(S,t) calibrated to fit today's entire vanilla surface exactly via the Dupire equation, but it predicts unrealistic, flattening future smile dynamics. Stochastic volatility makes variance itself a random process with its own vol-of-vol and spot correlation, capturing the realistic forward skew that matters for exotics and forward-starting products. Desks often use local-stochastic-vol (LSV) hybrids to get both an exact fit and sensible dynamics.

**Q633.** What do the Heston and SABR stochastic-volatility models add, and what do their parameters control?

Heston models variance as a mean-reverting CIR process dv = kappa*(theta - v)*dt + xi*sqrt(v)*dW2 correlated (rho) with spot: kappa is reversion speed, theta the long-run variance, xi the vol-of-vol setting smile convexity, and rho (negative for equities) the skew - it has a semi-closed form for vanillas making calibration fast. SABR models a forward as dF = alpha*F^beta*dW1, d(alpha) = nu*alpha*dW2: alpha sets the vol level, beta the backbone (0 normal, 1 lognormal), rho the skew, nu the curvature. SABR is the market standard for swaption smiles because Hagan's formula gives implied vol per strike directly.

**Q634.** What does Merton's jump-diffusion model capture that pure diffusion cannot?

Merton adds a compound-Poisson jump term to geometric Brownian motion, so the stock can gap: dS/S = (mu - lambda*k)*dt + sigma*dW + (J-1)*dN with jump intensity lambda. Jumps generate the steep short-dated skew and fat tails that pure diffusion cannot (diffusion smiles flatten at short maturities), and they make perfect delta-hedging impossible, so markets become incomplete. This matters for crash risk, gap risk on barriers, and overnight/earnings moves.

**Q635.** Explain delta-hedging P&L, the gamma-theta relationship and the breakeven daily move, and how transaction costs alter it.

For a delta-hedged book the P&L over a small interval is roughly 0.5*gamma*(dS)^2 + theta*dt - long gamma earns on big moves while theta bleeds time value - so the two break even at dS ~ S*sigma*sqrt(dt), about one implied standard deviation. Example: S=20000, sigma=15%, dt=1/252 gives breakeven ~ 20000*0.15*sqrt(1/252) ~ 189 points/day; move more and long gamma wins, less and theta wins. In reality you rehedge discretely, so transaction costs act like a volatility add-on (Leland) that scales with sqrt(rehedge frequency), and bid-ask plus impact can erode the theoretical realized-minus-implied-vol edge entirely.

**Q636.** Define vega, vanna, and volga and why a book must watch all three.

Vega is sensitivity to volatility (dV/dsigma); vanna is the cross term dVega/dSpot (equivalently dDelta/dsigma); and volga (vomma) is dVega/dsigma, the convexity of value in vol. A vega-neutral book can still bleed when spot moves (vanna) or when vol moves a lot (volga), so smile-risk management requires hedging all three - this underpins the vanna-volga pricing method in FX. Ignoring vanna/volga is why naive vega hedges fail in skewed, moving markets.

**Q637.** How does a variance swap pay off and how is it replicated with a strip of options?

A variance swap pays notional*(realized_variance - K_var), where realized variance is the annualized sum of squared log returns, giving pure variance exposure with no path-dependent delta. It is replicated statically by a portfolio of out-of-the-money calls and puts weighted by 1/K^2 across all strikes, plus a dynamic delta hedge; the fair strike is essentially the 1/K^2-weighted integral of option prices (the model-free basis for the VIX). The 1/K^2 weighting is precisely what makes the payoff track variance regardless of where spot goes.

**Q638.** How does a volatility swap differ from a variance swap, and why is the variance swap easier to hedge?

A variance swap settles on realized variance (vol squared) while a volatility swap settles on realized volatility itself, notional*(realized_vol - K_vol). The variance swap has clean static replication via the 1/K^2 option strip, but a vol swap's payoff is the concave square root of variance, so by Jensen's inequality its fair strike sits below sqrt(variance strike) by a convexity adjustment and it cannot be statically replicated. That vol-of-vol convexity is an extra risk that makes vol swaps harder to hedge.

**Q639.** What is dispersion trading and what view does it express?

Dispersion trading sells index volatility/variance and buys volatility on the constituent single names (or vice versa), profiting when realized correlation differs from the implied correlation embedded in index vol. Since index variance = sum of weighted single-name variances plus correlation terms, the trade is essentially short implied correlation - it makes money when stocks move idiosyncratically while index vol stays subdued. The main risk is a correlation spike in a sell-off, when everything moves together and the short-index-vol leg loses badly.

**Q640.** Decompose a capital-guaranteed (principal-protected) note into options.

A capital-guaranteed note is a zero-coupon bond plus a long call: the issuer puts most of the principal into a discount bond that accretes to par (guaranteeing capital) and spends the residual buying upside via a call, often capped or with a participation rate. Example: at 5% rates, ~78 of 100 buys a 5y zero maturing at 100, leaving ~22 to buy index calls; the participation rate is whatever option exposure that budget funds. This is why low rates make these notes far less attractive - less premium is left for the call.

**Q641.** Decompose a reverse convertible and an autocallable into options.

A reverse convertible is a high-coupon note where the investor is effectively short a put: you receive an enhanced coupon for selling downside protection and are repaid in shares (taking the loss) if the underlying breaches a level at maturity. An autocallable embeds a short down-and-in put plus a series of digital/barrier coupons with an automatic early-redemption feature - it pays attractive coupons and redeems early if the underlying is above a trigger on observation dates, but exposes the buyer to sharp losses through the protection barrier. Both are yield-enhancement products that sell tail risk to coupon-hungry investors.

**Q642.** What is a quanto option and what extra risk does it introduce?

A quanto pays off on a foreign-currency underlying but settles in the domestic currency at a fixed exchange rate - e.g. an option on the S&P 500 paying in INR at a pre-agreed rate, removing FX risk for the rupee investor. Pricing requires a quanto drift adjustment: the foreign asset's drift shifts by -rho*sigma_S*sigma_FX because the payoff is exposed to the asset-FX covariance. The key extra risk is therefore the unobservable asset-FX correlation, which must be estimated and hedged.

**Q643.** What is CVA and how does counterparty risk enter OTC derivative pricing?

Credit Valuation Adjustment is the market value of counterparty default risk - the amount you mark down a derivative's risk-free value because the counterparty might default when you are in-the-money. Conceptually CVA = integral over time of (loss-given-default * expected positive exposure * marginal default probability), discounted, so it depends on the counterparty's credit spread and your future exposure profile. Post-2008, XVA desks price CVA (plus DVA, FVA, MVA, KVA) into every OTC trade, which is why uncollateralized trades cost more than cleared/collateralized ones.

**Q644.** How do you compute and manage the Greeks of a whole derivatives portfolio?

Because the Greeks are partial derivatives of value, they add across positions on a common underlying: portfolio delta = sum of (position delta * quantity), and likewise for gamma, vega, theta. A desk nets these to a few aggregate numbers and hedges the residual - trade the underlying to zero delta, then use options to flatten gamma and vega within limits. The subtlety is that vega across strikes/expiries is not truly fungible (smile and term-structure risk), so books bucket vega by tenor and strike rather than summing one number.

**Q645.** Distinguish risk-neutral from real-world probabilities and explain why pricing uses the risk-neutral measure.

Real-world (physical) probabilities include a risk premium and are used for forecasting, P&L distributions, and risk metrics like VaR. Risk-neutral probabilities are an artificial measure under which all assets drift at the risk-free rate, so a derivative's price equals the discounted risk-neutral expected payoff - this holds because it enforces no-arbitrage via replication, not because investors are actually risk-neutral. The two are linked by the market price of risk (Girsanov): you price with risk-neutral but forecast and size risk with real-world.

**Q646.** Why does gamma explode near expiry for at-the-money index options, and what does it mean for weekly Nifty/Bank Nifty expiries?

For an at-the-money option, gamma scales roughly as 1/(S*sigma*sqrt(T)), so as T -> 0 gamma blows up and tiny spot moves cause huge delta swings. On NSE weekly expiries, dealers short these cheap near-dated options carry enormous gamma, and their delta-hedging forces large index buying/selling that can pin price near big open-interest strikes or amplify intraday moves on expiry day. This is why expiry sessions see sharp, mean-reverting moves around key strikes and why short-gamma sellers can be badly hurt by a single large move.

<a name="secF"></a>

# F. Investment Banking, M&A & LBO

<a name="F1"></a>

## F1. Mergers & Acquisitions  <span class="qrange">(Q647-Q676)</span>

**Q647.** What is the difference between a merger, an acquisition, and a takeover?

A merger is the combination of two companies into one new or surviving entity, often framed as a "merger of equals" with mutual agreement. An acquisition is one company (the acquirer) buying another (the target), with the target ceasing to exist independently or becoming a subsidiary. A takeover is simply an acquisition viewed from the angle of control changing hands; it can be friendly (board-approved) or hostile (pursued over the board's objection, e.g., via a tender offer directly to shareholders).

**Q648.** What are horizontal, vertical, and conglomerate mergers?

A horizontal merger combines two firms in the same industry and stage (e.g., two banks merging) to gain market share and scale. A vertical merger combines firms at different stages of the same supply chain (e.g., a car maker buying a parts supplier) to secure inputs or distribution. A conglomerate merger combines firms in unrelated businesses (e.g., a cement firm buying a telecom company), driven by diversification rather than operational overlap.

**Q649.** What are the main strategic rationales for doing an M&A deal?

The classic motives are: synergies (cost savings and revenue gains from combining), increased market share and pricing power, vertical integration to control the supply chain, geographic or product diversification, acquiring technology/talent/IP faster than building it, and tax benefits (using a target's losses or a more favorable structure). A buyer may also acquire simply to deploy excess cash or because organic growth has stalled.

**Q650.** What are synergies in M&A, and what are the two main types?

Synergies are the incremental value created when the combined firm is worth more than the sum of the two standalone firms. Cost synergies come from eliminating duplicate costs (overlapping staff, facilities, IT, procurement scale). Revenue synergies come from selling more (cross-selling, new geographies, pricing power). Total deal value justification often hinges on `Synergy value = PV of after-tax synergy cash flows - integration costs`.

**Q651.** Why are cost synergies considered more credible than revenue synergies?

Cost synergies are largely within management's control: you can decide to close a plant, cut a duplicate finance team, or consolidate vendors, and the savings are quantifiable and time-bound. Revenue synergies depend on customer behavior, competitive response, and sales execution, all of which are uncertain and slow to materialize. Experienced acquirers and analysts therefore discount or ignore revenue synergies and underwrite deals primarily on cost synergies.

**Q652.** Walk me through the typical M&A deal process from start to close.

On the sell-side, a banker pitches the engagement, then a teaser (anonymous one-pager) goes to buyers, followed by an NDA, then a detailed CIM (Confidential Information Memorandum). Interested buyers submit indicative bids, sign a Letter of Intent (LOI), and conduct due diligence in a data room. The parties negotiate and sign the SPA (Share/Stock Purchase Agreement), obtain regulatory and shareholder approvals, and then close (consideration paid, ownership transfers). Post-close comes integration.

**Q653.** What is a teaser versus a CIM in a sell-side process?

A teaser is a short, anonymous 1-2 page document describing the business at a high level (sector, financial highlights, investment thesis) sent to gauge interest before any confidentiality agreement. A CIM (Confidential Information Memorandum), also called an Information Memorandum (IM), is a detailed 30-100 page document with the company named, full financials, management, operations, and projections, shared only after an NDA is signed.

**Q654.** What is a Letter of Intent (LOI) and is it binding?

An LOI is a preliminary document outlining the key proposed deal terms: indicative price, structure (asset vs stock), exclusivity, and timeline. Most of the LOI is non-binding (price can still change after due diligence), but certain clauses, typically exclusivity/no-shop, confidentiality, and break fees, are binding. It signals serious intent and frames the subsequent definitive agreement (SPA).

**Q655.** What is the difference between an asset purchase and a stock (share) purchase?

In a stock purchase, the buyer acquires the target's shares and takes on the entire legal entity, including all assets, liabilities, and contracts (known and unknown). In an asset purchase, the buyer cherry-picks specific assets and assumed liabilities, leaving unwanted liabilities behind, and gets a stepped-up tax basis on the assets. Sellers usually prefer stock deals (clean exit, often better tax treatment); buyers often prefer asset deals (liability protection, tax step-up).

**Q656.** What is a control premium and why does an acquirer pay it?

A control premium is the amount paid above a target's pre-announcement market price to gain control of the company. `Control premium % = (Offer price per share - Unaffected share price) / Unaffected share price`. Acquirers pay it because control lets them direct strategy, capture synergies, replace management, and access cash flows that minority shareholders cannot; typical premiums run roughly 20-40%, e.g., offering INR 120 for a stock trading at INR 100 is a 20% premium.

**Q657.** How do you choose between cash, stock, and mixed consideration in a deal?

Cash is preferred when the acquirer has the funds or cheap debt, is confident in the deal, and wants to keep 100% of the upside (and avoid diluting EPS). Stock is used when cash/debt is constrained, the acquirer's shares are richly valued (an "expensive currency"), or the buyer wants the seller to share deal risk. Mixed consideration balances the two; the choice also reflects tax (stock deals can be tax-deferred for sellers) and signaling (all-stock can signal the buyer thinks its stock is overvalued).

**Q658.** What does it mean for an acquisition to be accretive or dilutive to EPS?

A deal is accretive if the acquirer's pro-forma EPS (post-deal) is higher than its standalone EPS, and dilutive if it is lower. `Pro-forma EPS = (Acquirer NI + Target NI + after-tax synergies - after-tax financing cost) / pro-forma share count`. Accretion/dilution is a quick first-pass screen of whether a deal "adds to earnings," though it says nothing about whether the deal actually creates value.

**Q659.** What is the rule of thumb for accretion/dilution based on P/E in an all-stock deal?

In an all-stock deal, if the acquirer's P/E is higher than the target's P/E (the price paid), the deal is accretive; if the acquirer's P/E is lower, it is dilutive. Intuitively, you are issuing your high-P/E (cheap-to-issue) shares to buy cheaper low-P/E earnings, which boosts EPS. The break-even is when the two P/Es are equal.

**Q660.** Walk me through a simple all-stock accretion/dilution example.

Acquirer: net income USD 100m, 100m shares, EPS USD 1.00, price USD 20 (P/E 20x). Target: net income USD 20m, bought for USD 240m all-stock (target P/E = 240/20 = 12x). Acquirer issues 240/20 = 12m new shares. Pro-forma NI = 100 + 20 = 120m; pro-forma shares = 112m; pro-forma EPS = 120/112 = USD 1.071. EPS rose from 1.00 to 1.07, so the deal is accretive, consistent with acquirer P/E (20x) > target P/E (12x).

**Q661.** How does financing with cash or debt change accretion/dilution versus stock?

Cash/debt deals are accretive when the after-tax yield given up or paid is lower than the target's earnings yield (E/P). Using cash, you forgo interest income (e.g., 2% after-tax) to buy earnings yielding, say, 8% (12x P/E), so it is usually accretive. Using debt, compare the after-tax cost of debt to the target's earnings yield: `accretive if after-tax cost of debt < target E/P`. Because cash and debt rates are typically far below equity earnings yields, cash/debt deals are more often accretive than stock deals.

**Q662.** Give a worked cash-financed accretion/dilution example with debt.

Acquirer EPS USD 1.00 (NI 100m, 100m shares). It buys a target's net income of USD 20m for USD 240m, funded with debt at 5% pre-tax, tax rate 25%. After-tax interest = 240 * 5% * (1-0.25) = USD 9m. Pro-forma NI = 100 + 20 - 9 = 111m; shares unchanged at 100m; pro-forma EPS = USD 1.11. Accretive, because the target's earnings yield (20/240 = 8.3%) exceeds the after-tax cost of debt (5% * 0.75 = 3.75%).

**Q663.** How is goodwill created in an acquisition?

Goodwill is the excess of the purchase price (equity purchase price / consideration) over the fair value of the target's identifiable net assets acquired. `Goodwill = Purchase price - Fair value of net identifiable assets (incl. identified intangibles)`. For example, paying INR 1,000 cr for a company whose net identifiable assets are fairly valued at INR 700 cr (after writing up assets and booking intangibles) creates INR 300 cr of goodwill on the consolidated balance sheet.

**Q664.** Is goodwill amortized, and how is it treated under IFRS/Ind-AS vs US GAAP?

Under IFRS and Ind-AS, goodwill is not amortized; it is tested for impairment at least annually and written down if impaired. US GAAP also requires impairment testing (private companies may elect amortization). So goodwill typically sits on the balance sheet indefinitely until an impairment charge reduces it, which hits the income statement as a non-cash expense. The old practice of routine goodwill amortization was eliminated in the early 2000s.

**Q665.** How do you value synergies in a deal?

You estimate the incremental after-tax cash flows from synergies (cost savings plus credible revenue gains, minus the costs to achieve them), then discount them to present value, usually at the acquirer's or combined firm's WACC. `Synergy value = sum of [after-tax synergy CF_t / (1+WACC)^t] - PV of integration/restructuring costs`. The maximum justifiable premium is bounded by this synergy value, since paying more than the synergies are worth transfers all the value to the target's shareholders.

**Q666.** What is the difference between a friendly and a hostile takeover?

In a friendly takeover, the target's board negotiates and recommends the deal to its shareholders. In a hostile takeover, the acquirer bypasses or overrides the board, going directly to shareholders, typically via a tender offer (public bid to buy shares) or a proxy fight (to replace directors). Hostile deals are riskier, costlier, and rarer because of due-diligence limitations and entrenched defenses.

**Q667.** What is a poison pill and how does it work?

A poison pill (shareholder rights plan) is a defense that makes a hostile takeover prohibitively expensive by diluting the acquirer. A "flip-in" pill lets all shareholders except the hostile bidder buy new shares at a steep discount once the bidder crosses a trigger threshold (e.g., 15% ownership), massively diluting the bidder's stake. It is designed not to be triggered but to force the bidder to negotiate with the board.

**Q668.** Explain white knight, golden parachute, and staggered board defenses.

A white knight is a friendlier third-party acquirer the target invites to outbid a hostile bidder. A golden parachute is a large severance package for executives triggered by a change of control, raising the deal's cost and aligning management. A staggered (classified) board elects only a fraction of directors each year, so a hostile bidder cannot replace the whole board in one proxy contest, slowing any takeover.

**Q669.** What is an earnout and when is it used?

An earnout is deferred contingent consideration: part of the purchase price is paid later only if the target hits agreed performance targets (revenue, EBITDA, milestones) post-close. It is used to bridge a valuation gap when buyer and seller disagree on future performance, and to retain and motivate selling founders/management. Risks include disputes over how targets are measured and misaligned incentives during the earnout period.

**Q670.** What are the main areas covered in due diligence?

Due diligence verifies the target across multiple dimensions: financial (quality of earnings, working capital, debt), legal (contracts, litigation, title), tax, commercial/market (customers, competition), operational, HR (key staff, pensions), IT/technology, environmental, and regulatory/compliance. The goal is to confirm the investment thesis, uncover hidden liabilities, and inform price, representations/warranties, and indemnities in the SPA.

**Q671.** What is a fairness opinion and who provides it?

A fairness opinion is a formal letter from an independent investment bank stating whether the consideration in a deal is fair, from a financial point of view, to a company's shareholders. The target's board obtains it to support its recommendation and to discharge its fiduciary duty (reducing litigation risk). It is an opinion on price fairness, not a guarantee the deal is the best possible outcome, and the bank is typically paid a fixed fee for it.

**Q672.** What is a minority squeeze-out (freeze-out)?

A squeeze-out is a transaction that compels remaining minority shareholders to sell their shares so the acquirer can reach 100% ownership, usually after crossing a high ownership threshold. It is done via a short-form merger, scheme of arrangement, or delisting, with minority holders receiving cash (often a court- or regulator-supervised fair price). It lets the acquirer fully consolidate, remove public-company costs, and avoid minority veto rights.

**Q673.** Why do studies suggest most M&A deals destroy value for the acquirer?

Acquirers frequently overpay (winner's curse in competitive auctions), pay control premiums that exceed realized synergies, and overestimate revenue synergies while underestimating integration difficulty, culture clash, and customer/employee attrition. The value created by synergies is often handed entirely to target shareholders via the premium, leaving acquirer shareholders worse off. Empirically, the target's stock usually pops on announcement while the acquirer's often falls.

**Q674.** Under India's SEBI Takeover Code (SAST), when is a mandatory open offer triggered?

Under the SEBI (Substantial Acquisition of Shares and Takeovers) Regulations, 2011, an acquirer crossing 25% of voting rights in a listed company must make a mandatory open offer to public shareholders for at least an additional 26% of shares. A separate "creeping acquisition" trigger applies between 25% and the maximum permissible non-public shareholding: acquiring more than 5% in a financial year also triggers an open offer. The open-offer price is set by a formula based on highest negotiated/market prices over defined look-back periods.

**Q675.** What is the role of the CCI in Indian M&A?

The Competition Commission of India (CCI) reviews combinations (mergers/acquisitions) above prescribed asset/turnover thresholds to ensure they do not cause an Appreciable Adverse Effect on Competition (AAEC) in India. Qualifying deals require prior CCI approval before closing; the CCI can approve, approve with modifications/remedies, or block them. India also added a "deal value threshold" (transactions above INR 2,000 cr with substantial Indian operations) bringing large digital/tech acquisitions into the review net.

**Q676.** What is an NCLT scheme of arrangement in Indian M&A?

A scheme of arrangement under Sections 230-232 of the Companies Act, 2013 is a court-supervised process, adjudicated by the National Company Law Tribunal (NCLT), used for mergers, demergers, amalgamations, and capital restructurings. It requires approval by the requisite majority of shareholders and creditors (75% in value), plus sanction from the NCLT, and clearances from regulators (SEBI/stock exchanges for listed firms, RBI, CCI as applicable). Once sanctioned, the scheme is binding on all stakeholders, which is why it is favored for complex restructurings and squeeze-outs.

<a name="F2"></a>

## F2. Leveraged Buyouts & Private Equity  <span class="qrange">(Q677-Q707)</span>

**Q677.** What is a leveraged buyout (LBO)?

An LBO is the acquisition of a company using a large amount of borrowed money (debt) to fund the purchase price, with a relatively small slice of equity from a private equity (PE) sponsor. The acquired company's own assets and cash flows secure and service the debt, so the target effectively pays for its own purchase over time. A typical structure might be 60-70% debt and 30-40% equity, versus an all-equity acquisition.

**Q678.** Why do PE firms use leverage instead of buying companies with all equity?

Leverage amplifies equity returns: by putting in less of their own money and borrowing the rest, the sponsor's percentage return on the equity invested is magnified when the deal goes well. Debt is also cheaper than equity (interest is tax-deductible, and lenders accept lower returns because they are senior and secured), which lowers the blended cost of capital. The trade-off is higher financial risk, since fixed interest and principal payments must be met regardless of business performance.

**Q679.** What are the three levers of value creation in an LBO?

The three levers are: (1) debt paydown / deleveraging, where the company's cash flow repays debt and transfers enterprise value from lenders to equity; (2) EBITDA growth, via revenue growth, margin expansion or cost cuts; and (3) multiple expansion, selling at a higher EV/EBITDA multiple than the entry multiple. Returns come from the combination of growing the numerator (EBITDA), shrinking net debt, and ideally re-rating the multiple.

**Q680.** Walk through the intuition of how an LBO generates returns.

You buy a company for an enterprise value funded mostly with debt and a thin equity check. Over the hold period the company's free cash flow pays down debt, so even if enterprise value stays flat, equity value (EV minus net debt) grows simply because debt shrank. Layer on EBITDA growth and a higher exit multiple, and the equity value at exit can be several times the original investment. It is analogous to buying a house with a mortgage: as you pay down the loan and the house appreciates, your home equity grows much faster than the house price.

**Q681.** Explain a "Sources & Uses" of funds table in an LBO.

Uses lists what the cash is needed for: the purchase of equity, refinancing of existing debt, and transaction fees (advisory, financing, legal). Sources lists where the cash comes from: new debt (term loans, bonds), sponsor equity, and sometimes rollover equity from management or cash on the target's balance sheet. Sources must equal Uses; the sponsor equity is typically the plug that makes the two sides balance.

**Q682.** Give a simple Sources & Uses example.

Suppose you buy a company for an enterprise value of 1,000 (USD millions). Uses: 1,000 for the purchase plus 30 of fees = 1,030. Sources: 600 of new debt, 30 of target cash used, and the remaining 400 as sponsor equity (the plug). Equity = Uses minus other sources = 1,030 - 600 - 30 = 400.

**Q683.** What are the main tranches of debt in an LBO capital structure, from most to least senior?

In rough order of seniority: revolver (revolving credit facility), Term Loan A, Term Loan B, senior secured notes, senior unsecured / high-yield bonds, mezzanine debt, and PIK instruments. Each lower tranche is riskier, more expensive, and sits further down the repayment waterfall. Below all debt sits the sponsor's common equity, which is the residual claim.

**Q684.** What is the difference between Term Loan A and Term Loan B?

Term Loan A (TLA) amortizes more heavily (meaningful annual principal repayment), has a shorter maturity (typically 5-6 years), a lower spread, and is usually held by banks. Term Loan B (TLB) has minimal amortization (often 1% per year with a bullet at maturity), a longer maturity (6-7 years), a higher spread, and is held by institutional investors (CLOs, credit funds). TLB is the workhorse of most US LBO structures.

**Q685.** What is a revolver and why is it part of the structure?

A revolving credit facility is a flexible line of credit the company can draw down and repay as needed, primarily to fund working capital and seasonal cash swings. It is usually left undrawn at close (so it is often not a "source" of funds) and carries an unused commitment fee on the undrawn portion. It provides liquidity insurance, ensuring the business can operate even if cash flow is temporarily tight.

**Q686.** What is mezzanine debt and where does it sit?

Mezzanine is subordinated debt that sits between senior debt and equity in the capital structure, often unsecured and carrying high coupons (frequently part cash, part PIK) plus sometimes equity warrants. It fills the financing gap when senior lenders will not lend the full amount needed, taking more risk for a higher return (often low-to-mid teens). It is junior to senior debt but senior to common equity in the waterfall.

**Q687.** What is PIK (payment-in-kind) debt?

PIK debt does not pay cash interest; instead the interest accrues and is added to the principal balance (it "pays in kind" with more debt). This preserves cash for the operating business or for debt paydown in early years, but the balance compounds, so the total payoff at maturity is larger. PIK toggles let the borrower choose between cash and PIK each period, useful when cash flow is uncertain.

**Q688.** What is the debt waterfall in an LBO?

The waterfall is the priority order in which cash flows and, in a default, liquidation proceeds are paid out. Senior secured lenders (revolver, term loans) are paid first, then senior unsecured/high-yield, then mezzanine and PIK, and finally common equity holders receive whatever residual is left. Higher tranches have lower risk and lower returns; equity is last in line but captures all the upside.

**Q689.** What characteristics make a good LBO candidate?

Ideal targets have stable, predictable cash flows (to service debt reliably), low and discretionary capex (so cash goes to debt paydown rather than reinvestment), strong tangible assets (to serve as collateral), a defensible market position with low cyclicality, and a reasonable/undervalued entry price. Opportunities for operational improvement and a clear exit path also help. Mature, cash-generative businesses (e.g., consumer staples, services) fit better than high-growth, capital-hungry tech.

**Q690.** Why is low capex important for an LBO target?

Free cash flow available for debt repayment equals operating cash flow minus capex. High capex consumes the cash that would otherwise pay down debt, slowing deleveraging and increasing default risk under a heavy debt load. Low-capex businesses convert more EBITDA into free cash flow, which is exactly what is needed to service and retire the LBO debt.

**Q691.** What is MOIC (also called MoM) and how is it calculated?

MOIC (Multiple on Invested Capital), also called MoM (Multiple of Money) or cash-on-cash, measures total value returned relative to equity invested: `MOIC = Total Equity Proceeds / Equity Invested`. If you invest 100 of equity and exit for 300, MOIC = 300/100 = 3.0x. It ignores time, so it is always paired with IRR.

**Q692.** What is IRR and how does it relate to MOIC?

IRR (Internal Rate of Return) is the annualized discount rate that sets the net present value of all cash flows to zero; it is the time-weighted return. For a single in-and-out investment, `IRR = MOIC^(1/n) - 1`, where n is the holding period in years. For a 3.0x MOIC over 5 years: IRR = 3^(1/5) - 1 = 1.2457 - 1 = 0.2457, about 24.6%.

**Q693.** Work through a returns example: invest 100, exit at 300 in 5 years.

MOIC = 300 / 100 = 3.0x. IRR = 3^(1/5) - 1. Since 3^(0.2) ~ 1.2457, IRR ~ 24.6%. A useful rule of thumb: roughly 3x in 5 years approximates a mid-20s percent IRR, and 2x in 5 years is about 15% (2^(1/5)-1 = 14.9%).

**Q694.** Why can MOIC and IRR tell different stories?

MOIC measures absolute multiple of capital but ignores timing; IRR measures annualized speed but rewards early cash returns. A deal returning 2.0x in 2 years has a strong IRR (41%) but modest MOIC, while 2.5x in 7 years has a higher MOIC but a lower IRR (~14%). LPs care about both: IRR for return rate and MOIC for absolute dollars (and to avoid being misled by quick flips that flatter IRR).

**Q695.** Walk through a conceptual "paper LBO."

A paper LBO is a back-of-envelope deal you compute mentally in an interview. Steps: (1) Entry — compute purchase EV = entry multiple x EBITDA, split into debt and equity. (2) Project EBITDA growth over ~5 years. (3) Build free cash flow each year and use it to pay down debt, tracking the falling net debt balance. (4) Exit — compute exit EV = exit multiple x final-year EBITDA, subtract remaining net debt to get exit equity value. (5) Returns — MOIC = exit equity / entry equity, then IRR = MOIC^(1/n) - 1.

**Q696.** Paper LBO: buy at 10x EBITDA of 100 with 5x debt, EBITDA grows to 150, exit at 10x, 50 debt repaid over 5 years. What are the returns?

Entry EV = 10 x 100 = 1,000; debt = 5 x 100 = 500; entry equity = 1,000 - 500 = 500. Exit EV = 10 x 150 = 1,500; net debt at exit = 500 - 50 = 450; exit equity = 1,500 - 450 = 1,050. MOIC = 1,050 / 500 = 2.1x; IRR = 2.1^(1/5) - 1 ~ 16%. Here all the gain came from EBITDA growth and debt paydown, with no multiple expansion.

**Q697.** How exactly does leverage amplify equity returns? Show with numbers.

Suppose EV grows from 1,000 to 1,300 (a 30% EV gain). All-equity: equity goes 1,000 to 1,300 = 1.3x. With 700 debt and 300 equity, if debt stays at 700, exit equity = 1,300 - 700 = 600 = 2.0x on the 300 invested. The same 30% asset move becomes a 100% equity gain because the fixed debt claim does not share in the upside, leaving more for equity, the essence of financial leverage (and the same mechanism magnifies losses on the downside).

**Q698.** What is the difference between maintenance and incurrence covenants?

Maintenance covenants require the borrower to satisfy financial tests on an ongoing basis (e.g., every quarter), such as keeping leverage below a ceiling, tested whether or not the company takes any action. Incurrence covenants are tested only when the company takes a specific action (incurring new debt, paying a dividend, making an acquisition). "Covenant-lite" (cov-lite) loans have only incurrence covenants, giving borrowers more flexibility, common in TLB markets.

**Q699.** What are the two most common financial covenants and how are they defined?

A leverage covenant caps `Net Debt / EBITDA` below some maximum (e.g., must stay under 6.0x). A coverage covenant requires `EBITDA / Interest Expense` (interest coverage) or `(EBITDA - Capex) / (Interest + Principal)` (fixed-charge coverage) to stay above a minimum (e.g., above 2.0x). They protect lenders by ensuring debt remains serviceable and proportionate to cash flow; breaching one is an event of default.

**Q700.** What is the J-curve in private equity returns?

The J-curve describes how a PE fund's net returns (or net cash flow to LPs) dip negative in early years before turning positive, tracing a "J" shape. Early on, management fees and deal costs are drawn while investments are immature and held at cost, so reported IRR is negative; later, as portfolio companies are improved and exited, distributions flow back and IRR climbs. It explains why judging a fund's performance in its first 2-3 years is misleading.

**Q701.** What is the difference between entry multiple and exit multiple, and why does it matter?

The entry multiple is the EV/EBITDA paid at acquisition; the exit multiple is the EV/EBITDA received at sale. Multiple expansion (exit > entry) boosts returns; multiple contraction destroys them. Conservative LBO models often assume exit multiple equals entry multiple, so returns rely only on EBITDA growth and debt paydown rather than betting on a market re-rating.

**Q702.** What is a dividend recapitalization?

A dividend recap is when a PE-owned company raises new debt and uses the proceeds to pay a special dividend to the sponsor, rather than reinvesting it. It lets the sponsor extract cash and de-risk the investment before a full exit, locking in returns early and improving IRR (cash returned sooner). The downside is it re-levers the company, increasing its financial risk and reducing cushion for the remaining equity.

**Q703.** How do PE firms exit their investments?

The main exit routes are: (1) strategic sale (trade sale) to a corporate buyer in the same industry, often the highest price due to synergies; (2) secondary buyout, selling to another financial sponsor (PE firm); (3) IPO, listing the company on a public exchange, which may be a partial exit with staged selldown; and occasionally (4) dividend recaps as a partial return. The chosen route depends on market conditions, company size, and which yields the best risk-adjusted proceeds.

**Q704.** Explain the "2 and 20" fee structure, GP, and LP.

In a PE fund, Limited Partners (LPs, e.g., pension funds, endowments) provide the capital; the General Partner (GP, the PE firm) manages it. "2 and 20" means a 2% annual management fee on committed/invested capital plus 20% carried interest (carry), the GP's share of profits. On a 1,000 fund, that is ~20/year in fees and 20% of gains above the return of capital (and typically above a hurdle). Carry aligns GP incentives with LP returns.

**Q705.** What is a hurdle rate and a GP catch-up?

The hurdle rate (preferred return, often 8%) is the minimum annualized return LPs must receive before the GP earns any carried interest. The catch-up is a provision after the hurdle is met that pays the GP a larger share (often 100%) of subsequent profits until the GP has received its full 20% of total profits-above-cost, then both revert to the standard 80/20 split. Together they ensure LPs get their preferred return first, but the GP eventually earns carry on all the profit, not just the excess above the hurdle.

**Q706.** What is the difference between private equity and venture capital?

PE typically buys mature, cash-generative companies using significant leverage, often acquiring control (majority/100%) and improving operations; VC invests minority equity in early-stage, high-growth, often unprofitable startups with little or no debt. PE returns come from cash flow, leverage and operational improvement with moderate failure rates; VC follows a power-law where a few big winners offset many write-offs. PE deal sizes and certainty are higher; VC upside per deal is far larger but riskier.

**Q707.** What are add-on (bolt-on) acquisitions and the buy-and-build strategy, and how does PE look in India?

An add-on (bolt-on) is a smaller acquisition made by an existing PE portfolio company ("platform") to expand scale, geography or capabilities; buy-and-build strings together many add-ons to create a larger, more valuable group. Because the larger combined entity often commands a higher exit multiple than the small targets were bought at, this creates "multiple arbitrage." In India, classic leveraged buyouts are less common because RBI regulations historically restrict banks from funding share acquisitions and acquisition financing is limited; consequently Indian PE skews toward growth capital and minority stakes (e.g., funding expansion in tech, financials and consumer), with control buyouts and leverage growing but still modest versus the US/Europe, all under SEBI's AIF (Alternative Investment Fund) framework.

<a name="F3"></a>

## F3. Deal Valuation & Capital Raising  <span class="qrange">(Q708-Q738)</span>

**Q708.** What is precedent transaction analysis and how does it differ from trading comps?

Precedent transaction analysis (also called "deal comps" or "M&A comps") values a company based on multiples actually paid in past acquisitions of similar companies, e.g. EV/EBITDA paid in completed deals. The key difference from trading comps is that trading comps use current market prices of standalone public peers (a minority, non-control value), while precedent transactions reflect prices paid to acquire whole companies, which include a control premium and often synergy expectations. As a result, transaction multiples are usually higher than trading-comp multiples for the same sector.

**Q709.** Why do transaction multiples include a control premium?

In a trading comp, an investor buys a small minority stake at the prevailing share price and gets no control over the business. In a transaction, the acquirer buys 100% (or a controlling stake) and gains the ability to change management, strategy, capital structure and capture synergies, so it pays extra for that control. This excess over the unaffected trading price is the control premium, typically around 20-40% over the target's pre-announcement share price, which is why EV/EBITDA paid in deals exceeds standalone trading EV/EBITDA.

**Q710.** How do you select comparable deals for a precedent transactions analysis?

You screen for deals involving targets in the same industry and of broadly similar size, business model and geography, ideally completed (announced) recently so the multiples reflect comparable market and financing conditions. You prefer deals with disclosed financials so the multiple can actually be computed, and you note the deal type (strategic vs financial buyer, distressed vs competitive auction) because that drives the premium. Older deals (more than ~3-5 years) and deals from very different market cycles are usually excluded or footnoted.

**Q711.** Why might a strategic buyer's deal multiple be a poor comp for a financial-sponsor deal?

A strategic (corporate) acquirer can realize cost and revenue synergies by combining the target with its existing operations, so it can justify paying a higher multiple. A financial sponsor (PE firm) generally has no operating synergies and is constrained by leverage and target IRR, so it typically pays less. Using a synergy-rich strategic deal to value a sponsor-led transaction will overstate value, so bankers separate the two buyer types when presenting the range.

**Q712.** What is a "football field" chart in a valuation?

A football field is a horizontal bar chart that summarizes the valuation ranges produced by each methodology (DCF, trading comps, precedent transactions, LBO analysis, 52-week trading range, analyst price targets) on a single page, so you can see where the ranges overlap. Each method is one bar showing its low-to-high value (e.g. enterprise value or per-share price), and the overlapping zone suggests a defensible valuation range. It is the standard one-slide "valuation summary" that bankers present to a board or client.

**Q713.** What are the three main valuation methodologies banks present, and how do they typically rank?

The three core methods are discounted cash flow (DCF), comparable companies (trading comps), and precedent transactions. As a rough generalization, DCF often produces the highest value (because it can capture full long-term growth and an optimistic terminal value), precedent transactions sit in the middle (they bake in a control premium and synergies), and trading comps tend to be lowest (minority, no-control market value). This ordering is not a rule, but it is the common pattern interviewers expect, and a banker should always be ready to explain why their specific case deviates.

**Q714.** Why does a DCF often give the highest valuation?

A DCF captures the full intrinsic value of all projected future cash flows, including years of growth and a terminal value that often represents 60-80% of total value, so small changes in the growth or discount-rate assumptions move value a lot. Because management projections tend to be optimistic and the terminal value is sensitive to the perpetuity growth or exit multiple chosen, the DCF range can run above market-based methods. That said, it is also the most assumption-dependent method, so a disciplined banker stress-tests it with sensitivity tables.

**Q715.** Why would an acquirer pay a premium over the target's current share price?

The acquirer pays a premium because it expects to be worth more than the standalone trading value: it gains control, can capture synergies (cost savings and revenue uplift), and must offer enough to persuade shareholders to give up their stock. In a competitive auction the premium also reflects the price needed to beat rival bidders. The premium is justified only if the present value of synergies plus the standalone value exceeds the price paid; otherwise the acquirer overpays and destroys value.

**Q716.** How do you justify an acquisition premium using synergies?

You estimate the after-tax run-rate synergies (e.g. cost savings from headcount, procurement, facility consolidation, plus any revenue synergies), discount their present value net of integration costs, and compare that to the dollar premium being paid. If PV of synergies exceeds the premium, the deal creates value for the acquirer's shareholders even at a premium price. Example: paying a $200m premium is justified if $40m/year of after-tax synergies discounted as a perpetuity at 10% is worth $400m in PV.

**Q717.** What is the difference between cost synergies and revenue synergies, and which do investors trust more?

Cost synergies are savings from eliminating duplicate functions, consolidating procurement, and shutting overlapping facilities; revenue synergies are incremental sales from cross-selling, broader distribution, or pricing power. Investors and analysts give far more credit to cost synergies because they are more controllable and quicker to realize, whereas revenue synergies are harder to deliver and often fail to materialize. Bankers typically present cost synergies as the credible base and treat revenue synergies as upside.

**Q718.** Walk through the IPO process end to end.

The company first runs a "beauty parade" and awards a mandate to lead underwriters (bookrunners), who then conduct due diligence and draft the registration document (S-1 in the US, DRHP in India). After regulator review (SEC / SEBI comments), the company markets the deal via a roadshow to institutional investors, the bankers build a book of demand at various prices (book-building), set the final offer price, allocate shares, list and trade, and may stabilize the price using a greenshoe over-allotment option. Insiders are usually subject to a lock-up (often 180 days) before they can sell.

**Q719.** What is an S-1 versus a DRHP?

The S-1 is the registration statement filed with the US SEC for an IPO; it contains the business description, risk factors, MD&A, audited financials and use-of-proceeds disclosure. The DRHP (Draft Red Herring Prospectus) is the Indian equivalent filed with SEBI and the stock exchanges; "red herring" refers to the fact that the price/number of shares is not finalized at filing. Both are the core disclosure documents that let investors evaluate the offering; the final versions are the "424B" prospectus (US) and RHP/Prospectus (India).

**Q720.** What happens during an IPO roadshow and book-building?

In the roadshow, management and bankers present the investment case to institutional investors over one to two weeks, while the syndicate desk collects indications of interest at different price levels. Book-building is the process of aggregating that demand into a "book" that shows how many shares investors will buy at each price, which lets the bankers gauge the demand curve. The final price is then set based on where the book is well-covered, balancing maximizing proceeds against leaving enough demand for a healthy aftermarket.

**Q721.** What is a greenshoe / over-allotment option?

The greenshoe (over-allotment option) lets the underwriters sell up to ~15% more shares than originally offered and then buy them back (or exercise the option) depending on aftermarket demand. If the stock trades up, underwriters exercise the option and the issuer sells the extra shares; if it trades down, they buy shares in the open market to cover the short, which supports (stabilizes) the price. It is named after the Green Shoe Manufacturing Company, the first issuer to use it.

**Q722.** What is an IPO lock-up period and why does it exist?

A lock-up is a contractual restriction (commonly 180 days in the US; for Indian IPOs promoter and pre-IPO shareholder lock-ins are set by SEBI rules) that prevents insiders and pre-IPO holders from selling their shares immediately after listing. It exists to prevent a flood of insider selling that would crater the price and signal a lack of confidence right after the offering. Lock-up expiry dates are watched because they can create selling pressure when large blocks become free to trade.

**Q723.** Why are IPOs often underpriced, and what is the evidence?

Underpricing means the offer price is set below the price the stock trades at on day one, so the stock "pops"; on average first-day returns are positive (historically high-single to double-digit percent). It happens because of information asymmetry (issuers and bankers set a price that ensures the book is covered and rewards institutional investors for revealing demand), risk aversion by underwriters who want a successful deal, and the desire to create positive momentum and goodwill. The cost to the issuer is "money left on the table" — the difference between proceeds at the offer price and what fuller pricing would have raised.

**Q724.** What is the difference between primary and secondary shares in an IPO?

Primary shares are newly issued by the company, so the cash raised goes to the company's balance sheet (the share count increases, funding growth or debt paydown). Secondary shares are existing shares sold by current holders (founders, PE/VC), so the proceeds go to those selling shareholders, not the company, and there is no new dilution from those shares. A single IPO can include both; an offer that is mostly secondary (in India, an "Offer for Sale") signals insiders cashing out rather than the company raising capital.

**Q725.** What is the difference between book-building and fixed-price issues in India?

In a fixed-price issue the company sets a single price in the prospectus before the offer opens, and investors apply at that price. In book-building the issuer specifies a price band (floor and cap), institutions and other investors bid within the band, and the final cut-off price is discovered from the book; most large Indian IPOs use the 100% book-built route. SEBI requires demand details to be disclosed, and retail investors can typically bid "at cut-off" to accept whatever final price is set.

**Q726.** Who are anchor investors in an Indian IPO?

Anchor investors are large institutional investors (QIBs) who are allotted shares one day before the IPO opens to the public, at a price within the band, to lend credibility and demand confidence to the offering. SEBI requires anchors to commit for a lock-in (currently 50% of anchor shares for 90 days and the remainder for 30 days from allotment), so they cannot immediately flip. Their participation, and the marquee names involved, is a signal retail and other investors watch closely.

**Q727.** What are the QIB, NII and retail categories in an Indian book-built IPO?

SEBI mandates reservation buckets: QIBs (Qualified Institutional Buyers — mutual funds, banks, FIIs, insurers) get up to 50%, Non-Institutional Investors (NIIs/HNIs applying for more than Rs 2 lakh) get at least 15%, and Retail Individual Investors (applications up to Rs 2 lakh) get at least 35% (these are the proportions for the standard profitable-company route). Retail and NII over-subscription is allotted by lottery/proportionate rules when demand exceeds supply. The split ensures broad public participation rather than the deal going entirely to institutions.

**Q728.** What is a follow-on public offering (FPO) and how does it differ from an IPO?

A follow-on (or secondary) public offering is a sale of shares by a company that is already listed, as opposed to an IPO, which is the first time shares are sold to the public. An FPO can be dilutive (new primary shares issued, raising cash for the company) or non-dilutive (existing holders selling). Because there is already a market price, an FPO is usually priced at a small discount to the prevailing trading price rather than discovered from scratch.

**Q729.** What is a rights issue and what is the theoretical ex-rights price (TERP)?

A rights issue offers existing shareholders the right to buy new shares pro-rata, usually at a discount to the market price, preserving their ownership percentage if they subscribe. The theoretical ex-rights price is the blended price after the new shares are added: TERP = (N_old * P_old + N_new * P_sub) / (N_old + N_new). Example: 1-for-1 rights at Rs 80 when the stock is Rs 120 gives TERP = (1*120 + 1*80)/2 = Rs 100, and the value of one right = market price minus TERP = 120 - 100 = Rs 20.

**Q730.** How does a rights issue cause dilution, and does subscribing protect a shareholder?

A rights issue increases the share count, so EPS and each share's claim on the company fall (the price drops toward TERP), and any shareholder who does not subscribe is diluted both in ownership percentage and in value as the share price re-bases below the old price. A shareholder who fully subscribes maintains their ownership percentage and is economically neutral — the loss on existing shares from the lower TERP is offset by buying new shares cheaply. Those who cannot subscribe may sell their rights (where renounceable) to recover the rights' value.

**Q731.** What is a QIP (Qualified Institutions Placement) in India and why is it popular?

A QIP is a SEBI mechanism that lets a listed company raise capital quickly by issuing shares (or convertibles) only to Qualified Institutional Buyers, without the lengthy documentation of a public offering. It is popular because it is fast, avoids extensive regulatory filing and roadshows, and the floor price is formula-based (broadly the average of the last two weeks' weekly high-low of closing prices), with a permitted discount of up to 5%. It is the Indian analog of a US private placement / registered direct to institutions.

**Q732.** What is the difference between Equity Capital Markets (ECM) and Debt Capital Markets (DCM)?

ECM teams help companies raise equity — IPOs, follow-ons, rights issues, QIPs, convertibles and block trades — which gives up ownership but adds no repayment obligation. DCM teams help companies raise debt — bonds, notes, and structured debt — which must be repaid with interest but does not dilute owners and the interest is usually tax-deductible. Equity is more sensitive to market sentiment and valuation, while debt issuance hinges on credit ratings, interest-rate conditions, and credit spreads.

**Q733.** Walk through the bond issuance process and the role of the credit rating.

The issuer mandates underwriters, who help structure the bond (size, tenor, seniority, covenants), obtain a credit rating from agencies (S&P/Moody's/Fitch globally; CRISIL/ICRA/CARE in India), and prepare an offering memorandum/prospectus. The rating is central because it largely determines the credit spread over the benchmark rate (e.g. US Treasuries or the G-sec yield), and thus the coupon the issuer must pay; a higher rating means a lower spread and cheaper funding. Bankers then market the bond, build a book of orders at a given spread, set the coupon/price, and allocate the bonds.

**Q734.** How is a bond's coupon set, and what does "yield to issuer" mean?

The coupon is set so the bond can be sold at (or near) par given prevailing yields: coupon roughly equals the benchmark risk-free yield plus the credit spread appropriate for the issuer's rating and tenor. If demand is strong, the book lets bankers tighten the spread (lower yield), reducing the issuer's cost; the all-in yield the company pays — including the spread and any fees — is effectively its cost of debt for that issue. For example, a BBB issuer pricing 150 bps over a 4.0% government benchmark would carry roughly a 5.5% coupon if issued at par.

**Q735.** What is a convertible bond and why do issuers use them?

A convertible bond is debt that the holder can convert into a set number of the issuer's shares, so it combines a bond with an embedded equity call option. Issuers use them because the equity option lets them pay a lower coupon than on straight debt (the investor accepts less yield for the upside), and they can effectively sell equity at a premium to the current price (the conversion price is set above today's stock price). The trade-off is potential future dilution if the stock rises and bonds convert; issuers often pair them with call-spread overlays to raise the effective conversion price.

**Q736.** What is the underwriting spread (gross spread) in an offering?

The gross spread is the underwriters' total fee: the difference between the price investors pay (offer price) and the net proceeds the issuer receives per share. For example, a $20.00 offer with a 7% gross spread nets the issuer $18.60 per share, with $1.40 going to the syndicate, split into the management fee, underwriting fee, and selling concession. US IPOs have historically clustered around a 7% gross spread; investment-grade bond and large follow-on fees are much smaller (often well under 1%).

**Q737.** What is the difference between a bought deal and a best-efforts offering, and what are SPACs?

In a bought deal the underwriter buys the entire issue from the company at a set price and resells it, taking the placement risk (common for follow-ons and bonds); in a best-efforts deal the bank only agrees to use its best efforts to sell shares and bears no risk for unsold stock, which is typical for riskier or smaller IPOs. A SPAC (Special Purpose Acquisition Company) is a shell company that IPOs to raise a cash trust, then merges with a private target to take it public; pros are speed, price certainty and the ability to share forward projections, while cons are sponsor dilution (the ~20% "promote"), redemptions that can drain the trust, and a poor average post-merger return record.

**Q738.** How does a banker pitch a financing, and what are league tables?

In a financing pitch the banker shows the client the optimal structure (equity vs debt vs convertible), the likely amount and pricing, market windows and investor demand, comparable recent deals, and the bank's relevant track record and distribution capability. To prove credibility the pitch cites league tables — published rankings (e.g. by Bloomberg, Dealogic, Refinitiv, Prime Database in India) that rank banks by deal volume or value in a product/region over a period. A top league-table position is used as a marketing credential to win the mandate, even though rankings can be sliced to favor the pitching bank.

<a name="secG"></a>

# G. Portfolio Management & Investments

<a name="G1"></a>

## G1. Modern Portfolio Theory  <span class="qrange">(Q739-Q768)</span>

**Q739.** What is the expected return of a portfolio, and how is it calculated?

A portfolio's expected return is the weighted average of the expected returns of its holdings: `E[Rp] = sum(w_i * E[R_i])`, where w_i are the portfolio weights (summing to 1). For example, 60% in equities at 12% and 40% in bonds at 6% gives `E[Rp] = 0.6*12% + 0.4*6% = 9.6%`. Note that return aggregates linearly, but risk does not.

**Q740.** Write the formula for the variance of a two-asset portfolio.

`var(Rp) = w1^2*s1^2 + w2^2*s2^2 + 2*w1*w2*s1*s2*corr`, where s1, s2 are the standard deviations and corr is the correlation between the two assets. The portfolio standard deviation is `sigma_p = sqrt(var(Rp))`. The cross term `2*w1*w2*s1*s2*corr` is the covariance contribution, and it is what makes portfolio risk lower than the weighted average of individual risks whenever corr < 1.

**Q741.** Work a quick two-asset risk/return example with corr = 0.3.

Assets: A has E[R]=10%, s=20%; B has E[R]=6%, s=10%; weights 50/50; corr=0.3. Return: `E[Rp] = 0.5*10% + 0.5*6% = 8%`. Variance: `0.25*0.04 + 0.25*0.01 + 2*0.5*0.5*0.20*0.10*0.3 = 0.01 + 0.0025 + 0.003 = 0.0155`, so `sigma_p = sqrt(0.0155) = 12.45%`. The simple weighted-average risk would have been 15%, so diversification saved about 2.55 percentage points.

**Q742.** What is the difference between covariance and correlation?

Covariance measures how two assets move together in absolute (unit-dependent) terms: `cov(A,B) = corr * sA * sB`, and can range over any value. Correlation is the standardized version, `corr = cov(A,B) / (sA*sB)`, always bounded between -1 and +1, which makes it comparable across asset pairs. Covariance enters the variance formula directly, but correlation is easier to interpret because it strips out the scale of each asset's volatility.

**Q743.** How does correlation drive the benefit of diversification?

The lower the correlation between assets, the smaller the cross (covariance) term in the variance formula, so portfolio volatility falls below the weighted average of individual volatilities. As long as corr < 1, combining assets reduces risk; the benefit grows as correlation approaches -1. Diversification is therefore "the only free lunch in finance" because it can lower risk without necessarily lowering expected return.

**Q744.** What happens to two-asset portfolio risk in the special cases corr = +1, 0, and -1?

If corr = +1, risk is just the weighted average, `sigma_p = w1*s1 + w2*s2` (no diversification benefit). If corr = 0, the cross term vanishes and `sigma_p = sqrt(w1^2*s1^2 + w2^2*s2^2)`, giving partial diversification. If corr = -1, risk can be driven to zero with the right weights, because the assets perfectly offset each other. Real-world correlations sit between these extremes, so diversification helps but cannot fully eliminate risk.

**Q745.** With corr = -1, what weights produce a zero-variance two-asset portfolio?

When corr = -1, set the weights inversely to volatility: `w1 = s2/(s1+s2)` and `w2 = s1/(s1+s2)`. For example with s1=20% and s2=10%, `w1 = 10/30 = 33.3%` in asset 1 and `w2 = 66.7%` in asset 2 gives `sigma_p = 0`. This is a theoretical edge case; perfect negative correlation rarely exists, but it illustrates the upper bound of diversification.

**Q746.** Distinguish systematic from unsystematic risk.

Systematic (market or non-diversifiable) risk affects all assets and stems from economy-wide factors like interest rates, inflation, recessions, or geopolitics. Unsystematic (specific, idiosyncratic, or diversifiable) risk is unique to a company or sector, such as a product recall, management fraud, or a plant fire. Total risk = systematic + unsystematic, and only systematic risk is compensated with a higher expected return because the rest can be diversified away.

**Q747.** Why can diversification not remove systematic risk?

Diversification works by averaging away the independent, asset-specific shocks that partially cancel out across many holdings. Systematic risk is common to every asset, so it does not cancel; adding more stocks just leaves you exposed to the same market-wide factor.
