As the number of holdings rises, portfolio variance falls toward the average covariance between assets, not toward zero, and that residual floor is the systematic risk.

**Q748.** How does the number of assets affect portfolio risk?

For an equal-weighted portfolio of N assets, variance is `var(Rp) = (1/N)*avg_variance + (1 - 1/N)*avg_covariance`. As N grows, the first term shrinks toward zero and the variance converges to the average covariance, the systematic floor. Empirically much of the diversifiable risk is removed with roughly 20-30 stocks; adding more gives diminishing benefit. In India, a Nifty 50 index fund captures most of this diversification within a single instrument.

**Q749.** Write portfolio variance in matrix form and explain the terms.

`var(Rp) = w' * Sigma * w`, where w is the column vector of weights, w' is its transpose, and Sigma is the covariance matrix of asset returns. The diagonal of Sigma holds the variances and the off-diagonal entries hold the covariances `cov(i,j) = corr_ij*s_i*s_j`. This compact form generalizes the two-asset formula to any number of assets and is what optimizers actually compute.

**Q750.** What is the efficient frontier and what does it represent?

The efficient frontier is the set of portfolios that deliver the maximum expected return for each level of risk (or the minimum risk for each level of return). Plotted in risk-return space it is the upward-sloping upper edge of the feasible region of all portfolio combinations. Any portfolio below the frontier is sub-optimal because you could earn more return for the same risk; rational mean-variance investors hold only frontier portfolios.

**Q751.** What is the minimum-variance portfolio (MVP)?

The MVP is the single portfolio on the efficient frontier with the lowest possible total risk, sitting at the leftmost tip of the frontier curve. For two assets its weight in asset 1 is `w1 = (s2^2 - cov12) / (s1^2 + s2^2 - 2*cov12)`. It is the starting point of the efficient frontier; portfolios below the MVP (the lower curve) are inefficient because another portfolio offers the same risk with higher return.

**Q752.** Compute the minimum-variance weights for a simple two-asset case.

With s1=20%, s2=10%, corr=0.3, covariance is `cov12 = 0.3*0.20*0.10 = 0.006`. Then `w1 = (0.01 - 0.006)/(0.04 + 0.01 - 2*0.006) = 0.004/0.038 = 10.5%`, so w2 = 89.5%. The portfolio tilts heavily toward the lower-volatility asset, which is the intuition behind the MVP minimizing total risk.

**Q753.** What changes when you add a risk-free asset to the opportunity set?

A risk-free asset (with zero variance and return Rf) lets you form straight-line combinations between Rf and any risky portfolio. The best such line is the one tangent to the efficient frontier, which dominates the curved frontier of risky-only portfolios at every risk level. Investors now hold a mix of the risk-free asset and that single tangency portfolio, achieving better risk-return trade-offs than with risky assets alone.

**Q754.** What is the Capital Market Line (CML)?

The CML is the straight line from the risk-free rate, tangent to the efficient frontier, representing the best attainable combinations of the risk-free asset and the market (tangency) portfolio. Its equation is `E[Rp] = Rf + ((E[Rm] - Rf)/sigma_m) * sigma_p`. The slope is the market price of risk, and only efficient (fully diversified) portfolios lie on the CML. It differs from the SML, which prices individual assets by beta.

**Q755.** What is the tangency (market) portfolio?

The tangency portfolio is the risky portfolio where the line from Rf just touches the efficient frontier; it has the highest Sharpe ratio of any risky portfolio. Under CAPM assumptions, in equilibrium every investor holds this same portfolio of risky assets, which must therefore be the market portfolio, value-weighted across all risky assets. All efficient portfolios are then a blend of Rf and this single portfolio.

**Q756.** What is the Sharpe ratio and how does it relate to the CML?

The Sharpe ratio is excess return per unit of total risk: `Sharpe = (Rp - Rf) / sigma_p`. It is exactly the slope of the line connecting the risk-free asset to a portfolio, so the CML is the line with the maximum Sharpe ratio, achieved by the tangency portfolio. For example, a fund returning 14% with Rf=6% and sigma=16% has `Sharpe = (14-6)/16 = 0.5`. Higher Sharpe means better risk-adjusted performance.

**Q757.** State the two-fund separation theorem.

Two-fund separation says that when a risk-free asset exists, every investor's optimal portfolio is a combination of just two funds: the risk-free asset and the single tangency (market) portfolio. Risk tolerance only changes the split between these two, not the composition of the risky fund. A conservative investor lends (holds more Rf), while an aggressive one can borrow at Rf to lever up the tangency portfolio beyond 100%.

**Q758.** How do investors with different risk appetites use the CML?

They all hold the same tangency portfolio but vary the weight placed on it versus the risk-free asset. A point left of the tangency portfolio means lending part of the money at Rf (lower risk and return); a point to the right means borrowing at Rf to invest more than 100% in the tangency portfolio (leverage, higher risk and return). This is the practical consequence of two-fund separation.

**Q759.** Why is equal-weighting sometimes preferred over mean-variance optimization?

Equal-weighting (1/N) ignores the estimated return and covariance inputs entirely, so it sidesteps the estimation error that plagues optimizers. Empirical studies (e.g., DeMiguel, Garlappi, Uppal) found 1/N often matches or beats "optimal" mean-variance portfolios out of sample. It also gives a natural small-cap tilt and forces contrarian rebalancing, though it can over-concentrate in volatile names since it ignores risk.

**Q760.** Contrast market-cap weighting with equal weighting.

Market-cap weighting holds each asset in proportion to its market value, so it is self-rebalancing, low-turnover, and reflects the consensus "market portfolio" (e.g., Nifty 50, S&P 500). Equal weighting holds the same amount in each, which tilts toward smaller and value names and requires periodic rebalancing as prices drift. Cap-weighting can become top-heavy in a few mega-caps, while equal-weight spreads exposure but trades more.

**Q761.** What is risk parity and how does it differ from cap-weighting?

Risk parity allocates so that each asset (or asset class) contributes equally to total portfolio risk, rather than equal capital or cap weights. Because bonds are less volatile than equities, this typically means a large bond allocation, often levered up to reach a target return (as in Bridgewater's All Weather). It aims for more balanced risk exposure across regimes, but depends on stable correlations and can suffer when leverage and rates move against it.

**Q762.** Why is rebalancing necessary, and what does it accomplish?

Over time, winners grow and losers shrink, so weights drift away from targets and the portfolio's risk profile changes (often becoming more equity-heavy). Rebalancing sells outperformers and buys underperformers back to target, enforcing a disciplined "sell high, buy low" and controlling risk. It can be calendar-based (e.g., quarterly) or threshold-based (e.g., when a weight drifts 5% off target); in India, watch STT and capital-gains tax as rebalancing costs.

**Q763.** What is estimation error in MPT, and why are the inputs fragile?

Mean-variance optimization needs expected returns, variances, and the full covariance matrix, all estimated from noisy historical data. Expected returns are especially hard to estimate and small input changes produce wildly different "optimal" weights. The optimizer treats estimation noise as if it were signal, piling into assets that merely look attractive in-sample, which is why MPT is fragile and often disappoints out of sample.

**Q764.** What did Michael Best and Robert Grauer (and Richard Michaud) mean by "error maximization"?

Michaud famously called mean-variance optimizers "estimation-error maximizers" because they overweight assets with overestimated returns or underestimated risk and short those with the opposite errors. Tiny changes in inputs can flip the recommended portfolio dramatically, producing extreme, unstable, and often un-investable weights. Practitioners counter this with constraints, shrinkage estimators (e.g., Ledoit-Wolf), Bayesian methods like Black-Litterman, and resampled efficiency.

**Q765.** How does the Black-Litterman model address MPT's input fragility?

Black-Litterman starts from a neutral equilibrium (the market-cap portfolio implied by reverse-optimization) rather than raw historical means, then blends in the investor's explicit views with confidence levels. This Bayesian shrinkage produces more stable, intuitive, and diversified weights that don't swing violently with small input changes. It directly fixes the "garbage in, garbage out" problem of feeding noisy expected returns into a naive optimizer.

**Q766.** Why does diversifying across asset classes help more than within one class?

Assets within a single class (e.g., Indian large-cap equities) tend to be highly correlated, so their idiosyncratic risks cancel but they still rise and fall together with that market. Combining low-correlation classes such as equities, bonds, gold, and real estate lowers the average covariance and pushes the efficient frontier up and to the left. Gold, for instance, often has low or negative correlation with equities and acts as a portfolio hedge during crises.

**Q767.** What is the case for international diversification, and what is its main limitation?

Holding foreign equities adds assets driven partly by different economies, currencies, and cycles, lowering portfolio correlation and risk (e.g., a global investor pairing US, European, and emerging-market stocks). The main limitation is that correlations tend to spike toward 1 during global crises, exactly when diversification is most needed, as seen in 2008. Currency exposure and home-country constraints (for Indian investors, LRS limits and taxation of foreign funds) also temper the benefit.

**Q768.** Put it together: build the efficient set for a two-asset stock-bond mix and identify the key portfolios.

Take equities (E[R]=12%, s=18%) and bonds (E[R]=6%, s=7%) with corr=0.2. Varying the equity weight from 0 to 100% traces a curved frontier; the MVP weight in equities is `w_eq = (s_bond^2 - cov)/(s_eq^2 + s_bond^2 - 2*cov)` with `cov = 0.2*0.18*0.07 = 0.00252`, giving `w_eq = (0.0049 - 0.00252)/(0.0324 + 0.0049 - 0.00504) = 0.00238/0.03226 = 7.4%`. Adding a risk-free asset at Rf=5% and drawing the tangent line locates the tangency portfolio (highest Sharpe), and all efficient holdings become blends of Rf and that tangency mix.

<a name="G2"></a>

## G2. CAPM, Factor Models & Performance  <span class="qrange">(Q769-Q798)</span>

**Q769.** What is the CAPM equation, and what does it say in words?

The Capital Asset Pricing Model states `E[Ri] = Rf + beta_i * (E[Rm] - Rf)`, where Rf is the risk-free rate, E[Rm] - Rf is the equity (market) risk premium, and beta_i measures the asset's sensitivity to market moves. In words: the expected return on an asset equals the risk-free rate plus a reward for the systematic risk it adds to a diversified portfolio. For example, with Rf = 7% (India 10Y G-Sec proxy), ERP = 6%, and beta = 1.2, E[R] = 7% + 1.2*6% = 14.2%. Only non-diversifiable (systematic) risk is priced; idiosyncratic risk earns no premium.

**Q770.** What are the key assumptions behind CAPM?

CAPM assumes investors are rational mean-variance optimizers with homogeneous expectations, can borrow and lend freely at a single risk-free rate, face no taxes or transaction costs, hold the same single-period horizon, and that all assets are perfectly divisible and markets are frictionless and competitive. Under these conditions everyone holds the same risky "market portfolio" combined with the risk-free asset, which is what makes beta the single relevant risk measure. The assumptions are unrealistic, but the model is valued for the clean intuition that only systematic risk is compensated.

**Q771.** Define beta and give its formula.

Beta measures an asset's systematic risk: how much its return moves with the overall market. `beta = Cov(Ri, Rm) / Var(Rm)`, which equals `rho(i,m) * sigma_i / sigma_m`, where rho is the correlation. Beta = 1 means the asset moves in line with the market; beta > 1 is more volatile/cyclical (e.g., a high-beta auto or metals stock), beta < 1 is defensive (e.g., FMCG/pharma). For instance, if Cov(Ri, Rm) = 0.024 and Var(Rm) = 0.020, beta = 1.2.

**Q772.** How do you interpret a stock with beta = 0.6 versus beta = 1.5?

A beta of 0.6 means that for a 1% market move the stock tends to move about 0.6% in the same direction — it is defensive and dampens portfolio volatility (typical of utilities, consumer staples). A beta of 1.5 means roughly a 1.5% move per 1% market move — it amplifies both gains and losses and is cyclical (typical of high-leverage or discretionary names). Higher beta implies a higher required return under CAPM, not necessarily a "better" stock.

**Q773.** What is the beta of cash and of the risk-free asset?

Cash and the risk-free asset have a beta of zero because their returns have essentially no covariance with the market. Under CAPM their required return is just Rf. This matters in portfolio aggregation: holding cash mechanically lowers a portfolio's beta, since portfolio beta is the value-weighted average of component betas, and the cash slice contributes zero.

**Q774.** How do you estimate beta in practice?

The standard approach regresses the stock's excess returns on the market's excess returns: `Ri - Rf = alpha + beta*(Rm - Rf) + e`, and the slope is beta. Practitioners commonly use 2–5 years of monthly data (or ~1–2 years of weekly) against a broad index (S&P 500 globally, Nifty 50 in India). Raw beta is often "adjusted" toward 1 using the Blume formula `adjusted beta = 0.67*raw + 0.33*1`, since betas tend to mean-revert. Estimates are sensitive to the index, frequency, and window chosen.

**Q775.** What is the difference between the Security Market Line (SML) and the Capital Market Line (CML)?

The CML plots expected return against total risk (standard deviation, sigma) and applies only to efficient portfolios — combinations of the risk-free asset and the market portfolio; its slope is the Sharpe ratio of the market. The SML plots expected return against systematic risk (beta) and applies to all assets and portfolios, efficient or not; its slope is the equity risk premium. The key contrast: CML uses total risk and holds only for efficient portfolios, while SML uses beta and prices every individual security.

**Q776.** What does it mean for a stock to plot above or below the SML?

The SML gives the CAPM-required return for a given beta. A stock plotting above the SML offers more return than required for its systematic risk — it is undervalued and has positive alpha. A stock below the line offers too little for its beta — overvalued, negative alpha. In equilibrium CAPM says all assets should lie exactly on the SML; deviations are the basis for active stock selection.

**Q777.** What is Jensen's alpha and what does a positive alpha indicate?

Jensen's alpha is the intercept from the CAPM regression — the return earned above (or below) what CAPM predicts for the portfolio's beta: `alpha = Rp - [Rf + beta*(Rm - Rf)]`. A positive alpha means the manager or asset delivered excess risk-adjusted return — skill, mispricing, or exposure to factors CAPM omits. Example: a fund returns 16% with beta 1.1 when Rf = 7% and Rm = 13%; CAPM expects 7% + 1.1*6% = 13.6%, so alpha = +2.4%.

**Q778.** What is the equity risk premium (ERP), and roughly what range is used?

The ERP is the extra return investors demand for holding equities over the risk-free asset: `ERP = E[Rm] - Rf`. It can be estimated historically (average realized equity return minus risk-free), via a forward-looking implied approach (e.g., dividend discount / Gordon model solved for the discount rate), or from surveys. Mature-market ERPs are commonly assumed around 4.5–6%; for India a country/equity risk premium is typically added, pushing total ERP higher (often ~7–9% depending on the source and date).

**Q779.** What is the difference between levered (equity) beta and unlevered (asset) beta?

Equity beta reflects both business risk and the amplification from financial leverage, while asset (unlevered) beta strips out leverage to capture pure business risk. They are linked by `beta_equity = beta_asset * [1 + (1 - Tc)*(D/E)]` (assuming debt beta ~ 0). More debt raises equity beta because fixed interest claims make residual equity cash flows more volatile. Asset beta lets you compare the underlying riskiness of firms with different capital structures.

**Q780.** Walk through unlevering and relevering beta for a comparable-company valuation.

To value a private or target firm you take listed comparables' equity betas, unlever each using `beta_asset = beta_equity / [1 + (1 - Tc)*(D/E)]`, average the asset betas, then relever to the target's own capital structure with `beta_equity = beta_asset * [1 + (1 - Tc)*(D/E_target)]`. Example: comp beta = 1.3, D/E = 0.5, Tc = 25% gives beta_asset = 1.3 / [1 + 0.75*0.5] = 1.3/1.375 = 0.945; relevering to a target D/E of 1.0 gives 0.945*[1 + 0.75*1.0] = 1.65. This isolates business risk before reapplying the target's leverage.

**Q781.** Should you assume debt beta is zero, and when does it matter?

Assuming debt beta = 0 is a common simplification (the Hamada relation), reasonable for investment-grade firms whose debt is nearly risk-free. But for highly levered or distressed companies, debt carries real market risk (debt beta > 0), and ignoring it overstates the asset beta and miscounts how risk shifts between debt and equity. A more complete relever uses `beta_asset = [E/(E+D)]*beta_equity + [D/(E+D)]*beta_debt`, allocating systematic risk across both claims.

**Q782.** What is the single-index (market) model, and how does it differ from CAPM?

The market model is the empirical regression `Ri = alpha_i + beta_i*Rm + e_i`, decomposing a security's return into a market-driven component and a firm-specific residual. It is a statistical/return-generating model that simplifies covariance estimation (only betas and one market variance are needed). CAPM is an equilibrium model predicting expected returns; it implies the intercept (in excess-return form) should be zero. The market model describes how returns behave; CAPM asserts what expected returns must be in equilibrium.

**Q783.** Under the single-index model, how is total risk decomposed?

Total variance splits into systematic and unsystematic parts: `Var(Ri) = beta_i^2 * Var(Rm) + Var(e_i)`. The first term is market (systematic) risk, the second is firm-specific (diversifiable) risk. As you add securities, the residual terms wash out and portfolio risk converges toward the systematic component. This is why diversification eliminates idiosyncratic risk but not market risk — and why only beta is priced.

**Q784.** What is Arbitrage Pricing Theory (APT) and how does it differ from CAPM?

APT, developed by Ross, says expected return is a linear function of multiple systematic risk factors: `E[Ri] = Rf + b_i1*RP1 + b_i2*RP2 + ... + b_ik*RPk`, where each b is the sensitivity to a factor and RP is that factor's risk premium. Unlike CAPM, it does not require the market portfolio, mean-variance investors, or normal returns — it rests on a no-arbitrage condition. Its weakness is that the theory does not specify which factors matter; they must be chosen empirically (e.g., inflation, industrial production, term and credit spreads).

**Q785.** What is the Fama-French three-factor model?

It extends CAPM with two empirically motivated factors: `E[Ri] - Rf = beta_m*(Rm - Rf) + s*SMB + h*HML`. SMB ("small minus big") captures the size premium — small caps historically outperforming large caps — and HML ("high minus low") captures the value premium — high book-to-market (value) stocks outperforming low book-to-market (growth) stocks. The model explains the cross-section of returns far better than CAPM alone, suggesting size and value proxy for systematic risks CAPM misses.

**Q786.** What did Fama and French add in the five-factor model?

The 2015 five-factor model adds profitability and investment to the three factors: `Rm - Rf`, SMB, HML, RMW, and CMA. RMW ("robust minus weak") is the return spread of firms with robust versus weak operating profitability; CMA ("conservative minus aggressive") is the spread of firms that invest conservatively versus aggressively. The intuition is that high-profitability, low-investment firms tend to earn higher returns. Notably, once RMW and CMA are included, HML often becomes redundant in explaining average returns.

**Q787.** What is the momentum factor and the Carhart four-factor model?

Momentum is the tendency of recent winners (past ~3–12 month returns) to keep outperforming recent losers over the next several months. Carhart (1997) added this as a fourth factor (UMD or WML, "up minus down"/"winners minus losers") to the Fama-French three-factor model: `... + beta_m*(Rm-Rf) + s*SMB + h*HML + w*WML`. It is widely used to evaluate fund performance because much apparent "skill" can be explained by passive exposure to the momentum factor.

**Q788.** What is Roll's critique of CAPM?

Roll (1977) argued that CAPM is not genuinely testable because the true "market portfolio" must include all risky assets — equities, bonds, real estate, human capital, etc. — which is unobservable. Any test uses a proxy (like the S&P 500 or Nifty), so a rejection of CAPM may simply mean the proxy is mean-variance inefficient, not that the model is wrong; conversely, if the proxy is efficient by construction, the SML relation holds tautologically. In short, tests of CAPM are really joint tests of the model and the chosen market proxy.

**Q789.** What is the low-beta (low-volatility) anomaly?

Empirically, low-beta stocks have delivered higher risk-adjusted returns than CAPM predicts, while high-beta stocks have underperformed — the realized SML is flatter than theory says. Explanations include leverage constraints (investors who cannot borrow bid up high-beta stocks to chase returns, depressing their future returns) and behavioral preferences for "lottery"-like volatile stocks. This anomaly underpins low-volatility and minimum-variance "smart beta" strategies.

**Q790.** Beyond Roll's critique, what are the main empirical failures of CAPM?

Studies find the relationship between beta and average returns is too flat (high-beta stocks earn less than predicted), and several characteristics — size, value (book-to-market), momentum, profitability, and low volatility — predict returns even after controlling for beta, which CAPM says should not happen. Fama and French's work showed beta adds little explanatory power once size and value are included. These findings motivated multifactor models as more complete descriptions of expected returns.

**Q791.** Define the Sharpe ratio and explain what it measures.

The Sharpe ratio is excess return per unit of total risk: `Sharpe = (Rp - Rf) / sigma_p`, where sigma_p is the portfolio's standard deviation. It tells you how much reward you earn for each unit of total volatility, making it useful for comparing standalone portfolios or for an investor's entire wealth. Example: a fund returning 14% with sigma = 12% when Rf = 7% has Sharpe = (14 - 7)/12 = 0.58. Higher is better; it is the slope of the line from the risk-free asset through the portfolio.

**Q792.** Define the Treynor ratio and contrast it with Sharpe.

The Treynor ratio is excess return per unit of systematic risk: `Treynor = (Rp - Rf) / beta_p`. It uses beta rather than total volatility, so it rewards return relative to market risk only. Use Treynor when the portfolio is one of several well-diversified sleeves within a larger fund (idiosyncratic risk already diversified away, so only beta matters); use Sharpe when the portfolio represents the investor's total holdings and total risk is what they bear. Example: Rp = 14%, Rf = 7%, beta = 1.1 gives Treynor = 7/1.1 = 6.36%.

**Q793.** What is the Information Ratio and how is it used?

The Information Ratio measures active return per unit of active risk: `IR = (Rp - Rb) / TE`, where Rb is the benchmark return and TE is the tracking error (the standard deviation of active returns Rp - Rb). It gauges the consistency and quality of a manager's outperformance relative to a benchmark. An IR around 0.5 is good and 1.0 is excellent. Example: active return of 3% with tracking error of 5% gives IR = 0.6.

**Q794.** What is tracking error, and what drives it?

Tracking error is the standard deviation of the difference between a portfolio's returns and its benchmark's returns: `TE = stdev(Rp - Rb)`. It quantifies how closely a portfolio follows its benchmark — a pure index fund targets near-zero TE, while a high-conviction active fund may run 6–10%. It is driven by active bets: sector tilts, factor exposures, position concentration, and cash holdings. Tracking error is the denominator of the Information Ratio and a core constraint in active mandates.

**Q795.** What is the Sortino ratio and why use it instead of Sharpe?

The Sortino ratio replaces total volatility with downside deviation, penalizing only returns below a target (often Rf or zero): `Sortino = (Rp - target) / downside_deviation`. The rationale is that investors dislike downside volatility, not upside, so penalizing both (as Sharpe does) can unfairly mark down strategies with large favorable swings. It is especially relevant for asymmetric or option-like return profiles where upside and downside risk differ materially.

**Q796.** What is M-squared (Modigliani-Modigliani) and what advantage does it offer?

M-squared restates the Sharpe ratio as a return figure by levering or de-levering the portfolio with the risk-free asset until its volatility matches the market's, then comparing that adjusted return to the market's: `M2 = Rf + Sharpe_p * sigma_m`. Because the answer is in percentage points (e.g., "the fund is 1.8% better than the market on a risk-adjusted basis"), it is more intuitive to communicate than a raw Sharpe number, while preserving the same ranking.

**Q797.** In active management, what is the distinction between alpha and beta?

Beta is the return earned from passive exposure to systematic risk factors (the market, or size/value/momentum tilts) — cheap and replicable via index or factor funds. Alpha is the genuine value a manager adds beyond those exposures — security selection or timing skill that cannot be captured passively. Performance attribution aims to separate the two, since investors should not pay active fees for returns that are really just packaged beta. The Information Ratio and Jensen's alpha are the standard tools for isolating true alpha.

**Q798.** What is "smart beta," and how does it relate to factor models and benchmark selection?

Smart beta refers to rules-based strategies that systematically tilt toward priced factors — value, size, momentum, quality, low volatility — instead of weighting by market capitalization, aiming to harvest factor premia at low cost. It sits between passive and active: the exposures come from research like Fama-French and Carhart, but they are delivered via transparent, index-like rules (e.g., the Nifty Alpha Low-Volatility 30 or value-weighted indices). This blurs benchmark selection: choosing an appropriate benchmark matters because a "smart beta" fund's apparent alpha against a cap-weighted index may simply be its deliberate factor exposure, which a factor-aware benchmark would reveal as beta, not skill.

<a name="G3"></a>

## G3. Asset & Investment Management  <span class="qrange">(Q799-Q831)</span>

**Q799.** What is the difference between active and passive investment management?

Passive management seeks to replicate a benchmark index (e.g. S&P 500, Nifty 50) at minimum cost, accepting market returns. Active management uses research, security selection and timing to try to beat the benchmark after fees. The trade-off: active offers the possibility of outperformance (alpha) but charges higher fees and incurs higher turnover/taxes, while passive locks in low cost and near-market returns.

**Q800.** Explain the "arithmetic of active management" and why most active funds underperform after fees.

William Sharpe's argument: before costs, the average actively managed dollar must earn exactly the market return, because all investors collectively hold the market. Since active investing costs more (fees, trading, taxes) than passive, the average actively managed dollar must underperform the average passive dollar AFTER costs. This is an accounting identity, not an empirical claim: e.g. SPIVA reports consistently show 70-90% of active large-cap funds trailing their index over 10-15 year horizons.

**Q801.** What does the SPIVA scorecard measure, and what is the typical finding?

SPIVA (S&P Indices Versus Active) tracks the percentage of actively managed funds that underperform their benchmark over various horizons, in the US, India and elsewhere. The consistent finding is that the majority underperform, and the share underperforming rises with the time horizon. Survivorship matters: many laggard funds are merged or closed, so reported active performance is flattered unless you correct for it.

**Q802.** Define strategic asset allocation versus tactical asset allocation.

Strategic asset allocation (SAA) sets the long-term target mix across asset classes (e.g. 60% equity / 40% bonds) based on the investor's objectives, risk tolerance and capital market expectations; it is the policy anchor. Tactical asset allocation (TAA) makes deliberate short-to-medium-term deviations from those targets to exploit perceived mispricings or cyclical views (e.g. overweighting equities when valuations are cheap). SAA drives most long-run risk and return; TAA is an active overlay that can add or subtract value.

**Q803.** What did the Brinson, Hood and Beebower studies conclude about asset allocation?

The 1986/1991 Brinson studies of US pension plans found that the policy (strategic) asset allocation explained about 90% of the variability of returns OVER TIME for a given fund. This is widely (and often loosely) cited as "asset allocation determines 90% of returns." Later work (Ibbotson & Kaplan, 2000) clarified the distinction: ~90% of the time-series variability, but only ~40% of the variation in returns ACROSS funds, and roughly 100% of the average return level.

**Q804.** Is the claim "asset allocation determines 90% of returns" accurate? Clarify the nuance.

It is a misinterpretation. Brinson's 90% refers to the variance of a single portfolio's returns over time being driven by its policy mix, not to the level of return. Across different funds, security selection and timing explain a larger share of the differences. The correct takeaway: asset allocation dominates the volatility experience and is the single most important decision, but it does not literally "determine 90% of the dollars you make."

**Q805.** What is an Investment Policy Statement (IPS) and why does it matter?

An IPS is the governing document that formalizes a client's objectives and constraints and the manager's responsibilities, serving as the blueprint for portfolio construction and a discipline against emotional decisions. It anchors the strategic asset allocation, defines the benchmark, and sets rebalancing and review procedures. In stressed markets it is the reference that prevents panic selling or style drift.

**Q806.** List the standard objectives and constraints captured in an IPS.

Objectives: return requirement and risk tolerance (ability + willingness to bear risk). Constraints, often remembered as "RR-TTLLU": Risk, Return, then Time horizon, Taxes, Liquidity, Legal/regulatory, and Unique circumstances (e.g. ESG screens, concentrated stock, religious restrictions like Shariah). A common mnemonic for the five constraints is TTLLU.

**Q807.** How do "ability" and "willingness" to take risk differ in an IPS, and which governs?

Ability to take risk is objective: time horizon, wealth relative to needs, liquidity demands, and income stability. Willingness is psychological and subjective: the client's stated comfort with volatility. When they conflict, the prudent rule is to adopt the LOWER of the two (the more conservative), while educating the client; you never let high willingness override low ability.

**Q808.** What is calendar rebalancing versus threshold (percentage-of-portfolio) rebalancing?

Calendar rebalancing restores target weights on a fixed schedule (e.g. quarterly or annually) regardless of market moves—simple but ignores intra-period drift. Threshold (or tolerance-band) rebalancing acts only when an asset's weight breaches a set corridor, e.g. a 5% absolute band so a 60% equity target triggers a trade at 55% or 65%. Threshold responds to actual risk drift; a hybrid (check on calendar, trade only if outside the band) is common in practice.

**Q809.** Why does disciplined rebalancing tend to add value, and what is its hidden cost?

Rebalancing forces "sell high, buy low" by trimming outperformers and topping up laggards, controlling risk and harvesting a small "rebalancing bonus" from mean-reverting, volatile assets. The hidden costs are transaction costs and, in taxable accounts, realized capital gains—so wider bands are used for taxable or illiquid portfolios. In a sustained trending market, frequent rebalancing can hurt by cutting winners too early.

**Q810.** What is a style box and how is it constructed?

Popularized by Morningstar, the equity style box is a 3x3 grid: the vertical axis is market capitalization (large / mid / small) and the horizontal axis is style (value / blend / growth). It lets investors see a fund's footprint and avoid unintended overlap or factor bets. The fixed-income version uses credit quality (high/medium/low) by interest-rate sensitivity (short/intermediate/long duration).

**Q811.** Distinguish value and growth investing styles.

Value investing buys stocks trading cheap relative to fundamentals—low P/E, low P/B, high dividend yield—betting on mean reversion or mispricing (Graham/Buffett lineage). Growth investing buys companies with high expected earnings/revenue growth, accepting rich multiples in anticipation of compounding. Value has a long-run premium in the Fama-French framework (the HML factor), though it underperformed growth through much of the 2010s.

**Q812.** What are the quality and momentum equity factors?

Quality favours firms with strong, stable profitability (high ROE/ROIC), low leverage, stable earnings and good accruals—it tends to be defensive. Momentum buys recent winners and sells recent losers, typically using the trailing 12-month return excluding the most recent month (12-1), exploiting return persistence. Momentum delivers high returns but suffers occasional sharp "crashes" during sharp market reversals, so it is often paired with low-volatility or quality screens.

**Q813.** What is GARP and the dividend strategy?

GARP (Growth At a Reasonable Price) blends growth and value—seeking above-average growth but only at undemanding valuations, often screened with the PEG ratio (P/E divided by earnings growth rate; PEG below ~1 is attractive). Dividend strategies target high or, better, growing and sustainable dividends as a source of income and a quality signal; "dividend aristocrats" are firms with decades of consecutive dividend increases. Watch payout ratios—an unsustainably high yield can signal distress (a value trap).

**Q814.** Explain bond ladder, barbell, and bullet strategies.

A ladder spreads bonds evenly across maturities (e.g. equal amounts maturing each year for 10 years), giving steady reinvestment and reduced timing risk. A bullet concentrates maturities around a single target date—useful for funding a known future liability. A barbell combines short and long maturities while avoiding the middle, giving liquidity at the short end plus yield/convexity at the long end; it outperforms a bullet of equal duration when the yield curve flattens non-parallel.

**Q815.** What is duration timing and credit strategy in fixed income?

Duration timing (rate anticipation) lengthens portfolio duration when you expect yields to fall (to maximize price gains) and shortens it when you expect yields to rise. Price sensitivity is approximated by: percent price change ≈ -ModifiedDuration * change_in_yield + 0.5*Convexity*(change_in_yield)^2. Credit strategy seeks excess return from spread—buying lower-rated or out-of-favour issuers expected to tighten or be upgraded, accepting default and liquidity risk.

**Q816.** Compare mutual funds, ETFs, and index funds on structure and cost.

An index fund is any fund (mutual or ETF) that passively tracks an index. A traditional mutual fund is bought/sold once daily at NAV directly with the fund; an ETF trades intraday on an exchange like a stock at a market price that can sit at a premium/discount to NAV. ETFs generally have lower expense ratios and use an in-kind creation/redemption mechanism that makes them more tax-efficient than equivalent mutual funds.

**Q817.** Why are ETFs typically more tax-efficient than mutual funds?

ETF shares are created and redeemed "in kind" with authorized participants—the fund hands over low-basis securities rather than selling them, so it rarely realizes capital gains that must be distributed to holders. A traditional open-end fund must sell securities to meet redemptions, triggering taxable distributions to all remaining holders. Note: this in-kind advantage is a US feature; in India both ETFs and mutual funds distribute, and taxation is at the investor's hands on sale.

**Q818.** What is tracking error and what drives it in an index product?

Tracking error is the standard deviation of the difference between the fund's return and its benchmark's return—a measure of how faithfully it replicates. It is driven by the expense ratio, cash drag, sampling vs full replication, transaction costs, securities-lending income, and dividend reinvestment timing. A good large-cap index fund may have tracking error well under 0.5% annually; higher tracking error signals either skill (for active) or inefficiency (for passive).

**Q819.** Distinguish open-end and closed-end funds.

An open-end fund continuously issues and redeems shares at NAV, so its size fluctuates with flows and it always trades at NAV. A closed-end fund (CEF) raises a fixed pool of capital via an IPO, then trades on an exchange; its market price can deviate—often at a persistent discount to NAV—driven by sentiment, liquidity and distribution policy. CEFs can stay fully invested in illiquid assets without redemption pressure, which is why the structure suits less-liquid strategies.

**Q820.** Describe long/short equity and equity market-neutral hedge fund strategies.

Long/short equity holds long positions in expected winners and short positions in expected losers, typically keeping a net long bias and aiming to profit from stock selection on both sides. Equity market-neutral tightens this to roughly zero net market exposure (beta ≈ 0), often dollar- and sector-neutral, so returns come almost purely from the long-short spread (alpha), not market direction. Market-neutral has lower volatility but relies heavily on leverage to generate meaningful returns.

**Q821.** What is global macro and managed futures (CTA) investing?

Global macro takes directional or relative-value positions across equities, rates, currencies and commodities based on top-down macroeconomic views, using cash and derivatives, often with leverage (e.g. shorting a currency on a balance-of-payments thesis). CTAs/managed futures are usually systematic trend-followers trading futures across asset classes; they tend to be long volatility and provide "crisis alpha," performing well in sustained trends like 2008 or 2022. Both can be highly diversifying because their returns have low correlation to equities.

**Q822.** Explain event-driven, merger arbitrage, and distressed strategies.

Event-driven strategies profit from corporate events—mergers, spin-offs, restructurings—where the catalyst, not the market, drives returns. Merger (risk) arbitrage typically buys the target and shorts the acquirer in a stock deal, capturing the spread between the deal price and current price as compensation for deal-break risk. Distressed investing buys deeply discounted debt/equity of firms near or in bankruptcy, aiming to profit from restructuring or recovery—illiquid, legally intensive, and long-horizon.

**Q823.** What is relative value, and how do quant strategies fit in?

Relative value seeks to profit from price discrepancies between related securities (e.g. convertible arbitrage, fixed-income arbitrage, capital-structure arb), typically hedging out market direction and earning small, leveraged spreads as mispricings converge. Quant strategies use systematic, data-driven models—statistical arbitrage, factor models, machine learning—to trade large baskets, relying on diversification across many small bets. Both are vulnerable to crowding and deleveraging shocks (e.g. the August 2007 "quant quake").

**Q824.** Explain the 2-and-20 fee model and a high-water mark.

"2-and-20" is the classic hedge fund/PE fee: a 2% annual management fee on assets plus a 20% performance (incentive) fee on profits. A high-water mark ensures the manager earns the 20% only on NEW net profits above the highest prior NAV—so after a loss, gains that merely recover the loss are not charged again. Example: a $100m fund rising to $120m earns 0.2*$20m = $4m incentive; if it then falls to $110m and recovers to $120m, no new incentive is due until NAV exceeds $120m.

**Q825.** What is a hurdle rate, and how do lock-ups and gates relate to liquidity?

A hurdle rate is a minimum return (e.g. a cash benchmark or fixed %) that must be cleared before the performance fee applies. A lock-up is a period (often 1-2 years) during which investors cannot redeem; gates limit the percentage of the fund that can be redeemed in any single period. These illiquidity terms let managers hold less-liquid, higher-return positions without forced selling—the investor is, in effect, paid an illiquidity premium for accepting them.

**Q826.** How do leverage and short selling amplify hedge fund returns and risks?

Leverage (borrowing or derivatives) multiplies both gains and losses on a position; gross exposure of 200/100 (200% long, 100% short) means 300% gross on 100% capital. Short selling sells borrowed securities to profit from declines, but losses are theoretically unlimited as the price can rise indefinitely, and shorts face borrow costs and squeeze risk. The combination is why even market-neutral funds can blow up: small adverse moves are magnified, and margin calls force deleveraging at the worst time.

**Q827.** What is portfolio risk budgeting?

Risk budgeting allocates a portfolio's total risk (rather than its capital) across positions or strategies, recognizing that a 5% capital weight in a volatile asset may consume far more than 5% of total risk. A common metric is each position's marginal/component contribution to total volatility (sum of component contributions = total portfolio volatility). Risk parity is the limiting case—sizing assets so each contributes equal risk, which typically means levering low-volatility bonds up to equity-like risk.

**Q828.** Outline ESG integration in investment mandates and the main approaches.

ESG integration systematically incorporates Environmental, Social and Governance factors into security analysis and portfolio construction—either as risk mitigation or to capture sustainability-linked alpha. Approaches range from negative screening (excluding tobacco, weapons, coal), to positive/best-in-class tilts, thematic investing, and active stewardship/engagement and proxy voting. Key challenges are inconsistent ESG ratings, greenwashing, and regulation (EU SFDR disclosure tiers; in India SEBI's BRSR Core and ESG mutual-fund sub-category rules).

**Q829.** Summarize the SEBI mutual fund framework and the PMS/AIF structures in India.

Indian mutual funds are regulated by SEBI under the 1996 MF Regulations, with a three-tier sponsor-trustee-AMC structure, standardized scheme categories, a TER cap, and mandatory daily NAV disclosure; distribution is direct (no commission) or regular plans. PMS (Portfolio Management Services) offers personalized, discretionary portfolios with a minimum investment of Rs 50 lakh. AIFs (Alternative Investment Funds) are pooled vehicles in three categories—Category I (VC, infrastructure, social), Category II (PE, debt funds), Category III (hedge-fund-style, long-short)—with a minimum commitment of Rs 1 crore, aimed at sophisticated investors.

**Q830.** What is goal-based investing and how does it differ from traditional portfolio theory?

Goal-based investing organizes wealth into separate "buckets," each mapped to a specific goal (retirement, child's education, home) with its own time horizon, target amount and risk level, rather than optimizing one aggregate portfolio against a single risk-tolerance number. It reframes risk as "probability of missing the goal" instead of return volatility, which improves client behaviour and discipline. It draws on mental-accounting and behavioural-portfolio theory and underpins much of modern wealth management and goal-planning advice.

**Q831.** Explain dollar-cost averaging / SIP and its mathematical edge.

Dollar-cost averaging (in India, a Systematic Investment Plan or SIP) invests a fixed amount at regular intervals regardless of price, so you automatically buy more units when prices are low and fewer when high. Because a fixed sum buys a varying number of units, the average COST per unit is the harmonic mean of prices, which is always less than or equal to the arithmetic average price paid. Example: investing Rs 6,000 monthly at NAVs of 100 then 150 buys 60+40=100 units for Rs 12,000—average cost Rs 120, below the Rs 125 simple-average price; SIPs also reduce timing risk and instill discipline.

<a name="G4"></a>

## G4. Market Efficiency & Behavioral Finance  <span class="qrange">(Q832-Q856)</span>

**Q832.** What is the Efficient Market Hypothesis (EMH) in one sentence?

The EMH states that asset prices fully and rapidly reflect all available information, so securities are always fairly priced given what is known and you cannot consistently earn risk-adjusted excess returns (alpha) by trading on that information. It implies that any new information is incorporated into prices almost instantly, leaving no predictable, exploitable patterns. Eugene Fama formalized the idea in 1970.

**Q833.** What are the three forms of EMH and what information does each say is already priced?

Weak form: prices reflect all past price and volume data, so technical analysis cannot add value. Semi-strong form: prices reflect all publicly available information (financials, news, analyst reports, macro data), so neither technical nor fundamental analysis on public data helps. Strong form: prices reflect all information, public and private/insider, so even insiders cannot beat the market. The forms are nested — strong implies semi-strong implies weak.

**Q834.** If markets are weak-form efficient, which type of analysis is rendered useless and why?

Technical analysis, because it relies solely on historical prices, volume, and chart patterns to predict future moves. If all past price information is already embedded in the current price, past patterns carry no predictive power and price changes follow a random walk. Most academic evidence supports weak-form efficiency in major liquid markets, including the NSE and BSE for large-cap stocks.

**Q835.** What does the random walk hypothesis say about stock prices?

It says successive price changes are independent and identically distributed, so the next move is unpredictable and prices wander randomly: P(t) = P(t-1) + drift + random error. A random walk is a sufficient (though stronger) condition for weak-form efficiency. The practical implication is that the best forecast of tomorrow's price is today's price plus a small expected drift, not any chart pattern.

**Q836.** What are the implications of EMH for active fund management?

If markets are efficient, active managers cannot reliably beat the market after fees, so the rational strategy is low-cost passive indexing. This underpins the rise of index funds and ETFs; SPIVA scorecards show the majority of active large-cap funds underperform their benchmark over 10-15 years in the US and increasingly in India. EMH does not say active managers are stupid — it says competition among them is exactly what keeps prices efficient.

**Q837.** Under semi-strong efficiency, can you profit by trading immediately after a company's earnings announcement?

In a strictly semi-strong efficient market, no — prices adjust to public news so fast that by the time you trade, the information is already priced in and you earn only a normal return. In reality, the post-earnings-announcement drift anomaly shows prices keep drifting in the direction of the surprise for weeks, which is evidence against perfect semi-strong efficiency. This drift is one of the most robust and persistent anomalies documented globally.

**Q838.** What is the joint-hypothesis problem in testing market efficiency?

Any test of market efficiency is simultaneously a test of efficiency AND of the asset-pricing model used to define "normal" expected returns. If you find abnormal returns, you cannot tell whether the market is inefficient or whether your risk model (e.g., CAPM) is simply wrong/incomplete. Fama emphasized this means market efficiency per se is not testable in isolation — a rejection could just mean a bad benchmark for risk.

**Q839.** What is the size effect anomaly?

Documented by Banz (1981), it is the historical tendency of small-capitalization stocks to earn higher average returns than large-cap stocks, even after adjusting for beta. It became the SMB (small-minus-big) factor in the Fama-French model. Its strength has weakened since publication, and much of the premium concentrates in tiny, illiquid stocks, raising the question of whether it is a true risk premium or a transaction-cost mirage.

**Q840.** What is the value effect, and is it considered a risk premium or a mispricing?

The value effect is the tendency of value stocks (high book-to-market, low P/E) to outperform growth stocks over long horizons; it is the HML (high-minus-low) factor in Fama-French. Fama-French argue it is compensation for risk (distress/financial leverage), while behavioralists argue it reflects investors over-extrapolating growth and overpaying for glamour stocks. Value suffered a long drought in the 2010s, reviving the debate over whether it has been arbitraged away.

**Q841.** What is the momentum anomaly?

Momentum (Jegadeesh-Titman, 1993) is the tendency of stocks that performed well over the past 3-12 months to keep outperforming over the next 3-12 months, and losers to keep losing. It is hard to reconcile with EMH and with risk-based explanations, making it a leading exhibit for behavioral finance (under-reaction then over-reaction). It works across markets and asset classes but suffers occasional sharp "momentum crashes" during market reversals.

**Q842.** What is the low-volatility anomaly and why is it puzzling?

It is the empirical finding that low-volatility/low-beta stocks deliver returns as high as or higher than high-volatility stocks on a risk-adjusted basis, directly contradicting CAPM's prediction that more risk should earn more return. Explanations include leverage constraints (investors who cannot borrow bid up risky stocks) and a lottery-preference for volatile names. It has spawned "min-vol" and "low-vol" smart-beta ETFs globally.

**Q843.** What is the January effect and does it still survive?

The January effect is the historical tendency for small-cap stocks to earn unusually high returns in early January, often linked to tax-loss selling in December followed by repurchasing. It is a calendar anomaly; like many such effects it has largely faded or reversed once widely publicized, consistent with the idea that arbitrage erodes anomalies once known. Its decay is often cited as evidence the market adapts.

**Q844.** What does the disappearance of many anomalies after publication tell us about EMH?

McLean and Pontiff (2016) showed that documented anomalies' returns decline roughly 30-60% after publication, as arbitrageurs trade against them. This supports an "adaptive" view: markets are not perfectly efficient, but they self-correct as profitable patterns become known, pushing returns back toward normal. It is a middle ground — anomalies are real but often not durable enough to exploit net of costs.

**Q845.** What is the Grossman-Stiglitz paradox?

Grossman and Stiglitz (1980) argued that if prices perfectly reflected all information, no one would have any incentive to spend money gathering information, since they could free-ride on prices. But then prices could not reflect information no one collected — a contradiction. The resolution is that markets must be "efficiently inefficient": small persistent mispricings must exist to reward information gatherers, so perfect efficiency is impossible in equilibrium.

**Q846.** What is overconfidence bias and how does it harm investors?

Overconfidence is the tendency to overestimate the accuracy of one's knowledge and predictions. It leads investors to trade too much and under-diversify; Barber and Odean found the most active retail traders underperform the market by several percent a year, largely due to transaction costs. Example: a trader who, after a few winning F&O trades on the Nifty, scales up position size believing they have an edge, only to give back gains.

**Q847.** What is anchoring bias, with an example?

Anchoring is over-relying on an initial reference number when making estimates. Example: an investor refuses to sell a stock bought at INR 500 even after fundamentals deteriorate, because they anchor on the purchase price and "want to get back to even." Analysts also anchor target prices to current prices or to round numbers, causing sluggish revisions when new information arrives.

**Q848.** What is loss aversion and how does prospect theory describe it?

Loss aversion (Kahneman and Tversky) is the finding that losses hurt roughly twice as much as equivalent gains feel good — the value function is steeper for losses. Prospect theory says people evaluate outcomes as gains/losses relative to a reference point (not absolute wealth), are risk-averse in gains but risk-seeking in losses, and overweight small probabilities. Practically, a INR 1,000 loss causes about as much pain as a INR 2,000 gain gives pleasure.

**Q849.** What is the disposition effect and how is it linked to prospect theory?

The disposition effect is the tendency to sell winners too early and hold losers too long. It flows from prospect theory: investors are risk-averse over gains (lock in the sure win) but risk-seeking over losses (gamble to avoid realizing a loss). It is tax-inefficient (you defer gains and pay tax sooner) and empirically reduces returns; it is widely observed among Indian and global retail investors.

**Q850.** What is mental accounting, with an example?

Mental accounting (Thaler) is treating money differently depending on arbitrary categories rather than recognizing money is fungible. Example: an investor keeps a "safe" fixed deposit earning 6% while simultaneously carrying credit-card debt at 36%, because they mentally separate "savings" from "debt." Another: treating dividend income as spendable "income" while refusing to sell a tiny slice of principal that is economically identical.

**Q851.** What is herding behavior and how does it affect markets?

Herding is following the crowd rather than one's own analysis, often from a fear of missing out or safety in numbers. It amplifies bubbles (everyone piles into hot stocks) and crashes (everyone sells together), increasing volatility and correlation. Example: retail investors rushing into a hyped IPO or a meme/penny stock purely because others are buying, ignoring valuation.

**Q852.** Distinguish confirmation bias, recency bias, and the availability heuristic.

Confirmation bias: seeking and weighting only evidence that supports your existing view (e.g., a bull reading only bullish research on a held stock). Recency bias: overweighting recent events, like assuming a market that rose all year will keep rising. Availability heuristic: judging probability by how easily examples come to mind, e.g., overestimating crash risk right after a vivid market crash because it is fresh in memory.

**Q853.** What is framing bias, with an example?

Framing is letting the way information is presented — not its substance — change your decision. Example: investors react more positively to a fund described as having a "90% success rate" than one with a "10% failure rate," though they are identical. In product sales, "this insurance protects your family" (gain frame) sells better than the economically equivalent "if you die uninsured your family suffers."

**Q854.** What are limits to arbitrage and why can't smart money always correct mispricing?

Limits to arbitrage are the frictions that stop rational traders from eliminating mispricings: fundamental risk (the asset's value can move against you), noise-trader risk (mispricing can worsen before it corrects — "the market can stay irrational longer than you can stay solvent"), short-selling constraints and costs, capital constraints, and career/redemption risk. Shleifer and Vishny showed arbitrageurs face margin calls and client withdrawals exactly when mispricings are widest, forcing them to unwind rather than press the bet.

**Q855.** Who are noise traders and what role do they play in behavioral models?

Noise traders are investors who trade on sentiment, rumor, or psychological biases rather than fundamental information. In the DeLong-Shleifer-Summers-Waldmann model, their unpredictable sentiment creates "noise-trader risk" that deters rational arbitrageurs, so noise traders can persist and even earn higher returns for bearing the risk they themselves create. Their presence helps explain why prices deviate from fundamentals and why those deviations are not quickly erased.

**Q856.** Explain the equity premium puzzle, behavioral drivers of bubbles, and the Adaptive Markets Hypothesis together.

The equity premium puzzle (Mehra-Prescott) is that the historical excess return of stocks over bonds (~5-7% in the US) is far too large to be justified by standard risk-aversion models; behavioral explanations include loss aversion plus "myopic" frequent evaluation (Benartzi-Thaler), which makes investors demand a big premium to hold volatile stocks. The same biases — herding, overconfidence, extrapolation and weak limits to arbitrage — drove historical bubbles like Dutch tulip mania (1637), the dot-com bubble (2000), and the 2008 housing/credit crisis, where prices detached from fundamentals before crashing. Andrew Lo's Adaptive Markets Hypothesis reconciles EMH and behavioral finance by treating markets as evolving ecosystems where efficiency is not absolute but depends on competition, adaptation, and changing conditions — explaining why anomalies appear, get arbitraged away, and why contrarian and value strategies (buying what fearful crowds oversell) can pay off over full cycles.

<a name="secH"></a>

# H. Markets & Instruments

<a name="H1"></a>

## H1. Fixed Income (Advanced)  <span class="qrange">(Q857-Q888)</span>

**Q857.** What is the term structure of interest rates, and what does a "normal" vs "inverted" yield curve signal?

The term structure is the relationship between yield and time to maturity for bonds of identical credit quality (e.g., G-Secs or US Treasuries), plotted as the yield curve. A normal (upward-sloping) curve has long yields above short yields, reflecting term premium and expectations of stable/rising rates; an inverted curve (short above long) has historically preceded recessions because markets expect future rate cuts. A flat curve signals transition or uncertainty. The curve is the backbone for discounting, relative-value, and forward-rate analysis.

**Q858.** State the pure expectations theory of the term structure and its key limitation.

Pure (unbiased) expectations theory says long-term rates are purely the geometric average of expected future short-term rates, so forward rates are unbiased predictors of future spot rates: (1+s2)^2 = (1+s1)*(1+E[r1,2]). Its limitation is that it ignores risk premia — it implies investors are indifferent between holding one long bond or rolling short bonds, which contradicts the observed average upward slope and the fact that long bonds carry more price risk. Empirically forwards are biased high as predictors, motivating the liquidity-premium refinement.

**Q859.** Contrast the liquidity preference theory with the market segmentation and preferred habitat theories.

Liquidity preference theory adds a positive, maturity-increasing term premium to expectations: forward rate = expected future spot + liquidity premium, explaining the typical upward slope even when rates are expected to be flat. Market segmentation theory says yields at each maturity are set by independent supply/demand of distinct investor clienteles (banks short, insurers/pension funds long) with no substitution across maturities. Preferred habitat is the middle ground: investors have a preferred maturity but will move out of it if compensated by a sufficient yield premium, so the curve reflects both expectations and supply/demand imbalances.

**Q860.** Define spot rate, par yield, and forward rate, and explain how they differ.

A spot rate s_n is the yield on a zero-coupon bond maturing in n periods — the rate to discount a single cash flow at time n. A par yield is the coupon rate that prices a coupon bond exactly at par (100) given the spot curve; it is a weighted blend of spot rates. A forward rate f(a,b) is the rate, agreed today, for borrowing/lending between two future dates a and b, implied by today's spot rates. When the spot curve is upward sloping, forwards lie above spots, which lie above par yields.

**Q861.** How do you bootstrap the spot (zero) curve from coupon-bond or par-yield data?

Bootstrapping solves for spot rates sequentially from shortest to longest maturity, using each newly found spot to discount earlier coupons of the next bond. For a 2-year par bond at coupon c with annual coupons: 100 = c/(1+s1) + (100+c)/(1+s2)^2, solve for s2 after s1 is known from the 1-year. Iterating outward recovers the full zero curve consistent with observed prices. This zero curve, not the coupon yields, is what should be used to value arbitrary cash-flow streams (no-arbitrage pricing).

**Q862.** Given s1 = 5% and s2 = 6% (annual), compute the implied 1-year forward rate one year from now, f(1,2).

Use (1+s2)^2 = (1+s1)*(1+f(1,2)), so 1+f(1,2) = (1.06)^2 / 1.05 = 1.1236 / 1.05 = 1.07010. Thus f(1,2) = 7.01%. The forward sits above both spots because the spot curve is rising — the second-year rate must exceed s2 to pull the average up from s1.

**Q863.** Why are forward rates "locked in" today, and how would you arbitrage a mispriced forward?

By buying a 2-year zero and shorting (or borrowing via) a 1-year zero in the right proportions, an investor can synthetically guarantee a known reinvestment rate for year 2 — that is the implied forward, enforced by no-arbitrage. If a traded forward/FRA differs from the implied f(1,2), you go long the cheap leg and short the rich leg (cash-and-carry): e.g., borrow 1-year, lend 2-year, and lock the FRA to capture the spread risk-free. In INR markets this shows up in OIS/MIBOR FRA vs G-Sec implied forwards.

**Q864.** What is the par yield curve and why is it not the same as the coupon yields of on-the-run bonds?

The par yield curve gives, for each maturity, the single coupon rate that makes a bond price exactly to par given the underlying zero curve: 100 = sum[ c/(1+s_t)^t ] + 100/(1+s_n)^n. It is a synthetic construct used to read off "the coupon you'd need to issue at par today" at any maturity. It differs from actual on-the-run yields because real bonds rarely price exactly at par and carry liquidity/coupon-effect differences; the par curve removes those by construction.

**Q865.** Distinguish modified duration from effective duration and say when each is appropriate.

Modified duration = Macaulay duration / (1 + y/k) and assumes fixed cash flows, so it is exact for option-free bonds. Effective duration uses a parallel shift of the benchmark curve and reprices with a model: EffDur = (P(-dy) - P(+dy)) / (2 * P0 * dy), capturing how cash flows themselves change when rates move. Use modified duration for plain vanilla bonds and effective duration for bonds with embedded options (callable, putable, MBS) where cash flows are rate-dependent.

**Q866.** Compute effective duration given P0 = 100.00, P(+25bp) = 98.30, P(-25bp) = 101.80.

EffDur = (P(-dy) - P(+dy)) / (2 * P0 * dy) = (101.80 - 98.30) / (2 * 100.00 * 0.0025) = 3.50 / 0.50 = 7.0. So a 100bp parallel shift moves price by roughly 7%. Because we reprice with the model on each side, this automatically reflects any optionality embedded in the bond.

**Q867.** What are key-rate (partial) durations and why do they matter beyond a single effective duration?

Key-rate durations measure price sensitivity to a 1bp shift at specific maturity points on the curve (e.g., 2y, 5y, 10y, 30y) while holding other points fixed, so they capture exposure to non-parallel moves (steepening, flattening, twists). A single effective duration assumes parallel shifts and can hide curve risk: two portfolios with identical total duration can react very differently to a flattener. Summing the key-rate durations recovers the overall effective duration.

**Q868.** How is portfolio duration computed, and what is its main assumption?

Portfolio duration is the market-value-weighted average of the component durations: D_p = sum[ w_i * D_i ], where w_i = market value of bond i / total portfolio value. For example, 60% in a 3-year-duration bond and 40% in an 8-year gives 0.6*3 + 0.4*8 = 5.0. The key assumption is a parallel shift of the yield curve; it understates risk when yields move by different amounts across maturities, which is where key-rate durations are needed.

**Q869.** What is immunization, and what are the conditions to immunize a single liability?

Immunization structures a bond portfolio so its value moves in step with a target liability, locking in a return regardless of rate moves. The classic conditions: (1) portfolio Macaulay duration equals the liability's horizon, (2) present value of assets >= present value of the liability, and (3) minimize convexity dispersion of asset cash flows around the horizon to reduce structural risk. Duration matching balances price risk and reinvestment risk so they offset at the horizon date.

**Q870.** Why does duration matching alone not fully immunize against non-parallel curve shifts, and what improves it?

Duration matching protects against small parallel shifts because price and reinvestment effects offset only at first order. Non-parallel shifts (twists/butterflies) break this, since cash flows clustered far from the liability date get re-priced differently — barbell structures with high dispersion are especially exposed. Matching also higher moments (convexity/dispersion) and minimizing the spread of asset cash flow maturities around the liability, or using key-rate duration matching, improves robustness. Rebalancing is required as durations drift with time and rates.

**Q871.** Define dollar duration (DV01/PV01) and show how to hedge a position with it.

Dollar duration (a.k.a. DV01 or PV01) is the change in a position's value for a 1bp yield move: DV01 = ModDur * Price * 0.0001 (per 100 face, then scaled by position). To hedge, set the notional of the hedge instrument so its DV01 offsets the position's: hedge notional = portfolio DV01 / hedge-per-unit DV01. E.g., a position with DV01 of INR 50,000 hedged with a futures contract worth INR 25 DV01 needs 2,000 contracts; in the US you'd hedge with Treasury futures or the cheapest-to-deliver.

**Q872.** A 10-year bond has modified duration 8.2 and price 96. What is its DV01 per INR 100 face, and the P&L on INR 50 crore for a +15bp move?

DV01 = ModDur * Price * 0.0001 = 8.2 * 96 * 0.0001 = 0.07872 per 100 face. On INR 50 crore face that scales by 50,00,00,000/100 = 50,00,000 units, so DV01 = 0.07872 * 50,00,000 = INR 3,93,600 per bp. A +15bp move loses about 15 * 3,93,600 = INR 59.0 lakh (long bond, rates up). This linear estimate ignores convexity, which would slightly reduce the actual loss.

**Q873.** Write the second-order (duration + convexity) price-change formula and explain each term.

Percentage price change ≈ -ModDur * dy + 0.5 * Convexity * dy^2, where dy is the yield change in decimal. The first term is the linear duration estimate (negative slope of the price-yield curve); the second is the convexity correction, which is always positive for option-free bonds because the price-yield relationship is curved (convex). Convexity makes the duration estimate too pessimistic for falling yields and too optimistic for rising yields, so the adjustment improves accuracy for large dy.

**Q874.** With ModDur = 7.0 and Convexity = 65, estimate the price change for a -100bp move, including convexity.

dy = -0.01. Duration term = -7.0 * (-0.01) = +0.070 (= +7.0%). Convexity term = 0.5 * 65 * (-0.01)^2 = 0.5 * 65 * 0.0001 = +0.00325 (= +0.325%). Total ≈ +7.325%, versus +7.0% from duration alone — convexity adds about 33bp of price gain on a large favorable move. For a +100bp move convexity would instead cushion the loss to about -6.675%.

**Q875.** Why do callable bonds exhibit negative convexity, and over what yield region?

A callable bond gives the issuer the right to redeem early, so when yields fall and the embedded call moves into the money, price appreciation is capped near the call price ("price compression") — the price-yield curve bends the wrong way, giving negative convexity. This occurs in the low-yield region where the call is likely to be exercised; at high yields the bond behaves like an option-free bond with positive convexity. The investor is short a call, so they earn a higher yield as compensation.

**Q876.** How does a putable bond's convexity and value differ from a callable bond's?

A putable bond lets the holder sell it back at a set price when yields rise, putting a floor under the price, so the holder is long a put: putable value = straight bond + put value, and it exhibits more positive convexity than an option-free bond at high yields. A callable bond holder is short a call: callable value = straight bond - call value, producing negative convexity at low yields. Hence putables trade at lower yields (holder pays for protection) while callables trade at higher yields.

**Q877.** Define nominal spread, I-spread, Z-spread, and OAS, and order them by sophistication.

Nominal spread is yield-to-maturity over the matching-maturity benchmark (single point). I-spread is the bond's yield over the interpolated swap rate of equal maturity. Z-spread (zero-volatility spread) is the constant spread added to every spot rate so discounted cash flows equal the market price — it accounts for the whole curve. OAS (option-adjusted spread) removes embedded-option value by averaging across interest-rate paths in a model: it is the Z-spread net of option cost, and is the cleanest measure for comparing bonds with optionality.

**Q878.** What is the relationship between Z-spread and OAS for callable vs putable bonds?

For a callable bond, OAS = Z-spread - call option cost (in bp), so OAS < Z-spread, because part of the apparently high Z-spread is just compensation for being short the call rather than true credit/liquidity spread. For a putable bond the holder owns the option, so OAS = Z-spread + put value benefit and OAS > Z-spread. For an option-free bond, OAS = Z-spread. OAS thus lets you compare a callable, a putable, and a bullet on an apples-to-apples basis.

**Q879.** What are mortgage-backed securities (MBS) and the prepayment risk they carry?

An MBS is a pass-through security backed by a pool of mortgages whose principal and interest are passed to investors; in the US these are issued by agencies (GNMA/FNMA/FHLMC). Prepayment risk is the homeowner's option to repay early: when rates fall, refinancing accelerates prepayments (contraction risk — cash comes back to be reinvested at low rates), and when rates rise prepayments slow (extension risk — duration lengthens just as rates rise). This embedded short call gives MBS negative convexity, modeled via measures like PSA/CPR.

**Q880.** Explain CPR, SMM, and the PSA benchmark used to model prepayments.

CPR (conditional prepayment rate) is the annualized fraction of the remaining pool expected to prepay; SMM (single monthly mortality) is its monthly equivalent: SMM = 1 - (1 - CPR)^(1/12). The PSA benchmark assumes CPR ramps linearly from 0.2% in month 1 by 0.2%/month to 6% at month 30, then stays flat — "100 PSA"; faster/slower speeds are quoted as multiples (e.g., 150 PSA, 50 PSA). These drive the timing of principal cash flows and hence average life and effective duration.

**Q881.** What is securitization and tranching, and how do senior/mezzanine/equity tranches differ?

Securitization pools illiquid assets (auto loans, credit-card receivables, mortgages) into an SPV that issues asset-backed securities; tranching slices the SPV's cash flows into a credit waterfall. The senior tranche is paid first and absorbs losses last (highest rating, lowest yield); mezzanine sits in the middle; the equity/first-loss tranche absorbs initial losses and earns the residual/highest yield. Subordination and overcollateralization provide credit enhancement so senior tranches can be rated above the underlying pool.

**Q882.** How did CDO tranching contribute to the 2008 financial crisis?

Subprime mortgages were pooled into RMBS, then the risky mezzanine tranches were re-securitized into CDOs and re-tranched, with the new senior pieces rated AAA on flawed assumptions of low, uncorrelated default correlation. When house prices fell nationally, defaults became highly correlated, so losses blew through subordination and reached supposedly safe senior/super-senior tranches. Mispriced correlation, rating-agency conflicts, and leverage turned a housing downturn into a systemic credit collapse.

**Q883.** For a convertible bond, define conversion ratio, conversion (parity) value, and conversion premium with a quick example.

Conversion ratio is the number of shares received per bond; conversion (parity) value = conversion ratio * current share price. Conversion premium = (convertible price - parity value) / parity value, the extra paid for the bond's downside protection and optionality. Example: ratio 20, share price INR 45 gives parity = 20*45 = INR 900; if the convertible trades at INR 1,000, the premium is 100/900 = 11.1%. The conversion price = par / ratio = 1000/20 = INR 50 is the breakeven share price.

**Q884.** How do you value a convertible bond and what embedded options does it contain?

A convertible's value has a floor at the greater of its straight-bond value (investment value) and its conversion/parity value, plus the time value of the embedded equity call option: convertible ≈ straight bond + call on the stock. Many are also callable by the issuer (an embedded short call to the holder) to force conversion, and some are putable. It behaves like debt when the stock is low (bond floor dominates) and like equity when the stock is high (delta approaches the conversion ratio).

**Q885.** How does a floating-rate note (FRN) work, and why is its price near par with low duration?

An FRN pays a coupon that resets each period to a reference rate plus a quoted margin (e.g., 3M T-Bill / MIBOR / SOFR + 80bp), so the coupon tracks market rates. Because the coupon resets, interest-rate (duration) risk is limited to the short period until the next reset, keeping price close to par; its effective rate duration is roughly the time to next reset. Price still moves if the issuer's credit (required margin) changes — captured by spread duration rather than rate duration.

**Q886.** Define credit spread and spread duration, and quantify a spread move's price impact.

Credit spread is the yield premium over the risk-free/benchmark curve compensating for default and liquidity risk; spread duration measures price sensitivity to a change in that spread: %ΔPrice ≈ -SpreadDur * Δspread. For a bond with spread duration 6, a +50bp widening costs about -6 * 0.005 = -3.0%. For fixed-rate bonds spread duration ≈ modified duration, but for FRNs rate duration is tiny while spread duration remains full-term, which is why FRNs still carry meaningful credit risk.

**Q887.** How do inflation-linked bonds like US TIPS and India's IIBs work, and what is the breakeven inflation rate?

Inflation-linked bonds protect real purchasing power: US TIPS index the principal to CPI and pay a fixed real coupon on the inflated principal, so investors earn a real yield. India's Inflation Indexed Bonds (IIBs) similarly link principal to WPI/CPI (the retail CGB-linked IINSS-C used CPI). Breakeven inflation = nominal Treasury yield - real TIPS yield; if 10y nominal is 4.3% and 10y TIPS real is 1.8%, breakeven ≈ 2.5%, and you prefer TIPS when you expect realized inflation to exceed breakeven.

**Q888.** Describe the Indian G-Sec yield curve and how corporate bond spreads are quoted against it.

The G-Sec curve is built from sovereign rupee bonds across tenors (benchmarked by the liquid 10-year on-the-run G-Sec), with the curve published via CCIL/FBIL and yields driven by RBI policy (repo rate), inflation, and the fiscal borrowing calendar. AAA PSU and corporate bonds are quoted as a spread over the matching-tenor G-Sec (e.g., AAA 10y at G-Sec + 60-90bp), widening down the rating ladder (AA, A) and in liquidity stress. SDLs (state development loans) trade at a spread above central G-Secs reflecting state credit and lower liquidity.

<a name="H2"></a>

## H2. Swaps, Interest-Rate & FX Derivatives  <span class="qrange">(Q889-Q918)</span>

**Q889.** What is an interest-rate swap (IRS)?

An interest-rate swap is an OTC contract in which two parties agree to exchange interest payments on a notional principal: typically one party pays a fixed rate while the other pays a floating rate (e.g. SOFR, EURIBOR, or in India MIBOR/MIFOR). Only interest cash flows are exchanged, and they are usually netted, so it is a tool to transform the interest character of an asset or liability without touching the underlying principal. A "plain vanilla" IRS is fixed-for-floating in a single currency.

**Q890.** In a plain-vanilla IRS, is the notional principal exchanged?

No. The notional is purely a reference amount used to compute the interest cash flows; it is never exchanged in a single-currency interest-rate swap.
