This is the key difference from a currency swap, where principal usually is exchanged. Because principal stays put, the credit exposure of an IRS is far smaller than its notional suggests.

**Q891.** What does "net settlement" mean in a swap?

On each payment date both legs are calculated, and only the difference between the fixed and floating amounts changes hands, paid by whichever party owes more. For example, on a USD 10m notional, if the fixed payer owes 5% (USD 500k for the year) and the floating receiver is owed 4.5% (USD 450k), only USD 50k is paid by the fixed payer. Netting reduces settlement risk and the size of cash flows actually moving.

**Q892.** Why would a company use an interest-rate swap?

The classic motive is converting floating-rate debt into fixed-rate (to lock in costs and hedge against rising rates) or vice versa. A firm with a floating loan can enter a "pay-fixed, receive-floating" swap: the floating it receives offsets the floating it owes on the loan, leaving it with a net fixed cost. Swaps also let firms exploit comparative advantage in different markets, manage duration, and speculate on rate moves.

**Q893.** Explain the comparative-advantage argument for swaps.

If Company A borrows more cheaply in fixed and Company B more cheaply in floating, but each actually wants the other type, they can each borrow where they have an advantage and swap. The total interest saving (the difference between the two fixed spreads and the two floating spreads) is shared between them, so both end up paying less than borrowing directly in their preferred market. Critics note part of this "free lunch" reflects hidden credit-risk differences and renewal risk in floating rates.

**Q894.** A firm has a floating loan at MIBOR+150 bps and wants certainty. How does a swap help?

It enters a pay-fixed/receive-MIBOR swap on the same notional, say paying 7% fixed and receiving MIBOR. Its net cost becomes (MIBOR + 1.50%) on the loan minus MIBOR received plus 7% paid = 8.50% fixed. The floating exposure cancels, locking the all-in cost at 8.50% regardless of where MIBOR moves.

**Q895.** Why is the value of a swap approximately zero at initiation?

At inception the fixed rate (the swap rate) is set precisely so that the present value of the fixed leg equals the present value of the expected floating leg, so neither party pays the other to enter. As market rates move afterward, one leg becomes more valuable and the swap acquires positive value to one party and negative to the other. Vmid = PV(fixed leg) - PV(floating leg) = 0 at t=0.

**Q896.** How can a swap be valued as a portfolio of bonds?

To the fixed-rate receiver, a swap equals being long a fixed-coupon bond and short a floating-rate bond: V = B_fixed - B_floating. The fixed bond is discounted at current rates; the floating-rate bond resets to par at each reset, so right after a reset it is worth its notional. Thus immediately after a reset, V (to fixed receiver) = B_fixed - Notional.

**Q897.** How can a swap be valued as a series of FRAs?

Each exchange of fixed-for-floating on a future date is economically a forward rate agreement: you can value each settlement by replacing the unknown future floating rate with today's forward rate, compute the net cash flow, and discount it back. The swap value is the sum of these discounted FRA values. This "strip of FRAs" view and the bond view give the same answer.

**Q898.** What is the swap rate?

The swap rate is the fixed rate that makes a new swap have zero value at initiation, i.e. the fixed rate at which PV(fixed leg) = PV(floating leg). It equals a weighted average of the forward rates implied by the curve. Formula: swap rate = (1 - DF_n) / (sum of DF_i * tau_i), where DF are discount factors and tau the accrual fractions.

**Q899.** What is the swap curve and why does it matter?

The swap curve plots par swap rates against maturity (2y, 5y, 10y, etc.) and is a key benchmark term structure, often more liquid than government bonds in some tenors. It is used to discount cash flows, price other derivatives, and gauge market rate expectations and credit/liquidity conditions. In India, the INR OIS (MIBOR-based) curve serves this role for rupee rates.

**Q900.** What is a forward rate agreement (FRA)?

An FRA is an OTC contract that fixes an interest rate on a notional for a future period; one party locks a borrowing/lending rate today for, say, a 3-month period starting in 6 months (a "6x9 FRA"). At settlement only the difference between the agreed rate and the realized reference rate is exchanged. It is effectively a single-period building block of a swap.

**Q901.** What is the payoff of an FRA and how is it settled?

Payoff to the fixed-rate payer (borrower) = Notional * (R_reference - R_FRA) * (days/360) / (1 + R_reference*(days/360)). The cash flow is settled at the start of the reference period, so it is discounted back from the period end, which is why the divisor appears. If realized rates exceed the locked rate, the fixed payer (who feared rising rates) receives the payment.

**Q902.** Worked FRA example: 6x12 FRA, notional USD 5m, agreed 4.00%, settles at 5.00%.

The period is 6 months (~182 days, ~0.5 year). Unadjusted gain to the fixed payer = 5m * (5.00% - 4.00%) * 0.5 = USD 25,000. Discounted to settlement at the start of the period: 25,000 / (1 + 0.05*0.5) = USD 24,390. The borrower who locked 4% benefits because rates rose to 5%.

**Q903.** What was LIBOR and why is the market transitioning away from it?

LIBOR (London Interbank Offered Rate) was a survey-based estimate of unsecured interbank borrowing costs, long used as the floating benchmark for trillions in loans and derivatives. It was discredited by the 2012 rigging scandal and undermined by the collapse of actual interbank lending, making it manipulable and unrepresentative. Regulators ceased its publication (USD LIBOR's last panel rates ended June 2023), forcing a move to transaction-based risk-free rates.

**Q904.** What is SOFR and how does it differ from LIBOR?

SOFR (Secured Overnight Financing Rate) is the US replacement: an overnight rate based on actual secured (repo) Treasury transactions, making it robust and nearly manipulation-proof. Unlike term, forward-looking, unsecured LIBOR, SOFR is overnight, secured (so it carries almost no bank-credit premium), and is typically compounded in arrears to build term rates. Because it lacks LIBOR's credit spread, contracts add a fixed spread adjustment (e.g. ~26 bps for 3-month USD) when converting.

**Q905.** Name the risk-free reference rates in major markets.

US: SOFR; UK: SONIA; Euro area: ESTR (and EURIBOR still survives reformed); Switzerland: SARON; Japan: TONA. India uses MIBOR (Mumbai Interbank Offer Rate / FBIL overnight MIBOR) as the basis for the rupee OIS market, plus MIFOR for FX-implied rupee rates. These are largely overnight, transaction-anchored rates compounded for term exposure.

**Q906.** What is OIS and what is OIS discounting?

An Overnight Indexed Swap exchanges a fixed rate for the geometric average of an overnight index (SOFR, ESTR, or MIBOR in India) over the period. Post-2008, dealers shifted to "OIS discounting" — using the OIS curve, not LIBOR, to discount collateralized swap cash flows, because cash collateral earns the overnight rate, making OIS the true funding/discount rate. This better reflects the near-risk-free cost of funding posted collateral.

**Q907.** What is a currency swap?

A currency swap exchanges principal and interest payments in one currency for principal and interest in another. Unlike an IRS, the principal usually IS exchanged at the start and re-exchanged at maturity (at the original spot rate). It lets a firm raise cheap funding in one currency and convert it into the currency it actually needs while hedging both interest and FX exposure.

**Q908.** What is cross-currency basis?

The cross-currency basis is the spread added to one leg of a cross-currency swap that deviates from what covered interest parity would predict, reflecting supply/demand imbalances for funding in a given currency. A negative USD basis means non-US borrowers pay a premium to swap into dollars, common in stress periods when dollar funding is scarce. It is a market measure of how expensive it is to obtain a currency via the FX swap market.

**Q909.** What is an FX forward?

An FX forward is an OTC agreement to exchange two currencies at a fixed rate on a future date, locking in the exchange rate today and removing uncertainty about the future spot. An importer owing USD in 3 months can buy USD forward to fix its INR cost. The forward rate is determined by spot and the interest-rate differential, not by a forecast of the future spot.

**Q910.** State covered interest rate parity (CIP).

CIP says the forward exchange rate is pinned by the interest-rate differential, otherwise arbitrage is possible: F = S * (1 + r_domestic) / (1 + r_foreign), where rates are for the contract horizon and S, F are quoted as domestic per foreign. The currency with the higher interest rate trades at a forward discount, exactly offsetting its rate advantage so no risk-free profit exists.

**Q911.** Worked CIP example: INR/USD, spot 83.00, INR rate 7%, USD rate 5%, 1 year.

Treating INR as domestic and USD as foreign: F = 83.00 * (1 + 0.07) / (1 + 0.05) = 83.00 * 1.07/1.05 = 83.00 * 1.01905 = 84.58. So the 1-year USD/INR forward is about 84.58, i.e. USD is at a forward premium of ~1.58 rupees because INR's higher rate must be offset.

**Q912.** What is a forward premium versus a forward discount?

A currency is at a forward premium if it is more expensive to buy forward than spot, and at a discount if cheaper. Annualized forward premium ~ (F - S)/S * (12/months). By CIP the low-interest-rate currency trades at a forward premium and the high-rate currency at a discount; in INR/USD terms the rupee, with higher rates, trades at a forward discount (you get more INR per USD forward).

**Q913.** How does an FX swap differ from a currency swap?

An FX swap is a single contract combining a spot (or near-date) exchange with a simultaneous reverse forward exchange of the same two currencies — it is a short-dated funding/rollover tool with no intermediate interest payments; the rate differential is baked into the forward points. A cross-currency (currency) swap is longer-dated and involves periodic interest payments on both legs throughout its life. In short: FX swap = two principal exchanges, no coupons; currency swap = principal plus a stream of interest payments.

**Q914.** What is an interest-rate cap, and how does it relate to caplets?

A cap is an option-based hedge that pays the holder whenever a floating reference rate rises above a strike (cap) rate, effectively setting a ceiling on borrowing cost. It is a strip (portfolio) of "caplets," one per reset period, each a call option on the interest rate. Caplet payoff = Notional * max(R_reference - R_strike, 0) * (days/360), and the cap premium is the sum of the caplet premiums.

**Q915.** What are floors and collars?

A floor is the mirror image of a cap: a strip of "floorlets" (puts on the rate) that pays when the floating rate falls below a strike, guaranteeing a minimum return to a lender/investor. A collar combines buying a cap and selling a floor (for a borrower), so the rate is confined to a band; the premium received from the sold floor offsets the cost of the bought cap, and a "zero-cost collar" sets strikes so net premium is nil.

**Q916.** What is a swaption?

A swaption is an option granting the right, but not the obligation, to enter a swap at a preset fixed rate on a future date. A "payer swaption" gives the right to pay fixed/receive floating (valuable if rates rise); a "receiver swaption" gives the right to receive fixed (valuable if rates fall). Firms use them to hedge anticipated financing or to lock a future borrowing rate with optionality, paying an upfront premium.

**Q917.** What is basis risk in interest-rate/FX hedging?

Basis risk is the residual risk that a hedge does not move perfectly with the exposure because the hedge references a different rate, index, or tenor than the underlying. For example, hedging a loan tied to 1-month MIBOR with a 3-month MIBOR swap, or hedging SOFR-linked debt against a term-rate exposure, leaves a "basis" that can move against you. It is why LIBOR-to-SOFR transition and tenor mismatches matter to hedgers.

**Q918.** How did post-2008 reform change swap markets via clearing and netting, and what is India's framework?

After 2008, the G20 mandated that standardized OTC derivatives be centrally cleared through CCPs (which novate trades and net exposures), reported to trade repositories, and margined, sharply reducing counterparty risk; netting agreements (ISDA master agreements) collapse many trades into a single net obligation on default. In India, rupee IRS/OIS and MIFOR swaps clear through the Clearing Corporation of India (CCIL), while currency and FX derivatives are jointly regulated by RBI and SEBI — exchange-traded currency futures/options trade on NSE/BSE with RBI-set position limits, and OTC forwards require an underlying exposure under RBI's FEMA rules.

<a name="H3"></a>

## H3. Foreign Exchange, Commodities & Money Markets  <span class="qrange">(Q919-Q948)</span>

**Q919.** In a quote like USD/INR = 83, which is the base currency and which is the quote currency, and what does the number mean?

USD is the base currency and INR is the quote (or counter) currency; the rate tells you how many units of the quote currency buy ONE unit of the base. So USD/INR = 83 means 1 US dollar costs 83 Indian rupees. The convention is base/quote, and you always read the price as "units of quote per one base."

**Q920.** What is the spot FX market and what is a typical settlement convention?

The spot market is for immediate exchange of one currency for another at the current rate, but "immediate" settlement is conventionally T+2 (two business days) for most pairs, and T+1 for USD/CAD and USD/INR. It is the largest, most liquid FX segment, with daily global turnover around USD 7-8 trillion. The spot rate is the benchmark off which forwards and swaps are priced.

**Q921.** Explain bid, ask, and the bid-ask spread in FX.

The bid is the price at which the market maker buys the base currency from you; the ask (offer) is the price at which it sells the base to you, and ask > bid. For USD/INR quoted 82.98 / 83.02, you sell dollars at 82.98 and buy dollars at 83.02; the 0.04 spread is the dealer's compensation and a transaction cost to you. Liquid pairs like EUR/USD have spreads of a fraction of a pip; thin or emerging-market pairs are wider.

**Q922.** What is a pip, and what is a pip worth?

A pip ("percentage in point") is the smallest standard increment in an FX quote, conventionally the 4th decimal place for most pairs (0.0001) and the 2nd decimal (0.01) for JPY pairs. For EUR/USD moving 1.1050 to 1.1051 that is one pip; on a standard lot of 100,000 EUR the value is 100,000 * 0.0001 = USD 10 per pip. Fractional pips (a 5th decimal, the "pipette") are now common in electronic quoting.

**Q923.** What is the difference between appreciation and depreciation, and how does it show in the quote?

A currency appreciates when it gains value (buys more of another currency) and depreciates when it loses value. If USD/INR moves from 83 to 85, the dollar appreciated and the rupee depreciated (it now takes more rupees per dollar). Confusingly, a RISING USD/INR number means a WEAKER rupee, because INR is the quote currency.

**Q924.** What is a cross rate and how do you compute one?

A cross rate is an exchange rate between two currencies derived through a common third currency (usually USD). If EUR/USD = 1.10 and USD/INR = 83, then EUR/INR = 1.10 * 83 = 91.3. When both quotes share the same base or quote you divide instead of multiply, so always line up the units so the common currency cancels.

**Q925.** What are the main drivers of exchange rates?

Key drivers are interest-rate differentials (higher real rates attract capital and strengthen a currency), inflation differentials (higher inflation tends to weaken a currency), the balance of payments (a current-account deficit creates net selling pressure that must be financed by capital inflows), risk sentiment (risk-off flows favor safe havens like USD, JPY, CHF), and central-bank intervention. Over the long run fundamentals like productivity and terms of trade dominate; over short horizons rate expectations and sentiment dominate. India's rupee is especially sensitive to its oil-driven trade deficit and to FII equity flows.

**Q926.** State covered interest rate parity (CIP), give the formula, and work an example.

CIP says the forward rate offsets the interest-rate differential exactly, leaving no risk-free arbitrage: F = S * (1 + r_quote) / (1 + r_base), for the same horizon. Example: spot USD/INR = 83, 1-year INR rate 7 percent, USD rate 5 percent, so F = 83 * (1.07 / 1.05) = 84.58. The higher-yielding currency (INR) trades at a forward DISCOUNT, compensating for the rate gap so covered arbitrage profit is zero.

**Q927.** What is uncovered interest rate parity (UIP), how does it differ from CIP, and is the forward a good predictor of spot?

UIP states the EXPECTED change in spot equals the interest differential, so a higher-yielding currency is expected to depreciate by about that amount: E[S_future]/S = (1 + r_quote)/(1 + r_base). Unlike CIP there is no forward locking the rate, so UIP is an expectations condition that bears FX risk, not an arbitrage relation, and it fails empirically. Because of this "forward premium puzzle," the forward rate is a poor, biased predictor of future spot — it mainly reflects the rate differential, not a genuine forecast.

**Q928.** State the law of one price and absolute purchasing power parity (PPP).

The law of one price says an identical good should cost the same everywhere once converted to a common currency. Absolute PPP extends this to a basket: S = P_domestic / P_foreign, so the exchange rate equals the ratio of price levels. If a basket costs 8300 INR and USD 100, absolute PPP implies USD/INR = 83.

**Q929.** What is relative PPP and its formula?

Relative PPP focuses on CHANGES rather than levels: the percentage change in the exchange rate equals the inflation differential, so change in S is approximately inflation_quote - inflation_base. If India's inflation is 6 percent and US inflation 2 percent, the rupee should depreciate roughly 4 percent per year against the dollar (USD/INR rises about 4 percent). It tends to hold better over long horizons than short ones.

**Q930.** What is the Big Mac Index and what does it illustrate?

The Big Mac Index, published by The Economist, uses the price of a Big Mac across countries as a one-good proxy basket to gauge whether currencies look over- or under-valued versus PPP. If a Big Mac costs USD 5 in the US and 250 INR in India, the implied PPP rate is 250/5 = 50; with the market rate at 83 the rupee looks "undervalued" on that crude measure. It is a teaching tool, not a precise valuation, because it ignores non-tradables, taxes, and local labor costs.

**Q931.** What is the carry trade, why does it earn returns, and why does it blow up?

A carry trade borrows in a low-yielding currency (e.g., JPY) and invests in a high-yielding one (e.g., BRL, INR, AUD), pocketing the interest differential; it works because UIP fails, so high-yield currencies don't depreciate enough to wipe out the carry. The catch is negative skew: in risk-off episodes everyone unwinds at once, the funding currency sharply appreciates and the high-yield currency crashes, erasing months of small gains in days. It is essentially selling crash insurance, and leverage amplifies both the carry and the wipeout.

**Q932.** Distinguish transaction, translation, and economic FX exposure.

Transaction exposure is the risk that a specific contracted cash flow (e.g., a USD receivable) changes value in home currency before settlement. Translation (accounting) exposure arises when consolidating foreign-subsidiary financial statements into the parent's reporting currency. Economic (operating) exposure is the broader, long-run effect of currency moves on competitiveness, future cash flows, and firm value, even without explicit foreign-currency contracts.

**Q933.** How do corporates hedge FX risk?

Common tools are forwards (lock a rate for a future date), futures (exchange-traded, standardized), and currency options (pay a premium for downside protection while keeping upside). A money-market hedge replicates a forward: for a USD receivable, borrow its present value in USD, convert to INR at spot, and invest domestically, so the receivable repays the loan — by CIP this gives essentially the same rate as the forward. Natural hedging (matching revenue and cost currencies, netting intercompany flows) reduces exposure without derivatives; an Indian IT exporter typically sells USD forwards to protect rupee earnings.

**Q934.** What roles does the RBI play in managing the rupee?

The RBI runs a "managed float": it lets the rupee trade with market forces but intervenes in spot and forward markets to curb excessive volatility, buying dollars when the rupee is strong and selling them to defend it when weak. It also sets the policy (repo) rate, manages capital-flow rules, and operates a forward book. It does not target a fixed level but smooths sharp moves.

**Q935.** What are forex reserves and why do they matter?

Forex reserves are a central bank's holdings of foreign currencies (mostly USD), gold, SDRs, and the IMF reserve position, used to back the currency, intervene in FX markets, and ensure import and external-debt cover. India's reserves (around USD 600+ billion) provide a cushion of roughly 10-11 months of imports, supporting confidence and credit ratings. Reserves rise when the RBI buys dollars and fall when it sells to defend the rupee.

**Q936.** What is the NDF market and why does it matter for the rupee?

A Non-Deliverable Forward (NDF) is a cash-settled forward on a currency like INR, traded offshore in centers such as Singapore, London, and Dubai, settled in USD with no physical delivery of rupees. It exists because capital-account restrictions limit onshore access for non-residents, and it can drive or front-run the onshore rate, especially in stress. The RBI watches the NDF closely and has expanded onshore access to reduce offshore price-setting power.

**Q937.** Distinguish the spot and futures markets for commodities.

The spot (cash) market is for immediate delivery and payment of the physical commodity, while a futures contract is a standardized, exchange-traded agreement to buy/sell a set quantity and grade at a fixed price on a future date. Futures enable hedging and price discovery without immediate physical handling, and most are closed out or rolled before delivery. The futures price links to spot via the cost of carry.

**Q938.** Explain cost of carry and convenience yield for commodities.

Cost of carry is what it costs to hold a commodity to a future date — storage, insurance, and financing — which pushes the futures price above spot: F = S * (1 + r + storage). Convenience yield is the implicit benefit of holding the physical (availability for production, avoiding stockouts), which pulls the futures price down: F = S * (1 + r + storage - convenience yield). When convenience yield is high (tight supply), futures can trade below spot.

**Q939.** Define contango and backwardation for commodities.

Contango is when futures trade above spot (and far months above near months), typical when carrying costs dominate and supply is ample — e.g., crude in oversupply. Backwardation is when futures trade below spot, signaling tight physical supply and high convenience yield, so holders of the physical demand a premium. A long futures position earns positive "roll yield" in backwardation and suffers negative roll yield in contango.

**Q940.** How do producers and consumers hedge with commodity futures, and why is gold a safe haven?

A producer (e.g., a farmer or oil driller) is naturally LONG the physical, so it SELLS futures to lock a sale price; a consumer (e.g., an airline or jeweler) is short the commodity, so it BUYS futures to lock a purchase price. Gold is treated as a safe haven because it holds value in inflation, crises, and currency debasement, has low or negative correlation with risk assets, and carries no credit risk — central banks and investors buy it when confidence in fiat or markets falls.

**Q941.** Name the major commodity exchanges in India and globally.

In India, MCX (Multi Commodity Exchange) dominates metals and energy (gold, silver, crude, natural gas) and NCDEX leads agri commodities, both regulated by SEBI (which absorbed the former FMC). Globally, the CME Group (including NYMEX/COMEX) handles energy and metals, and the LME (London Metal Exchange) is the benchmark for industrial base metals like copper, aluminium, and zinc. ICE is key for Brent crude and soft commodities.

**Q942.** What is the purpose of the money market and what instruments trade there?

The money market is where short-term debt (maturities up to one year) is borrowed and lent, providing liquidity management for banks, firms, and governments at low risk. Instruments include Treasury bills, commercial paper (CP), certificates of deposit (CDs), repurchase agreements (repo), and call/notice money. It is the channel through which central-bank policy rates transmit to the rest of the economy.

**Q943.** Describe T-bills, commercial paper, CDs, repo, and call money.

Treasury bills are short-term sovereign debt issued at a discount (in India, 91/182/364-day tenors). Commercial paper is unsecured short-term debt issued by high-rated corporates; certificates of deposit are negotiable time deposits issued by banks. A repo is a collateralized short-term loan (sell-and-repurchase of securities); call money is uncollateralized interbank overnight lending. All price off the prevailing policy rate plus a credit/liquidity spread.

**Q944.** How do money-market rates price off the policy rate?

The policy rate (the RBI repo rate, or the Fed funds target) anchors the shortest, safest rate, and other money-market rates sit above it by a spread reflecting credit risk, term, and liquidity. When the central bank hikes the repo rate, interbank call money, T-bill yields, CP, and CD rates all reprice upward; the central bank uses open-market and repo operations to keep the overnight rate inside its corridor (in India, the LAF corridor with the SDF floor and MSF ceiling). This is the front end of monetary-policy transmission.

**Q945.** Explain repo mechanics and the haircut.

In a repo, the borrower sells securities (collateral) to the lender and agrees to repurchase them later at a slightly higher price; the price difference is the implicit interest (the repo rate). The haircut is the discount applied to the collateral's market value — e.g., a 2 percent haircut means USD 100 of bonds secures only USD 98 of cash — protecting the lender against a fall in collateral value or borrower default. Riskier or more volatile collateral carries a larger haircut.

**Q946.** What are LIBOR, SOFR, and MIBOR, and what changed?

LIBOR was the London Interbank Offered Rate, a survey-based benchmark for unsecured interbank lending that was discredited by manipulation and phased out (most settings ended by mid-2023). SOFR (Secured Overnight Financing Rate) replaced USD LIBOR; it is a transaction-based rate derived from overnight US Treasury repo, so it is secured and nearly risk-free. MIBOR (Mumbai Interbank Offered Rate) is India's benchmark for the rupee overnight/call market, now computed by FBIL from actual transactions.

**Q947.** What is the difference between the money market and the capital market?

The money market trades short-term instruments (under one year) for liquidity, with low risk and low return — T-bills, CP, CDs, repo. The capital market trades long-term instruments (over one year) — bonds and equities — to raise investment capital, with higher risk and return. Money markets are about managing cash; capital markets are about funding long-term growth.

**Q948.** How do you compute the discount yield on a T-bill, and how does it differ from bond-equivalent yield?

The discount yield (bank discount basis) is (Face - Price)/Face * (360/days), which understates true return because it divides by face value and uses a 360-day year. The bond-equivalent yield (BEY) is (Face - Price)/Price * (365/days), dividing by the price actually paid and using 365 days, giving a more accurate annualized return. Example: a 91-day bill bought at 98 (face 100) has discount yield = 2/100 * 360/91 = 7.91 percent, but BEY = 2/98 * 365/91 = 8.18 percent.

<a name="secI"></a>

# I. Economy & Financial Institutions

<a name="I1"></a>

## I1. Macroeconomics, Monetary & Fiscal Policy  <span class="qrange">(Q949-Q978)</span>

**Q949.** What are the four components of GDP under the expenditure approach?

GDP = C + I + G + NX, where C is private consumption, I is gross investment (business capex plus inventory changes plus residential construction), G is government spending on goods and services (not transfers), and NX is net exports (exports minus imports). In most economies C is the largest slice; for India, consumption is roughly 55-60% of GDP and investment (gross fixed capital formation) is the key growth swing factor. Note G excludes transfer payments like pensions or subsidies because those are not purchases of current output.

**Q950.** GDP can be measured three ways. What are they and why do they give the same number?

The three approaches are expenditure (sum of all spending: C+I+G+NX), income (sum of all factor incomes: wages + rent + interest + profits + taxes-less-subsidies), and output/value-added (sum of value added at each production stage, avoiding double counting). They are identical because every rupee of output sold becomes someone's income and someone's expenditure — one entity's spending is another's revenue. India's CSO/NSO historically emphasized the production (gross value added, GVA) approach; GDP = GVA + net taxes on products.

**Q951.** Distinguish nominal GDP from real GDP.

Nominal GDP values output at current-year prices, so it rises with both higher quantities and higher prices. Real GDP values output at constant base-year prices, stripping out inflation so it reflects only changes in the volume of goods and services. Only real GDP growth tells you whether the economy genuinely produced more; if nominal GDP grows 11% while prices rise 6%, real growth is roughly 5%.

**Q952.** What is the GDP deflator and how does it differ from CPI?

GDP deflator = (Nominal GDP / Real GDP) * 100; it is the broadest price index because it covers every good and service in GDP, with weights that change each year as the output mix changes. CPI tracks only a fixed basket of consumer goods, includes imports, and uses fixed weights, so it can diverge from the deflator. Example: nominal GDP 220, real GDP 200 gives a deflator of 110, implying 10% price growth versus the base year.

**Q953.** What is the difference between GDP and GNP (and GNI)?

GDP measures output produced within a country's borders regardless of who owns the factors; GNP (now usually called GNI) measures output produced by a country's residents/nationals regardless of location. GNP = GDP + net factor income from abroad (income residents earn overseas minus income foreigners earn domestically). A country with large remittance inflows or overseas investments (e.g., India, the Philippines) has GNP slightly above GDP; one hosting heavy foreign-owned production (e.g., Ireland) has GNP below GDP.

**Q954.** Name the phases of the business cycle.

The classic phases are expansion (rising output, employment, incomes), peak (the cycle's high point where growth tops out), contraction/recession (falling output, often defined as two consecutive quarters of negative real GDP growth), and trough (the low point before recovery begins). A full cycle runs trough-to-trough or peak-to-peak. The NBER in the US dates US recessions using a broad set of indicators, not just the two-quarter rule.

**Q955.** Give examples of leading, coincident, and lagging economic indicators.

Leading indicators turn before the economy does: stock prices, the yield curve slope, building permits, new orders/PMI, and initial jobless claims. Coincident indicators move with the cycle: real GDP, industrial production, employment, and personal income. Lagging indicators turn after: unemployment rate, CPI inflation, and the average duration of unemployment. Markets watch leading indicators (e.g., India's manufacturing PMI) to anticipate turning points.

**Q956.** Explain demand-pull versus cost-push inflation.

Demand-pull inflation occurs when aggregate demand outpaces the economy's productive capacity — "too much money chasing too few goods" — typical of an overheating boom. Cost-push inflation arises when input costs rise (oil shocks, wage spikes, supply disruptions), shifting aggregate supply left so prices rise even as output falls. The 1970s oil crises and the 2021-22 supply-chain/energy spike were largely cost-push; a credit-fueled boom is demand-pull. Policy is harder for cost-push because curbing demand worsens the output loss.

**Q957.** How does CPI differ from WPI, and which does India target?

CPI measures retail price changes for a consumer basket and includes services; WPI (Wholesale Price Index) measures prices at the wholesale/producer stage and excludes services, with a heavy weight on manufactured goods and commodities. WPI is more volatile because of commodity swings. India's RBI conducts flexible inflation targeting against CPI (Combined) with a target of 4% +/- 2%; WPI is no longer the policy anchor though it is still tracked.

**Q958.** What is core inflation and why do central banks watch it?

Core inflation strips out food and energy (and sometimes other volatile items) from headline CPI because those prices are noisy and supply-driven rather than demand-driven. It gives a cleaner read on underlying, persistent inflation pressure that monetary policy can actually influence. The RBI and the Fed both monitor core measures; a falling headline but sticky core (say headline 4.5% but core 6%) signals broad-based price pressure that warrants caution on rate cuts.

**Q959.** What are the costs of inflation?

Inflation erodes the purchasing power of money and fixed incomes, creates "menu costs" and "shoe-leather costs" of constantly repricing and economizing on cash, distorts the tax system (taxing nominal gains), and arbitrarily redistributes wealth from creditors to debtors. Unanticipated inflation is worse because it injects uncertainty into long-term contracts and raises required risk premia, lifting nominal interest rates. Even moderate inflation distorts relative price signals that markets rely on to allocate resources.

**Q960.** What is the difference between deflation and stagflation, and why is each dangerous?

Deflation is a sustained fall in the general price level; it is dangerous because consumers defer purchases expecting lower prices, real debt burdens rise, and central banks hit the zero lower bound (Japan's "lost decades"). Stagflation is the toxic combination of stagnant/falling growth plus high inflation plus high unemployment, which defeats standard policy because fighting inflation (tightening) worsens the recession and vice versa. The 1970s OPEC-driven stagflation broke the simple Phillips-curve consensus.

**Q961.** Define the monetary aggregates M0, M1, M2, M3.

M0 (reserve/base money) is currency in circulation plus bankers' deposits with the central bank. M1 (narrow money) is currency with the public plus demand/checkable deposits. M2 adds savings and small time deposits. M3 (broad money) adds larger time/term deposits and is the most common policy aggregate. In India: M0 is reserve money; M3 = currency with public + demand deposits + time deposits + other deposits with RBI, and M3 is the headline "broad money" growth figure.

**Q962.** What is the money multiplier?

The money multiplier links base money to broad money: in a simple model, multiplier = 1 / reserve ratio, so a 10% reserve requirement implies a 10x multiplier (a Rs 100 injection can support up to Rs 1,000 of deposits). More generally, multiplier = (1 + c) / (c + r) where c is the currency-to-deposit ratio and r the reserve ratio. Leakages into cash holdings or excess reserves shrink the actual multiplier below the theoretical maximum.

**Q963.** How do central banks "create money"?

Central banks create base money by crediting commercial banks' reserve accounts — typically when buying assets (government bonds in open market operations or QE) or lending to banks. Commercial banks then create the bulk of broad money endogenously by extending loans, which simultaneously create deposits; the central bank influences but does not mechanically dictate this through reserves and the policy rate. So most money in a modern economy is bank-created deposit money, not physical currency.

**Q964.** What are the main tools of monetary policy?

The key tools are: (1) the policy interest rate (the price of central-bank liquidity — Fed funds target, RBI repo rate), (2) reserve requirements (the fraction of deposits banks must hold, India's CRR), (3) open market operations (buying/selling government securities to adjust liquidity), and (4) unconventional tools like quantitative easing/tightening and forward guidance at the zero bound. India adds the SLR (statutory liquidity ratio) and a liquidity corridor bounded by the repo and reverse-repo/SDF rates.

**Q965.** What are CRR and SLR in the Indian context?

CRR (Cash Reserve Ratio) is the percentage of a bank's net demand and time liabilities (NDTL) it must keep as cash reserves with the RBI, earning no interest — a direct lever on lendable liquidity. SLR (Statutory Liquidity Ratio) is the percentage of NDTL banks must hold in approved liquid assets (mainly government securities, gold). Raising CRR drains liquidity and tightens credit; SLR both ensures solvency and creates captive demand for government bonds.

**Q966.** Explain quantitative easing (QE) and quantitative tightening (QT).

QE is large-scale central-bank purchase of longer-dated government and sometimes private bonds, used when the policy rate is already near zero; it expands the central bank's balance sheet, injects reserves, lowers long-term yields, and pushes investors toward riskier assets (the portfolio-balance and signaling channels). QT is the reverse — shrinking the balance sheet by letting bonds mature or selling them, draining liquidity and putting upward pressure on yields. The Fed ran massive QE in 2008-14 and 2020, then QT from 2022.

**Q967.** Describe the monetary policy transmission mechanism.

A policy-rate change propagates through several channels: the interest-rate channel (loan and deposit rates adjust, affecting borrowing and saving), the credit/bank-lending channel (changes in bank reserves and risk appetite alter loan supply), the asset-price/wealth channel (rates move equity and bond prices and household wealth), and the exchange-rate channel (rate differentials move the currency, affecting trade). Transmission has lags of several quarters and can be "long and variable"; in India transmission to bank lending rates improved after linking loans to external benchmarks (the EBLR regime).

**Q968.** What does the Taylor rule say, conceptually?

The Taylor rule is a guideline for setting the policy rate as a function of inflation and the output gap: roughly, nominal rate = neutral real rate + inflation + 0.5*(inflation gap) + 0.5*(output gap). It implies that when inflation rises above target the central bank should raise the nominal rate by more than one-for-one (the "Taylor principle") so the real rate increases and actually restrains demand. It is a descriptive benchmark, not a mechanical mandate, but it captures how systematic, rules-based policy should react.

**Q969.** Contrast the classical, Keynesian, and monetarist schools.

Classical economists believe markets clear quickly, prices/wages are flexible, output is supply-determined at full employment, and money is "neutral" (affects only prices, not real output) — so government intervention is largely unnecessary. Keynesians argue prices/wages are sticky, demand drives short-run output, and active fiscal/monetary stimulus can close output gaps in recessions. Monetarists (Friedman) accept demand matters short-run but stress that "inflation is always and everywhere a monetary phenomenon" and favor stable, rules-based money-supply growth over discretionary fine-tuning.

**Q970.** Explain the IS-LM framework conceptually.

IS-LM models short-run equilibrium between the goods market and the money market on axes of interest rate (r) and output (Y). The IS curve (downward-sloping) shows goods-market equilibrium: lower rates spur investment and raise output. The LM curve (upward-sloping) shows money-market equilibrium: higher output raises money demand and thus the rate for a fixed money supply. Their intersection sets r and Y; expansionary fiscal policy shifts IS right (higher Y and r), while expansionary monetary policy shifts LM right (higher Y, lower r).

**Q971.** What is the Phillips curve and the inflation-unemployment tradeoff?

The original Phillips curve posited a stable inverse relationship between unemployment and wage/price inflation: pushing unemployment lower stokes inflation. The 1970s stagflation broke the naive version, leading to the expectations-augmented Phillips curve, where the tradeoff exists only short-run and any attempt to hold unemployment below the "natural rate" (NAIRU) just accelerates inflation. Long-run, the curve is vertical at the natural rate — there is no permanent tradeoff, only a short-run one driven by inflation surprises.

**Q972.** What is the fiscal multiplier and what makes it larger or smaller?

The fiscal multiplier is the ratio of the change in output to the change in government spending or taxes; in a simple Keynesian model, spending multiplier = 1/(1 - MPC), so an MPC of 0.8 gives a multiplier of 5. In practice it is much smaller (often well below 1) because of leakages into savings, imports, and taxes, plus crowding out and offsetting monetary policy. Multipliers are larger in recessions, at the zero lower bound, and for spending targeted at high-MPC (low-income) households; smaller in open economies with high import propensity.

**Q973.** What is crowding out?

Crowding out occurs when increased government borrowing to fund a deficit raises interest rates, which discourages (crowds out) private investment and consumption, partially offsetting the fiscal stimulus. The effect is strong when the economy is near full capacity and central banks respond by tightening; it is weak in a deep recession with slack resources and accommodative monetary policy. There is also "financial" crowding out, where government bond demand absorbs savings that could have funded private projects.

**Q974.** What are automatic stabilizers?

Automatic stabilizers are features of the budget that dampen the cycle without any new legislation: in a downturn, tax receipts fall (progressive income tax) and transfer spending rises (unemployment benefits, welfare), automatically supporting demand; in a boom the reverse cools the economy. They make fiscal policy countercyclical with no decision lag, unlike discretionary stimulus. India relies more on discretionary measures because its safety-net coverage is narrower, though schemes like MGNREGA act as a partial stabilizer.

**Q975.** Why does the debt-to-GDP ratio matter, and what drives its trajectory?

Debt-to-GDP scales government debt by the economy's ability to service it; a rising ratio raises default/inflation risk premia and interest costs. Its dynamics hinge on the relationship between the average interest rate (r) on debt and the nominal growth rate (g): if g exceeds r, an economy can run modest primary deficits and still see the ratio fall, whereas r above g makes debt self-compounding. India's general government debt is roughly 80-85% of GDP; the FRBM framework targets gradual consolidation toward a debt anchor.

**Q976.** What is the balance of payments, and how do the current and capital accounts differ?

The balance of payments records all transactions between residents and the rest of the world and always sums to zero (every flow is financed). The current account covers trade in goods and services, primary income (interest/dividends), and secondary income (remittances/transfers); a deficit means the country imports more than it exports of these. The capital and financial account records cross-border asset flows (FDI, portfolio investment, loans, reserve changes); a current-account deficit must be financed by a capital-account surplus. India typically runs a current-account deficit funded by capital inflows.

**Q977.** Compare fixed, floating, and managed-float exchange-rate regimes.

Under a fixed (pegged) regime the central bank commits to a specific exchange rate and uses reserves and policy to defend it, gaining stability but losing monetary autonomy. Under a pure float the rate is set entirely by the market, giving full monetary independence but more volatility. A managed float (dirty float) lets the market set the rate but with periodic central-bank intervention to smooth volatility — India's regime, where the RBI lets the rupee move but intervenes to curb sharp swings and build reserves.

**Q978.** What is the impossible trinity, and how does it connect to the yield curve as a recession signal?

The impossible trinity (Mundell-Fleming trilemma) states a country can have at most two of: a fixed exchange rate, free capital mobility, and an independent monetary policy — never all three. India, for instance, chooses partial capital controls and a managed float to retain meaningful monetary autonomy. Separately, an inverted yield curve (short-term yields above long-term, e.g., the US 10-year minus 2-year going negative) has preceded most US recessions because it signals markets expect future rate cuts amid slowing growth; in India twin deficits (simultaneous fiscal and current-account deficits) similarly flag macro vulnerability, and rate hikes transmit to markets by lifting bond yields (pushing bond prices down) and compressing equity valuations through a higher discount rate.

<a name="I2"></a>

## I2. Banking & Financial Institutions  <span class="qrange">(Q979-Q1008)</span>

**Q979.** How does a commercial bank fundamentally make money?

A bank makes money in two broad ways: net interest income (NII) and non-interest (fee) income. NII is the spread between interest earned on assets (loans, securities) and interest paid on liabilities (deposits, borrowings); fee income comes from things like account charges, card interchange, processing fees, trade finance, and wealth/distribution commissions. The core model is borrowing short and cheap (deposits) and lending long and dearer (loans), capturing the spread while managing credit, liquidity and rate risk.

**Q980.** What is Net Interest Income (NII) and how is it different from Net Interest Margin (NIM)?

NII = Interest Income - Interest Expense, an absolute rupee/dollar amount. NIM expresses that spread as a percentage of average earning assets: `NIM = (Interest Income - Interest Expense) / Average Earning Assets`. NII tells you the size of the spread; NIM normalises it so you can compare banks of different sizes. A large bank can have huge NII but a thin NIM, and vice versa.

**Q981.** Compute NIM for a simple example.

If a bank earns interest income of 100 and pays interest expense of 55 on average earning assets of 1,000, then NII = 100 - 55 = 45 and NIM = 45 / 1,000 = 4.5%. Indian private banks like HDFC/Kotak historically run NIMs around 4-4.5%, while public sector banks tend to be lower (2.5-3%) due to higher cost of funds and lower-yielding asset mixes.

**Q982.** Why are deposits a bank's liabilities and loans its assets — isn't that backwards from how a customer sees it?

From the customer's view a deposit is "my money" (an asset to them), but to the bank it is money it owes back, so it sits on the liability side. A loan is "my debt" to the borrower but to the bank it is a claim that will be repaid with interest, hence an asset. The bank's balance sheet is essentially the mirror image of its customers': customer deposits fund customer loans.

**Q983.** Why is a bank's balance sheet considered "special" and highly leveraged?

Banks operate with very thin equity relative to assets — typically equity is only about 8-12% of assets, meaning leverage of roughly 8x to 12x (assets/equity). They fund mostly with other people's money (deposits and borrowings) and hold illiquid loans against liquid, on-demand liabilities. This maturity transformation plus high leverage makes banks profitable in good times but fragile to losses and runs, which is why they are heavily regulated.

**Q984.** What is the CASA ratio and why do banks obsess over it?

CASA = Current Account and Savings Account deposits as a share of total deposits: `CASA Ratio = (CA + SA balances) / Total Deposits`. Current accounts pay zero interest and savings accounts pay very little, so they are low-cost, sticky funds. A high CASA ratio (say 45%+) lowers the bank's overall cost of funds and structurally lifts NIM, which is why CASA-rich banks like HDFC, Kotak and SBI enjoy a funding advantage over deposit-hungry rivals.

**Q985.** How does a high CASA ratio translate into better margins, numerically?

Suppose a bank funds 100 of loans yielding 10%. If 50 is CASA at 1% and 50 is term deposits at 7%, blended cost = (50*1% + 50*7%)/100 = 4%, so spread = 6%. If CASA falls and the mix becomes 20 CASA / 80 term, cost = (20*1% + 80*7%)/100 = 5.8%, spread shrinks to 4.2%. Same loan yield, but the cheaper deposit mix adds nearly 2 percentage points of margin.

**Q986.** What is Asset-Liability Management (ALM) in a bank?

ALM is the practice of managing the structure and risk of the balance sheet so that mismatches between assets and liabilities — in maturity, repricing, currency and liquidity — stay within acceptable limits. Its main goals are to protect net interest income and economic value from interest-rate moves and to ensure the bank can always meet cash outflows. ALM is overseen by an Asset-Liability Committee (ALCO) using tools like gap analysis, duration and liquidity buckets.

**Q987.** What is interest-rate risk in the banking book (IRRBB) and the maturity/duration gap?

IRRBB is the risk that changes in interest rates hurt the bank's earnings or economic value because assets and liabilities reprice at different times. The repricing gap = rate-sensitive assets minus rate-sensitive liabilities in a given time bucket; a positive gap means rising rates help NII, a negative gap means rising rates hurt it. Duration gap extends this to economic value: `Duration Gap = D_assets - (L/A)*D_liabilities`, and a positive duration gap means the bank's net worth falls when rates rise.

**Q988.** Give a quick duration-gap intuition with numbers.

If assets of 100 have duration 4 years and liabilities of 92 have duration 1.5 years, duration gap = 4 - (92/100)*1.5 = 4 - 1.38 = 2.62 years. For a +1% (100 bps) rate rise, the change in economic value of equity is approximately -Duration Gap * (rate change) * Assets = -2.62 * 0.01 * 100 = -2.62, i.e. equity value drops by about 2.62. A large positive duration gap is exactly what sank some banks (e.g. SVB in 2023) when rates spiked.

**Q989.** What is liquidity management and how does it differ from solvency?

Liquidity management ensures the bank can meet cash outflows (deposit withdrawals, loan drawdowns) as they fall due, by holding liquid assets and maintaining funding access. Solvency is about having enough capital so assets exceed liabilities. A bank can be solvent (assets > liabilities) yet fail from a liquidity crisis if it cannot convert assets to cash fast enough — which is precisely how bank runs kill otherwise viable banks. Basel introduced the LCR and NSFR specifically to backstop liquidity.

**Q990.** Walk through the typical loan/credit process at a bank.

It runs from origination and application, through credit appraisal (assessing repayment capacity, collateral and risk), to sanction with terms and pricing, documentation, disbursement, and then ongoing monitoring and collection/recovery. The pricing reflects the borrower's risk grade, cost of funds, operating cost and a risk premium. Good underwriting at appraisal is what protects the bank, because once a bad loan is disbursed, recovery is costly and uncertain.

**Q991.** What are the 5 Cs of credit?

Character (borrower's track record and willingness to repay), Capacity (cash flow/ability to service debt), Capital (the borrower's own skin in the game), Collateral (security pledged), and Conditions (purpose of loan and the macro/industry environment). Lenders use the 5 Cs as a structured checklist to judge both the willingness and the ability to repay. Some add a sixth C — Credit history — though it overlaps with Character.

**Q992.** What is a Non-Performing Asset (NPA)?

An NPA is a loan or advance where the borrower has stopped servicing it — under RBI norms, a term loan becomes an NPA when principal or interest is overdue for more than 90 days. Once classified NPA, the bank must stop booking accrued interest as income and start setting aside provisions. NPAs directly erode profitability (lost interest plus provisions) and signal asset-quality stress.

**Q993.** How does RBI's IRAC framework classify NPAs?

Under the Income Recognition and Asset Classification (IRAC) norms, assets are graded as Standard (performing), Sub-standard (NPA for up to 12 months), Doubtful (NPA beyond 12 months), and Loss (uncollectible, identified as a write-off). The classification drives provisioning — the worse the grade, the higher the provision. A loan also passes through SMA (Special Mention Account) stages (SMA-0/1/2 at 0-30/31-60/61-90 days overdue) as an early-warning bucket before turning NPA at 90 days.

**Q994.** What is provisioning, and roughly how much is required by category?

A provision is an amount charged to the P&L and held against expected loss on a loan. Indicative RBI minimums: Standard assets carry a small general provision (around 0.25-1% by sector); Sub-standard ~15% (25% if unsecured); Doubtful escalates with age from ~25% up to 100% on the secured portion plus 100% on the unsecured portion; Loss assets 100%. Banks often hold provisions above the regulatory floor, tracked via the Provision Coverage Ratio (PCR).

**Q995.** Distinguish Gross NPA from Net NPA.

Gross NPA is the total stock of bad loans before provisions; Net NPA = Gross NPA - provisions held against them. As ratios: `Gross NPA Ratio = Gross NPAs / Gross Advances` and `Net NPA Ratio = Net NPAs / Net Advances`. Net NPA shows the residual risk the bank's capital still carries; a bank with high Gross NPA but near-zero Net NPA has already absorbed most of the hit through provisioning.

**Q996.** What do "slippage" and "restructuring" mean in NPA management?

Slippage is the fresh addition of loans moving from standard (performing) into NPA during a period; the slippage ratio measures new NPAs as a fraction of opening standard advances and indicates the pace of asset-quality deterioration. Restructuring is renegotiating loan terms (lower rate, longer tenor, moratorium) to help a stressed but viable borrower keep performing. Restructuring can mask stress if overused, so RBI tightened rules (e.g. post-2015 AQR and the June 2019 stressed-asset framework) to prevent "evergreening".

**Q997.** What is the credit cycle and why does it matter for banks?

The credit cycle is the recurring expansion and contraction of credit availability: in upswings banks lend freely, underwriting standards loosen and asset prices rise; in downswings defaults climb, banks pull back, and provisioning spikes. Bank earnings are highly cyclical because loan losses are back-loaded — the bad loans made in the boom only surface in the bust. This is why bank valuations swing with the cycle and why "the loans of the best years go bad in the worst years".

**Q998.** Why is the central bank called the lender of last resort?

In a panic, even solvent banks can run out of cash if depositors withdraw en masse; the central bank lends to such banks against good collateral to stop the panic spreading — the classic Bagehot rule is to lend freely, at a penalty rate, against good collateral. In India the RBI provides this via facilities like the Marginal Standing Facility (MSF) and the LAF window. The backstop exists because a liquidity failure at one bank can cascade into a systemic crisis.

**Q999.** Explain fractional reserve banking and how it leads to bank runs.

Banks keep only a fraction of deposits as reserves and lend out the rest, so they cannot repay all depositors simultaneously — the money is tied up in illiquid loans. If enough depositors fear losses and rush to withdraw at once, the bank cannot meet demand and fails, even if fundamentally solvent; the fear becomes self-fulfilling. Deposit insurance and the lender-of-last-resort exist precisely to break this run dynamic.

**Q1000.** What is deposit insurance, and how does DICGC work in India?

Deposit insurance guarantees depositors a set amount if their bank fails, removing the incentive to run. In India the DICGC (a RBI subsidiary) insures each depositor up to INR 5 lakh per bank (raised from INR 1 lakh in 2020), covering principal plus interest across all accounts at that bank. In the US the FDIC insures up to USD 250,000 per depositor per bank. The cap is deliberate so large/sophisticated creditors still exert market discipline.

**Q1001.** Compare commercial, investment, and universal banks.

A commercial bank takes deposits and makes loans (retail and corporate). An investment bank does not take retail deposits; it underwrites securities, advises on M&A, and trades — earning fees and trading income rather than NII. A universal bank combines both under one roof (e.g. JPMorgan, ICICI Bank-style groups). After 1999 (Gramm-Leach-Bliley repeal of Glass-Steagall) US banks could combine these, while post-2008 rules like Volcker re-restricted proprietary trading.

**Q1002.** What distinguishes cooperative, small finance, and payments banks in India?

Cooperative banks are member-owned, locally focused, and dually regulated (RBI + state registrars), historically prone to governance lapses (e.g. PMC Bank). Small Finance Banks (SFBs like AU, Equitas, Ujjivan) are full banks aimed at financial inclusion, required to direct a high share of lending to priority sectors and small-ticket loans. Payments Banks (e.g. Airtel, Paytm, India Post) can take deposits up to INR 2 lakh per customer and offer payments/remittances but cannot lend or issue credit cards — they earn on float and fees, not NII.

**Q1003.** How does an NBFC differ from a bank, and why is that riskier on funding?

A Non-Banking Financial Company lends like a bank but cannot accept demand deposits and has no access to CASA or the central bank's liquidity window. It funds itself from banks, bonds, commercial paper and debentures — wholesale, costlier and shorter-dated money. This creates a structural asset-liability mismatch: NBFCs often lend long (infrastructure, housing) against short-term borrowings, so a sudden loss of market confidence can freeze their funding even if their loan book is sound.

**Q1004.** What were the IL&FS and DHFL episodes, and what lessons did they teach?

IL&FS (2018) defaulted on its debt despite high credit ratings, exposing hidden leverage, governance failures and rating-agency complacency; it triggered a liquidity crunch across NBFCs. DHFL (2019) followed with fraud and ALM mismatch, becoming the first NBFC taken to insolvency under the IBC. The lessons: short-term borrowing to fund long-term assets is fragile, ratings can mislead, and NBFCs are vulnerable to confidence-driven funding freezes — prompting RBI's scale-based regulation and tighter liquidity (LCR) norms for large NBFCs.

**Q1005.** What is Priority Sector Lending (PSL) in India?

PSL is an RBI mandate requiring banks to lend a minimum share of credit to sectors deemed nationally important — agriculture, MSMEs, education, housing, weaker sections, renewables. The overall target is 40% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit for domestic banks, with sub-targets (e.g. 18% to agriculture). Banks short of target buy Priority Sector Lending Certificates (PSLCs) or park funds with NABARD/SIDBI at lower yields, so PSL acts as a directed-credit and cross-subsidy mechanism.

**Q1006.** What are financial intermediation and disintermediation?

Financial intermediation is banks standing between savers and borrowers — pooling deposits, assessing credit, transforming maturity and bearing risk — so neither side has to find the other directly. Disintermediation is the bypassing of banks: borrowers raise money directly from markets (bonds, commercial paper) and savers invest directly (mutual funds, equities), shrinking the bank's traditional spread business. Fintech, capital-market deepening and rising investor sophistication all accelerate disintermediation.

**Q1007.** Why does a bank's ROE look high while its ROA looks low?

`ROA = Net Income / Average Assets` and `ROE = Net Income / Average Equity`, linked by leverage: `ROE = ROA * (Assets/Equity)`. Banks earn a thin ROA — around 1% is considered healthy — but run leverage of ~10x, so a 1% ROA times 10x equals roughly a 10-15% ROE. The high ROE is mostly leverage, not superior profitability per dollar of assets, which is why analysts watch ROA to judge true earning quality.

**Q1008.** Why do banks trade on Price-to-Book (P/B) rather than P/E, and how is book value relevant; plus a word on insurance and fintech?

Banks' assets and liabilities are largely financial and marked close to fair value, so book value (equity per share) is a meaningful anchor, and P/B (often paired with ROE — high-ROE banks deserve P/B above 1) captures value better than volatile, provision-driven earnings. By analogy, insurers earn on "float" — premiums held before claims are paid — splitting profit into underwriting income (premiums minus claims/costs) and investment income on the float, with a combined ratio below 100% meaning underwriting profit. Fintechs disrupt banking by unbundling high-margin pieces (payments, lending, wealth) with lower cost-to-serve and better UX, pushing banks toward platform models while still relying on banks for the regulated deposit-and-balance-sheet core.

<a name="secJ"></a>

# J. Advanced Corporate Finance & Contemporary Finance

<a name="J1"></a>

## J1. Advanced Corporate Finance  <span class="qrange">(Q1009-Q1039)</span>

**Q1009.** What is Economic Value Added (EVA) and how does it differ from accounting profit?

EVA is economic profit: `EVA = NOPAT - WACC*Invested Capital`, where NOPAT is net operating profit after tax. Unlike accounting profit (net income), which only deducts interest and ignores the cost of equity, EVA charges for ALL capital — debt and equity — so a firm can report positive net income yet destroy value if it does not out-earn its full cost of capital. Example: NOPAT = INR 120 cr, invested capital = INR 1,000 cr, WACC = 11% gives EVA = 120 - 0.11*1000 = INR 10 cr of true value created.

**Q1010.** Why can a company show accounting profit but still have negative economic profit?

Accounting net income deducts the explicit cost of debt (interest) but never charges for the opportunity cost of equity, which is real and often the largest financing cost. Economic profit subtracts a capital charge on the entire invested base. A firm earning a 9% return on capital that cost 12% has positive net income but is destroying roughly 3% of its capital base each year — the gap accounting statements hide.

**Q1011.** State the ROIC vs WACC value-creation test and what it tells you.

`ROIC = NOPAT / Invested Capital`; value is created only when `ROIC > WACC`. The spread (ROIC - WACC) times invested capital equals EVA. If ROIC = WACC the firm merely earns its cost of capital and growth adds no value; if ROIC < WACC, growth actively destroys value. This single test reframes capital budgeting: the goal is not growth or even accounting returns, but earning a positive spread over the cost of capital.

**Q1012.** How does the value driver formula link growth, ROIC and value?

The key driver formula for value is `Value = NOPAT*(1 - g/ROIC) / (WACC - g)`, where g is the growth rate of NOPAT. The term `(1 - g/ROIC)` is the reinvestment-adjusted free cash flow. When ROIC > WACC, raising g increases value; when ROIC = WACC the g terms cancel and value reduces to NOPAT/WACC regardless of growth; when ROIC < WACC, faster growth lowers value. So growth and ROIC are joint drivers, not independent ones.

**Q1013.** "A high-growth company is always more valuable." Critique this.

Growth only creates value when invested at returns above the cost of capital. A company growing 15% at ROIC of 8% against a 12% WACC is consuming cash on negative-spread projects and is worth less than a slower-growing, high-ROIC peer. Growth is a multiplier on the ROIC-WACC spread: it amplifies value creation when the spread is positive and amplifies destruction when negative.

**Q1014.** Which is more valuable to improve when ROIC already exceeds WACC: growth or the ROIC spread?

It depends on diminishing returns, but mathematically improving ROIC raises value through two channels — a higher current spread AND a lower reinvestment need for any given growth (less capital tied up per unit of NOPAT). For low-ROIC firms (ROIC near WACC), lifting ROIC is far more powerful than chasing growth; for already high-ROIC firms, profitable growth becomes the dominant lever since each incremental rupee of investment earns a wide spread.

**Q1015.** What is a real option in capital budgeting and why does NPV understate it?

A real option is managerial flexibility to alter a project after new information arrives — the option to expand, abandon, defer, or switch. Standard NPV assumes a fixed, now-or-never cash-flow plan and ignores the asymmetric value of acting on good news while limiting downside on bad news. Because option value rises with uncertainty, NPV systematically undervalues flexible projects: `Strategic NPV = Static NPV + Value of Real Options`.

**Q1016.** Describe the option to expand and how it changes a project decision.

The option to expand lets management scale up if early results are favourable — like a call option on follow-on investment. A pilot plant with a negative standalone NPV may be worth funding because it creates the right (not obligation) to build full capacity later if demand proves strong. The pilot's true value = its own NPV + value of the embedded expansion call; ignoring the latter wrongly kills strategically valuable seed investments.

**Q1017.** Explain the option to abandon and the option to defer.

The option to abandon is a put: if a project sours, management can sell assets or shut down to recover salvage value, truncating the downside — most valuable when assets have high resale/redeployment value. The option to defer is a call on waiting: delaying an irreversible investment until uncertainty resolves can beat investing today, even with positive NPV, because waiting preserves the chance to avoid a bad commitment. Both raise value as uncertainty increases.

**Q1018.** What drives the value of a real option, and which inputs matter most?

Real options are valued with option-pricing logic (Black-Scholes or binomial), so the key drivers are the underlying asset value, the exercise cost (investment), time to expiry (decision window), and especially volatility of the underlying cash flows. Higher uncertainty raises option value because it widens the favourable upside while the downside is capped by not exercising. This is why high-uncertainty R&D, mining, and pharma projects are best evaluated with a real-options lens.

**Q1019.** What is the profitability index and how is it used under capital rationing?

`PI = PV of future cash flows / Initial Investment` = 1 + NPV/Investment. Under capital rationing (a hard budget limit), you cannot fund every positive-NPV project, so you rank by PI to maximise NPV per rupee of scarce capital rather than total NPV. Example: a project with NPV INR 30 cr on INR 100 cr (PI 1.30) beats one with NPV INR 40 cr on INR 200 cr (PI 1.20) per unit of capital.

**Q1020.** When can simple PI ranking fail under capital rationing?

PI ranking is exact only for perfectly divisible projects and a single-period constraint. With indivisible (whole-or-nothing) projects, the highest-PI set may leave budget stranded, so a lower-PI combination that fully uses the budget can yield higher total NPV. Multi-period rationing requires integer/linear programming to maximise total NPV subject to each period's cash constraint, since PI cannot capture inter-temporal trade-offs.

**Q1021.** Distinguish sensitivity, scenario, and Monte-Carlo analysis in capital budgeting.

Sensitivity analysis flexes ONE input at a time (e.g., NPV vs unit price) to find which variable the project is most exposed to — but ignores correlations. Scenario analysis moves a consistent SET of inputs together into coherent cases (base/best/worst), capturing variable interactions but only a few discrete outcomes. Monte-Carlo assigns probability distributions to inputs, samples thousands of times, and produces a full NPV distribution with probabilities (e.g., P(NPV<0) = 18%), capturing the complete risk profile.

**Q1022.** What is the cash conversion cycle and how is it optimized?

`CCC = DIO + DSO - DPO` (days inventory outstanding + days sales outstanding - days payables outstanding) measures how long cash is tied up in operations. Lowering CCC frees cash: reduce inventory (JIT, better forecasting), collect receivables faster (discounts, tighter terms), and stretch payables without harming supplier relationships. A retailer moving CCC from 60 to 40 days on INR 1,000 cr daily-equivalent sales releases roughly 20 days of working capital in cash.

**Q1023.** Compute the implied annual cost of forgoing a "2/10 net 30" trade-credit discount.

Forgoing the 2% discount means paying full price 20 days later (day 30 instead of day 10). Cost per period = 2/98 = 2.04%; annualised: `(1 + 2/98)^(365/20) - 1 ≈ 44.6%` effective (or about 37.2% simple = 2.04%*365/20). Because this implicit rate dwarfs most borrowing costs, a firm with access to cheaper financing should almost always take the discount and pay on day 10.

**Q1024.** What is factoring and how does it affect working capital?

Factoring is selling accounts receivable to a third party (factor) at a discount for immediate cash, accelerating collections and shrinking DSO. In recourse factoring the seller bears default risk; in non-recourse the factor absorbs it for a higher fee. The cost is the discount/fee plus interest for the advance period; it suits firms needing liquidity faster than customers pay, trading some margin for cash and offloaded collection effort. In India, TReDS platforms (e.g., RXIL, M1xchange) digitise MSME receivable financing.

**Q1025.** Explain supply-chain finance (reverse factoring) and why both parties benefit.

In supply-chain finance the BUYER initiates the arrangement: a financier pays the supplier early at a discount based on the buyer's (typically stronger) credit rating, and the buyer repays the financier on the original due date. The supplier gets cheap early cash (priced off the buyer's low credit spread), while the buyer can extend its own DPO — improving both parties' cash conversion cycles. It differs from factoring, which is supplier-initiated and priced on the supplier's weaker credit.

**Q1026.** How does just-in-time inventory improve financial performance, and what is the risk?

JIT minimises inventory by receiving goods only as needed, cutting DIO, storage/obsolescence costs, and the working capital locked in stock — directly raising ROIC by shrinking invested capital. The trade-off is fragility: thin buffers leave the firm exposed to supply shocks, demand spikes, and logistics disruptions (as global supply-chain crises exposed). Optimal policy balances lower carrying cost against higher stock-out and disruption risk.

**Q1027.** Describe the manager-shareholder agency problem and two mechanisms that mitigate it.

Managers (agents) may pursue private benefits — empire-building, perks, excessive cash hoarding, low-risk quiet life — at the expense of shareholder (principal) value. Mechanisms include performance-linked pay/equity (aligning manager wealth with the share price), independent boards and active monitoring, debt (which disgorges free cash via fixed obligations — Jensen's free-cash-flow control), the market for corporate control (takeover threat), and disclosure. Each narrows the gap between manager incentives and owner value.

**Q1028.** Explain the shareholder-bondholder agency conflict and give two specific examples.

Once debt is in place, shareholders (via managers) can transfer wealth from bondholders. Asset substitution: swap safe assets for risky ones — shareholders capture the upside while bondholders bear added default risk. Debt overhang/underinvestment: shareholders skip positive-NPV projects whose gains accrue mainly to existing creditors. Other forms include excessive dividends and claim dilution. Bondholders price this in via higher yields and protective covenants (dividend limits, leverage caps).

**Q1029.** What governance mechanisms reduce agency costs, including India-specific ones?

Globally: independent boards, audit committees, executive equity, say-on-pay, takeover markets, and creditor covenants. India layers SEBI's LODR Regulations (mandatory independent directors, separation of chair/CEO for large firms, related-party-transaction approval), Companies Act 2013 governance norms, mandatory auditor rotation, and SEBI's stewardship code for institutional investors. These specifically target the concentrated-promoter ownership common in India, where the key conflict is controlling vs minority shareholders rather than diffuse manager-owner separation.

**Q1030.** State the static trade-off theory of capital structure and value the tax shield.

Trade-off theory says the optimal debt level balances the marginal value of the interest tax shield against the marginal expected cost of financial distress. For permanent debt, the tax-shield value is `Tc*Debt` (e.g., Tc = 25%, Debt = INR 400 cr gives INR 100 cr of value). Optimal leverage is where the present value of incremental tax shields equals the present value of incremental distress costs — implying an interior, firm-specific target ratio, not maximum debt.

**Q1031.** What are the costs of financial distress, direct and indirect?

Direct costs are out-of-pocket: legal, advisory, court, and administrative fees of restructuring or bankruptcy (often a small % of asset value). Indirect costs are larger and subtler: lost customers and suppliers (who fear non-performance), key-employee flight, fire-sale asset disposals, distracted management, foregone positive-NPV investment (debt overhang), and tighter, costlier financing. Indirect costs scale with asset intangibility and reliance on stakeholder confidence, which is why R&D/brand-heavy firms carry less debt.

**Q1032.** Why do profitable, asset-light tech firms often carry little debt despite the tax shield?

Trade-off logic predicts low leverage when expected distress costs are high relative to tax benefits. Asset-light firms have few tangible assets to pledge and high indirect distress costs (value lives in people, brand, and growth options that evaporate in distress), so the marginal cost of debt is steep. Their volatile cash flows raise default probability, and many have ample internal cash, lowering the need for debt's discipline — pushing the optimum toward equity, consistent with both trade-off and pecking-order theories.

**Q1033.** Compare US Chapter 11 reorganisation with India's IBC/NCLT process.

US Chapter 11 is debtor-in-possession: existing management generally stays and runs the firm while proposing a reorganisation plan, with an automatic stay on creditors. India's Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code (2016) is creditor-in-control: on admission by the NCLT, an Interim/Resolution Professional displaces the board, a Committee of Creditors drives the Corporate Insolvency Resolution Process, and a resolution plan must be approved within a target ~180+90 (now up to 330) days or the firm goes to liquidation. IBC thus shifts control to creditors far more decisively than Chapter 11.

**Q1034.** Explain the priority of claims (the "waterfall") in liquidation, including India's IBC order.

Liquidation proceeds follow absolute priority — senior claims are paid in full before junior ones receive anything: secured creditors, then unsecured creditors, then preferred equity, then common equity last. Under IBC Section 53, the waterfall is: insolvency-resolution/liquidation costs first, then workmen's dues (24 months) and secured creditors who relinquish security pari passu, then other employee wages, unsecured financial creditors, government dues and remaining secured-creditor shortfalls, then any remaining debts, preference shareholders, and finally equity shareholders.

**Q1035.** What does a financing choice signal under information asymmetry (signaling theory)?

When managers know more than investors, the financing they choose conveys information. Issuing equity signals managers may believe the stock is overvalued (adverse selection), so announcements often push the price DOWN; issuing debt or doing a buyback signals confidence that cash flows can service obligations or that the stock is cheap, and is read positively. This underpins the pecking order: internal funds first, then debt, then equity as a last resort to avoid the negative equity-issue signal.

**Q1036.** When does a share buyback create value versus a dividend, and what is the EPS effect?

A buyback creates value when shares are repurchased below intrinsic value, transferring wealth to remaining holders, and offers flexibility (no implied commitment) plus tax efficiency where capital gains are taxed lighter than dividends. Mechanically, buying back shares cuts the share count, so EPS rises if the after-tax earnings yield foregone on cash is below the inverse P/E — but a buyback above fair value destroys value regardless of the cosmetic EPS lift. In India, buybacks are now taxed in the company's hands (post-2024 changes shifting the burden), altering the dividend-vs-buyback calculus.

**Q1037.** What is project finance with an SPV, and why is it structured that way?

Project finance funds a large standalone asset (toll road, power plant, port) through a special purpose vehicle whose debt is repaid primarily from the project's own cash flows, with limited or no recourse to the sponsors' balance sheets. The SPV ring-fences risk (sponsor failure does not sink the project and vice versa), enables high leverage against contracted cash flows, and isolates lenders' claims to project assets. It suits capital-intensive infrastructure with predictable, often contracted revenues — common in India's PPP and renewable-energy financing.
