**Q1038.** What is a leveraged recapitalisation and why might a firm do one?

A leveraged recap raises a large slug of new debt and returns the proceeds to shareholders via a special dividend or buyback, sharply increasing leverage without changing the assets. Motives: capture more interest tax shields, return excess cash, impose debt discipline on management (reducing free-cash-flow agency costs), and sometimes deter a takeover by loading up debt. The trade-off is higher financial risk and distress probability, so it suits stable-cash-flow firms that were previously underleveraged.

**Q1039.** Why do firms hold cash despite the drag on ROIC, and what is the optimal cash policy?

Firms hold cash for the transactions motive (smooth operating outflows), the precautionary motive (buffer against shocks and funding-market freezes), and to preserve financial flexibility for future investment/acquisition options without issuing securities under bad conditions. Cash earns below the cost of capital and dilutes ROIC, and excess cash invites agency problems (wasteful spending), so the optimum balances the convenience/insurance value of liquidity against this carrying cost. High-uncertainty, R&D-intensive, and financially constrained firms optimally hold more cash.

<a name="J2"></a>

## J2. ESG, Sustainable Finance & Fintech  <span class="qrange">(Q1040-Q1064)</span>

**Q1040.** What does ESG stand for, and what kinds of factors fall under each pillar?

ESG stands for Environmental, Social, and Governance — a framework for assessing a company's non-financial risks and impacts alongside financial analysis. Environmental covers carbon emissions, energy use, water, waste, and biodiversity; Social covers labour practices, health and safety, diversity, community relations, and product safety; Governance covers board independence and structure, executive pay, shareholder rights, audit quality, and anti-corruption controls. The premise is that these factors can be financially material and that integrating them improves risk-adjusted decisions, not merely ethics.

**Q1041.** What is the difference between negative/exclusionary screening and positive/best-in-class screening?

Negative (exclusionary) screening removes companies or sectors that fail an ethical or risk test — for example excluding tobacco, weapons, thermal coal, or gambling from a portfolio. Positive (best-in-class) screening instead keeps a sector but selects the companies with the strongest ESG performance relative to their peers, e.g. holding the best-rated oil major rather than excluding the whole energy sector. Negative screening shrinks the investable universe by rule; best-in-class tilts within it toward leaders.

**Q1042.** Name the main approaches to ESG/responsible investing.

The commonly cited approaches are: (1) negative/exclusionary screening; (2) positive/best-in-class selection; (3) ESG integration — systematically factoring ESG data into traditional financial analysis and valuation; (4) thematic investing — targeting a theme like clean energy or water; (5) impact investing — seeking measurable, intentional social/environmental outcomes alongside return; and (6) active ownership/engagement and proxy voting. Many funds blend several. These categories are reflected in the UN PRI framework and surveys by bodies like the GSIA (Global Sustainable Investment Alliance).

**Q1043.** How does impact investing differ from ESG integration?

ESG integration is a risk-management lens: it incorporates ESG data to sharpen valuation and avoid mispriced risks, but the goal remains financial return. Impact investing is intentional — it deliberately seeks measurable, additional social or environmental outcomes (e.g. clean water access, affordable housing) and holds itself accountable for measuring that impact, while still targeting a financial return that can range from below-market (concessionary) to market rate. The distinguishing features of impact investing are intentionality, additionality, and measurement of the outcome.

**Q1044.** What is greenwashing, and why is it a concern in sustainable finance?

Greenwashing is making a product, fund, or company appear more environmentally or socially responsible than it actually is — for instance labelling a fund "sustainable" while it holds material fossil-fuel exposure, or touting a vague "net zero" pledge with no credible plan. It misleads investors, misallocates capital away from genuinely sustainable activities, and erodes trust in the whole ESG market. Regulators have responded with disclosure rules such as the EU's SFDR fund classifications and SEBI's disclosure norms for ESG mutual fund schemes in India, plus fund-naming requirements that mandate minimum sustainable holdings.

**Q1045.** Why do ESG ratings often disagree across providers like MSCI, Sustainalytics, and S&P?

Different providers use different frameworks, indicators, weights, and data sources, so the same company can score well with one and poorly with another. Studies (e.g. Berg, Koelbl and Rigobon's "Aggregate Confusion") attribute the divergence mainly to differences in scope (which issues are measured), measurement (how an indicator is proxied), and weighting (how issues are combined). Correlations between major ESG ratings are roughly 0.5-0.6, far below the ~0.99 seen across credit ratings — meaning ESG scores are noisier and less comparable, which complicates benchmarking and can mask greenwashing.

**Q1046.** What does "materiality" mean in an ESG context, and what is double materiality?

Materiality refers to whether an ESG factor is significant enough to affect a company's financial value or an investor's decision — the same threshold concept used in financial reporting. Single (financial) materiality asks how sustainability issues affect the company's value (outside-in). Double materiality, central to the EU's CSRD, adds the reverse view: how the company's activities affect society and the environment (inside-out). SASB/ISSB standards focus on financially material issues that are often industry-specific — water is material for a beverage maker, data privacy for a software firm.

**Q1047.** What is a green bond, and what distinguishes it from an ordinary bond?

A green bond is a debt instrument whose proceeds are earmarked (ring-fenced) exclusively for environmentally beneficial projects — renewable energy, clean transport, green buildings, water management. It has the same credit risk and ranking as the issuer's other senior debt; the difference is the use-of-proceeds commitment plus reporting on how the money was deployed and its environmental impact. Most follow ICMA's Green Bond Principles, often with a second-party opinion to verify alignment, and India's SEBI has its own framework for green debt securities.

**Q1048.** How do sustainability-linked bonds/loans differ from green or social bonds?

Use-of-proceeds bonds (green, social, sustainability bonds) restrict where the raised money is spent. Sustainability-linked bonds and loans (SLBs/SLLs) place no restriction on use of proceeds — instead the financial terms are tied to the issuer's overall performance against predefined KPIs and sustainability performance targets (SPTs), such as cutting emissions by a set percentage by a target date. If the issuer misses the target, the coupon typically steps up (a penalty); they follow ICMA's Sustainability-Linked Bond Principles. The structure rewards entity-level transformation rather than project earmarking.

**Q1049.** Conceptually, how does carbon pricing work, and what are the two main mechanisms?

Carbon pricing puts a cost on greenhouse-gas emissions so emitters internalise the externality they impose on society. The two main mechanisms are a carbon tax (government sets a fixed price per tonne of CO2-equivalent and the market determines the quantity) and a cap-and-trade emissions trading system (the government caps total allowable emissions, issues allowances, and lets firms trade them so the market sets the price). The EU ETS is the largest cap-and-trade system; India has notified a Carbon Credit Trading Scheme (CCTS) to develop a domestic compliance market.

**Q1050.** What is the difference between compliance carbon markets and voluntary carbon markets?

Compliance markets are created by law or regulation — emitters subject to a cap (like the EU ETS) must surrender allowances for their emissions, and trading is mandatory for covered entities. Voluntary carbon markets let companies and individuals buy carbon credits (each representing one tonne of CO2 reduced or removed, e.g. from reforestation) on their own initiative to offset emissions toward voluntary net-zero claims. Voluntary credits face persistent concerns over additionality, permanence, double-counting, and quality, which is why integrity bodies like ICVCM have emerged to set standards.

**Q1051.** Distinguish physical climate risk from transition climate risk in finance.

Physical risk is the financial damage from the physical effects of climate change: acute events (floods, cyclones, wildfires) and chronic shifts (rising sea levels, heat stress, changing rainfall) that destroy assets, disrupt supply chains, and raise insurance losses. Transition risk is the financial risk from the move to a low-carbon economy — policy changes, carbon pricing, new technology, and shifting consumer preferences that can erode the value of carbon-intensive businesses. A coastal factory faces physical risk; a coal-fired power producer faces transition risk, and many firms face both.

**Q1052.** What are stranded assets?

Stranded assets are assets that suffer unanticipated or premature write-downs, devaluation, or conversion to liabilities because of changes such as climate policy, carbon pricing, technological disruption, or shifting demand. The classic example is fossil-fuel reserves or infrastructure (coal mines, oil fields, coal plants) that may become uneconomic to extract or operate under aggressive decarbonisation, leaving "unburnable carbon" on balance sheets. The concept matters for valuation because reserves booked today may never be monetised, implying impairment risk for energy and resource companies.

**Q1053.** What were the TCFD recommendations, and how do they relate to the ISSB standards?

The Task Force on Climate-related Financial Disclosures (TCFD), set up by the Financial Stability Board, recommended that companies disclose climate-related risks and opportunities across four pillars: governance, strategy (including scenario analysis), risk management, and metrics and targets (including Scope 1, 2, and where relevant Scope 3 emissions). The TCFD framework has now been consolidated into the IFRS Foundation's ISSB standards — IFRS S1 (general sustainability disclosures) and IFRS S2 (climate), which fully incorporate the TCFD recommendations and are becoming the global baseline; the TCFD itself was disbanded in 2023 after its work was handed to the ISSB.

**Q1054.** What is SEBI's BRSR, and who must comply with it in India?

BRSR (Business Responsibility and Sustainability Reporting) is SEBI's mandatory ESG disclosure framework for listed companies in India, replacing the earlier Business Responsibility Report. It requires reporting against the nine principles of the National Guidelines on Responsible Business Conduct (NGRBC), covering environmental, social, and governance metrics. It is mandatory for the top 1,000 listed companies by market capitalisation, and a subset of quantitative, assured "BRSR Core" KPIs is being phased in with reasonable assurance for the largest companies, alongside value-chain disclosures on a comply-or-explain basis.

**Q1055.** Does ESG investing help or hurt returns — what does the evidence say?

The evidence is genuinely mixed and depends on approach and period. Proponents argue ESG integration manages downside risk and that good governance correlates with quality; some meta-studies find a neutral-to-slightly-positive link between ESG and financial performance. Critics note that exclusionary screening shrinks the universe and can reduce diversification, that much outperformance reflects sector/factor tilts (e.g. underweight energy, overweight tech), and that returns are period-dependent. The honest answer is that ESG is best framed as a risk lens rather than a guaranteed alpha source, and results vary by implementation.

**Q1056.** How does fiduciary duty interact with ESG considerations?

A fiduciary (e.g. a pension trustee or asset manager) must act in the best financial interest of beneficiaries. The modern interpretation — supported by reports like the UN/UNEP "Fiduciary Duty in the 21st Century" — is that considering financially material ESG factors is consistent with, and may even be required by, fiduciary duty, since ignoring material risks would be imprudent. The tension arises when ESG is used to pursue non-financial goals at the expense of returns; in the US this has become politicised, with debate over whether ERISA fiduciaries may weigh ESG, generally permitted only where it is financially relevant or as a tie-breaker.

**Q1057.** Give a high-level overview of the main verticals within fintech.

Fintech spans: digital payments (UPI, wallets, cards, real-time rails); lending and credit, including digital lending and BNPL (buy now, pay later); neobanks (digital-only banks, often partnering with a licensed bank); wealthtech and robo-advisors (automated, algorithm-driven portfolio management); insurtech (digital insurance distribution and underwriting); regtech (technology for compliance, KYC/AML, reporting); and the infrastructure layer — payment gateways, APIs, account aggregators, and core-banking platforms. The common thread is using technology to make financial services cheaper, faster, and more accessible.

**Q1058.** What is the "India Stack" and UPI, and why are they significant?

India Stack is a set of open APIs and digital public infrastructure built around identity (Aadhaar), payments, and data, enabling presence-less, paperless, cashless services. UPI (Unified Payments Interface), run by NPCI, is its flagship real-time payment system that lets users send money instantly between bank accounts using a virtual ID (VPA) across apps, with no per-transaction fee for users (P2P). UPI is significant for its massive scale — billions of transactions per month — interoperability, and role in financial inclusion, and it has become a model studied by other countries.

**Q1059.** What are robo-advisors, and what are their main limitations?

Robo-advisors are digital platforms that build and manage investment portfolios algorithmically based on a questionnaire about goals, risk tolerance, and horizon, typically using low-cost ETFs and automatic rebalancing at a fraction of a human advisor's fee. Their strengths are low cost, accessibility, discipline, and tax features like loss harvesting (in markets where it applies). Limitations include limited handling of complex or unusual situations, reliance on the quality of risk-profiling questions, model and herding risk in stressed markets, and weaker behavioural coaching during panics, when human advice often adds the most value.

**Q1060.** At a conceptual level, what are blockchain and DLT, and what finance use cases are realistic?

A blockchain is a type of distributed ledger (DLT) — a shared, append-only database replicated across many participants, where transactions are grouped into cryptographically linked blocks and validated by consensus rather than a single central authority, making records tamper-evident. Realistic finance use cases include securities settlement (potentially shortening or atomising settlement via delivery-versus-payment), tokenisation of assets, trade finance, and smart contracts — self-executing code that automatically enforces agreed terms. The benefits are reduced reconciliation, fewer intermediaries, and transparency; the trade-offs are scalability, governance, legal enforceability, and integration with existing systems.

**Q1061.** How should one think about cryptocurrencies, stablecoins, and CBDCs as a balanced finance topic?

Cryptocurrencies like Bitcoin are decentralised digital assets not backed by any issuer; they are highly volatile, used for speculation and some payments, and raise concerns over consumer protection, illicit use, and energy (for proof-of-work). Stablecoins aim to hold a steady value by pegging to a fiat currency or basket, usually backed by reserves — useful for crypto trading and cross-border transfers, but exposing holders to reserve-quality and de-pegging (run) risk, prompting regulation like the EU's MiCA. CBDCs (central bank digital currencies) are sovereign digital money issued by a central bank — a direct liability of the state — offering stability and policy control; India's RBI is piloting the Digital Rupee (e-rupee) in wholesale and retail forms.

**Q1062.** What is open banking, and how do Account Aggregators implement the idea in India?

Open banking is a framework where customers can securely share their financial data with third-party providers, via standardised APIs and explicit consent, to access better products like tailored loans or aggregated dashboards. In India this is implemented through the RBI-regulated Account Aggregator (AA) framework: licensed AAs are consent-managers (not data holders) that move data between Financial Information Providers (e.g. banks) and Financial Information Users (e.g. lenders) only with the customer's granular, revocable consent, built on a standardised consent artefact. The aim is to reduce friction in lending and advisory while keeping the customer in control of their data.

**Q1063.** Where is AI/ML used in finance, and what is model risk?

AI/ML is used in credit scoring (assessing default probability from richer/alternative data), algorithmic and high-frequency trading, fraud and anomaly detection, AML transaction monitoring, robo-advisory, document processing, and customer chatbots. Model risk is the risk of loss from decisions based on models that are wrong or misused — from flawed assumptions, biased or non-representative training data, overfitting, or deployment outside the conditions the model was built for. Sound practice (e.g. the US Fed/OCC SR 11-7 guidance) requires model validation, governance, explainability, monitoring for drift, and controls against discriminatory bias — especially important as opaque ML models can be hard to interpret and can entrench unfair outcomes.

**Q1064.** What is financial inclusion, and how have fintech and policy advanced it in India?

Financial inclusion means ensuring affordable, timely access to basic financial services — a transaction account, savings, credit, insurance, and payments — for all, especially underserved and low-income populations. In India the JAM trinity (Jan Dhan bank accounts, Aadhaar identity, Mobile) plus UPI enabled hundreds of millions of new accounts and direct benefit transfers that cut leakage, while account aggregators and alternative-data lending expand credit to thin-file borrowers. Inclusion supports economic development and is measured by indicators like the World Bank Findex and the RBI's Financial Inclusion Index, though challenges remain around account dormancy, digital literacy, and consumer protection against fraud and over-indebtedness.
